NDHCE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

Case C001

Aleta Nowak is 79 and attends an Ontario clinic with her adult daughter. Her medical history lists moderate Alzheimer disease, hypertension and osteoarthritis. She greets the hygienist warmly, says she is at the dentist, and reports that her gums bleed when she brushes. Assessment shows generalized 4 to 6 mm probing depths, heavy supragingival calculus and bleeding at most sites. The daughter says she now manages her mother's finances and asks the hygienist to book a full course of scaling and root planing today.

  1. The correct answer is B (Assess whether the client herself can understand and appreciate this particular treatment decision): capacity is presumed in every adult and is specific to the decision at hand, so a diagnosis of Alzheimer disease does not by itself remove it. The hygienist must first find out whether this client can understand the relevant information and appreciate the consequences of consenting or refusing. An attorney for property has authority over finances only, never over personal care decisions. Capacity also fluctuates, so it is assessed at the time of the decision rather than handed to another practitioner.
  2. The correct answer is D (Her children, who rank next on the provincial substitute decision maker list): provinces set a ranked hierarchy that begins with a court appointed guardian, then an attorney for personal care, then a spouse or partner, then adult children. With no guardian, no attorney for personal care and no living spouse, authority passes to the three adult children together. Attending appointments and handling household finances confers no legal authority to consent on its own. Care is not simply abandoned either, because a substitute exists for exactly this situation.
  3. The correct answer is A (Follow the capable wish the client expressed earlier, because prior capable wishes govern): a substitute decision maker must act on the client's known wishes expressed while capable, and only where no such wish is known may they turn to a best interests analysis. The recalled statement about wanting nothing beyond cleanings and refusing needles is exactly such a wish. Counting votes among the children has no legal standing, because substitutes are bound by the client's own values rather than by family consensus. A clinician's recommendation informs the decision but does not replace the client's prior instruction.
  4. The correct answer is C (The capacity finding, who consented, what was discussed, what was declined and why): the record must show what was assessed, what was recommended, what the client was told, who authorized the care and what was refused. Documenting the capacity assessment and the identity of the substitute is what makes the authority for this appointment defensible years later. A brief note about a routine cleaning omits every element that would actually be examined. A signed form filed on its own is only evidence that a conversation happened, and it is not the consent itself.
  5. The correct answer is A (Stop immediately, because a client's objection during care must always be respected): consent is a continuing process and may be withdrawn at any moment, including mid procedure. Substitute authorization permits care to begin, but it does not license continued physical contact once the client herself objects by word or gesture. Finishing the quadrant would mean proceeding against an expressed refusal, which can amount to battery as well as professional misconduct. Recruiting a family member to press her to continue undermines the voluntariness that valid consent depends on.
Case C002

Kiran is 6 years old and attends a community clinic in New Brunswick with an adult who identifies himself as a family friend. Kiran has rampant untreated decay, a healing bruise on the left cheek and a torn upper labial frenum. He flinches when the hygienist adjusts the chair light and answers questions in single words. The chart shows four broken appointments in eight months. The adult says Kiran fell off a bicycle last week and asks that nothing be written down about it.

  1. The correct answer is C (Reasonable grounds to suspect a child needs protection already require her to report): every Canadian province and territory sets the threshold at suspicion rather than proof, and the duty is triggered as soon as reasonable grounds exist. The hygienist is not required to be certain, and she is not the investigator. Gathering proof first delays protection and is not part of the duty. Passing the concern to the dentist and stopping there does not discharge a personal statutory obligation, and no caregiver permission is needed to report.
  2. The correct answer is B (Make the report herself, because the duty belongs to the person who suspects): the reporting duty is personal, cannot be delegated to a supervisor or employer, and is not extinguished by a colleague's disagreement. Telling the dentist and stopping there leaves the child unprotected and exposes the hygienist to penalties for failing to report. Reporting the dentist to the regulator may be considered later, but it does nothing for this child today. Waiting for further injuries to appear accepts a foreseeable risk of harm that the statute exists to prevent.
  3. The correct answer is D (Record the findings objectively, because the record must be accurate and complete): the chart is the legal record of what was assessed and found, and a caregiver has no authority to dictate what a clinician may record. Omitting the injuries would falsify the record, which is itself professional misconduct, and would remove the very evidence a child protection investigation may depend on. Keeping the findings in a private note outside the chart hides them from anyone lawfully entitled to the record. A verbal account to an investigator does not replace a contemporaneous written entry.
  4. The correct answer is D (Yellow-green bruise 2 cm on left cheek, torn upper labial frenum, adult reported bicycle fall): the entry should record objective observations, measurements and quoted explanations, leaving conclusions about cause to the investigating authority. Written this way the note stays reliable evidence whether or not abuse is later confirmed. Stating that the injuries were inflicted by the accompanying adult records a conclusion the hygienist cannot support. Words such as neglectful are judgmental rather than observed, and a vague note about an unclear facial injury loses the detail that makes the record useful.
  5. The correct answer is A (Good faith reporting is protected, and a fresh suspicion later requires a new report): child protection statutes shield anyone who reports in good faith from liability, and the reporting duty is ongoing rather than spent by a single call. New findings at a later visit create a new obligation to report. Reporting where a statute compels it is a recognized exception to confidentiality, so no breach occurs at all. Transferring the child's care to avoid involvement abandons a client who is already at risk.
Case C003

A registered dental hygienist in Nova Scotia receives a letter from the registrar of her regulatory college. A client has complained that she was billed for four quadrants of root planing she does not remember receiving, that costs were never explained to her, and that she was never told a periodontal referral had been recommended. The letter asks for a written response within thirty days and states that an investigator may attend the practice. The hygienist has been registered eleven years with no prior complaints.

  1. The correct answer is C (Respond in writing within the stated deadline and notify her liability insurer): a registrant must reply to her own regulator, and failing to respond is itself defined as professional misconduct that turns a small matter into a serious one. Notifying the liability insurer promptly protects her coverage and secures advice. Ignoring the registrar on an employer's say so leaves her personally exposed, because accountability to the college cannot be transferred to an employer. Contacting the complainant to seek withdrawal risks being seen as interference with the investigation.
  2. The correct answer is B (The undocumented discussions will generally be treated as conversations that never occurred): in both regulatory and civil proceedings an absent entry is normally treated as care or a discussion that did not happen, and the burden falls on the registrant. Fee discussions, the referral recommendation and the client's agreement all needed to be recorded at the time. Treatment codes show only what was billed, not what the client was told or consented to. Adding the missing detail now, after an investigation has begun, would be altering the record rather than making a properly identified late entry.
  3. The correct answer is A (It may also caution the registrant or require remediation such as further education): a screening committee has a graded range of dispositions, including taking no further action, issuing advice or a caution, requiring remedial education or a practice monitor, and referring specified allegations onward. That range is precisely why most complaints never reach a hearing. Deciding whether allegations are proven and imposing a suspension are functions of a discipline panel, not of the screening committee. Referral is also discretionary and turns on the committee's own assessment of the file.
  4. The correct answer is C (The proceedings are independent, and one incident can produce findings in both): regulatory proceedings decide professional misconduct against the standards of practice, while a civil action decides negligence against the standard of a reasonably prudent practitioner, and neither binds the other. A single episode of care can therefore produce a college finding, a court finding, both or neither. A civil action is not automatically stayed while a college matter runs. Negligence also requires duty, breach, causation and real damage, so tooth loss alone proves nothing.
  5. The correct answer is D (It meets one registration requirement, and registration comes from the provincial regulator): passing the NDHCE makes a candidate certified, not registered, and each provincial or territorial regulator uses that result as only one of several registration requirements. The FDHRC develops and administers the examination but is not a regulator, so it registers nobody and issues no licence. There is no national licence in dental hygiene, because health professions fall under provincial jurisdiction. Jurisprudence assessments, good character declarations, liability insurance and fees are all set separately by the regulator.
  6. The correct answer is B (To the provincial regulatory college, which handles complaints about its own registrants): the college holds the statutory mandate to receive complaints, investigate, screen and where warranted refer matters to discipline, and it does so in the public interest. The Canadian Dental Hygienists Association is a professional and advocacy body with no authority to investigate or discipline anyone, and confusing the two is a classic distractor. The FDHRC administers the certification examination and sets no practice standards. A practice owner may address service concerns internally but cannot resolve a regulatory complaint.
  7. The correct answer is C (Decline for now, and seek education and supervised practice before performing it): three limits stack, and legislated scope, personal competence and the regulator's standards must all be satisfied before an act is performed. Six years without performing the procedure means current competence is genuinely in doubt, so the right response is to stop, update her knowledge and practise under supervision. Employer permission can narrow those limits but can never widen them into competence she does not currently hold. A dentist's order does not transfer her personal accountability for her own clinical acts.
  8. The correct answer is A (Refuse, because performing an act outside the legislated scope is her own misconduct): legislated scope is set by provincial law, and an employer cannot expand it by instruction, contract or assurance. If she performs the act the violation is hers as well as his, and accountability cannot be transferred to a supervisor. Client consent cannot authorize an act the practitioner has no legal authority to perform. Regulators do not issue individual temporary permissions to work outside scope while unlawful practice continues.
  9. The correct answer is D (The program is remedial, and its information is kept separate from the complaints stream): quality assurance exists to identify gaps and support continuing competence rather than to punish, so regulators deliberately wall it off from complaints and discipline. That separation is what makes honest self assessment safe and useful. Self assessment answers are confidential to the program and never appear on the public register. Withholding real gaps also defeats the purpose, because the learning plan is supposed to be built directly from the gaps identified.
  10. The correct answer is B (The learning activities she chose do not address the gaps her self assessment identified): a continuing competence program expects a written learning plan tied to the gaps the registrant actually found, so hours spent entirely elsewhere do not close the identified deficit in caries risk assessment. The assessor looks for that link between self assessment, plan and activity. Whitening education is legitimate learning and can be counted, so the problem is relevance rather than eligibility. Random audit selection carries no implication of concern, since selection is by chance and not by suspicion.
  11. The correct answer is A (Respect the refusal, document what was explained, and continue to offer the therapy): once a capable client has been told the diagnosis, the options and the consequences of declining in plain language, autonomy prevails over beneficence even when the decision looks unwise. The obligation is to record the recommendation, the information given and the refusal, and to keep the door open at future visits. Treating him despite the refusal would be battery as well as misconduct. Recruiting a spouse to apply pressure attacks the voluntariness that valid consent requires.
  12. The correct answer is C (Tell the client now, document the event fully, and arrange prompt retrieval of it): veracity requires disclosing your own error to the client, and a retained metal fragment in the sulcus is a material finding the client needs to know about and act on. Prompt disclosure and retrieval also limit the harm, which is what non maleficence demands. Concealment on a colleague's advice compounds the original error and is itself professional misconduct. Reporting the dentist before speaking to the client puts a collegial dispute ahead of the person actually affected.
  13. The correct answer is D (Explain that the record must be accurate, and describe who may actually access it): the record obligation prevails over a client request to omit history, because substance use affects clinical decisions and the chart is a legal record. Explaining the real access rules usually resolves the fear, since privacy legislation limits use to the purpose collected and gives the client rights over her own information. Agreeing to omit the history would falsify the record and put later care at risk. Shadow notebooks and verbal only handovers create a hidden record that satisfies neither privacy law nor the standard of documentation.
  14. The correct answer is B (A privacy breach occurred, because she accessed the record without a care related reason): access to personal health information is limited to what is needed for a purpose connected to the person's care, and curiosity is not such a purpose. Snooping is a breach at the moment of access, whether or not anything is repeated afterward. Employment at the clinic grants no general entitlement to read charts, which is why audit trails and individual logins exist. The breach also does not depend on the affected person learning of it or objecting.
  15. The correct answer is A (Ontario's health privacy statute governs, having been declared substantially similar federally): where a province has health privacy legislation that the federal government has declared substantially similar, that provincial statute governs the health sector in place of the federal law. Ontario's Personal Health Information Protection Act is the best known example of exactly that arrangement. The federal law does reach personal information handled in commercial activity, but it steps aside for the health sector here. Privacy in health care is a legal duty backed by statute, not a matter of ethics alone.
  16. The correct answer is D (Notify the affected clients and the appropriate privacy commissioner about the breach): Canadian health privacy statutes require notification of significant breaches to the affected individuals and to the relevant commissioner, and an unencrypted device holding hundreds of clinical records qualifies. Notification duties are triggered by the risk of harm rather than by proof that data was read. Telling only the police addresses the theft but not the privacy obligation owed to clients. Waiting for clients to ask would defeat the purpose, which is to let them protect themselves promptly.
  17. The correct answer is C (Disclosure to a regulator conducting an investigation is a recognized exception): confidentiality has defined limits, and disclosure to a regulator investigating a registrant is one of them, alongside statutory reporting duties and court orders. The investigator is appointed under legislation and may attend the practice and copy the relevant records. Requiring individual client consent would make investigations impossible and is not what the statute provides. Supplying edited portions or self prepared summaries substitutes the registrant's judgment for the investigator's lawful access to the original record.
  18. The correct answer is B (Disclose to the appropriate authority, because a serious risk to an identifiable person exists): confidentiality yields where there is a serious and imminent risk of harm to an identifiable person, and a named target, a described workplace and a purchased weapon meet that threshold. Only the minimum information needed to avert the harm should be disclosed. Keeping the confidence would leave a foreseeable and preventable danger unaddressed. Routing the decision through the dentist or demanding a written threat both introduce delay in exactly the situation where delay is most dangerous.
  19. The correct answer is A (Report to the registrar of that professional's college, without naming the client): the duty arises when a regulated health professional obtains, in the course of practising, reasonable grounds to believe another regulated professional has sexually abused a client, and it runs to the registrar of that professional's college. The client's name is not included unless the client consents, which is exactly what protects her here. The duty is mandatory and does not depend on the client agreeing to be identified or complaining herself. Sexual abuse is defined broadly and includes remarks of a sexual nature as well as touching.
  20. The correct answer is C (Decline all three and explain the professional limits of the clinical relationship): boundary crossings usually begin small and well intentioned, and a valuable gift, a personal social media connection and late night personal messaging each blur the line between a therapeutic and a personal relationship. Naming the limit kindly and early preserves the relationship rather than damaging it. Treating the tickets as the only problem misses that the social media contact and the messaging carry the same risk. Abruptly transferring the client without explanation abandons her and leaves the underlying issue unaddressed.
  21. The correct answer is D (Recognize each second role, keep clinical objectivity, and refer when care would suffer): dual relationships are not automatically prohibited and are unavoidable in small or rural communities, where refusing everyone known socially would leave people without care. The professional response is to name the second role, guard objectivity and the client's freedom to refuse, and refer elsewhere when the overlap would compromise care. Treating them as raising no concern ignores a real risk to objectivity and confidentiality. A signed waiver does not remove a boundary risk that belongs to the practitioner to manage.
  22. The correct answer is B (A conflict of interest that should be avoided or, where unavoidable, disclosed): a conflict exists whenever a personal or financial interest could reasonably be seen to influence professional judgment, whether or not it actually did. Commission on retail sales is a textbook example, and her own observation that her recommendations have shifted confirms the influence. The right sequence is to avoid the conflict where possible, disclose where avoidance is impossible, and never let the interest drive the recommendation. Neither a client's freedom to decline nor a product's effectiveness cures the underlying financial incentive.
  23. The correct answer is C (She may consent herself if she understands the treatment and its likely consequences): most Canadian provinces outside Quebec use the mature minor approach rather than a fixed age, and Ontario's health care consent legislation sets no minimum age at all. This client has demonstrated understanding of the procedure and of what happens without it, which is the actual test. Age of majority governs other legal matters, not capacity to consent to health care. Capacity is decision specific rather than limited to risk free procedures, and delaying care for a parent's attendance is unnecessary here.
  24. The correct answer is A (Consent is not valid, because the required information was never actually provided): informed consent requires the nature of the proposed treatment, its benefits, material risks, reasonable alternatives including no treatment, the consequences of declining and the cost, together with a genuine opportunity to ask questions. This client asked directly and was given nothing. A signed form is only evidence that a conversation occurred and is never the consent itself. Consent is also a continuing process rather than a per visit signature, and scaling is invasive enough to require express consent.
  25. The correct answer is B (Voluntariness, because consent must be free of pressure from the practice or others): valid consent must be given freely, without pressure from the practitioner, the employer or the family, and an exaggerated warning combined with a same day fee deadline is exactly such pressure. The manipulation is what makes any resulting agreement invalid. Capacity is unaffected here, because the client can still understand the information and appreciate the consequences. The consent still relates to the treatment proposed, and the problem is coercive framing rather than an excess of disclosure.
  26. The correct answer is D (It is insufficient, because radiation exposure requires an express informed consent discussion): implied consent covers only low risk, routine and non invasive steps, while anything involving radiation, material risk, invasiveness or a significant fee requires express consent after a real discussion. Reclining and opening the mouth shows cooperation, not an informed decision about exposure. Radiographs do carry material risks and alternatives that a reasonable client would want discussed, including the option of deferring them. A signed form at every visit is not the requirement either, because the conversation rather than the signature is what constitutes consent.
  27. The correct answer is A (Use the qualified interpreter service and record in the chart who interpreted): informed consent requires that the client receive the information in a language she understands and have a real chance to ask questions, so a qualified interpreter should be used wherever one is available. Recording who interpreted documents that the requirement was met. Using a teenage child risks inaccurate translation, breaches the client's privacy within her own family and places an unfair burden on the child. Postponing care or handing over an English form leaves the client uninformed and untreated.
  28. The correct answer is C (Object, because routine practices apply to all clients and this policy discriminates): human rights codes prohibit discrimination in services on grounds that include disability, which covers a bloodborne infection, and the codes reach client care as well as employment. Routine practices already treat every client as potentially infectious, so segregating clients by diagnosis adds no safety and simply stigmatizes them. Booking at the end of the day for this reason is differential treatment rather than a precaution. Asking a client to consent to a discriminatory arrangement does not make it lawful.
  29. The correct answer is B (Cultural safety is judged by the client, and requires examining one's own power and bias): cultural safety goes beyond awareness and sensitivity by asking the practitioner to examine their own power, bias and assumptions, and it leaves the judgment of whether an encounter felt safe to the client. It is supported by cultural humility, which is lifelong self reflection rather than a single workshop. Learning a list of customs is cultural awareness and risks stereotyping. Matching clients to practitioners by background is neither required nor achievable, and it does not address the power imbalance itself.
  30. The correct answer is D (Strike through so it stays readable, add the correction, then date and initial it): an existing entry is never altered or obliterated, because the original text must remain legible to show what was recorded and when it was changed. A single line through the error, the correction beside it, and a dated initial gives a transparent and defensible record. Erasing or covering the entry destroys evidence and can be treated as falsifying a record. Leaving a known error uncorrected until a later visit leaves inaccurate clinical data guiding care in the meantime.
Case C004

Priya is a dental hygienist registered in Manitoba for eleven years. Her regulatory college has selected her for a routine practice assessment under its quality assurance program. She completes the required self assessment against the standards of practice and identifies that she has not placed a pit and fissure sealant in more than four years, that her charting of periodontal findings is inconsistent, and that her only documented continuing education in three years was a lunch and learn hosted by a product supplier.

  1. The correct answer is B (A directed learning plan with agreed activities and a follow up review): quality assurance is remedial and forward looking, so an identified gap normally leads to an agreed learning plan with specific activities and a later check that the gap has closed. Noticing a gap does not close it, so treating identification as the end point fails the purpose of the program. Suspension and discipline referrals belong to the separate complaints and discipline stream and are not triggered by an honest self assessment.
  2. The correct answer is D (Decline the appointments until she completes education and supervised practice in sealant placement): three limits stack on every registrant, namely legislated scope, personal competence and the regulator's standards. Sealant placement sits inside the legislated scope of a dental hygienist, but Priya has identified that her own current competence in the technique has lapsed, and scope alone does not authorize her to proceed. The correct remedy when a registrant has fallen behind in a technique is to stop performing it and obtain education plus supervised practice before resuming. Accountability is personal, so no written promise from the practice owner transfers responsibility away from her.
  3. The correct answer is A (Measurable learning goals tied directly to the specific gaps her self assessment found): a learning plan in a continuing competence program is the bridge between the gaps a registrant identifies and the activities she undertakes to close them. Goals must be specific and measurable so that the registrant and the assessor can both tell whether the gap has actually been addressed. Listing every available course is not a plan, because it is not tied to any identified need. Building the plan around employer profitability or around strengths she already has would leave the charting inconsistency and the outdated sealant technique exactly where they are.
  4. The correct answer is C (Whether the content was evidence informed and addressed a gap she actually identified): continuing competence credit turns on the quality and relevance of the learning, not on the venue or the sponsor. A supplier session promoting one manufacturer's product carries an obvious commercial interest, so Priya must judge whether the content was evidence informed and whether it addressed a documented gap in her own practice. Manufacturer hosted sessions are not banned outright, and a certificate bearing a company logo proves attendance rather than educational value. Attending during paid working hours says nothing at all about whether her competence improved.
  5. The correct answer is B (Provide the records, because disclosure to a regulator investigating or assessing is permitted): health privacy legislation across Canada permits disclosure of personal health information without client consent where a regulatory college is carrying out its statutory functions, including quality assurance assessment and investigation. An assessor appointed by the college may attend the practice and review or copy records for that purpose. Requiring individual client consent would make regulation impossible and is not what the legislation demands. Stripping out the clinical findings would defeat the assessment entirely, and the practice owner cannot override a registrant's own duty to cooperate with her regulator.
Case C005

A dental hygienist working in a Nova Scotia clinic learns that an unencrypted clinic laptop holding an offline copy of about four hundred client charts was stolen from a staff member's car overnight. The same week she notices a coworker opening the electronic chart of a neighbour who has no appointment booked that day. Three of the five operatory computers use a shared staff login, and the practice owner is away on vacation for the next two weeks.

  1. The correct answer is A (Contain the breach, determine what information was exposed, and begin notifying those affected): breach response follows a standard sequence of containing the incident, evaluating what information was involved and how sensitive it is, notifying affected individuals and the appropriate commissioner, and then preventing recurrence. Roughly four hundred unencrypted charts is a serious exposure and the response cannot wait two weeks for the owner to return from vacation. Buying a replacement laptop addresses recurrence but does nothing for the people whose information is already at risk. Deleting the clinic's own backup would destroy the evidence needed to work out exactly who was affected.
  2. The correct answer is D (A breach creating a real risk of significant harm to affected individuals): Canadian health privacy statutes and the federal legislation both frame the reporting trigger around the risk that the breach creates, not around whether anyone has complained or been harmed yet. An unencrypted device holding hundreds of health records meets that threshold on its face, because the information is sensitive and the probability of misuse cannot be ruled out. Waiting for proof of actual harm inverts the purpose of the duty, which is early warning. Police characterization of the theft as random does not reduce the exposure of the information itself.
  3. The correct answer is B (Notify the affected clients and the privacy commissioner of the breach): Nova Scotia's health privacy legislation requires notification when personal health information is lost or accessed without authority and there is potential for harm, and an unencrypted device holding four hundred charts clearly meets that threshold. Reporting the theft to police addresses the crime but not the separate privacy duty. Waiting for recovery, or telling only those who happen to ask, leaves clients unable to protect themselves from misuse of their own information.
  4. The correct answer is C (Only individual logins let an audit trail identify who opened a chart): electronic health records are required to keep audit trails so that every access can be traced to the individual who made it. A shared login destroys that link, so the clinic cannot tell who opened the neighbour's chart or investigate any future snooping allegation. Individual logins also let the clinic remove access promptly when someone leaves. Charting speed and software licensing are operational matters unrelated to privacy, and the federal statute does not apply uniformly across provinces because substantially similar provincial health privacy laws govern the health sector where they exist.
  5. The correct answer is A (Add a correction that is dated and initialled, leaving the original entry readable): privacy legislation gives clients a right of access to their own record and a right to request correction, but correction never means erasure. The record is a legal document and the original entry must remain readable so the chronology of care can be reconstructed later. The accepted method is to strike through or flag the disputed entry, add the corrected information, and date and initial the change. Refusing access is not open to the clinic, and rewriting or discarding pages would look like falsification if the record were ever examined.
Case C006

A First Nations mother brings her seven year old daughter to a community clinic in northern Ontario. The child has early childhood caries affecting several primary molars and has missed two previous appointments because the family travels four hours each way. The mother says a previous provider told her the decay was caused by her not caring enough. She asks whether the treatment will be covered, and mentions that a funding dispute delayed her son's care last year.

  1. The correct answer is C (Examining her own assumptions and letting the client judge whether the encounter felt safe): cultural safety differs from awareness and sensitivity precisely because the practitioner must examine her own power, bias and assumptions, and because the client, not the provider, decides whether the encounter was safe. Blaming a mother for her child's caries is exactly the kind of judgment cultural safety asks providers to recognize and stop. Artwork in the waiting room and quoting traditional teachings back to a client are surface gestures that do not change how power operates in the operatory. Treating everyone identically ignores the unequal starting points that produce unequal outcomes.
  2. The correct answer is D (Jordan's Principle, requiring the needed service be provided first and funding settled after): Jordan's Principle exists so that a First Nations child receives a needed public service without delay while governments sort out which of them pays. The mother's account of a jurisdictional funding dispute delaying her son's care is the precise situation the principle addresses. The undue hardship standard belongs to human rights accommodation analysis and concerns the limits of an employer's or service provider's obligation. The mature minor approach governs whether a young person can consent for herself, and the duty to accommodate concerns adjusting service delivery for a protected characteristic rather than resolving funding disputes.
  3. The correct answer is B (Describe the findings plainly and build a plan around the food and travel realities): oral health is shaped far more by income, food access, water quality and geography than by personal willpower, so findings should be explained without moral judgment and the plan should fit the family's actual circumstances. A realistic plan here might combine fluoride varnish at every possible contact, coordination with visiting services, and appointment scheduling that reflects an eight hour round trip. Telling the mother that brushing alone would have prevented the lesions repeats the blame she already received. Advising the family to move, or asking her to justify the decay, damages trust and changes nothing clinically.
  4. The correct answer is A (Skills based training in intercultural competency, conflict resolution, human rights and anti racism): the Truth and Reconciliation Commission's health related calls to action ask for cultural competency training for all health care professionals and require health discipline students to take training in Indigenous health, the legacy of residential schools, Indigenous rights, and skills based training in intercultural competency, conflict resolution, human rights and anti racism. A one time lecture for new hires does not meet an ongoing skills based expectation. A policy of treating everyone the same is the colour blind approach that anti racism work specifically rejects, and charitable fundraising is not the education the calls to action describe.
  5. The correct answer is D (Duty of care, breach of the standard, causation and actual resulting damage): negligence is a civil matter decided by a court, and the client must prove all four elements on the balance of probabilities. She must show that the hygienist owed her a duty of care, that the care fell below the standard of a reasonably prudent dental hygienist in similar circumstances, that this breach actually caused the injury, and that real damage followed. Intent is not required in negligence, which distinguishes it from an intentional tort. Consent forms, referral letters and regulatory investigations may all be evidence, but none of them is an element of the claim.
  6. The correct answer is C (What a reasonably prudent dental hygienist would do in similar circumstances): the standard of care is an objective benchmark set by the conduct of a reasonably prudent practitioner of the same profession facing similar circumstances, informed by the regulator's standards of practice and current evidence. It is not defined by an individual clinic's internal policy, which may lawfully be narrower than the standard but can never lower it. A supervising dentist's personal preference does not set the benchmark either, since accountability is personal and cannot be transferred. The standard is also not perfection or a specialist's best achievable outcome, because the comparison is with reasonable competence, not excellence.
  7. The correct answer is A (About ten years from the last entry, and for children from age of majority): retention periods are set provincially, and the common Canadian rule is roughly ten years from the date of the last entry for an adult client, and for a child ten years from the date the child reaches the age of majority. The nine year old's chart therefore has many years left to run. Closing a practice does not extinguish the obligation, so records must be transferred to a custodian or stored securely for the remainder of the period. Software licensing has nothing to do with the legal retention requirement.
  8. The correct answer is B (Make the entry now, identify it clearly as a late entry, and date it): entries should be made at the time of care or as soon as possible afterward, and where that is not possible the entry is still made but is clearly identified as a late entry with the true date of writing. The concentration and dose of a therapeutic agent must appear in the record. Slotting the note above the morning entry to make the chart look chronological misrepresents when it was written and edges toward falsification. Leaving the record blank means the varnish is treated as care that never happened, and no one may chart under another person's login.
  9. The correct answer is D (Strike through the error so it stays readable, add the correction, date and initial): the client record is legal evidence, so an existing entry is never obliterated. The accepted method is a single line through the error that leaves it readable, the corrected value written alongside, and the change dated and initialled by the person making it. Erasing the figure or covering it with correction fluid hides the original and can be characterized as falsifying a record, which is professional misconduct. Leaving an 8 mm reading in the chart and passing the correction on verbally guarantees the wrong depth will drive future treatment decisions for tooth 36.
  10. The correct answer is C (Report the concern to the regulatory college, because clients are now at risk): the duty to act on an impaired or unsafe colleague is graded. Where the risk is low and remediable a direct conversation comes first, but here the pattern is repeated, the colleague denies any problem, clinical care has already been affected, and she continues to treat clients. At that point the risk to clients is live and the matter goes to the regulator, which has processes for incapacity that are supportive rather than purely punitive. Doing nothing is never correct, and a staff room notice neither protects clients nor respects the colleague.
  11. The correct answer is B (Raise the concern directly with the colleague and describe what she has observed): the response to a colleague's practice concern is proportionate to the risk. Here the omission is real but no client has been harmed, the colleague is otherwise careful, and the problem looks readily correctable, so a direct and respectful conversation is the appropriate first step. Reporting straight to the regulator skips the graded response the situation calls for. Quietly performing the examinations on someone else's clients without telling her hides the problem, and charting the omissions while saying nothing leaves clients exposed to a missed oral cancer screening at every rushed appointment.
  12. The correct answer is A (Report the termination or resignation, because it relates to the registrant's conduct): employers are generally required to report to the regulator when a registrant is terminated, suspended, or resigns for reasons relating to conduct, competence or capacity. Recasting a conduct based dismissal as a voluntary resignation does not remove the duty, and a regulator would view the relabelling as an attempt to defeat it. Billing for services never provided is professional misconduct in its own right and goes to the registrant's fitness to practise elsewhere. Repaying the insurers resolves the financial loss but not the regulatory question, and the duty does not depend on a client complaint.
  13. The correct answer is C (Refuse, because billing for services not provided is professional misconduct): recording four units of scaling when two were performed is a false claim, and billing for services not rendered appears expressly in professional misconduct regulations across Canada. Accountability is personal, so the hygienist cannot shelter behind an office manager's instruction or a claim about practice finances. A private notebook recording the true units proves she knew the submitted claims were false, which makes her position worse rather than better. Applying the practice only to insured clients is simply insurance fraud aimed at a third party payer, not a compromise.
  14. The correct answer is D (Decline, because submitting a false service date is fraud and professional misconduct): the date of service on a claim is a factual assertion about when care was delivered, and knowingly submitting a false date to obtain coverage the client is not entitled to is fraud against the insurer. The hygienist is the provider of record, so her name attaches to the false claim regardless of who asked for it. A chart note admitting the client requested the change documents the deception rather than excusing it. Passing the decision to the dentist does not transfer her personal accountability for a claim submitted in her name.
  15. The correct answer is A (Express written consent from the client for that specific use of the image): a clinical photograph is personal health information, and posting it publicly is a use beyond the purpose for which it was collected, so it requires express consent that names that specific use. Consent for a photograph taken for the record does not extend to marketing or social media. Cropping out the face does not reliably deidentify an image, since distinctive dentition, restorations and pathology can identify a person to anyone who knows them. Approval from the owner or a marketing consultant is irrelevant, because the right being waived belongs to the client.
  16. The correct answer is B (Accepting blurs the therapeutic boundary and mixes personal with professional roles): the therapeutic relationship is unequal because the practitioner holds clinical knowledge, private information and physical access to the client's body, and maintaining the boundary is the practitioner's responsibility rather than the client's. Boundary crossings usually begin small and well intentioned, and adding a client to a personal account that carries family photographs and political opinions is a recognized example. The fact that the client initiated the request does not shift the duty. Restricting what the client can see manages the symptom while still converting a professional relationship into a personal one.
  17. The correct answer is D (The claims are unverifiable and misleading, and misleading advertising is misconduct): a claim to be the gentlest in the city cannot be verified or substantiated, and a promise that a device removes the need for anesthesia in every client is a guarantee of outcome that no clinician can make. Misleading advertising appears in professional misconduct regulations, and any registrant who approves the wording shares responsibility for it. Advertising by regulated health professionals is permitted in Canada, subject to truthfulness and verifiability requirements set by each regulator. Manufacturer approval and a published fee list do not cure a claim that is false on its face.
  18. The correct answer is C (Cost, available outside funding, and health and safety, not mere inconvenience): the duty to accommodate under human rights legislation runs to the point of undue hardship, and that test is assessed on cost, on outside sources of funding, and on health and safety. Inconvenience, staff preference and schedule disruption do not meet the threshold. A dental hygiene practice serves the public, so the codes reach client care as well as employment, and disability is a protected ground everywhere in Canada. How many clients with disabilities the practice has treated before, and what neighbouring clinics offer, are irrelevant to whether this client can be accommodated.
  19. The correct answer is A (Discriminatory and clinically unnecessary, since routine practices already treat every client alike): routine practices, previously called universal precautions, are applied to every client on the basis that any person may carry a bloodborne pathogen, whether or not it is disclosed. Adding special barriers and an end of day booking for a client with hepatitis C provides no additional protection and singles the client out on the basis of a disability, which is a protected ground under human rights legislation. Occupational health legislation requires routine practices, not segregation of identified clients. A client's written agreement cannot convert discriminatory treatment into acceptable treatment.
  20. The correct answer is B (Offer a quiet low stimulation appointment at a scheduled time, with steps explained first): accommodation means adjusting how care is delivered so a person with a protected characteristic can actually receive it, and a quiet low stimulation appointment with predictable sequencing and advance explanation is a recognized, low cost adjustment for an autistic client. Reducing lighting, avoiding unexpected touch, using tell show do and allowing extra time cost the practice little and fall well short of undue hardship. Treating sedation as the only option escalates unnecessarily and carries its own risks. Referring the client away or booking an unmodified appointment repeats the failed encounter rather than accommodating it.
  21. The correct answer is C (Gender identity is a protected ground, so the clinic must accommodate the request): gender identity and gender expression are protected grounds in provincial and territorial human rights codes, and a practice that serves the public must not discriminate on those grounds. Practice management software can carry both a legal name for billing purposes and a chosen name and pronouns for communication, so the insurer's requirements and the client's rights are not actually in conflict. Requiring a legal name change before staff will use a chosen name imposes a barrier on a protected ground. Accurate records do not require staff to address a client by a name he no longer uses.
  22. The correct answer is D (Arrange a qualified interpreter where possible and record who interpreted in the chart): informed consent requires that the client receive the nature of the treatment, the material risks, the alternatives and the consequences of declining, in language she genuinely understands, with a real opportunity to ask questions. A qualified interpreter should be used wherever possible rather than a family member, because a relative may filter information, may have interests of their own, and is not bound to interpret accurately. The chart should record who interpreted. A signed form with written risks is not a consent conversation, and postponing care indefinitely denies service on a protected ground.
  23. The correct answer is B (Reflective practice, which uses structured review of experience to change future behaviour): reflective practice is the deliberate, structured review of an experience covering what happened, what the practitioner felt and assumed, how it may have been experienced by the client, and what will be done differently next time. It is the habit of self examination that underpins cultural humility and the self assessment component of every continuing competence program. Peer review requires a colleague to evaluate the work. Root cause analysis is a systems investigation of an adverse event, and quality assurance auditing measures documentation against published standards rather than examining a practitioner's own reactions.
  24. The correct answer is A (Tell the client what happened, record it, and arrange appropriate clinical follow up): veracity is the duty of truth telling, and it expressly includes telling a client about your own error even when nobody else observed it and the client felt nothing. A gouged root surface on tooth 46 has clinical consequences for sensitivity, plaque retention and future monitoring, so the client needs the information to make decisions about follow up. Recording the event privately without disclosure protects the clinician rather than the client. Telling only the dentist does not discharge a personal duty, and embarrassment is not a reason to withhold material information.
  25. The correct answer is C (The client's right to know what happened outweighs loyalty to the colleague): when a colleague's error has harmed a client, loyalty to the colleague is set against veracity toward the client, and the client's right to know prevails. The client needs the information to understand why tooth 26 now requires endodontic treatment, to consent to what comes next, and to make her own decisions about the relationship and any remedy. Agreeing to silence makes the second clinician a participant in concealment. Neither the dentist nor the practice controls whether a client is told, and expecting a client to discover harm by requesting her own chart is not disclosure.
  26. The correct answer is D (Avoid the conflict by declining the commission arrangement offered by the supplier): a conflict of interest exists whenever a personal interest could reasonably be seen to influence professional judgment, whether or not the practitioner actually acted on it. A per unit commission on a product she recommends is a textbook example, and the correct approach is to avoid the conflict where avoidance is possible, disclose it only where it cannot be avoided, and never let the interest shape the recommendation. Believing the product is genuinely good does not remove the appearance of influence. Donating the money or disclosing only when asked leaves the incentive in place.
  27. The correct answer is A (Failing to reply to the regulator is itself a form of professional misconduct): professional misconduct regulations expressly include failing to respond to a regulator, so ignoring the college's correspondence converts a minor fee dispute into a separate and more serious allegation about the registrant's cooperation. A complaint does not lapse because a registrant stays silent, and the college can and will proceed on the information it has, with the registrant losing the opportunity to give her side. Regulators do not need a registrant's consent to investigate. Responsibility for responding to a complaint is personal and does not pass to an employer.
  28. The correct answer is C (Required remediation such as education or a practice monitor for the registrant): a screening committee reviewing a complaint may take no further action, issue advice or a caution, require remediation such as education or a practice monitor, or refer specified allegations to a discipline hearing. Remediation matches a documentation problem in a registrant with no prior history and reflects the regulator's protective rather than punitive purpose. Revocation is a penalty imposed only after allegations are proven at a hearing, and it is reserved for the most serious findings. Referral to a hearing escalates beyond what this file calls for, and regulators do not award civil damages.
  29. The correct answer is B (Employer policy may narrow but never widen a registrant's legislated scope): legislated scope defines what dental hygienists may lawfully do, personal competence defines what a given registrant may do, and employer policy can restrict either of those but has no power to expand them. Performing an act that provincial legislation ties to a dentist's order, without that order, is practising outside the authorized scope and is professional misconduct by the registrant who performs it. A dentist merely being present in the building does not constitute an order. Accountability is personal, so neither the employer nor the dentist absorbs the registrant's own responsibility for the act.
  30. The correct answer is D (Document the recommendation, the information given and the refusal, and continue offering care): autonomy prevails once a capable client has been told the consequences of declining in plain language, even when the clinician considers the decision unwise. The hygienist records what she recommended, what information she provided, that the client refused, and what she did instead, and she keeps the door open by raising the recommendation again at future visits. Withholding all care as leverage is coercive and would compromise the voluntariness of any later agreement. Deleting the periodontal diagnosis would falsify the record, and enlisting the dentist to override the refusal ignores the client's decision entirely.
Case C007

A dental hygienist appraises a published trial. Investigators enrolled 600 Canadian children aged 6 to 9 who were assessed as high caries risk. A computer generated sequence allocated 300 children to fluoride varnish four times a year and 300 to no varnish. Examiners who recorded new carious lesions did not know which group a child was in. Over 24 months, 75 children in the no varnish group developed at least one new lesion, compared with 45 children in the varnish group.

  1. The correct answer is B (Allocating children to groups by a computer generated random sequence): random allocation distributes both measured and unmeasured confounders evenly between the groups, which is what lets a randomized trial support a causal claim. Blinding the examiners protects the measurement of the outcome once it occurs, but it does nothing about baseline differences between the two groups. Restricting entry to high risk children defines the population rather than balancing it, and a longer follow up only allows more outcomes to accumulate.
  2. The correct answer is D (10 percentage points, the difference between 25 percent and 15 percent): the control risk is 75 divided by 300, which equals 0.25, and the varnish risk is 45 divided by 300, which equals 0.15. Absolute risk reduction is the plain difference between the two risks, so 0.25 minus 0.15 equals 0.10, or 10 percentage points. The 40 percent figure is the relative risk reduction, which describes the same result in a way that sounds much larger. The value of 0.60 is the relative risk itself, and 15 percent is simply the event rate in the treated group.
  3. The correct answer is A (10 children, because 1 divided by 0.10 gives a value of 10): number needed to treat is the reciprocal of the absolute risk reduction. The absolute risk reduction here is 0.25 minus 0.15, which equals 0.10, so 1 divided by 0.10 equals 10. Ten high risk children must receive the varnish programme for two years for one additional child to avoid a new lesion. Taking the reciprocal of the relative risk reduction, or dividing raw lesion counts by group size, answers a different question and gives the wrong figure.
  4. The correct answer is A (Relative risk is 0.60, so the relative risk reduction is 40 percent): relative risk is the treated risk divided by the control risk, so 0.15 divided by 0.25 equals 0.60. Relative risk reduction is 1 minus 0.60, which equals 0.40, or 40 percent, so the colleague has quoted a genuine figure. A value of 1.67 is the control risk divided by the treated risk, which reverses the comparison, and 0.10 is the absolute risk reduction rather than a ratio. The relative figure is correct but incomplete, because the same result is only a 10 point absolute reduction.
  5. The correct answer is C (Of every ten high risk children treated, about one avoids a cavity): natural frequencies drawn from the absolute risk reduction let a parent picture the real size of the benefit, and a number needed to treat of 10 translates directly into that sentence. Quoting only the 40 percent relative reduction makes the benefit sound larger than it is, which is why marketing material prefers relative figures. Reporting a relative risk of 0.60 uses vocabulary the parent has no way to interpret. Saying the result was statistically significant says nothing about effect size and overstates what the trial can promise one individual child.
Case C008

A chairside test for active periodontal breakdown is evaluated against a full mouth clinical and radiographic examination, which serves as the reference standard. The test is applied to 1000 adults attending a hospital periodontal clinic. Of these adults, 300 truly have active disease and 700 do not. The test result is positive in 240 of the 300 adults who have active disease, and positive in 70 of the 700 adults who do not have it.

  1. The correct answer is B (0.80, because 240 of the 300 diseased adults tested positive): sensitivity is the proportion of people who truly have the condition that the test correctly identifies, so it reads down the diseased column. Here 240 divided by 300 equals 0.80, or 80 percent, leaving 60 false negatives. The value of 0.90 reads the healthy column instead and is the specificity. The figures of 0.77 and 0.91 read across the rows and are predictive values, which depend on how common the disease is in the population tested.
  2. The correct answer is D (0.90, from 630 healthy adults correctly cleared out of 700 healthy): specificity is the proportion of people without the condition that the test correctly clears. Of the 700 adults without active disease, 70 tested positive falsely, leaving 630 true negatives, so 630 divided by 700 equals 0.90, or 90 percent. The figure of 0.10 is the false positive rate, which is 1 minus specificity, and 0.80 reads the diseased column and gives sensitivity. The value of 0.77 reads across a row and is a predictive value rather than a property of the test.
  3. The correct answer is C (0.77, since 240 of the 310 positive results are true positives): positive predictive value reads across the positive row of the table and answers the question the client actually asked. The test produced 240 true positives and 70 false positives, giving 310 positives in total, so 240 divided by 310 equals 0.774, or about 77 percent. Roughly one positive result in four in this clinic is therefore a false alarm. The values of 0.80 and 0.90 are sensitivity and specificity, which describe the test itself, and 0.91 is the negative predictive value.
  4. The correct answer is A (About 0.30, because 80 true positives sit among 270 positive results): at 5 percent prevalence, 100 of the 2000 adults have disease and 1900 do not. A sensitivity of 0.80 gives 80 true positives, and a specificity of 0.90 leaves 10 percent of 1900, which is 190 false positives, so 80 divided by 270 equals 0.296. Predictive value falls sharply as prevalence falls even though the test itself has not changed, which is why screening low risk groups generates so many false alarms. Holding the predictive value at its hospital clinic figure ignores this dependence on prevalence entirely.
  5. The correct answer is D (Sensitivity and specificity are test properties while predictive values follow prevalence): sensitivity and specificity are calculated within the diseased and non diseased columns, so neither depends on how many diseased people are in the sample. Predictive values are calculated across the rows and mix the two columns together, which is why they shift with prevalence. In this example both sensitivity and specificity stayed at 0.80 and 0.90 while the positive predictive value dropped from about 0.77 to about 0.30. The reversed claims describe the opposite of how a two by two table behaves.
Case C009

A public health dental hygienist is asked to write a school sealant policy. A guideline published in 2016 by a recognized national body recommends sealing permanent first molars only in children judged to be at high caries risk. A guideline published in 2024 by a different recognized body recommends sealing sound and non cavitated pit and fissure surfaces of permanent molars in all children. Both guidelines name their panels and grade their recommendations. The team is divided over which to follow.

  1. The correct answer is B (Compare the search dates and the trials each guideline was able to include): guidelines on one topic differ for understandable reasons, and an eight year gap means the newer panel could weigh trials the older panel never saw. Two guidelines disagreeing is not by itself proof that either one is wrong. Choosing by the reputation of the issuing body substitutes authority for evidence, and counting citations rewards volume rather than study quality. A vote among team members records preference, not evidence.
  2. The correct answer is C (The newer recommendation rests on a firmer evidence base for the same strength): every graded recommendation carries two separate labels, the strength of the recommendation and the quality of the evidence behind it. When the strength is equal, the recommendation supported by moderate quality evidence is less likely to be overturned by the next trial than one supported by low quality evidence. Ignoring evidence quality throws away half the information the grading system exists to convey, and length of use is not an evidence claim at all.
  3. The correct answer is C (Teeth 16, 26, 36 and 46 in the standard two digit notation): the first digit names the quadrant, with 1 upper right, 2 upper left, 3 lower left and 4 lower right, and the second digit counts from the midline, so position 6 is the first molar in each permanent quadrant. Teeth ending in 4 are the first premolars and teeth ending in 7 are the second molars. Numbers in the 50 to 80 range name primary teeth, which are not the target of a permanent molar sealant programme.
  4. The correct answer is A (Explain that guidance differs, give the benefits and costs, and let her decide): evidence informed practice joins research, clinical judgment and the client's own values, and remaining uncertainty is information the parent is entitled to have. Presenting both options with the uncertainty named allows a real choice about a preventive procedure that carries cost and time. Sealing without mentioning the disagreement removes the parent from a decision that is hers, and refusing to act until guidance converges withholds a low harm intervention indefinitely. Saying no useful advice exists misrepresents evidence that clearly favours sealing higher risk children.
Case C010

A dental hygienist who holds an academic appointment plans a study comparing two brief smoking cessation scripts. She intends to recruit clients from her own recall list, allocate each client to one script, and then retrieve self reported quit rates from clinic charts at six months. Funding comes in part from a company that markets a nicotine replacement product. The protocol will go to the institution's Research Ethics Board before any client is approached.

  1. The correct answer is B (Clients may feel their ongoing care depends on agreeing to take part): consent must be voluntary, and a client who depends on the researcher for care may agree because refusing feels risky rather than because she wants to participate. Canadian research policy names people who depend on the researcher as needing extra protection for exactly this reason. Random allocation is not itself a bar to consent, and payment is not a requirement and can become undue influence. Whether the recall list is representative is a question about generalizability, not about the ethics of consent.
  2. The correct answer is D (The board, not the researcher, decides the review level, and approval precedes recruitment): a Research Ethics Board reviews and approves research before it begins and then monitors it, and minimal risk research may qualify for delegated review only when the board makes that determination. A researcher who assesses her own risk level and starts recruiting has bypassed the safeguard entirely. Using procedures that are routine in care does not by itself make a study minimal risk, since the research use of them is what is under review. Industry funding requires declaration rather than automatic exclusion from a lower review stream.
  3. The correct answer is A (That the study is partly funded by a nicotine replacement product manufacturer): Canadian research policy requires participants to be told the purpose of the study, what taking part involves, the foreseeable risks and benefits, who is funding the work, and that they may withdraw at any time without penalty and without affecting their care. Commercial funding must be declared rather than hidden, because a sponsor who profits from the result gives a participant reason to weigh the invitation differently. The remaining items describe the design or the researcher's post, which are useful context but are not the specific disclosure the policy requires here.
  4. The correct answer is C (Fresh consent from participants or specific approval from the Research Ethics Board): secondary use means using data collected for one purpose to answer a different question, and Canadian research policy treats that as requiring either new consent or a specific board decision permitting it. Coding the data reduces the risk of identification but does not authorize a purpose the participants never agreed to. The funder has no authority over participant data in this respect. Studying the same people does not make a new question part of the original consent.
  5. The correct answer is D (An outcome must be measurable, such as new root caries lesions at 12 months): the outcome element has to be stated in terms a study could actually record, because a vague phrase gives the database nothing to match and gives the hygienist no way to tell whether the intervention worked. Better oral health has no unit, no timeframe and no threshold. The comparison treatment belongs in its own element, and while a comparison may be left out when none is meaningful, the outcome cannot be.
  6. The correct answer is B (Adults over 65 who take three or more xerostomic medications): the population element describes who the question is about, and describing it precisely is what stops a search returning trials in populations the hygienist does not treat. Naming the medication burden narrows the group to people whose caries risk is driven by reduced salivary flow. The high strength paste is the intervention, the standard strength paste is the comparison, and counted root caries lesions at 12 months form the measurable outcome.
  7. The correct answer is A (A randomized controlled trial, or a systematic review of such trials): the question asks whether a treatment works, and random allocation is what distributes known and unknown confounders evenly so that a difference in outcome can be attributed to the toothpaste. A systematic review of such trials sits above any single trial because it appraises and synthesizes the whole body of work. A survey measured at one moment cannot show which came first, a study that starts from the outcome and looks backwards is exposed to recall bias, and a series without a comparison group can only generate hypotheses.
  8. The correct answer is C (A cohort study following rinse users and non users forward over time): a cohort observes an exposure people have already chosen and follows the groups forward, so it measures incidence and establishes that the exposure came before the outcome without anyone being assigned to a suspected harm. Because allocation is not random, residual confounding remains possible and has to be addressed in the analysis. Randomizing people to a suspected harm is the design that raises the ethical objection in the first place. A survey taken at one moment cannot establish temporal order, and a series without a comparison group cannot show that users fare worse than non users.
  9. The correct answer is B (It is a case control study and reports an odds ratio, not relative risk): a case control study starts from the outcome and looks backwards at past exposures, which makes it efficient for rare conditions where a forward study would need enormous numbers. Because the investigator chooses how many people with and without the lesion to enrol, the design cannot measure the underlying risk of the lesion, so it reports an odds ratio instead. Collecting exposure information after people already know they have the disease also makes recall bias a constant threat in this design.
  10. The correct answer is B (Exposure and outcome were measured together, so temporal order is unknown): a cross sectional design captures both variables at one moment, so nothing in the data shows whether infrequent flossing preceded the periodontal breakdown or whether sore, bleeding tissues led people to stop flossing. Establishing that the exposure came first is a minimum requirement for a causal claim. A sample of 3000 is ample for detecting an association, self report is a legitimate if imperfect survey measure, and reporting an association is exactly what this design is able to do.
  11. The correct answer is D (It cannot show effectiveness, because there is no comparison group at all): a case series describes a handful of clients who received a treatment and reports what happened to them, which generates hypotheses but leaves no way to know what would have happened without the agent. Reported sensitivity often improves on its own and improves further when clients know they are being watched, so improvement in eight people is not evidence of effect. Peer review checks reporting quality rather than supplying the missing control group, and reported sensitivity is a legitimate outcome when it is measured against a comparison.
  12. The correct answer is A (The second is a meta-analysis, which pools results into one summary estimate): both documents are systematic reviews, since each follows a declared protocol, appraises the included studies and synthesizes them. A meta-analysis adds a statistical step that combines the individual results into a single estimate with a confidence interval. Pooling is only as sound as the studies fed into it, so a meta-analysis of weak trials produces a precise looking number built on weak material. Neither document is a narrative review, a clinical guideline, or a cohort study.
  13. The correct answer is C (The estimate looks precise but rests on weak trials, so confidence stays low): a review is never better than the studies inside it, and pooling increases the total sample size without repairing bias that was built into each contributing trial. A narrow confidence interval describes precision, which is a statement about random error, not about whether the studies were biased in the same direction. Heavy loss to follow up and absent blinding both push results away from the truth systematically, and combining them carries that bias straight into the summary figure.
  14. The correct answer is D (The hierarchy ranks designs by default, but conduct and size can reverse it): the hierarchy summarizes how well each design protects against bias when the design is executed properly, and a trial with 24 participants and 40 percent dropout has lost most of that protection. A well conducted large cohort can therefore give a more dependable answer than a tiny compromised trial. Treating the ranking as absolute ignores appraisal altogether, and no rule places cohorts above trials in general. Size matters here but is not the only consideration, since a large poorly conducted study can still mislead.
  15. The correct answer is C (The next assignment is predictable, so allocation cannot be concealed from enrollers): a genuinely random sequence is unknowable in advance, whereas alternation or assignment by day of the week lets whoever enrols the next client see which group is coming. That person can then delay, redirect or encourage a client, which reintroduces exactly the imbalance randomization exists to prevent. Alternation actually keeps group sizes close to equal, so imbalance in numbers is not the problem. Blinding of the outcome assessor and the required sample size are separate design questions.
  16. The correct answer is A (Have a separate examiner who does not know the allocation record the depths): when an intervention cannot be concealed from the operator or the participant, blinding the person who measures the outcome preserves most of the protection. An operator who believes in the instrument she used will tend to read borderline sites in its favour, which is measurement bias rather than dishonesty. Having her record the depths herself immediately after treatment makes that worse. Giving up on blinding discards an achievable safeguard, and clients cannot probe their own periodontal tissues.
  17. The correct answer is B (The trial may lack power to detect a real difference, a type II error): with 20 participants per group a trial can easily miss a difference that genuinely exists, so no significant difference is not the same as no difference. A sample size calculation naming the effect the study was powered to detect is what tells a reader whether a null result is informative. Concluding that the aid is ineffective treats a failure to detect as proof of absence. The trial did report a significance test, and 20 participants per group is small rather than generous for this comparison.
  18. The correct answer is D (It exaggerates benefit, because dropout was linked to the product itself): analyzing only those who complied is a per protocol analysis, and dropout is rarely random. Here the people who left did so because of the product, so the survivors are a self selected group who tolerated it, and their results flatter the treatment. Intention to treat analysis counts every participant in the group originally allocated, whether or not they finished, which preserves the balance randomization created. Nothing in the description suggests dropout was equal between the groups.
  19. The correct answer is A (Those who remained may differ systematically from those who left the study): losing nearly half the participants without explanation means the analyzed group may no longer resemble the group that was randomized, and any difference between leavers and stayers can create or hide an apparent treatment effect. Reporting the reasons for loss in each group, and comparing the baseline characteristics of leavers and completers, is what allows a reader to judge the risk. High attrition raises suspicion of harm but does not prove it, and losses of this magnitude are not something an appraiser can pass over.
  20. The correct answer is C (Confounding, because smoking is linked to both coffee intake and attachment loss): a confounder is a third variable associated with both the exposure and the outcome, and it distorts the apparent relationship between them. Smoking travels with heavy coffee drinking and is itself a powerful driver of attachment loss, so the reported association may reflect tobacco rather than coffee. A confounder that was never recorded cannot be adjusted for in the analysis, which is the specific failure here. Recall, attrition and measurement problems are real threats in other studies, but none of them explains how an unmeasured third variable creates a false association.
  21. The correct answer is B (Selection bias, because volunteers differ systematically from the wider population): people who answer a poster at a teaching clinic tend to be more motivated, more available and more health engaged than the general client population, so a programme that works for them may not work elsewhere. This is a threat to whether the result transfers, and no amount of internal rigour inside the trial repairs it. Recall bias concerns how accurately people remember past exposures, detection bias concerns how the outcome is measured, and publication bias concerns which studies reach print rather than who was enrolled in this one.
  22. The correct answer is A (Recall bias, because people with a diagnosis search their past more thoroughly): someone who has just been told she has oral cancer has strong reason to review her history for a cause, while a healthy control has no such prompt, so the two groups remember differently even when their true exposures were the same. This threat is built into any design that collects exposure information after the outcome is already known.
