Matching controls to cases is a design safeguard rather than a bias, a backward looking study has no follow up period from which to lose people, and self report by itself is not confounding.
  23. The correct answer is D (A predatory journal, so anything it publishes is an unverified claim): unsolicited invitations, a promise of acceptance in days for a fee, a title mimicking an established publication and a scope spanning unrelated fields are the standard warning signs. No genuine peer review can be completed in five days across dentistry, engineering and economics. An article in such a journal has not been vetted by anyone, so it should be read as a claim rather than as evidence, however confident its wording. Legitimate open access journals do charge fees but still run real review and appear in recognized indexes.
  24. The correct answer is C (Read the methods and results, and check the conclusion matches what was found): industry funding does not make a study invalid, but company sponsored trials report favourable results more often than independently funded ones, so a declared commercial interest is a reason to read the methods closely rather than to stop reading. A common problem is an abstract whose conclusion overstates what the results section actually shows. Discarding all industry funded work would remove much of the evidence on dental products, and accepting an abstract on the strength of publication alone skips appraisal entirely.
  25. The correct answer is B (CINAHL, which indexes allied health, nursing and behaviour change literature): the Cumulative Index to Nursing and Allied Health Literature covers dental hygiene, nursing and allied health titles that the large biomedical databases index less completely, which is exactly where education and behaviour change research is published. The Cochrane Library is the first place to look when a systematic review of a therapy question may exist, but it will not hold the primary behavioural literature she is missing. Repeating a search in a database that does not index those journals cannot surface them, and an unfiltered web search returns material with no quality control.
  26. The correct answer is D (It retrieves papers by concept regardless of the wording each author chose): a controlled vocabulary such as MeSH assigns each paper subject headings drawn from a fixed list, so a search on the heading returns everything indexed under that concept even when the authors used different words in the title and abstract. That is what stops synonyms fragmenting a search. Date range, study design and funding source are all separate filters that can be applied on top of a search, and none of them is what subject headings do.
  27. The correct answer is C (18 percent, the proportion with the condition at a single point in time): prevalence is the number of existing cases divided by the total population examined, so 720 divided by 4000 equals 0.18, or 18 percent. Prevalence counts everyone who has the condition on the survey day, whether they developed it last month or ten years ago, which is why planners use it to size services. A rate of new cases arising over a period is incidence, a different measure calculated only among people who were free of the condition at the start.
  28. The correct answer is A (5 percent per year, from 164 new cases among 3280 disease free adults): incidence counts new cases arising over a defined period, and its denominator is only the people who could still develop the condition. So 164 divided by 3280 equals 0.05, or 5 percent per year. Using the full 4000 adults as the denominator wrongly includes people who already had periodontitis and could not become new cases, which understates the true rate. Prevalence at the first survey describes existing cases and is not a rate of new disease, and adding existing to new cases mixes the two measures together.
  29. The correct answer is B (Mean 5 mm and median 4 mm, with the mean pulled up by outliers): the seven readings sum to 35, so the mean is 35 divided by 7, which equals 5 mm. With seven ordered values the median is the fourth, which is 4 mm, and the mode is 3 mm. The single 11 mm site drags the mean a full millimetre above the median, which is the signature of skewed data. Periodontal readings are almost always skewed in this direction, so the median usually describes a typical site better than the mean does.
  30. The correct answer is C (2.2 to 4.6 mm, which spans two standard deviations either side of the mean): in a normal distribution roughly 95 percent of values fall within two standard deviations of the mean. Two standard deviations here is 2 multiplied by 0.6, which equals 1.2 mm, so the range runs from 3.4 minus 1.2 up to 3.4 plus 1.2, that is 2.2 to 4.6 mm. The narrower range of 2.8 to 4.0 mm is one standard deviation either side and captures only about 68 percent of readings, and a one sided range leaves out half the distribution.
  31. The correct answer is D (2.0, because a risk of 0.40 divided by a risk of 0.20 equals 2.0): relative risk compares the risk in the exposed group with the risk in the unexposed group. Risk among the smokers is 100 divided by 250, which equals 0.40, and risk among the never smokers is 100 divided by 500, which equals 0.20, so the ratio is 2.0. Smokers carried twice the risk over five years. Comparing the raw counts alone ignores the very different group sizes, which is exactly the error a risk calculation exists to prevent.
  32. The correct answer is A (3.5, from 120 multiplied by 140 divided by 80 multiplied by 60): the odds of exposure among the cases are 120 to 80, which equals 1.5, and the odds among the controls are 60 to 140, which equals 0.4286. Dividing 1.5 by 0.4286 gives 3.5, and the cross product of 120 multiplied by 140, which is 16800, divided by 80 multiplied by 60, which is 4800, gives the same 3.5. Comparing the two exposed counts alone ignores the unexposed people in each group, and 1.5 is only the case odds before any comparison is made.
  33. The correct answer is B (A result this extreme would arise 3 times in 100 if there were no difference): a p value is the probability of observing a result at least as extreme as the one found, calculated on the assumption that there is truly no difference. It is a statement about the data given that assumption, not about the probability that the assumption itself is true. It also says nothing about how large the effect was, so it cannot be read as a percentage reduction in bleeding sites, and it makes no prediction about whether a future trial will reproduce the finding.
  34. The correct answer is D (The interval crosses 1, so the data fit both benefit and harm): for a ratio measure such as relative risk the null value is 1, meaning no difference between the groups. An interval running from 0.42 to 1.45 contains values well below 1 and values above it, so the true effect could plausibly be a substantial benefit, no effect at all, or a modest harm. A point estimate below 1 does not on its own establish significance, because that estimate carries uncertainty which the interval exists to display. The width of this interval also shows the estimate is imprecise rather than precise.
  35. The correct answer is C (The difference is statistically significant but far too small to matter clinically): a very large sample can detect differences so small that they carry no consequence for a client, and a p value of 0.001 speaks only to how unlikely the result would be if there were no difference at all. A change of 0.05 mm sits below the measurement error of a periodontal probe, so no clinician could reliably observe it in the chair. The finding is probably real and still not worth acting on, and a small p value describes strength of evidence against no difference rather than the size of the effect.
  36. The correct answer is A (Association alone can arise from a third factor, so causation is not proven): two variables can move together because one causes the other, because a third factor such as overall diet, income or oral hygiene habits drives both, or by coincidence. A causal claim needs controlled allocation, or observational evidence showing consistency across studies, a dose response relationship, biological plausibility and the correct temporal order. A large sample improves precision but does nothing to rule out a third factor. Dismissing every reported association is the opposite error, since sugar frequency does have strong supporting evidence from other designs.
Case C011

Nadia, 34, attends a dental hygiene appointment after seven years away from care. She tells the receptionist she almost cancelled. Her medical history is unremarkable apart from generalized anxiety, for which she takes no medication. In the operatory she is breathing quickly and gripping the armrests. She says she had a painful extraction as a teenager and was told to stop being dramatic. Generalized marginal redness and heavy supragingival calculus are visible. She is capable and consents to an examination.

  1. The correct answer is B (Raise the chair upright, sit at eye level, and talk with her): a supine client with a clinician above them and instruments near their face is not a conversation between equals, and that physical arrangement amplifies fear. Bringing the client upright and sitting at eye level restores the balance before any significant discussion of history, findings or consent. Dimming the light and pressing on with the assessment leaves the client flat and still powerless, and hurrying the appointment or handing the conversation to the dentist does not address the position she is in.
  2. The correct answer is D (Agree a raised hand stop signal and honour it every single time): trauma informed care rests on safety, trustworthiness, choice, collaboration and empowerment, and a stop signal that is always honoured is the practical way to give a client real control in the operatory. Explaining that stopping would leave calculus behind removes that control by making her responsible for the clinical outcome. Promising no pain is a guarantee that cannot be kept, and probing for details of the extraction asks her to disclose trauma she has not offered.
  3. The correct answer is A (Stop, raise the chair, and ask what would make this easier now): stopping immediately, changing the position that triggered the distress and asking what would help returns control to the client without requiring any disclosure. The hygienist does not need the details of the trauma and should not probe for them, so asking her to describe the memory is outside the role and may retraumatize her. Working on quietly while she is distressed ignores her signal, and a general remark that everyone feels this way dismisses her experience.
  4. The correct answer is C (Client used stop signal after quadrant four, scaling paused, shorter visit booked): a chart entry must be objective, specific, contemporaneous and free of judgmental language, and this entry records exactly what was done, what stopped it and what was arranged. Calling the client difficult and emotional is a judgment rather than an observation. Recording childhood abuse as a fact states something the client never disclosed and adds a diagnosis the hygienist cannot make, and describing the visit as uneventful is simply inaccurate.
  5. The correct answer is D (Explore what she expects from sedation and discuss the options together): shared decision making means making it clear that a decision exists, discussing the realistic options with their benefits, costs and limits, and reaching a decision with the client. Exploring what she is hoping sedation will do also reveals whether her real concern is pain, loss of control or anticipation. Telling her sedation is unnecessary overrides her stated preference, booking it immediately skips the discussion entirely, and pushing breathing techniques while refusing to discuss sedation dismisses her request.
Case C012

Mr Osei, 62, arrived in Canada eight months ago and speaks limited English. He attends with his 11-year-old granddaughter, who says she will translate for him. His medical history sheet has been left blank. Examination shows generalized probing depths of 5 to 7 mm with bleeding on probing, heavy plaque and calculus, and he requires non surgical periodontal therapy over several visits. He has no private insurance and asks repeatedly what the treatment will cost.

  1. The correct answer is B (Arrange the telephone interpreter and thank the granddaughter for offering): a trained interpreter is required when the client and clinician do not share a language well enough for informed consent, and children are never appropriate interpreters. A child may not know clinical terms, routinely softens or edits what is said, and is placed under an unfair burden, and confidentiality is compromised. Written questions do not solve a language barrier, and an untrained bilingual staff member carries the same accuracy and confidentiality problems as a family member.
  2. The correct answer is A (Speak to Mr Osei directly in short segments and watch him): speaking in the first person straight to the client, in short segments the interpreter can carry accurately, keeps the conversation between the hygienist and the client rather than between the hygienist and the interpreter. Watching the client also gives access to the non verbal responses that carry most of the emotional content. Addressing the interpreter turns the client into a bystander, technical terminology and idiom translate poorly, and long uninterrupted passages force the interpreter to summarize and drop detail.
  3. The correct answer is D (Ask him to explain the plan back in his own words): teach back is the standard check on understanding, and it is the only one of these options that produces evidence rather than agreement. Clients with low health literacy commonly say yes out of politeness or embarrassment, so asking again whether anything is unclear will produce the same nod. A translated handout supports the spoken message but does not test comprehension, and asking the granddaughter to verify understanding reintroduces the family interpreter problem and breaches confidentiality.
  4. The correct answer is C (The gums are infected and the bone holding your teeth is shrinking): plain language uses everyday words that carry the clinical meaning accurately, and this sentence conveys infection and bone loss without technical vocabulary. Naming generalized chronic periodontitis with radiographic bone loss or describing the periodontium and attachment apparatus uses professional terms that will not survive translation or be understood. Telling him there is nothing to worry about is both false and removes any reason for him to attend the remaining visits.
  5. The correct answer is B (Give a written estimate and explain public coverage such as the CDCP): newcomers often do not know how Canadian dental care is organized or paid for, so a plain written estimate together with an explanation of public programs such as the Canadian Dental Care Plan gives him what he needs to decide. Cost is a legitimate part of shared decision making, so telling him it should not influence his health decision dismisses a real barrier. Starting treatment before the cost is settled and warning him about future expense both apply pressure instead of information.
Case C013

Devon, 27, attends a recall appointment. He has smoked about 15 cigarettes a day since he was 17. Examination shows generalized extrinsic staining, moderate subgingival calculus, localized probing depths of 5 mm at the molars, and bleeding on probing at 22 percent of sites. When tobacco use is raised he says he already knows it is bad for him, that he has thought about quitting once or twice, and that smoking is the only thing that settles him after work.

  1. The correct answer is C (Contemplation, so exploring both sides of his ambivalence is the task): Devon sees a reason to change and a reason to keep smoking at the same time, which is the defining feature of contemplation in the transtheoretical model. The task at this stage is to explore the ambivalence and draw out his own reasons for change, which is where motivational interviewing does the most work. He is not in precontemplation because he already recognizes a problem, he has set no date or plan so he is not preparing, and he has made no attempt to stop.
  2. The correct answer is A (It sounds like quitting may not feel worth the effort right now): rolling with resistance means not arguing back, because argument makes the client defend the behaviour out loud and entrenches it. A reflection acknowledges what he said, invites correction and keeps the conversation open. Correcting him about his uncle's luck or citing research against his personal example are both counter arguments that trigger the righting reflex. Offering a pamphlet at this point supplies information he did not ask for and closes down the exchange.
  3. The correct answer is D (Ask what else makes him think about stopping at some point): Devon has just produced change talk, a statement arguing for change in his own words, and the response should ask for more of it so he hears himself building the case. People are persuaded far more by what they say themselves than by what a professional tells them. Adding a lecture about consequences replaces his reasons with the hygienist's, moving on to home care abandons the opening, and offering a product assumes a readiness to act that he has not expressed.
  4. The correct answer is B (Ask what he already knows about smoking and healing of gums): elicit provide elicit begins with finding out what the client already knows, then asking permission before adding anything, then giving the information plainly, then asking what the client makes of it. Starting with what he knows avoids repeating what he has heard many times and shows respect for his existing understanding. Stating the information first skips the opening step, a summary of his reasons is a separate skill, and a confidence rating measures readiness rather than knowledge.
Case C014

Ellen, 58, attends for a recall appointment. She smokes and drinks alcohol most evenings. During the extra oral and intra oral examination the hygienist finds a firm, non wiping white and red patch on the left lateral border of the tongue, about 12 mm across, which Ellen says she has never noticed. She is otherwise well and takes no medication. The dentist is on site today. Ellen is chatty and says she is in a hurry to collect her son.

  1. The correct answer is A (Bring her upright, state the finding plainly, then pause for her): an unwelcome finding is delivered upright, privately and in plain words, with a short warning, a simple statement and then silence so the client can react. Telling her at the front desk removes privacy and gives her no chance to respond. Handing the conversation to the dentist while tidying up abandons the client at the moment she needs support, and a note on a recall card leaves her to read serious news alone with no opportunity to ask anything.
  2. The correct answer is C (I cannot tell by looking, most are not cancer, we are checking): this is honest about the limits of what the hygienist can determine, gives accurate reassurance that most such lesions are not malignant, and states that assessment is happening now. Telling her it is almost certainly nothing is false reassurance that may cause her to skip the referral. Refusing to say anything at all abandons her at the worst moment, and leading with her smoking and drinking sounds like blame and confirms her fear before anyone has assessed the lesion.
  3. The correct answer is B (Allow the silence, then ask what she would like to know): silence is an active tool, and a few seconds of quiet after difficult news gives the client room to think and to say what is actually on her mind. Clinicians who fear silence fill it with reassurance and stop the client saying the thing she was about to say. Continuing with process detail buries the news under information she cannot absorb, handing over a leaflet and leaving isolates her, and changing the subject signals that her reaction is unwelcome.
  4. The correct answer is D (Listen fully, acknowledge her experience, apologize, then explain the next step): a complaint is managed by listening without interrupting or defending, acknowledging the client's experience, apologizing for that experience, establishing the facts and explaining what happens next, with follow up and notification of the practice owner. Defending the practice by saying she was already told is the defensive response that escalates complaints. Transferring the call passes her on without acknowledgement, and offering a free appointment buys her off before anyone has understood what went wrong.
  5. The correct answer is A (What would you most like to get out of today's appointment?): an open question invites an answer of any length and produces information the hygienist did not know to ask for, including what the client values and fears. Opening wide and narrowing later is the recommended sequence. Asking about current pain, brushing habits or changes to the medical history are all closed questions that produce a yes, a no or a single fact, and stacking them at the start of an appointment reads as an interrogation.
  6. The correct answer is C (Stop typing, turn toward the client, and let her finish speaking): active listening is attention that is visible, which means stopping the charting, turning toward the client, not interrupting and allowing short pauses. Listening while typing reads as not listening however much is retained. Interrupting to defend the current practice dismisses her account before she has finished it, and a closed question aimed at speeding her up tells her the story is unwelcome and closes off information relevant to how she will be treated here.
  7. The correct answer is D (It sounds like you feel let down after spending all that money): a reflection of feeling names the emotion behind the statement and hands it back, which proves listening and invites the client to correct or expand. Restating when the crowns were placed is a reflection of content rather than feeling. Redirecting to the clinical examination skips the emotion entirely, and a general remark about how much dental treatment costs generalizes away from this client and closes the conversation instead of opening it.
  8. The correct answer is B (Losing a tooth you fought to keep must be hard to hear): empathy is understanding the experience from the client's own frame of reference and reflecting it back without judgment, which keeps the client talking. Telling her not to worry because it is only one tooth minimizes the loss she has just described. Expressing pity for her situation is sympathy, which is felt from outside and closes the conversation, and jumping to replacement options answers a practical question she has not yet asked.
  9. The correct answer is C (So there is stress, a dry mouth from the medication, and cost): summarizing gathers several threads together and hands them back, which lets the client correct the record and signals a transition to the next part of the appointment. Reflecting only the cost picks up one thread and is a reflection rather than a summary. Asking her to rank the three is a new question, and offering dry mouth products moves straight to a solution before the whole picture has been confirmed with her.
  10. The correct answer is A (A yes or no check invites agreement rather than showing understanding): asking whether something makes sense produces a polite yes, especially where low health literacy is hidden by embarrassment, so it is never a reliable check. Teach back, where the client explains the instruction in her own words, is the standard alternative. A handout supports but does not test understanding, repetition of the same words does not confirm it either, and health literacy is a distinct ability that is not the same as general literacy or intelligence.
  11. The correct answer is B (Ask him to demonstrate the brush at the chair using a mirror): show back, where the client demonstrates with the aid in his own hand, is stronger than a verbal answer for anything manual, because a client can describe a technique accurately and still angle or insert the brush incorrectly. A verbal teach back is a reasonable check but weaker for a skill. A diagram gives information rather than confirming performance, and a review in four weeks discovers the problem only after a month of incorrect use.
  12. The correct answer is D (Too many messages were given, so most of them will not stick): an appointment should be limited to two or three key messages, because information beyond that is mostly lost, and seven instructions with four items to take home guarantees that little is retained or acted on. Prioritizing and returning to the rest at a later visit is the fix. There is no bar on providing a rinse sample or on introducing interdental brushes alongside it, and a handout is a support rather than the source of the problem here.
  13. The correct answer is A (I want to check I explained that clearly, can you tell me back?): teach back is framed as a check on the quality of the teaching rather than a test of the client, which removes the shame that keeps clients from admitting they did not follow something. If the answer is wrong the hygienist re-teaches in different words and asks again. Asking her to prove she listened and openly testing her both put the burden on the client, and a leading question inviting her to agree she remembers everything checks nothing at all.
  14. The correct answer is C (Evocation, because reasons for change were supplied rather than drawn out): the spirit of motivational interviewing is partnership, acceptance, compassion and evocation, and evocation means the reasons for change are drawn out of the client because people are persuaded more by what they hear themselves say. Supplying reasons and countering every objection is the righting reflex and is closer to persuasion than to motivational interviewing. Nothing described suggests the client's welfare was not the aim, scheduling is not part of the spirit, and acceptance is about respecting autonomy rather than giving praise.
  15. The correct answer is B (Explore what gets in the way and what he would consider changing): advice giving is one directional and suits people who are already ready and asking what to do, but it has now failed four times with this client, which is exactly the situation where motivational interviewing is the better choice. Exploring barriers and what he would actually be willing to do produces a plan he helped build. Repeating the demonstration, adding consequences or setting a firm expectation are all the same one directional approach that has already not worked.
  16. The correct answer is D (You found a way to fit that in on three busy evenings): an affirmation recognizes a genuine strength or effort specifically rather than offering generic praise, and naming what she actually did under difficult circumstances reinforces her own sense of ability. Generic praise such as well done carries no specific content. Immediately raising the target to seven evenings withdraws the recognition and reads as criticism, and predicting a clinical result shifts the focus back to the hygienist's measurements instead of the client's effort.
  17. The correct answer is C (Ask permission to share what was found and leave the decision open): a client who sees no problem is in precontemplation, where the task is to raise awareness without pressure and to offer information with permission, leaving the choice with him. Setting a twice daily brushing goal or providing a detailed site by site schedule are preparation stage interventions that waste the appointment on someone who has not decided to change anything. Warning him about future insurance coverage is a pressure tactic rather than an awareness raising one.
  18. The correct answer is B (Note that returning to smoking is common and ask what worked before): in the transtheoretical model relapse is expected rather than exceptional and is a return to an earlier stage, not a failure, so it should be normalized while what previously worked is identified and used again. Clients commonly cycle through the stages several times before change holds. Agreeing to drop the subject abandons her at the point she most needs re-engagement, arguing about willpower is a counter argument, and going straight to a product at the previous dose skips her readiness entirely when she has just said she sees no point in trying again.
  19. The correct answer is A (Name the brush simply, show it on his finger, then apply it): tell show do means explaining in child friendly language, demonstrating on a finger or a model, then performing the procedure, in that order. The child has asked a direct question and deserves an answer at his level even though the caregiver gives consent. Explaining only to the caregiver leaves the child out of a conversation about his own body, applying quickly skips both the telling and the showing, and promising something will not hurt sets up a promise that may not hold.
  20. The correct answer is D (Acknowledge the caregiver's own anxiety and agree how she can help): the child's distress is being transferred from the caregiver, so the caregiver's anxiety is the thing to address, along with an agreed role that supports rather than undermines the appointment. Sending the caregiver out abruptly removes the child's source of security and may increase the distress. Simply sounding brighter ignores the actual source, and telling the child the caregiver's experience was long ago contradicts a trusted adult in front of him without changing what she says next.
  21. The correct answer is B (Offer the client time without her mother and explain why beforehand): adolescents should be spoken to directly rather than over, and where provincial rules and capacity allow, part of the appointment without the parent present lets questions about vaping, piercings and substance use be answered honestly. Explaining the reason first keeps the parent informed and reduces suspicion. Asking the mother the sensitive questions bypasses the client entirely, asking them with the parent present will produce guarded answers, and skipping them omits information needed for the assessment.
  22. The correct answer is C (Link vaping to dry mouth, staining and breath she notices now): appeals to appearance, breath and sport performance land far better with adolescents than the threat of tooth loss decades away, because near term and visible consequences are what they weigh. Describing radiographic bone loss in long term smokers is the same distant argument she has already dismissed. Restating that periodontal disease can start early is still a future health warning, and involving her parents removes her autonomy and reliably produces disengagement.
  23. The correct answer is D (Continue addressing the client directly and allow her time to answer): an older adult who is alert and oriented is the person to speak to, even when an accompanying family member answers first, and allowing extra time is often all that is needed. Redirecting the history to the son treats her as absent from her own appointment. Asking the son to leave is unnecessary when she has not objected to his presence, and raising the voice assumes hearing loss from age alone, which is exactly the assumption to avoid.
  24. The correct answer is A (Stop the suction, face her, and remove the mask at a distance): operatory noise interferes with hearing aids, and a mask hides the mouth and muffles the voice, so stopping the suction, facing the client with light on the face and removing the mask at a safe distance when infection prevention allows addresses all three barriers at once. Shouting distorts mouth shape and sound rather than helping, writing everything down abandons speech unnecessarily, and removing the hearing aid takes away the amplification she depends on.
  25. The correct answer is C (Describe each step aloud before touching her face or her neck): touch without warning is startling for a client who cannot see it coming, so describing what is about to happen before doing it is the basic courtesy and the safety measure. Announcing arrival and departure follows the same principle. Steering her by the arm removes her control, since a guiding arm should be offered rather than taken. Written information remains useful in large print or a screen reader format, and shortening explanations gives her less rather than more.
  26. The correct answer is B (Use one short step at a time with demonstration and gentle repetition): a client with moderate dementia is best supported by reduced distraction, short single step instructions, extra time, and demonstration with gentle repetition rather than complex verbal explanation followed by a recall test. Giving the instruction only to the support worker excludes the client from his own care, a higher pitched voice is harder to hear and can sound patronizing, and a written list does not help a client who cannot process a three step sequence.
  27. The correct answer is D (Stop, because his refusal in the moment must be respected and recorded): consent from a substitute decision maker authorizes the treatment but does not override the client's own assent at the chair, and turning away and pushing the instrument aside is a clear refusal. Restraint of any kind is not acceptable, so continuing or asking the support worker to hold his hands are both wrong. Rushing to finish the arch prioritizes the task over the person and will make future appointments harder for everyone involved.
  28. The correct answer is A (Cultural safety is determined by the person receiving the care): cultural safety is defined by the client's experience, meaning care is culturally safe only if the client experiences it as safe, free of racism and free of judgment, and the provider does not get to declare their own care safe. Cultural competence suggests a finite body of knowledge that can be acquired and completed, which is a different and weaker idea, so equating the two or treating an annual course as the endpoint both miss the definition.
  29. The correct answer is B (Ask the client what makes attending difficult rather than assuming a reason): cultural humility means asking rather than assuming, not treating an individual as a representative of a group, and not attributing decisions to culture when the real barriers may be cost, transport, work hours or a previous bad experience. Agreeing with the colleague adopts a stereotype as an explanation. Reading about the group's beliefs still substitutes group knowledge for this person's reasons, and referring him elsewhere avoids the conversation rather than having it.
  30. The correct answer is C (Build the relationship first, welcome family, and revisit radiographs later): the continuing effects of colonization, the residential school system and documented racism in Canadian health care produce well founded mistrust, so building the relationship before the clinical agenda and never rushing consent are the appropriate responses. Radiographs are not mandatory and a capable client may decline them, so insisting is both inaccurate and coercive. Recording him as uncooperative is a judgment that compounds the problem, and asking him to recount mistrustful experiences puts the burden of disclosure on him.
  31. The correct answer is A (Explain the specific consequences, offer alternatives, accept the decision, and document): a capable adult may decline a referral even when the hygienist believes that is a poor decision, and the professional obligation is to make the refusal informed, offer any alternative that exists, accept the answer without pressure, and record what was explained and declined. Repeating the recommendation at every visit or bringing the dentist in to secure agreement are pressure rather than advocacy, and discharging him punishes him for exercising a right he holds.
  32. The correct answer is D (Ask permission to raise it, name the likely causes, offer options): halitosis is embarrassing and rarely raised by the client, so the approach is to ask permission, frame it as a health finding with identifiable causes such as tongue coating, periodontal infection or xerostomia, then move to what can be done. Hinting through a rinse recommendation leaves him confused about what is actually being said. Humour adds shame to embarrassment, and waiting until therapy is finished delays a conversation he needs to take part in now.
  33. The correct answer is C (Tell the client what happened, apologize, give aftercare, and document it): disclosure of an error is an ethical and, in most provinces, a regulatory obligation, and it means telling the client promptly in plain words, apologizing, describing the consequences and what is being done, then recording the disclosure factually. Concealing an error is a far more serious professional failure than the error itself, so staying silent or recording it without telling her are both worse than the injury. Passing the decision to the dentist does not transfer the hygienist's own duty.
  34. The correct answer is B (Invite him to a private room, sit down, and reflect his frustration): anger is usually driven by fear, cost or feeling unheard, so the response is to stay calm, lower your own voice, move to a private space, sit down, and reflect the feeling before dealing with the facts. Explaining the policy first argues about who is right while he is still angry. Telling him to lower his voice adds a reprimand in public, and fetching the practice owner leaves him standing in reception with no acknowledgement at all.
  35. The correct answer is D (Risks of not taking radiographs explained, client declined, stated reason recorded): documentation of a refusal must show what was explained, that the risks were discussed, what was declined and the client's own stated reason, because what is not recorded is treated as not done. Noting only that risks were advised omits the reason and the specifics of what was explained. Wording that assigns all consequences to the client is defensive rather than factual, and calling the client uncooperative is judgmental language that does not belong in a record.
  36. The correct answer is A (State the concern clearly, decline to proceed, and document the exchange): communication with the dentist is a professional exchange between colleagues, and accountability for the hygienist's own assessment stays with the hygienist, so a concern that is not acted on must be escalated rather than dropped when the client is at risk. Proceeding as directed transfers a responsibility that cannot actually be transferred. Shortening the appointment still exposes the client to treatment at a pressure that contraindicates it, and asking the client to decide passes a clinical judgment to someone without the information to make it.
Case C015

Mr. Chen is 84 and lives in a long term care home. He has advanced dementia, is dependent for all personal care, and has had two episodes of pneumonia in the past year. A personal support worker provides his daily mouth care. On assessment you find heavy plaque on his remaining natural teeth, an upper partial denture that has not been removed for cleaning in some time, generalized bleeding on probing, and dry mucosa. A care conference is scheduled for next week.

  1. The correct answer is B (A short written protocol naming the product, frequency, technique and who to call): personal support workers deliver the daily mouth care in long term care, and they need an instruction they can follow in a few minutes on a rotating shift. A protocol that names the product, how often, how to do it and the trigger for calling you back survives staff turnover because it lives on the unit rather than in one person's memory. A periodontal chart is a clinical record that unit staff are not trained to interpret and it tells them nothing about what to do. Verbal requests to one nurse disappear the moment that nurse changes shift.
  2. The correct answer is D (Structured daily oral care reduces pneumonia incidence and mortality in residents): aspiration pneumonia in dependent older adults is one of the oral systemic links with genuine intervention evidence, not just association. Randomized trials of structured daily oral care in long term care have shown reductions in pneumonia incidence and in death from pneumonia, which is a stronger evidence base than the cardiovascular or pregnancy links. Saying there is no measurable effect understates well replicated trial findings. Antibiotics are not part of the intervention that produced the benefit, and describing the evidence as observational only misrepresents what has been tested.
  3. The correct answer is A (Have the agreed oral care protocol entered into his nursing care plan): a plan that lives only in the dental chart is not seen by the staff who provide daily care, so it does not get carried out. Entering the protocol into the nursing care plan puts it in the document the unit actually works from every shift. Filing your assessment in the dental chart keeps the information where nobody on the unit will look for it. Relying on a family member to remind staff makes the plan depend on visits rather than on the care system, and reciting the periodontal findings uses your fifteen minutes on information the team cannot act on.
  4. The correct answer is C (Role clarification, because nobody confirmed who was accountable for the task): role clarification means each member describes their own scope and the scope of others accurately, and the team confirms who is accountable for each task. When everyone assumes someone else removed and cleaned the denture, the task falls through the gap that overlapping roles create. There was no disagreement here to resolve, so conflict resolution does not describe what happened. No one was competing for leadership, and the omission was not a failure to consult the resident about his preferences.
  5. The correct answer is C (Contact the pharmacist serving the home for the full dispensing history): the pharmacist is the fastest and most reliable route to a complete medication list, an interaction check and advice on which agents cause xerostomia. Long term care homes have a serving pharmacy that holds the current dispensing record, so the information exists and can be released with appropriate consent. A photograph of bottles is incomplete and delayed, and recording the list as unknown abandons information you can readily obtain. Sending a request asking the nurse practitioner to advise is a vague open question of exactly the kind that produces no useful reply.
  6. The correct answer is A (Call the hygienist if the mouth bleeds, or if he repeatedly refuses care): a trigger written for personal support workers has to use signs they can see without dental training and without measuring anything. Bleeding during care and repeated refusal are both observable and both signal a change that warrants your reassessment. Probing depths and plaque indices require instruments, training and a chart that unit staff do not use. Restricting contact to the scheduled reassessment removes the safety net entirely and leaves a deteriorating mouth unreported for months.
Case C016

Amrit is 27 years old and 22 weeks pregnant with her first child. She is followed by a registered midwife and has had an uncomplicated pregnancy so far. She attends because her gums bleed whenever she brushes. You find generalized bleeding on probing, probing depths of 4 to 5 mm in the posterior sextants, and heavy plaque. She tells you that a relative warned her dental treatment during pregnancy could bring on early labour.

  1. The correct answer is D (Therapy has not consistently reduced preterm birth, but it is safe): observational studies link periodontitis with preterm birth and low birth weight, but large randomized multicentre trials have not consistently shown that treating periodontitis during pregnancy reduces those outcomes. Those same trials did establish that non surgical periodontal therapy during pregnancy is safe. Promising a better birth outcome is an overclaim the evidence does not support, and it damages trust if the pregnancy goes badly. Deferring care is also wrong, because periodontal care is indicated during pregnancy for the client's own oral health.
  2. The correct answer is B (Treat now, using a semi supine position with a left lateral tilt): oral care is safe and appropriate in every trimester, and there is no reason to defer debridement for a client with bleeding and 4 to 5 mm probing depths. Her lightheadedness lying flat reflects pressure of the gravid uterus on the inferior vena cava, which is relieved by a semi supine position with the client tilted to her left side. Keeping her fully supine makes that compression worse rather than better. Deferring all treatment until after delivery leaves active inflammation untreated through the rest of the pregnancy and postpartum period.
  3. The correct answer is A (Contact her midwife today to report the readings and the swelling): raised blood pressure appearing after 20 weeks of pregnancy together with new swelling raises the possibility of a hypertensive disorder of pregnancy, which is assessed by the maternity care provider without delay. The midwife is her primary maternity provider and can order the testing and follow up the same day. Waiting four weeks or leaving the client to mention it at her own next appointment risks missing a condition that can progress quickly. A cardiology referral is the wrong pathway for hypertension arising in pregnancy.
  4. The correct answer is C (Send her midwife your findings and proposed plan, with Amrit's consent): information changes behaviour most reliably when it comes from a provider the client already trusts, and the midwife is the person Amrit believes. Sharing your findings and plan also lets the maternity and oral health plans line up rather than contradict each other. Consent to release information is required before you contact any external provider. A pamphlet and a flat contradiction both compete with the relative's advice rather than reinforce a trusted source, and bringing the relative in makes the appointment about the disagreement rather than about Amrit.
  5. The correct answer is B (Refer to a registered dietitian to plan settling foods with lower risk): the driver here is frequent exposure to fermentable carbohydrate and sweetened drinks used to control nausea, layered on acid from vomiting. A registered dietitian works with you on caries risk from dietary sugars and can build a pattern of settling foods that does not feed the lesions. Gastroenterology is not indicated for ordinary pregnancy nausea already being followed by her midwife. Swallowing function is not in question, and a periodontist manages periodontal conditions rather than erosive tooth surface loss.
Case C017

Bernard is 61 and attends for his first appointment in four years. He reports type 2 diabetes managed with metformin, but he has not seen his family physician in over two years and does not know his most recent glycated hemoglobin. His blood pressure today is 156/94 mmHg on two readings. You find generalized probing depths of 5 to 7 mm, heavy calculus, bleeding on probing at most sites, and mobility of tooth 46. He is keen to start treatment today.

  1. The correct answer is D (Provide oral hygiene instruction and radiographs, then rebook for debridement): while you wait for a consultation reply you neither cancel and forget nor treat on an assumption of what the physician will say. Care that adds no risk, including oral hygiene instruction, dietary counselling, indicated radiographs and non invasive assessment, can go ahead and keeps the client engaged. Starting debridement before his glycemic control is known treats on an assumption, and doing one quadrant is the same error at a smaller scale. Cancelling outright wastes the visit and loses a client who has already been away for four years.
  2. The correct answer is C (What is his most recent glycated hemoglobin value and the date taken?): a useful consultation request ends with a closed, specific question whose answer changes what you do. A value and a date tell you how well controlled his diabetes is and how current that information is, which is what your treatment decision turns on. Asking a physician to advise, or to confirm that things are managed, invites a one word reply that leaves you exactly where you started. Asking about scaling a quadrant uses dental language the physician cannot interpret and shifts a dental hygiene decision onto the wrong professional.
  3. The correct answer is A (Non surgical periodontal debridement with local anesthesia over four appointments): describing planned treatment in terms a physician understands means naming the procedure, the anesthesia and the number of visits, because those are the elements that bear on bleeding risk, stress and glycemic management. Sextant labels and FDI tooth numbers are dental notation that a family physician has no reason to be able to read. A vague description such as therapy as clinically indicated gives the physician nothing concrete to respond to, which produces a vague reply in return.
  4. The correct answer is B (Treating your gums gives a modest improvement, alongside your diabetes care): intervention trials show that non surgical periodontal therapy produces a modest reduction in glycated hemoglobin in people with type 2 diabetes, on the order of a few tenths of a percentage point at three to six months. That effect is real and measurable, and it does not replace medical management by his diabetes care team. Promising normal blood sugar overstates the effect badly, and denying any effect understates a well replicated finding. Waiting for full control before treating also reverses the relationship, because periodontal therapy supports control rather than following it.
  5. The correct answer is B (Include the readings in the consultation and advise medical follow up): a reading of 156/94 mmHg on two occasions in a client who has not seen a physician in two years is a finding you noticed first and that belongs in your consultation request and in his chart. Advising him to arrange medical follow up puts the assessment with the provider who can diagnose and treat hypertension. Rechecking at his next appointment delays the finding without acting on it. He has no symptoms of a hypertensive emergency, so emergency department referral is disproportionate, and asking him to self monitor for a month substitutes a device for a medical assessment.
  6. The correct answer is D (A denturist may see her directly and remake the denture for her): denturists design, construct and fit removable complete and partial dentures, and in most Canadian provinces they see clients directly without a dentist referral. Requiring a dentist diagnosis first misstates the denturist's independent access to clients. Dental technologists work in a laboratory on a written prescription and have no direct clinical contact with clients, so they cannot reline or fit anything in her mouth. A prosthodontist holds specialist expertise in complex restorative rehabilitation, but a routine complete denture does not require that specialty.
  7. The correct answer is A (They fabricate the crown in a laboratory without direct clinical contact): dental technicians and technologists fabricate crowns, bridges and appliances in a laboratory on the written prescription of a dentist or denturist. The defining feature of the role is that there is no direct clinical contact with the client, so nothing about examining, shade taking at chairside, diagnosing or fitting belongs to it. The dentist diagnoses the need for the crown and prescribes it, and the dentist or hygienist working in the operatory takes the shade and the dentist fits and adjusts the finished restoration.
  8. The correct answer is C (Her intraoral duties are delegated and supervised rather than independent): certified or level II dental assistants hold intraoral duties such as placing rubber dam, applying fluoride varnish, taking impressions and polishing, and those duties are delegated by and performed under the supervision of a dentist. That supervisory structure is the main difference from the dental hygienist role, where assessment, dental hygiene diagnosis, planning, debridement and evaluation are carried out on the hygienist's own professional accountability. The assistant pathway is shorter than the dental hygiene pathway rather than longer, assistants do take radiographs where certified, and preventive agents such as fluoride varnish are one of the overlaps between the two roles.
  9. The correct answer is D (Dental therapists provide simple restorations and primary tooth extractions): dental therapists deliver a defined set of restorative and surgical services alongside preventive care, and in Canada their practice has been concentrated in Saskatchewan and in northern and Indigenous communities. Their training and regulation follow a different pathway from dental hygiene, so the two roles are not interchangeable and the titles are not synonyms. Dental therapy is not an add on course taken by hygienists, therapists are not supervised by hygienists, and limiting them to prevention ignores the restorative and surgical services that define the role.
  10. The correct answer is B (Generalized plaque induced gingivitis with contributing drug induced xerostomia): a dental hygiene diagnosis names a condition the hygienist is educated and authorized to treat, and it identifies the contributing factors that the dental hygiene care plan will address. Gingivitis with medication related xerostomia fits that description exactly, since both the inflammation and the dry mouth are managed within dental hygiene practice. Diagnosing recurrent caries and diagnosing pulpal or apical pathology are dentist diagnoses that lead to restorative or endodontic treatment. Naming a suspected malignancy is a diagnosis that follows biopsy by an appropriate specialist, and the hygienist's role there is to describe the lesion and refer.
  11. The correct answer is A (Send the consultation request to her nurse practitioner with her consent): in much of Canada a nurse practitioner is the client's primary care provider and can confirm a diagnosis, report control of a chronic condition, order and report laboratory values including an international normalized ratio, and adjust medications. A consultation request to that nurse practitioner will be answered in full. Telling the client to find a physician creates a barrier where none exists, and diverting the request to a walk in clinic sends it to a provider without her records. Proceeding with extensive instrumentation on an unconfirmed anticoagulation status treats on an assumption.
  12. The correct answer is A (Her community pharmacist, who holds the complete dispensing history): the pharmacist is the fastest route to accurate medication information, an interaction check and a complete dispensing history, which is exactly what a bag of unlabelled confusion requires. That record also shows what has actually been dispensed recently rather than what was prescribed years ago. The prescribing physician holds only the prescriptions written from that one practice and may not know what other providers added. A family member's recollection is not a medication record, and package leaflets confirm what a drug is without telling you whether she still takes it.
  13. The correct answer is C (An occupational therapist, to assess and adapt her daily self care): occupational therapists solve access and function problems in daily activities, including adapting a toothbrush handle, recommending grip aids and powered brushes, and training a caregiver where the client cannot manage alone. That is the profession whose core work matches a declining ability to perform self care. Physiotherapy addresses movement and strength but not the adaptation of daily self care tasks. A rheumatologist manages the disease itself rather than the brushing problem, and a social worker addresses determinants such as income and housing rather than hand function.
  14. The correct answer is D (A speech language pathologist, for a swallowing assessment guiding positioning): a client who coughs on thin liquids after a stroke may be aspirating, and the water from ultrasonic instrumentation carries the same risk as any thin liquid. A speech language pathologist assesses swallowing and shares territory with dental hygiene on dysphagia, tongue and lip function, and the results change how you position the client, how you suction and whether you use an ultrasonic at all. Meal texture, feeding technique and seated posture are all useful, but none of them tells you whether this client can protect his airway in your chair.
  15. The correct answer is B (A social worker, who can address income, housing and transportation): the barriers described are social determinants of health, specifically transportation cost, housing instability and food insecurity, and social workers are the team members whose role is to address them. Removing those barriers is what will get the child to the appointments, so it comes before any further clinical planning. A dietitian addresses the content of the diet but cannot resolve housing or transportation. A home visiting nurse and a pediatrician both provide clinical care that does not touch the reasons the family cannot attend.
  16. The correct answer is C (Contact the oncology team now to plan dental clearance and timing): when the radiation field includes the mandible, decisions about which teeth are removed and how long before radiotherapy the extractions occur are made jointly with the oncology team, because healing time before treatment determines the later risk of osteoradionecrosis. Three weeks is a short window, so contact happens immediately rather than after further appointments. Scheduling debridement during radiotherapy ignores the pre treatment clearance question entirely. Fluoride trays are part of long term care but do not resolve two grossly carious molars with periapical radiolucencies, and extracting from an irradiated mandible afterward carries exactly the risk that pre treatment planning exists to avoid.
  17. The correct answer is A (Refer her to a physician or nurse practitioner for medical assessment): polydipsia, polyuria and unexplained weight loss in a client with rapidly progressing periodontitis and periodontal abscesses are classic signs of undiagnosed diabetes, and referral for medical assessment is a core part of the dental hygienist's collaborative role. You are frequently the first provider to notice systemic disease, and acting on that observation can change the client's whole trajectory. A periodontist would treat the periodontal manifestation without addressing the likely underlying cause. Dietary advice and a three month wait delay diagnosis, and beginning debridement while ignoring the systemic picture treats the effect and misses the cause.
  18. The correct answer is D (No trial shows treatment prevents events, though the conditions are associated): people with periodontitis do have higher rates of atherosclerotic cardiovascular events, and plausible mechanisms including systemic inflammation and bacteremia exist, so an association is real. Major cardiology and periodontal bodies have stated that the evidence does not establish periodontal disease as a cause of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease, and no trial shows that periodontal treatment prevents heart attacks or strokes. Telling him treatment prevents cardiac events, or that the bacteria are a proven cause, overclaims what the research supports. Denying any relationship swings too far the other way and contradicts consistent observational findings.
  19. The correct answer is D (The two are associated, with no established direction of causation): rheumatoid arthritis and periodontitis occur together more often than chance predicts, and they share inflammatory pathways and risk factors, but no causal direction has been established in either direction. The honest answer is that treating the periodontitis is worth doing for her oral health without any promise about her arthritis. Claiming that either condition causes the other states more than the evidence supports. Saying there is no consistent relationship at all contradicts a body of observational research that repeatedly finds the two together.
  20. The correct answer is B (Each condition worsens the other, so the relationship runs in both directions): periodontitis and diabetes have the strongest of the oral systemic relationships, and it is genuinely bidirectional. Poorly controlled diabetes increases the risk, extent and severity of periodontitis and impairs healing after therapy, and severe periodontitis is in turn associated with worse glycemic control, with treatment producing a modest improvement in glycated hemoglobin. Describing the effect as running in one direction only captures half of a well established two way relationship. Attributing the pairing solely to shared ageing ignores intervention trial evidence that treating one condition changes a measure of the other.
  21. The correct answer is C (A modest reduction of a few tenths of a percentage point): intervention trials of non surgical periodontal therapy in people with type 2 diabetes show a reduction in glycated hemoglobin on the order of a few tenths of a percentage point at three to six months. That effect is real, measurable and modest, and it supplements rather than replaces medical management. A reduction of two to three percentage points overstates the effect by roughly an order of magnitude. Saying there is no measurable change ignores replicated trial results, and no evidence supports discontinuing metformin on the strength of periodontal therapy.
  22. The correct answer is A (Residual confounding and reverse causation can still produce this association): observational designs cannot establish causation, because shared risk factors such as smoking, age, obesity and low income are never fully removed by statistical adjustment, and the disease of interest may itself have followed rather than preceded the exposure. Both of those explanations remain live after adjustment and are the reason major bodies decline to call the relationship causal. Sample size is not the issue in large cardiovascular cohorts, and adjusting for smoking reduces rather than eliminates its influence. Cardiovascular events are among the most reliably measured outcomes in observational research, so measurement is not the weak point here.
  23. The correct answer is B (Multidisciplinary care, because each profession works its own part in parallel): multidisciplinary care means several professions each do their own part alongside each other without building a shared plan. Interprofessional care would require the same three providers to construct one plan together, agree who does what, and report results back to one another, which is precisely what is missing here. Collaborative leadership describes leadership shifting to whoever holds the relevant expertise, which cannot happen in a team that never meets. Client centred care means the client is a member of the team rather than a subject of three separate assessments.
  24. The correct answer is D (Collaborative leadership, because leadership shifted to the relevant expertise): collaborative leadership means leadership moves to whoever holds the expertise the situation requires, rather than staying with the most senior person in the room. Xerostomia and root caries sit squarely within dental hygiene expertise, so handing the plan to the hygienist while the physician chairs the meeting is the competency in action. Team functioning describes how teams form, set norms and reach decisions. Role clarification is about describing scopes accurately, and interprofessional communication is about sending information in a form another profession can use.
  25. The correct answer is A (Oral hygiene instruction, dietary counselling and radiographs where indicated): while a consultation reply is outstanding you provide care that carries no added risk, which includes oral hygiene instruction, dietary counselling, indicated radiographs and non invasive assessment. That keeps the client engaged, uses the appointment and creates no bleeding risk. Even anterior supragingival scaling involves instrumentation you have deliberately deferred pending the anticoagulation answer. Treating a full mouth on the reasoning that silence implies agreement is treating on an assumption of what the physician will say. Providing nothing at all wastes the visit and discourages a client who attended as asked.
  26. The correct answer is C (Speak with the physician yourself, then record who, when and what): a telephone reply to a consultation request is acceptable, provided you record who you spoke with, the date and the content of the reply in the client's chart. A number relayed second hand on a sticky note gives you no context, no verification and no defensible record. Refusing telephone replies outright is unnecessary and delays care that could proceed. Having the receptionist transcribe her own note into the chart preserves the same unverified information and puts a clinical entry in the hands of someone who did not evaluate it.
  27. The correct answer is C (Reschedule, arrange the documented prophylaxis regimen, and provide non invasive care): a prosthetic heart valve places this client in the highest risk group for whom prophylaxis before gingival manipulation is recommended, and debridement is exactly the kind of procedure it covers. Because no dose was taken, the appointment is rebooked with the documented regimen arranged in advance, and non invasive care can proceed today. Proceeding on the assumption of what the cardiologist would have said is treating on an assumption, and limiting instrumentation to supragingival deposits still manipulates gingival tissue. Directing her to take any leftover antibiotic risks the wrong drug at the wrong dose and outside the correct timing window.
  28. The correct answer is B (Situation, background, assessment and recommendation, stated in that order): SBAR exists because unstructured communication fails under pressure, and it maps directly onto an urgent telephone call to another provider. It puts the immediate problem first, then the context, your assessment and what you are asking for, so the receiver can act without extracting the facts from you. A chronological narrative buries the urgent finding at the end. The diagnosis and consent sequence describes a treatment discussion with a client rather than a handover, and the subjective and objective note format is a charting structure for a written record rather than a spoken escalation.
  29. The correct answer is D (A closed loop readback, repeating the instruction for the sender to confirm): a closed loop readback has the receiver repeat the drug name and dose back and the sender explicitly confirm it, which is the technique designed to catch exactly this error before anything is drawn up. It works in the moment, across masks and noise, and it takes seconds. A safety huddle happens at the start of the day and cannot catch a verbal order given later. A written handover note comes at the end of the appointment, far too late, and graded escalation is the tool for raising a safety concern rather than for verifying an instruction.
  30. The correct answer is A (Who the client is, what was done, what is outstanding, and risks): handover fails at transitions, so the same structure is used every time, covering who the client is, what has been done, what remains outstanding, what the risks are and what the receiver must do next. That gives the locum enough to continue care safely without rereading every chart. A transcribed medical history duplicates a record the locum can already access and omits the plan entirely. Financial and scheduling details are administrative rather than clinical, and radiographic exposure settings do not tell the receiving clinician what care is still owed.
  31. The correct answer is B (Pediatricians and primary care providers, who see infants for immunizations): pediatricians and other primary care providers see infants repeatedly through the first two years for well child visits and immunizations, long before most families attend for a first dental visit. That makes them natural partners for fluoride varnish programs and early childhood caries screening, and the contact is scheduled rather than opportunistic. Pharmacies see families but have no scheduled infant contact tied to a visit. Denturists serve an older population entirely, and kindergarten screening reaches children at four or five, which is later than the window this program is trying to hit.
  32. The correct answer is D (He started denosumab injections for osteoporosis three months ago): antiresorptive therapy such as denosumab or a bisphosphonate is a recognized trigger for medical consultation, because the medication, its route, its duration and the client's other risk factors bear on the risk of medication related osteonecrosis of the jaw and on how invasive procedures are planned. A recently started statin does not change dental hygiene management. A coronary stent placed eleven years ago no longer warrants antibiotic prophylaxis and needs no consultation on its own. Well controlled asthma with occasional reliever use is managed by having the inhaler available at the chair.
  33. The correct answer is C (Yes, provided the conditions set by your provincial regulator are met): dental hygiene is regulated provincially, and several provinces permit hygienists to self initiate treatment without a prior dentist order, subject to conditions such as the client having seen a dentist within a defined period and the hygienist holding the required authorization. The answer therefore depends on meeting those specific conditions rather than on a blanket yes or no. There is no national rule requiring a dentist order, and self initiation is not unconditional anywhere. Restricting self initiation to preventive services misdescribes the authority, which covers dental hygiene therapy where the conditions are satisfied.
  34. The correct answer is A (Agree in writing which role records vital signs at each appointment): overlapping roles are efficient but they make role clarification a safety issue, because when everyone assumes someone else took the blood pressure nobody did. Assigning the task to a named role in writing removes the ambiguity and survives staff changes and busy days. Having every member take a reading wastes time and produces conflicting entries without fixing accountability. A newsletter reminder raises awareness without assigning the task, and asking the client to report a reading from her physician replaces a measurement you are responsible for taking.
  35. The correct answer is B (Discuss the findings with her and seek consent to involve her physician): palatal erosion with parotid enlargement and self induced vomiting points to an eating disorder, which is outside dental hygiene scope to diagnose or treat and requires medical involvement. Discussing what you have found and seeking her consent to contact her physician respects her autonomy as an adult while opening the pathway to the care she needs. Agreeing to keep it confidential and treating only the erosion leaves the cause untouched. Contacting her parents without consent breaches confidentiality for a capable adult, and a dietitian referral that avoids naming the vomiting sends her to the wrong scope with incomplete information.
  36. The correct answer is C (Send a specific consultation, keep her booked, and follow up in writing): the general rule in this domain is to prefer consulting over cancelling and to gather the missing information before treating. A specific, answerable consultation request, a booking that stays in place and a written follow up if no reply arrives keeps the care moving while the question is resolved. Cancelling until she produces her own clearance places the work on the client and usually ends in her not returning. Proceeding because she feels well treats on an assumption, and asking her to relay clinical questions herself loses the detail and produces no documented reply.
Case C018

Mr. Dalisay is 62 and attends a recall appointment at a general practice. He has smoked for 40 years and drinks alcohol daily. He reports a sore spot under his tongue that he first noticed about three weeks ago. On the left lateral border of the tongue, adjacent to tooth 36, there is a firm indurated red and white patch about 12 mm across with irregular borders. It is not tender. A left submandibular node is enlarged and firm. He wears no appliance and has had no recent dental work.

  1. The correct answer is B (Refer urgently for definitive assessment and biopsy, and document the findings today): an indurated red and white patch with irregular borders that has persisted about three weeks in a heavy smoker who drinks daily, together with a firm enlarged regional node, is suspicious for malignancy. A lesion suspicious for malignancy is an urgent referral, not a routine one. Waiting a further four weeks, dispensing a rinse or removing irritants and looking again all delay diagnosis in a client who has already passed the two week point with no identified local cause.
  2. The correct answer is C (Site named in FDI terms, size, colour, borders, texture, symptoms and duration): a suspicious lesion record must let another clinician locate and compare the lesion exactly, so the site is described in FDI terms such as the left lateral tongue adjacent to tooth 36, with measured size, colour, border character, texture, symptoms and duration, ideally with a photograph. A hygienist does not record a diagnosis of carcinoma, because tissue diagnosis belongs to the pathologist. Recording only the client's words, or an assumption that the lesion is traumatic, loses the objective baseline the specialist needs.
  3. The correct answer is A (Describe the finding, explain the reason for referral, and confirm that he attends): the client is entitled to a plain description of what was seen and why definitive assessment is needed, and the referring clinician closes the loop by confirming attendance. False reassurance that it is probably nothing is the classic error, because it leads clients to skip the specialist appointment. Predicting cancer and surgery overstates what is known before biopsy, and refusing to discuss the finding at all leaves the client uninformed and unable to consent.
  4. The correct answer is D (Persistence beyond two weeks after an obvious local cause is removed warrants referral): the two week rule is the practical threshold used for oral lesions. An ulcer, a red, white or mixed patch, an indurated mass or unexplained numbness that is still present two weeks after any obvious local cause has been removed needs referral for definitive assessment and biopsy. Two days is far too short to distinguish ordinary trauma from pathology, pain and bleeding are late and unreliable signs, and six weeks of observation delays diagnosis of a cancer that spreads with time.
  5. The correct answer is A (An oral and maxillofacial surgeon, for definitive assessment and tissue biopsy): biopsy and the management of oral soft tissue and bone pathology sit with oral and maxillofacial surgery, or with an oral medicine service where one exists. A periodontist manages periodontal disease and does not provide the diagnostic pathway for a suspicious tongue lesion. An orthodontist manages malocclusion, and a denturist provides removable prostheses, which is irrelevant here because this client wears no appliance.
  6. The correct answer is B (Continue the planned periodontal therapy while tracking the referral to completion): referral transfers part of the care to another provider while the referring clinician stays accountable for the part retained, in this case the diagnosed periodontitis. Non surgical periodontal therapy carries no added risk while the lesion is being assessed and the disease continues to progress if it is left. Suspending all care or discharging the client abandons the periodontal problem, and an arbitrary six month delay is not justified by any finding in this case.
Case C019

Mrs. Kowalchuk is 71 and attends for non surgical periodontal debridement with local anesthesia. Her history includes a total knee replacement 14 months ago, mitral valve prolapse diagnosed in her thirties, a coronary artery stent placed four years ago, and type 2 diabetes. She takes metformin, ramipril and rosuvastatin, and her most recent glycated hemoglobin was 6.8 percent. Her blood pressure today is 128/78 mmHg. She says a previous hygienist always gave her antibiotics before cleanings, and she has brought no letter from any physician or surgeon.

  1. The correct answer is D (Prophylaxis is not indicated, because none of her conditions is a highest risk one): current guidance restricts infective endocarditis prophylaxis to a prosthetic valve or prosthetic repair material, previous infective endocarditis, specific congenital heart disease and cardiac transplant valvulopathy. Mitral valve prolapse and coronary stents were both removed from the list years ago, and routine prophylaxis before dental treatment for prosthetic joints is no longer generally recommended. With no written direction from her surgeon on file, there is nothing that requires an antibiotic for this appointment.
  2. The correct answer is C (Explain the current guidance, document the discussion, and offer to contact her surgeon): the client's belief is based on advice that has since changed, so she needs a clear explanation, a record of the conversation, and a route to confirmation if she is still unsure. Giving an antibiotic that is not indicated exposes her to adverse effects and contributes to resistance for no benefit. Cancelling treatment punishes her for asking a reasonable question, and criticizing the previous clinician damages the client's trust in the profession without resolving anything.
  3. The correct answer is B (Follow the written direction and document who issued it and on what date): although routine prophylaxis for prosthetic joints is no longer generally recommended, the treating orthopedic surgeon may still direct prophylaxis for an individual client, and a previous deep infection of that joint is exactly the situation in which that happens. A written direction from the physician who manages the prosthesis is followed and recorded, not overruled from the chair. Handing the decision to the client or bouncing the letter to the dentist avoids a decision that has already been made by the appropriate provider.
  4. The correct answer is A (Periodontal debridement with an intraligamentary injection of local anesthetic solution): prophylaxis applies to procedures that manipulate gingival tissue or the periapical region, or that perforate oral mucosa, and both subgingival debridement and an intraligamentary injection do exactly that. Radiographs involve no tissue manipulation at all.
