Taking impressions and adjusting a denture clasp are listed explicitly as procedures that do not require a prophylactic dose, because neither breaches the gingival tissue.
  5. The correct answer is C (Proceed with therapy and report the periodontal findings to her diabetes team): a glycated hemoglobin of 6.8 percent indicates reasonable control, so periodontal therapy proceeds, and sharing the findings closes the loop with the provider managing her diabetes. Non surgical periodontal therapy produces a modest reduction in glycated hemoglobin of a few tenths of a percentage point at three to six months, so it supports medical management without replacing it. Promising that periodontal treatment will halve her result, or that it can replace metformin, is a serious overclaim.
Case C020

Mr. Beaudoin is 84 and lives in a long term care home where you provide dental hygiene services. He has moderate dementia and Parkinson disease and has had two chest infections in the past year. A speech language pathologist has assessed him as aspirating thin fluids. His mouth shows heavy plaque, generalized gingival bleeding, three retained roots and a partial denture he has not worn for months. Personal support workers report that he turns his head away and clenches when they try to brush his teeth.

  1. The correct answer is D (Put a written daily mouth care protocol into his nursing care plan): structured daily oral care in long term care has good intervention evidence for reducing pneumonia incidence and mortality, and this resident aspirates and has already had two chest infections. A plan that lives only in the dental chart will not be carried out, so it has to reach the nursing care plan that staff actually use. Recording refusals changes nothing, a periodontal chart is unusable by personal support workers, and extraction planning does not address the daily care gap.
  2. The correct answer is A (The product, the frequency, the technique, who performs it, and the callback trigger): personal support workers need short, concrete instructions they can follow on any shift, naming what to use, how often, how to do it, who is responsible, and when to call the hygienist back. Probing depths and bleeding maps are clinical data those staff are not trained to interpret. A diagnosis written in regulatory terminology does not tell anyone what to do, and a medication list alone gives no care instruction at all.
  3. The correct answer is C (Keep him upright, use minimal water with suction, and apply paste sparingly): a client who aspirates thin fluids is at risk from the water used during care, so an upright position, minimal water with continuous suction, and only a small smear of a non foaming paste reduce that risk. Copious irrigation increases the volume he could aspirate. Reclining him fully worsens airway protection rather than improving it, and daily mouth care is not within the speech language pathologist's role, although her swallowing assessment shapes how the care is delivered.
  4. The correct answer is B (Attend with a one page summary of findings, protocol and callback triggers): a care conference gives each service only a few minutes, so the hygienist arrives in person with one page setting out the oral findings, the daily protocol, the products, who performs the care and when to call back. Sending the chart in advance without attending removes the chance to answer questions and to get the protocol into the care plan. The dentist cannot speak to a dental hygiene plan, and waiting to be invited leaves oral health out of the resident's plan entirely.
  5. The correct answer is D (Raise it privately with the evidence, then escalate if the concern is unresolved): speaking up about a safety concern is a professional obligation, and graded escalation starts privately with the person involved, focused on the resident and the evidence rather than on personality. If the risk remains, the concern moves up through the home's own process and, where necessary, to the appropriate authority. Accepting the decision leaves a known pneumonia risk unaddressed, going first to the family bypasses the process, and withdrawing service removes the only oral health advocate the resident has.
  6. The correct answer is C (Arrange immediate assessment today through the urgent pathway or emergency department): spreading facial swelling, fever and trismus point to a spreading odontogenic infection that can compromise the airway, which is an urgent referral rather than a routine one. Swelling that closes the eye and limits opening to 15 mm indicates the infection has moved well beyond the tooth. A two week appointment or salt water rinses allow the infection to progress, and referral to an orthodontist misreads trismus caused by infection as a joint problem.
  7. The correct answer is A (Refer to a periodontist for assessment of surgical management of these sites): deep residual sites that keep bleeding after thorough non surgical therapy and re-instrumentation, especially with grade II furcation involvement, are exactly the situation that warrants periodontal referral. Home care is good and she does not smoke, so the usual modifiable factors have already been addressed. Extraction is premature before a specialist opinion, deep pockets do not indicate pulpal disease, and simply re-evaluating in a year allows further attachment loss.
  8. The correct answer is B (An endodontist, because the findings suggest pulpal necrosis with apical infection): no response to cold testing, tenderness to percussion, a sinus tract and an isolated narrow probing defect on a single surface together describe a necrotic pulp draining along the root, not generalized periodontal breakdown. A narrow solitary defect beside healthy adjacent teeth is a drainage tract rather than a periodontal pocket. Malocclusion and restorative coverage do not explain the loss of pulp vitality, so neither orthodontic nor prosthodontic referral addresses the actual problem.
  9. The correct answer is A (An orthodontist, because the crowding is contributing to the gingival disease): orthodontic referral is indicated for malocclusion and crowding that contributes to disease, and here severe rotation and lingual displacement of 31 and 41 make plaque control impossible despite a motivated client and repeated instruction. Surgery is not indicated for gingival bleeding at two sites that has a mechanical cause. Extracting sound lower incisors is not the first response to crowding, and crowding does not resolve spontaneously as the jaws grow.
  10. The correct answer is D (Refer him to a physician or nurse practitioner for assessment of hypertension): repeated readings well above normal on three separate occasions in a client with no diagnosis are a finding the hygienist may be the first to detect, and the appropriate route is referral to a primary care provider for assessment and diagnosis. Recording the readings and waiting a year leaves untreated hypertension. Telling him to self monitor and self interpret shifts a clinical judgment onto the client, and he has no acute symptoms that would justify emergency admission.
  11. The correct answer is B (Arrange referral for medical assessment of possible undiagnosed diabetes without delay): polydipsia, nocturia and unexplained weight loss alongside rapidly worsening periodontitis and multiple abscesses are classic signs of undiagnosed or poorly controlled diabetes, and a hygienist who sees them first refers for medical assessment. Extensive debridement before the systemic picture is known does not address the driver of the disease and healing will be impaired. Periodontal referral and dietary advice both leave a potentially serious undiagnosed systemic condition unassessed.
  12. The correct answer is C (The SBAR format, closing with a specific recommendation or clear request): SBAR gives situation, background, assessment and recommendation, which delivers the essential information in order and finishes with something the physician can act on immediately. A long chronological narrative buries the urgent detail. An open ended request to advise gives the physician nothing specific to answer, and posting a letter for an acute infection with fever delays the response by days.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Repeat the drug and the dose back and have the dentist confirm it): a closed loop readback, where the receiver repeats the instruction and the sender confirms it, is the standard defence against drug name and dose errors given verbally in a noisy environment. It takes seconds and resolves the uncertainty before anything is given. Charting an instruction that may have been misheard preserves the error, acting on a guess risks harm, and asking a third person who also heard it through the noise only adds another layer of assumption.
  2. The correct answer is B (Who the client is, what was done, what is outstanding, risks and next steps): handover fails at transitions, so the same structure is used every time and it names the client, the treatment completed, the outstanding items such as pending consultations, the risks, and what the receiving clinician must do next. Contact details and appointment dates carry none of the clinical risk. Radiographs without a summary leave the locum to reconstruct the plan, and a purely verbal handover of 40 clients leaves no record when a detail is forgotten.
  3. The correct answer is D (Asking for the most recent international normalized ratio value and its date): a useful consultation request ends in a closed, specific and answerable question, and the value with its date is exactly what determines whether extensive instrumentation is safe. A request to please advise gives the physician no idea what decision is being made. Quadrant terminology is not language a physician can interpret, and asking for a complete medical record is a disproportionate request that still would not deliver a current value.
  4. The correct answer is A (Provide care carrying no added risk, document, and follow up the request): while waiting for a reply the client is not abandoned, so oral hygiene instruction, dietary counselling, radiographs if indicated and non invasive assessment all proceed, the outstanding request is documented, and the follow up is made in writing. Treating on an assumption of what the physician would probably say is exactly what the consultation was meant to prevent. Cancelling outright wastes the visit, and the client is not the person to interpret her own medical risk.
  5. The correct answer is C (To the nurse practitioner, who can answer the consultation request in full): in much of Canada a nurse practitioner is the client's primary provider and can confirm a diagnosis, report control of a chronic condition, release laboratory values and adjust medications. Sending the request elsewhere delays the answer and duplicates work for no benefit. A hospital holds no responsibility for her routine care, a distant physician has never assessed her, and asking the client to interpret her own results places a clinical judgment on someone not trained to make it.
  6. The correct answer is A (Her community pharmacist, who holds the complete dispensing history): the pharmacist is the fastest accurate route to a reconciled medication list, an interaction check and a full dispensing history, which is precisely what is missing when a client arrives with unlabelled bottles from several sources. A receptionist may read out a chart summary but cannot verify what was actually dispensed or check interactions. A family member's recollection is not a medication record, and a manufacturer can describe a product but knows nothing about this client.
  7. The correct answer is B (Discuss the concern privately with the evidence and involve the client in deciding): when a dentist and a hygienist disagree about a plan, the resolution is neither automatic deference nor unilateral action. The hygienist raises the concern privately with her reasoning and evidence, and the client is brought into the conversation because informed consent belongs to the client. Proceeding as instructed with a blood pressure of 186/112 mmHg and uncontrolled diabetes ignores an independent professional accountability that employment does not transfer, and criticizing the dentist in front of the client or silently shortening treatment resolves nothing.
  8. The correct answer is C (State clearly that she is not comfortable proceeding until instruments are reprocessed): graded escalation means stating the observation, stating the concern, proposing an action, and then stopping clearly when the answer does not resolve the risk. Visible debris and gaps in the sterilizer log are an immediate infection prevention risk to the client now sitting in the chair. Using the cassette once accepts a known risk, asking the client to consent to unsafe instruments is not a legitimate transfer of the decision, and resigning skips the step that actually protects this client.
  9. The correct answer is B (The task is within scope, the person is competent and authorized, and supervision is available): delegation transfers performance of a task while accountability for the decision to delegate stays with the person delegating, so the task must be within her own authorized scope, the receiver must be competent and authorized to accept it, supervision must be available and the client's situation must be stable. Willingness and parental consent alone do not establish competence or authority. A signature after the fact does not make an unsafe delegation safe, and watching a procedure three times is not a measure of competence.
  10. The correct answer is D (Decline, state the competence gap, and arrange training or another provider): accepting a delegated act carries its own duty, and a clinician who is not currently competent to perform it safely declines and says so. Twelve years without performing the task and no experience with this equipment is a genuine currency gap, not a preference. Attempting the tasks because someone else called them straightforward, splitting them, or raising the issue only afterwards all expose the client to a procedure performed by someone who knows they are not currently competent.
  11. The correct answer is A (Decline for now, verify the provincial scope, and obtain formal training first): working within competence means recognizing the boundary of your own education, experience and currency, then seeking training, consulting or referring rather than proceeding. Because scope, self initiation and authorized procedures vary between provinces, the scope question has to be answered before the training question. A client request does not create competence or authority, lasers are not automatically outside every hygienist's scope, and a low setting does not remove the risk of using an unfamiliar device.
  12. The correct answer is C (Collaborating, exploring the interest behind each position with the client involved): collaborating looks for the interest behind each stated position and is the preferred approach for a clinical disagreement that affects a client, particularly when both positions have merit and the client can express a preference. Avoiding leaves the client without a decision. Settling it by seniority substitutes rank for reasoning, and accommodating simply hands the outcome to whoever argues hardest rather than to whichever option serves this client best.
  13. The correct answer is D (Competing, because immediate action is required to protect the client's life): competing is defensible in an immediate safety emergency, and an unresponsive client with agonal breathing requires the emergency protocol to begin now rather than after a telephone call. Consent from a family member is not required to provide emergency care to an unresponsive person. Accommodating, compromising and avoiding all consume time the client does not have, and each leaves the emergency kit in the corridor while the situation deteriorates.
  14. The correct answer is B (The client with the mechanical prosthetic aortic valve, which is highest risk): a prosthetic heart valve, or prosthetic material used in valve repair, sits in the narrow highest risk group for which infective endocarditis prophylaxis is still recommended. A bicuspid aortic valve is isolated acquired or congenital valve disease without prosthetic material and no longer warrants cover. Pacemakers and coronary bypass surgery were both removed from the recommendation, because neither creates the highest risk of an adverse outcome from endocarditis.
  15. The correct answer is A (Amoxicillin 2 g by mouth, 30 to 60 minutes before the appointment): the standard adult regimen is a single 2 g oral dose of amoxicillin taken 30 to 60 minutes before the procedure, with 50 mg/kg for children. A single pre-procedure dose is the point, so repeated smaller doses the day before do not produce adequate blood levels at the time of bacteremia. Clindamycin is no longer recommended because of serious adverse effects, and a dose taken only after the appointment misses the period it was meant to cover.
  16. The correct answer is D (Azithromycin or doxycycline, which are recommended alternatives in penicillin allergy): current guidance for a penicillin allergic client uses doxycycline, azithromycin or clarithromycin. Clindamycin was removed because of serious adverse effects including Clostridioides difficile infection. A cephalosporin such as cephalexin is only an option when the allergy history does not include anaphylaxis, angioedema or urticaria, and hives with lip swelling within an hour describes exactly the reaction that rules it out. Omitting prophylaxis altogether is not needed, because effective alternatives exist.
  17. The correct answer is C (The dose may be given now, since it is within two hours afterwards): a missed prophylactic dose may still be given up to two hours after the procedure, and 20 minutes is comfortably inside that window. Withholding the dose loses the only remaining opportunity to provide cover for the bacteremia that has already occurred. Doubling the dose adds toxicity without added benefit, and postponing it to the next appointment would cover a procedure that has not yet happened while leaving today's completely uncovered.
  18. The correct answer is A (A visit for radiographs, alginate impressions and orthodontic bracket placement): prophylaxis applies only to procedures that manipulate gingival tissue or the periapical region, or that perforate oral mucosa, and radiographs, impressions and bracket placement are all named as procedures that do not. Subgingival debridement with local anesthesia manipulates gingival tissue directly. Full mouth probing with subgingival exploration also breaches the sulcular epithelium, and an extraction is the clearest example of a covered procedure.
  19. The correct answer is B (Prophylaxis is indicated, because transplant valvulopathy is a highest risk condition): cardiac transplant recipients who go on to develop valvulopathy are one of the four groups in the narrow highest risk list, alongside prosthetic valve material, previous infective endocarditis and specific congenital heart disease. Immunosuppression does not remove the indication, it reinforces the concern. Subgingival debridement manipulates gingival tissue and is a covered procedure just as an extraction is, and an indication established by guideline is not left to the client to choose on the day.
  20. The correct answer is C (No, because rheumatic fever without valve dysfunction is not a highest risk condition): a history of rheumatic fever with no resulting valve dysfunction and no prosthetic material is explicitly among the conditions that no longer warrant prophylaxis, and her cardiologist's letter confirms both. Historical practice covered every rheumatic fever history for life, which is the outdated recommendation the exam tests. Scaling does manipulate gingival tissue and would be a covered procedure if she had a qualifying cardiac condition, so that reasoning is wrong even though the conclusion of no antibiotic happens to be right.
  21. The correct answer is D (Routine prophylaxis for prosthetic joints is no longer generally recommended): orthopedic and dental organizations concluded that the evidence does not support a link between dental procedures and prosthetic joint infection, and that the harms of routine antibiotics outweigh any benefit. This client has no previous joint infection, no immunosuppression and no written direction from his surgeon, so nothing individual applies. Two year and lifelong cover are both outdated blanket rules, and the duration of an appointment has never been an indication for prophylaxis.
  22. The correct answer is B (Attend immediately, because replantation success falls sharply with each passing minute): avulsion of a permanent tooth is an urgent referral, and the prognosis for replantation depends heavily on how long the periodontal ligament cells survive out of the socket. Milk is an appropriate transport medium, but it buys time rather than removing the urgency. Waiting two days makes replantation far less likely to succeed, discarding a viable permanent incisor forfeits the tooth altogether, and hot tap water destroys the remaining ligament cells.
  23. The correct answer is C (Contact the oncology team now to plan dental clearance before radiotherapy starts): dental clearance, the timing of any extractions and the management of mucositis and osteoradionecrosis risk are decided jointly with the oncology team, and three weeks is a narrow window in which extraction sites still need time to heal. Scaling alone leaves two grossly carious molars in a field about to be irradiated. Postponing all care until after radiotherapy is the opposite of what is needed, and prosthodontic planning does not address the immediate radiation risk.
  24. The correct answer is A (She remains accountable for the care she kept and confirms the referral outcome): a referral transfers part of the care to a provider with different expertise, while the referring clinician stays accountable for the portion retained and for closing the loop, which includes confirming that the client attended and obtaining the outcome. Six weeks of silence is a prompt to follow up in writing. Accountability does not transfer wholesale on sending a letter, maintenance care should continue in the meantime, and leaving the whole process to the client is how referrals are lost.
Case C021

Mr. Cardinal is 84 and lives in a long term care home. He has moderate dementia, takes an anticholinergic bladder medication, and wears a maxillary partial denture. A personal support worker reports that he clenches his mouth shut during morning care and that his gums bleed. On assessment you find heavy plaque, generalized marginal redness, dry mucosa and two retained roots in the lower left quadrant.

  1. The correct answer is B (Write a short protocol naming the product, frequency, technique and when to call you.): personal support workers deliver daily mouth care and need a task level instruction, not a clinical record. A protocol that names the product, how often, how to do it and the trigger for calling the hygienist can be followed by any staff member on any shift. Leaving a periodontal chart at the bedside gives staff data they are not trained to interpret and does not tell them what to do. Teaching one worker and relying on informal handover fails as soon as that worker is off or leaves.
  2. The correct answer is D (Ask the prescriber and pharmacist to review whether the anticholinergic can be changed.): the anticholinergic bladder medication is the probable cause of his xerostomia, and only the prescriber can change it, with the pharmacist advising on alternatives and interactions. Addressing the cause is more effective than treating the symptom over and over. Lemon glycerin swabs are acidic and drying and are not appropriate for a dry mouth. Advising the family to stop a prescribed medication is outside the hygienist's authority and is unsafe, and lengthening the fluoride interval increases caries risk in a client whose saliva is already reduced.
  3. The correct answer is A (An occupational therapist, for adapted equipment and safe positioning during resistive care.): occupational therapists solve access and positioning problems that a hygienist cannot solve from the chair, including seating a leaning resident safely and adapting equipment so staff can reach the mouth. They can also coach staff on approaches to resistive care in dementia. A denturist or a dental technician would address the appliance, which is not what is preventing daily brushing. A social worker arranges services and supports but does not assess seating or hand function.
  4. The correct answer is C (Request a speech language pathologist swallowing assessment and use suction with minimal water.): coughing at meals, a wet voice after drinking and weight loss are classic signs of dysphagia, and a client who aspirates is at risk from water used during oral care. The speech language pathologist assesses swallowing and advises on positioning, consistency and safe technique, while minimal water and good suction reduce risk in the meantime. A high volume water spray increases the volume he could aspirate. A chest radiograph investigates a consequence rather than the swallowing problem, and withholding oral care altogether raises the risk of aspiration pneumonia rather than lowering it.
  5. The correct answer is A (Routine referral to an oral and maxillofacial surgeon, recording the sites as 36 and 37.): retained roots that are asymptomatic, with no swelling and no sinus tract, warrant a routine surgical opinion rather than an urgent one, and the sites must be recorded in FDI notation so the receiving surgeon reads the same teeth you examined. His daughter as substitute decision maker can give consent on his behalf, so incapacity is not a reason to withhold referral. Urgent referral is reserved for spreading swelling, trismus with fever, airway compromise or uncontrolled bleeding. A periodontist treats the supporting tissues around teeth that are being retained, so surgical removal of retained roots in a medically and cognitively complex resident belongs with the oral and maxillofacial surgeon.
  6. The correct answer is B (Enter the oral care protocol into the resident's nursing care plan.): the nursing care plan is the document staff actually work from every shift, so a protocol that lives only in the dental chart will not be carried out. Getting oral care written into the care plan makes it an assigned task with an owner and a frequency. Filing it in the dental chart or emailing the director of care leaves it invisible to the people at the bedside. A sheet posted in a shared bathroom is not part of the resident's record, breaches his privacy and disappears quickly.
Case C022

Ms. Okonkwo is 57 and has a diagnosed squamous cell carcinoma of the left lateral tongue. Her radiation oncologist plans six weeks of radiotherapy to the head and neck beginning in three weeks, with concurrent chemotherapy. She has generalized moderate periodontitis, a heavily broken down tooth 37 with a periapical radiolucency, and reports that she brushes once a day. She has no other medical conditions and takes no regular medication.

  1. The correct answer is C (Contact the radiation oncologist now to agree dental clearance and extraction timing.): dental clearance before head and neck radiotherapy is a joint decision, because which teeth come out and when depends on the radiation field, the dose and the start date. Contacting the oncologist first sets the timeline every other step depends on. Starting periodontal therapy without that contact risks missing the window for extractions. Waiting for radiotherapy to begin wastes the only safe interval for surgery, and deferring the extraction of a non restorable tooth until after radiotherapy exposes her to osteoradionecrosis risk.
  2. The correct answer is A (Extraction should be completed at least two to three weeks before radiotherapy begins.): teeth that cannot be saved are removed before radiotherapy so that the extraction sockets have time to heal while the bone still has a normal blood supply. Two to three weeks of healing before the first fraction is the usual target, agreed with the oncology team. Extracting during treatment or after radiotherapy carries a much higher risk of osteoradionecrosis in irradiated bone. A tooth with a periapical radiolucency and gross breakdown will not be resolved by fluoride and debridement.
  3. The correct answer is D (Bland saline and sodium bicarbonate rinses with a soft brush and daily fluoride.): mucositis management is built on bland, non irritating rinses, gentle mechanical cleaning with a soft brush and continued fluoride to protect teeth while saliva falls. This keeps plaque down without adding chemical injury to ulcerated mucosa. Alcohol containing mouthwash and hydrogen peroxide both irritate and dehydrate damaged mucosa and are not recommended. Stopping brushing entirely lets plaque and infection build at the very point when her defences are lowest.
  4. The correct answer is B (Daily high concentration fluoride in custom trays, with dietitian input on supplement choice.): post radiation xerostomia produces rapidly progressing cervical caries, and daily high concentration fluoride delivered in custom trays is the standard preventive measure. Working with the oncology dietitian lets her meet her calorie needs with products and timing that do not bathe the teeth in sugar. Frequent sipping of sweetened supplements is exactly the pattern that drives radiation caries. A six monthly varnish recall is far too infrequent for this level of risk, and salivary function after head and neck radiotherapy does not recover within a month.
  5. The correct answer is D (Consult the radiation oncologist about the dose delivered to that site and osteoradionecrosis risk.): the risk of osteoradionecrosis depends on the dose the specific site received and on the radiation field, which only the oncology team can supply. That information shapes whether the tooth is extracted, how, and what precautions are used. The risk does not return to baseline after twelve months, so a routine referral without consultation is unsafe. Antibiotics alone do not prevent osteoradionecrosis, and hyperbaric oxygen is a selective adjunct used on assessment, not a lifelong requirement before every dental procedure.
Case C023

Ms. Tremblay is 29 and is 26 weeks pregnant with her first child. Her care is led by a registered midwife. She reports bleeding gums when she brushes, nausea in the mornings with occasional vomiting, and frequent sipping of sweetened ginger tea through the day. Clinical assessment shows generalized marginal inflammation, bleeding on probing at most sites, no attachment loss, and early erosion on the palatal surfaces of the upper anterior teeth.

  1. The correct answer is A (Provide non surgical periodontal therapy now, because care is safe in every trimester.): non surgical periodontal therapy during pregnancy has been studied in large trials and is safe, so there is no reason to leave active inflammation untreated for three more months. Treating now also removes the bleeding and discomfort she is reporting. Deferring until after delivery leaves disease active during a period of exaggerated gingival response. Restricting care to the first trimester is a myth, and written obstetric clearance is not required for routine dental hygiene care in an uncomplicated pregnancy.
  2. The correct answer is C (Treatment is safe and supports her own oral health, with no promised birth benefit.): observational studies link periodontitis with preterm birth and low birth weight, but randomized treatment trials have not consistently shown that treating periodontitis during pregnancy reduces those outcomes. Those same trials did establish that the treatment is safe. Promising a lower risk of preterm birth or telling her that gingivitis is a proven cause of low birth weight overstates evidence that does not exist. Refusing treatment because the evidence is uncertain confuses the birth outcome question with her own clear need for periodontal care.
  3. The correct answer is B (Use shorter appointments with a left lateral tilt to avoid supine hypotension.): at 26 weeks the gravid uterus can compress the inferior vena cava when the client lies flat, producing faintness and breathlessness, and a left lateral tilt with a wedge under the right hip relieves it. Shorter appointments with position changes keep her comfortable enough to complete the plan. Keeping her fully supine reproduces the symptom she has already described. A rigid upright position is not necessary and makes access difficult, and delaying to 36 weeks leaves active inflammation untreated when comfort will be worse, not better.
  4. The correct answer is A (Rinse with water or a sodium bicarbonate solution and delay brushing afterwards.): gastric acid softens the enamel surface, and brushing immediately abrades the softened layer away. Rinsing with water or a bicarbonate solution raises the pH and allows remineralization before the brush is used. Brushing straight afterwards with a medium bristle brush is the behaviour driving her palatal erosion. An acidic mouthwash adds more acid rather than buffering it, and fluoride is protective in erosion, so avoiding fluoride toothpaste would remove one of her best defences.
  5. The correct answer is D (A short note with your findings, the care provided and the infant guidance.): a brief, readable summary lets the midwife reinforce the same messages at prenatal visits and follow up on the baby's early oral health. Sharing findings, what was done and the anticipatory guidance is enough for another profession to act on. A full periodontal chart with six point probing depths is data the midwife cannot use. Asking for antibiotics has no indication here, and sending nothing at all breaks a collaborative relationship the midwife has specifically asked for.
  6. The correct answer is C (Work with school staff and a community health worker to gather consent in person.): consent return rates are the limiting step in school based varnish programs, and in person contact through people families already trust raises them far more than paper does. School staff and community health workers know which families need help with the form and can answer questions in the family's own language. Repeat mailings reach the same families who did not respond the first time. Making the program mandatory removes informed consent, and moving the clinic off site adds the transport barrier the school program was designed to remove.
  7. The correct answer is B (Demonstrate the technique with him present, watch her repeat it, then leave written steps.): teaching a caregiver works when it is done on the actual client, with a return demonstration that lets the hygienist correct technique and address the fear of causing bleeding. Written steps left behind then act as the reminder between visits. A pamphlet alone does not change a behaviour driven by anxiety about hurting him. Asking the agency for a differently trained worker is not usually possible and does not help this worker, and substituting a rinse for brushing leaves the plaque in place.
  8. The correct answer is A (Ask for the most recent glycated hemoglobin value and the date it was taken.): a consultation request should ask one closed question the physician can answer from the record, and the glycated hemoglobin with its date tells you how well controlled his diabetes has been over the preceding months. That single value shapes healing expectations, appointment planning and the periodontal prognosis you discuss with him. Asking the physician whether periodontal therapy is appropriate hands over a dental hygiene decision that belongs to the hygienist. Requesting a medication change is outside the request, and prosthetic joint style prophylaxis is not indicated by diabetes alone.
  9. The correct answer is D (Periodontal therapy gives a modest reduction in glycated hemoglobin alongside her medical care.): intervention trials in people with type 2 diabetes show that non surgical periodontal therapy lowers glycated hemoglobin by a few tenths of a percentage point at three to six months. That effect is real and worth describing honestly, and it sits beside her medical management rather than replacing any part of it. Claiming that it replaces a medication or drops her result by two full points is a serious overclaim that could lead her to stop treatment. Saying it has no effect at all understates evidence that is among the strongest in this area.
  10. The correct answer is D (A short summary of inflammation resolution, remaining risk and the maintenance interval.): the diabetes team needs to know whether the periodontal infection settled, what risk remains and how often she will be seen, because that information feeds into their own management. A concise summary in language another profession understands is what makes the loop useful. Sending the full chart buries the answer in probing data the team cannot interpret. Claiming a cure is inaccurate for a chronic condition under maintenance, and a verbal message left with a receptionist creates no record and often never arrives.
  11. The correct answer is C (Refer for dental assessment and arrange fluoride varnish at well child medical visits.): white spot lesions at age two are active early childhood caries and need dental assessment now, while the family physician the child already sees regularly is the practical place to deliver repeated varnish applications. Using both pathways closes the gap for a family with no dental home. Waiting until age three lets cavitation develop, and the recommended first dental visit is by the first birthday. Telling the parent to stop the bottle and returning in a year abandons follow up, and long term systemic fluoride supplements are not first line here.
  12. The correct answer is B (Coordinate with the speech language pathologist and the cleft palate team on shared goals.): a child with a repaired cleft palate is already followed by a coordinated team, and the speech language pathologist works on tongue and lip function that overlaps directly with oral hygiene, feeding and the open bite. Aligning goals prevents the family receiving contradictory instructions from different providers. An orthodontic referral alone treats the occlusion and ignores the speech and hygiene picture. Plaque removal does not change speech, and delaying oral hygiene instruction until therapy ends leaves an active caries risk unaddressed.
  13. The correct answer is D (An occupational therapist, to assess hand function and adapt her brush handle.): the barrier is a mechanical one, because she cannot grip the handle, and occupational therapists assess hand function and build up or adapt handles so a client can keep doing the task herself. That preserves the independence she wants. A physiotherapist works on strength and movement but is not the usual route for adapted daily living equipment. A social worker cannot arrange support she has refused, and asking a rheumatologist to change medication to improve grip is neither realistic nor within this referral's purpose.
  14. The correct answer is A (Involve a social worker to look at transport, funding and appointment timing options.): she has named three social barriers, transport, shift work and cost, and social workers are the team members who connect clients with subsidy programs, transport supports and community resources. Addressing those barriers is what will get her into the chair. Discharging a client who wants treatment punishes her for circumstances she did not choose. Shortening the recall interval adds more appointments she cannot reach, and repeating the education assumes a knowledge problem when the problem is access.
  15. The correct answer is C (Provide non invasive care, document the delay and follow up the request in writing.): while a consultation is outstanding you neither treat on an assumption nor send the client away with nothing. Oral hygiene instruction, dietary counselling, radiographs if indicated and non invasive assessment carry no added bleeding risk and keep her engaged, while a written follow up creates the record that the request was chased. Proceeding with full mouth debridement treats on a guess about her anticoagulation. Cancelling outright loses the client, and asking her to chase her own physician transfers a professional responsibility onto her.
  16. The correct answer is B (The date, the physician's name, the content of the advice and a readback confirmation.): a telephone reply is acceptable collaboration, but only if it is recorded with enough detail to stand as part of the clinical record, including who gave the advice, when, exactly what was said, and confirmation that it was read back and agreed. Closed loop readback is what prevents drug name and dose errors. A day sheet note that a call happened records nothing clinically useful. Passing the task to the dentist to write up later loses detail, and marking the request answered does not capture the answer itself.
  17. The correct answer is B (What the hygienist wants done now, such as same day medical assessment.): in a structured handover the recommendation is the specific action being requested, stated clearly enough that the receiver can agree or decline. Naming same day assessment leaves no doubt about what is being asked. The medication list and previous readings belong to background, the interpretation of the reading belongs to assessment, and identifying the client and the reason for the visit belongs to situation. Placing content in the wrong section is what makes an urgent call ramble and lose its point.
  18. The correct answer is D (State the concern, decline to proceed, arrange medical assessment and document the facts.): new exertional chest pain within the last two days may be unstable angina, and elective treatment is deferred until the client has been medically assessed. Graded escalation means stating the observation and the concern, saying plainly that you are not comfortable proceeding, and recording the facts. Every Canadian college expects a registrant to refuse an act that could harm a client regardless of who directed it, so following the instruction is not a defence. Treating one quadrant is the same risk in a smaller dose, and refusing silently leaves the client unassessed and uninformed.
  19. The correct answer is A (Decline to delegate, because the assistant is not authorized and trained for the task.): delegation is only valid when the task is within the delegator's own scope, the receiver is authorized and competent, and supervision is available. An assistant without provincial authorization and training for the duty fails that test, and staffing pressure does not change it. Close supervision does not create authority the assistant does not hold. A same day demonstration is not training, and asking the dentist to delegate instead does not remove the hygienist's accountability for a task she knows the assistant is not authorized to perform.
  20. The correct answer is C (Decline, explain the gap in current competence and arrange further training first.): accepting a delegated or directed act carries its own duty, and competence means current education, experience and currency, not something learned once and never practised. Declining and arranging training protects the client and is what a college expects. The dentist being in the next operatory does not make an unfamiliar procedure safe. Recording that the dentist directed it does not transfer accountability, and trying it on an easier client first is an experiment carried out on a person who has not consented to that.
  21. The correct answer is D (One page giving oral findings, the daily protocol, who performs it and when to call.): a care conference decides who does what for a resident, so the most useful contribution is a single page the team can act on and attach to the care plan, naming the findings, the daily routine, the person responsible and the trigger for calling the hygienist back. Detailed pocket depths at each surface mean nothing to nursing, dietary or rehabilitation staff. Monthly transport to a dental office is rarely feasible and does not address daily care, and asking nursing to score plaque at every session adds a workload the team will not sustain.
  22. The correct answer is A (Structured daily oral care reduces pneumonia incidence and mortality in dependent older adults.): this is one of the few oral systemic relationships supported by intervention evidence rather than association alone, with trials in long term care showing fewer pneumonia cases and fewer deaths where structured daily oral care is delivered. That is a defensible answer to give a director of care weighing staff time. No trial shows that oral care prevents myocardial infarction, and periodontal disease and cardiovascular disease remain an association rather than an established cause. Oral care does not prevent the onset of type 2 diabetes, and calling the evidence too weak to act on misstates it.
  23. The correct answer is B (Obtain her current neutrophil and platelet counts from the oncology team before treating.): instrumentation causes bacteremia and bleeding, so the neutrophil and platelet counts determine whether treatment is safe today and whether any precautions are needed. The oncology team can supply current values quickly. Proceeding gently without knowing the counts treats on an assumption in a client who may be neutropenic or thrombocytopenic. Postponing all care until chemotherapy finishes leaves active infection through the period of greatest vulnerability, and substituting a rinse for assessment does not address the bleeding or the inflammation.
  24. The correct answer is C (When blood counts have recovered, usually shortly before the next cycle begins.): elective dental hygiene care is scheduled in the recovery window between cycles, when neutrophil and platelet counts have come back up and the risk of infection and bleeding is lowest, with the timing confirmed against actual counts. The nadir is the point of highest risk, so it is the worst time to instrument. Treating on the day of infusion adds a physiological burden the client does not need. Counts affect dental hygiene care directly, so treating at any point in the cycle without checking is unsafe.
  25. The correct answer is A (Present the evidence on caries reduction and safety alongside public health colleagues.): advocacy on population level oral health measures is part of the dental hygiene role, and the contribution the council needs is an evidence based summary delivered together with public health colleagues so the health message is consistent. Declining to speak abandons a population health responsibility that sits well within the profession. Varnish programs reach fewer people and cost more per child, so they do not replace water fluoridation. Following whichever view polls best substitutes popularity for evidence on a public health decision.
  26. The correct answer is D (Plan the program with community leaders and local health workers from the start.): programs designed elsewhere and delivered into a community have a poor record, and the previous low uptake is the evidence of that. Planning with community leaders and local health workers from the beginning builds a program that fits local priorities, language and the way services are actually used, and it builds the relationships that sustain it after the funding ends. Repeating a failed program with more posters changes the volume rather than the approach. Restricting delivery to the school leaves adults out, and importing an urban protocol unchanged repeats the original mistake.
  27. The correct answer is B (Multidisciplinary, because each profession worked in parallel without a shared plan.): multidisciplinary care means several professions each contribute their own part separately, which is exactly what separate assessments, separate notes and separate recommendations describe. Interprofessional care would mean the three built one plan together and agreed who does what. Collaborative leadership describes leadership shifting to whoever holds the relevant expertise, which cannot happen when the providers never meet. Role clarification is the competency of describing one's own scope and that of others, not a description of how this team is structured.
  28. The correct answer is C (Send the consultation request to the nurse practitioner, who can answer it fully.): in much of Canada a nurse practitioner is the client's primary care provider, and can confirm a diagnosis, report control of a chronic condition, supply laboratory values and adjust medication. The request goes to the provider who manages the condition. Telling the client to find a physician delays care and misunderstands the nurse practitioner's role. Routing the request to a medical director adds a step and a person who does not know the client, and a nurse practitioner can release clinical information with the client's consent like any other provider.
  29. The correct answer is A (With her consent, contact the client's pharmacy for a complete dispensing history.): the pharmacist is the fastest route to an accurate and complete medication list, including doses, dates and interactions, which is exactly the problem when a client brings unlabelled bottles from more than one pharmacy. Consent is obtained first, then the request is made. Searching product names online identifies drugs but not her doses, her adherence or what she has stopped. Treating on a partial list leaves unknown interactions and bleeding risks in play, and a written physician consultation is slower and often less complete than the dispensing record.
  30. The correct answer is B (Refer urgently, document site, size, colour, borders and duration, and confirm attendance.): an indurated ulcer with no obvious local cause that has persisted beyond two weeks in a client who smokes requires urgent referral for definitive assessment and biopsy, with the description recorded in full and attendance confirmed afterwards. Watching it for another four weeks is the delay that costs a stage in oral cancer. A routine referral does not match the urgency, and telling the client it is probably nothing is both unfounded and likely to stop her attending. Adjusting a restoration assumes a local cause that has already been excluded.
  31. The correct answer is D (Recording the referral date, the reason, the provider and the report received back.): a referral is only complete when the loop closes, and the record should show when it was made, why, to whom, and what came back. That is what lets any provider in the office answer the client's question and know what care remains outstanding. Recording only that a referral was discussed does not show it was made or acted on. A telephone number in a contact list is not part of the client's record, and relying on the client to carry the specialist's report shifts continuity onto her.
  32. The correct answer is C (Discuss it privately with the evidence, then present the options to the client together.): a clinical disagreement that affects a client is resolved by collaborating, which means working out the reasoning behind each position privately first, then bringing the client into a conversation she is entitled to have because informed consent belongs to her. Automatic deference is not the answer, since the hygienist holds independent accountability for her own care. Telling the client privately that you disagree undermines the team and puts her in the middle. Proceeding unilaterally with non surgical therapy makes the decision without either the dentist or the client.
  33. The correct answer is A (Escalate the concern to the practice owner, then to the regulatory college if unresolved.): speaking up about a safety concern is a professional obligation, and graded escalation means starting with the person involved, then moving to the practice owner, then to the regulatory college when the risk continues unresolved. Reusing single use devices exposes every client to cross infection, so it cannot be left. Documenting in charts records the harm without preventing it. The police are not the body that regulates infection prevention in a dental practice, and waiting for an infection to appear means waiting for a client to be harmed.
  34. The correct answer is B (She remains accountable to her college and must not record inaccurate treatment times.): being an employee does not transfer professional accountability, and a registrant who records false treatment information participates in it regardless of who wrote the policy or who submits the claim. The college holds her to accurate records and honest billing information. Saying the employer carries the responsibility is the defence colleges explicitly reject. Noting a private disagreement while still recording the false times does not make the record accurate, and waiting for a written direction from the college before acting honestly reverses the obligation.
  35. The correct answer is C (Follow up to confirm the client attended and obtain the outcome with her consent.): the hygienist who first notices signs of undiagnosed systemic disease stays accountable for the part of the care she keeps, which includes confirming that the referral was acted on and learning the outcome so her periodontal plan reflects it. Confirming attendance is what turns a referral into care. Recording the referral and closing the file leaves the finding unresolved. Starting therapy immediately ignores that a diabetes diagnosis changes healing expectations and maintenance, and leaving the follow up to a phone call the client may never make abandons the loop.
Case C024

A dental hygiene clinic runs one tabletop steam sterilizer. On Monday the weekly biological indicator is processed with the first load of the day. On Tuesday the monitoring service reports that the spore test was positive. The mechanical printout for that load shows the cycle reached temperature and pressure, and the autoclave tape on every package in the load had changed colour. The clinic has since run eleven further loads and treated clients from all of them.

  1. The correct answer is B (Take the sterilizer out of service immediately and repeat the spore test): A positive biological indicator means live, highly resistant spores survived the cycle, so the unit cannot be trusted to sterilize anything until the failure is investigated. The immediate steps are removal from service, a repeat test with a fresh biological indicator, and a review of loading, cycle selection and packaging technique. Repeating the cycle and reading autoclave tape proves nothing, because a chemical indicator only records exposure to a condition. Continuing to use the unit exposes every subsequent client to instruments that may not be sterile.
  2. The correct answer is D (Recall and reprocess every load back to the last negative spore test): The failure could have begun at any point after the last documented negative biological test, so every load processed in that window is suspect. Recalling and reprocessing back to the last negative spore test is the standard response, which is why packages carry the sterilizer identifier, load number and date. Treating only the failed load as suspect assumes the fault appeared at that exact cycle, which the monitoring record cannot support. Chemical indicators on those packages do not resolve the question, since they show exposure and not the death of microorganisms.
  3. The correct answer is A (It shows exposure to a condition, not that microorganisms were killed): Chemical indicators change colour in response to one or more cycle conditions, so a changed indicator confirms the package was processed and met those conditions. It cannot confirm that living organisms died, which is why a package can pass every chemical indicator and still be non-sterile. Only a biological indicator challenges the cycle with live spores more resistant than any pathogen on the instruments, so only it proves sterilization was achieved. Claiming the spores were destroyed confuses the two very different kinds of evidence.
  4. The correct answer is C (Yes, because an operator error was identified and the repeat test was negative): Overloading and stacking block steam penetration and are a recognized operator cause of a failed spore test. When a clear operator error is identified and the repeat biological test is negative, the unit may return to service with the incident and the corrective action documented. Servicing plus three consecutive negative biological tests is required only when no operator error is found or the repeat test is also positive. Dismissing the result as a laboratory error ignores the investigation that actually explained it.
  5. The correct answer is C (At least weekly, and with every load containing an implantable device): Biological monitoring is run at least weekly, and additionally with every load that contains an implantable device, because an implant failure carries far greater consequences for the client. That load is ideally held until the biological result is known. Relying on the routine weekly test alone leaves implant instruments unverified for up to seven days. Monthly testing is too infrequent to limit the size of a recall when a failure occurs.
Case C025

A hygienist is preparing to treat a client who requires full mouth ultrasonic debridement. The client reports a recent gastrointestinal illness with vomiting and diarrhea that was diagnosed as Clostridioides difficile infection and treated three weeks ago. The hygienist has just finished cleaning a spill in the sterilizing area wearing utility gloves, and her hands are visibly soiled. The operatory is a single closed room with one chair and a standard ventilation system.

  1. The correct answer is A (Wash with soap and water, because alcohol does not remove bacterial spores): Two separate conditions each require soap and water here. Hands that are visibly soiled cannot be decontaminated by an alcohol rub, and alcohol has no activity against bacterial spores such as Clostridioides difficile. Mechanical removal by lathering for fifteen to twenty seconds, rinsing with the fingertips down and drying with a single use towel is what physically carries spores and soil away. Alcohol rub is the preferred option only when hands are clean and dry and no spore forming organism is in question.
  2. The correct answer is D (Gown, mask, eye protection, then gloves after hand hygiene is performed): Donning runs hand hygiene, gown, mask, eye protection, then gloves last. Gloves go on last so they cover the gown cuffs and stay clean while the mask and eyewear are being positioned and adjusted. Putting gloves on before the mask or eyewear means the gloves are handled and adjusted around the face, which contaminates them before care even begins. Pulling a gown on over a mask and eyewear disturbs both and leaves the cuffs exposed.
  3. The correct answer is B (Gloves, gown, hand hygiene, eye protection, mask, then hand hygiene again): Doffing follows the level of contamination rather than the reverse of donning, and reversing it is a classic trap. Gloves are the most contaminated item and come off first, then the gown is rolled away from the body so the soiled outer surface folds inward, then hand hygiene is performed. Eye protection is removed by the arms or headband and the mask comes off last by its ties or ear loops, followed by hand hygiene again. Removing the face covering early means it is handled while the hands are still grossly contaminated.
  4. The correct answer is A (High volume evacuation with a wide bore tip near the working end): High volume evacuation removes a large air volume close to the source and is the most effective aerosol control available in the operatory. The tip must be wide bore and held near the working end of the instrument, because capture falls away sharply with distance. A saliva ejector moves far less air, does not capture aerosol, and can allow backflow if the client closes the lips around it. A pre-procedural rinse lowers the oral microbial load and therefore the microbial content of the aerosol, but it does not reduce the aerosol itself.
  5. The correct answer is D (Pause, remove and replace the mask, because a damp mask loses filtration): A mask is changed between clients and whenever it becomes damp, because moisture destroys filtration efficiency and wicks fluid through the material to the wearer's face. Pausing to replace it mid appointment is the correct response, with hand hygiene before the fresh mask is handled and before gloving again. Dampness never improves protection; it defeats it. A face shield protects against spatter and droplets reaching the eyes and face but does not filter inhaled air, so it cannot substitute for a mask.
Case C026

A hygienist works alone in a small independent practice. After a morning of scaling she carries a tray of used curettes, a mouth mirror, a slow speed handpiece with a prophylaxis angle, and a used blood pressure cuff into the reprocessing room. The room has one bench with a sink at one end, an ultrasonic cleaner, a wrapping station and a tabletop steam sterilizer at the other end. The waterline test result from last month was 780 colony forming units per millilitre.

  1. The correct answer is C (Clean ultrasonically, rinse, dry, inspect, package, sterilize, then store the packages): Reprocessing runs one way from dirty to clean with no backtracking: clean, rinse, dry, inspect, package, sterilize, then store. Drying before packaging prevents wet packs and corrosion, and inspection under good light catches retained debris, damage and separated tips before an instrument is sealed away. Packaging or sterilizing before cleaning leaves bioburden sealed inside, which no cycle can correct. Rinsing or drying after sterilization would break the package and defeat the whole process.
  2. The correct answer is B (Dried debris shields organisms from the steam, so items can leave contaminated): Blood, saliva and calculus physically shield microorganisms from contact with steam, and dried bioburden insulates them further, so a soiled instrument can complete a perfect cycle and still carry viable organisms. This is why cleaning always precedes sterilization, using an ultrasonic cleaner or instrument washer rather than hand scrubbing where possible. Steam does not reliably destroy organisms it cannot reach, so relying on the cycle to burn debris off is unsafe. The chemical indicator and the printout are unaffected by the debris, which is exactly why they give false reassurance.
  3. The correct answer is B (Sterilize the curettes, mirror and handpiece, clean and disinfect the cuff): Curettes are critical items because they penetrate soft tissue, so they are heat sterilized or single use. The mouth mirror and the handpiece are semi critical items that contact mucous membranes, and both are heat tolerant, so both are heat sterilized rather than disinfected. The blood pressure cuff is non-critical because it contacts intact skin only, so cleaning with low or intermediate level disinfection, or a barrier, is appropriate. Disinfecting a heat tolerant semi critical item when sterilization is possible falls below the required standard.
  4. The correct answer is A (The handpiece is heat tolerant and must be steam sterilized between clients): Handpieces are semi critical items that contact mucous membranes, and they draw fluids internally during use, so external wiping cannot decontaminate the internal channels. They are heat tolerant, and modern handpieces are heat sterilized between every client according to the manufacturer's instructions, which for most units means steam. Surface disinfecting a handpiece is never acceptable, regardless of whether visible blood was present. Flushing the lines is part of waterline management and does nothing to reprocess the device itself.
  5. The correct answer is D (Use sterile saline delivered through a sterile single use irrigation device): Surgical procedures that cut bone or open soft tissue require a sterile irrigant delivered through a sterile device, because a dental unit waterline cannot deliver sterile water under any treatment regimen. The 500 colony forming units per millilitre drinking water standard applies to non-surgical care only, so a compliant count does not authorize surgical use. Flushing reduces the organisms released from stagnant tubing but does not sterilize the water. A point of use filter lowers counts and still does not produce sterile water.
  6. The correct answer is C (Biofilm forms on the narrow tubing walls and sheds organisms into the water): Waterline tubing has a very narrow bore, a high surface area to volume ratio and long stagnant periods between clients, which is close to ideal for biofilm formation on the inner walls. The established biofilm continuously sheds heterotrophic bacteria, Legionella and Pseudomonas into the delivered water, so new tubing is recolonized within weeks unless a treatment regimen is in place. Control needs treatment products, independent reservoirs, filters, periodic shocking, anti-retraction valves and routine testing rather than tubing replacement alone. Municipal water supplied to the clinic meets the drinking water standard when it arrives.
  7. The correct answer is A (Flush the lines and the attached devices for twenty to thirty seconds): Lines and any attached devices are flushed for twenty to thirty seconds after each client, which discharges water and any material retracted into the device during treatment. Several minutes of flushing belongs at the start of the day, when water has been standing in the lines overnight. Shocking is a periodic high concentration treatment used on a defined schedule or after a failed test, not a between client procedure. An anti-retraction valve reduces suck back but does not remove the need to flush, and valves fail without warning.
  8. The correct answer is A (Intact barriers can be replaced without disinfecting the surface between clients): A barrier placed on a clean surface and removed while gloved keeps the surface underneath uncontaminated, so it can simply be replaced with a fresh barrier between clients. Those surfaces are still cleaned and disinfected at the end of the day, and immediately whenever a barrier is found torn, displaced or breached. Barriers therefore reduce the between client workload rather than removing the cleaning requirement altogether. A barrier is never applied over a contaminated surface, since it traps rather than removes the bioburden.
  9. The correct answer is B (The surface must stay visibly wet for the full stated contact time): The manufacturer's contact time is the period the product needs to be in wet contact with the surface to achieve its claimed kill, and the surface must remain visibly wet for all of it. A surface wiped dry after fifteen seconds has been cleaned but not disinfected, so the correct action is to reapply and allow the full three minutes, reapplying again if it dries early. Cleaning first is necessary but is only the first of the two steps. No hospital grade disinfectant achieves its label claim on momentary contact.
  10. The correct answer is D (Clean the area first, then disinfect with the tuberculocidal intermediate product): Any blood contaminated surface needs an intermediate level disinfectant carrying a tuberculocidal claim, and disinfection is always the second of two steps. Cleaning removes the visible blood and organic load that would otherwise inactivate the product and shield organisms, and the disinfectant is then applied for its full stated contact time. A low level product without a tuberculocidal claim is limited to housekeeping surfaces such as floors and walls. Covering visible blood with a barrier leaves the contamination in place until the end of the day.
  11. The correct answer is C (The portal of exit, since fluids are captured as they leave the mouth): The portal of exit is the route organisms take out of the reservoir, which in dentistry means coughing, spatter, sulcular bleeding and ultrasonic aerosol. High volume evacuation and a rubber dam capture or block those fluids at the moment they leave the mouth, so they act on that link. Measures at the portal of entry are the mask, eye protection, gloves and sharps safety, which protect the receiving surface instead. Immunization and general health protect the susceptible host, and neither device destroys organisms within the reservoir itself.
  12. The correct answer is A (The same protective measures are used for every client, every appointment): Routine practices are applied to every client at every appointment regardless of diagnosis or presumed infectious status, because many infections are asymptomatic or in a window period. Blood, saliva and all body fluids except sweat are treated as potentially infectious for everyone, so no additional measures are triggered by this disclosure. Adding precautions only for disclosed conditions gives a false sense of safety with the undisclosed and undiagnosed majority. Additional precautions are reserved for a suspected transmissible organism such as an airborne infection, not for a household contact of a bloodborne virus.
  13. The correct answer is D (Defer elective care and refer the client for medical assessment first): This symptom pattern with a known exposure raises suspicion of active pulmonary tuberculosis, which requires airborne precautions. Airborne precautions need a fit tested N95 respirator and a negative pressure room, and a dental operatory cannot provide negative pressure, so elective care is deferred and the client is referred for medical assessment. A surgical mask and a face shield do not protect against airborne transmission, so proceeding with them exposes the team and later clients. Shortening the appointment or scheduling it last reduces neither the airborne route nor the risk to staff.
  14. The correct answer is B (She should perform hand hygiene after removing gloves and before donning again): Gloves never replace hand hygiene, because hands become contaminated during removal and through undetected glove defects. Hand hygiene is performed immediately after gloves come off and again before a fresh pair goes on, which covers the moments after client and environment contact and before the next task. Wearing the used gloves onward would transfer contamination to the drawer, the chart and the clean tray. Examination gloves are single use and are never disinfected or washed for reuse.
  15. The correct answer is C (She should cover all surfaces for twenty to thirty seconds and air dry): An alcohol based rub of sixty to ninety percent alcohol works only if enough product covers every surface of the hands, including the fingertips, thumbs, between the fingers and the wrists, for twenty to thirty seconds until dry. Fanning or wiping the hands shortens that contact time and defeats the process, so the hands are allowed to dry on their own. Eight seconds on the palms alone leaves most of the hand surface untreated. Soap and water is required for visibly soiled hands and for spore forming organisms, neither of which applies here.
  16. The correct answer is D (Use the recapping device or a one handed scoop technique on the tray): Most percutaneous injuries happen during recapping, so a mechanical recapping device or a one handed scoop technique is used and the second hand never goes near the needle. Two handed recapping puts a hand directly in the path of the needle and is the classic mechanism of a needlestick. Needles are never bent, broken or sheared, since that creates a jagged tip and can aerosolize blood. Discarding the needle is correct only when the syringe is finished with, and here another cartridge is still to be given.
  17. The correct answer is B (Close and replace the container, since it is past the fill line): A sharps container is closed and replaced at the fill line, because an overfilled container causes injuries when the next sharp is forced in or protrudes from the opening. A fresh puncture resistant, leak proof, labelled container is mounted at the point of use and the sealed one is stored for pickup. Pressing the contents down or decanting them means handling sharps directly, which is the highest risk act in the whole process. Leaving an uncapped needle on a tray creates a hazard for every person who passes through the operatory.
  18. The correct answer is C (Remove the glove and wash the site with soap and running water): The first action at the site is to stop, remove the glove and wash the wound with soap and running water, letting it bleed gently. Squeezing the site or applying caustic agents is avoided, because it damages tissue and may increase local uptake rather than reduce it. Reporting immediately, medical assessment ideally within one to two hours and the incident report all follow, but they come after the wound is washed. Mucous membrane or eye exposures are flushed with water or saline for about fifteen minutes instead.
  19. The correct answer is A (Within hours, and it is generally not offered beyond seventy two hours): HIV post exposure prophylaxis is most effective when started within hours of the exposure, and it is generally not offered beyond seventy two hours, which is why medical assessment is arranged urgently rather than the next working day. The assessment covers the exposure type, the worker's immune status and the source individual's hepatitis B, hepatitis C and HIV status, tested with consent. Waiting for the source result before being assessed can push the worker past the window in which prophylaxis is useful. Baseline serology on the worker establishes her pre-exposure status and is never a precondition for treatment.
  20. The correct answer is B (Hepatitis B immune globulin together with the hepatitis B vaccine series): A non-immune worker exposed to hepatitis B receives hepatitis B immune globulin for immediate passive protection plus the hepatitis B vaccine series to build lasting active immunity, both started as soon as possible. Immune globulin alone would leave her unprotected against any future exposure, so the vaccine series is begun at the same time. Hepatitis B is the one bloodborne pathogen in dentistry with a vaccine, which is why immunization and post-vaccination antibody testing are recommended before any exposure occurs. There is no antiviral prophylaxis for hepatitis C to model a regimen on.
  21. The correct answer is D (No prophylaxis exists, so management is early detection and treatment): There is no vaccine and no post exposure prophylaxis for hepatitis C, so management rests on baseline and follow-up serology over the months after the exposure and on early referral for treatment if infection is detected. Modern direct acting antiviral therapy cures most hepatitis C infections, which makes early detection genuinely valuable rather than merely observational. Immune globulin has no demonstrated benefit for hepatitis C exposure. Antiretroviral prophylaxis is directed at HIV and provides no protection against hepatitis C.
  22. The correct answer is A (Post-vaccination anti-HBs testing to confirm a protective antibody response was achieved): Post-vaccination testing for hepatitis B surface antibody confirms that the worker actually responded to the series, and it is done after the series is completed. A proportion of people are non-responders, and that must be known in advance so the correct management can be given quickly if an exposure ever happens. Finding out after an exposure is far too late to plan protection calmly. Routine boosters and annual antigen screening are not part of the recommended schedule for a documented responder, and tuberculin screening addresses a completely different organism.
  23. The correct answer is C (Barrier and high level disinfect the sensor, and sterilize the holder): Both items are semi critical because they contact mucous membranes without penetrating tissue. A semi critical item is heat sterilized whenever it tolerates heat, so the holder is sterilized, while the heat sensitive sensor is barriered during use and then cleaned and high level disinfected between clients. Steam sterilizing the sensor would destroy it, and the manufacturer's instructions govern which method may be used. A low level disinfectant is limited to housekeeping surfaces and falls far below what a mucosal contact device requires.
  24. The correct answer is C (Fully immerse the instruments, open the hinges and avoid stacking them): Ultrasonic cleaning works by cavitation, which acts only on surfaces that are in direct contact with the solution. Instruments are therefore fully immersed, hinges are opened so the joint surfaces are exposed, and items are not stacked or shielded from one another, which may mean running a second basket. Closed hinges hide exactly the surfaces where debris collects. Any part left above the solution is simply not cleaned, and overloading reduces cleaning for every item in the load.
  25. The correct answer is B (The sterilizer identifier, the load number and the date on each package): Each package is labelled with the sterilizer used, the load number and the date, which is what makes a recall back to the last negative spore test possible. Without a load number the clinic cannot tell which packages belong to a suspect cycle, so it would have to recall far more than necessary or fail to trace the load at all. A chemical indicator records exposure to cycle conditions and carries no identifying cycle data. Under event related shelf life a package is not given an expiry date, so a date alone does not identify the load.
  26. The correct answer is A (The package stays sterile unless the wrap is wet, torn or otherwise compromised): Event related shelf life is the usual Canadian approach: a package remains sterile until an event compromises the packaging, rather than expiring on a set calendar date. A wrap that is dry, intact and undamaged, stored clean, dry and closed, is still acceptable at fourteen months, and it is inspected at the point of use. Time based expiry dating is a different system and does not apply in a clinic that has adopted event related storage. An internal indicator that cannot be seen from outside is the normal reason an external indicator is added, and it is read when the package is opened.
  27. The correct answer is D (Dry heat at 170 degrees C for sixty minutes, timed from reaching temperature): Dry heat suits carbon steel instruments precisely because there is no moisture to rust or dull them, and the standard parameters are about 160 degrees C for 120 minutes or 170 degrees C for 60 minutes. Timing begins only once the chamber has reached the set temperature, since the heat up period does not count toward the cycle. Any steam cycle exposes the pliers to the moisture the manufacturer says they cannot tolerate. Pliers used in the mouth are not adequately reprocessed by disinfection when a heat method is available.
  28.
