The correct answer is B (A Class 5 indicator cannot replace a biological indicator run at least weekly): A Class 5 integrating indicator responds to all critical variables and is correlated to biological indicator performance, which makes it a strong in-package chemical indicator, but it remains a chemical indicator. Only a biological indicator challenges the cycle with live, highly resistant spores, so only it demonstrates that sterilization was actually achieved. Weekly testing is the absolute minimum anywhere, several Canadian provinces including Alberta and Ontario require a biological indicator every day the sterilizer is used, and a biological indicator is run with every load containing an implantable device, so a monthly interval fails on every reading of the standard. The three forms of monitoring are used together and prove different things, so none of them substitutes for another, and mechanical monitoring is still checked and recorded every cycle.
  29. The correct answer is C (The package is not usable, because conditions inside it are not confirmed): Mechanical monitoring records what the machine registered at its own sensor, not what happened inside an individual package, so a normal printout cannot vouch for a package whose internal indicator did not respond. An unchanged internal indicator points to a real problem such as overloading, a wet or overly thick wrap, or a package that steam could not penetrate, so the package is not used and is reprocessed. The external tape only shows the outside of the package was exposed and adds nothing about the interior. Assuming indicator failure rather than cycle failure reverses the safe interpretation.
  30. The correct answer is D (A rigid, closed, puncture resistant, labelled container handled with utility gloves): Contaminated instruments are transported in a rigid, closed, puncture resistant, leak proof and labelled container, handled with heavy utility gloves rather than examination gloves. That combination protects the carrier from a puncture, prevents spillage in a shared corridor and makes the contents obvious to anyone who handles the container. An open tray leaves sharps exposed and can spill, and examination gloves offer no puncture protection. A cloth wrap is neither leak proof nor puncture resistant, and rinsing at the chairside creates spatter in an area that is not designed for reprocessing.
Case C027

A dental hygienist in an Ontario general practice completes periodontal debridement for a 52-year-old client with generalized periodontitis and type 2 diabetes. Two days later the hygienist realizes the appointment entry omitted the anesthetic details and the client's report of postoperative sensitivity. The practice uses an electronic record with unique logins and an audit trail. The office manager suggests opening the original note and typing the missing information into it.

  1. The correct answer is B (Create a late entry dated today that names the appointment date): information discovered after an entry has been completed is added as a clearly labelled late entry that carries the date it was written and identifies the date of the event it describes. That keeps the chronology of the chart honest and shows any later reviewer exactly when the hygienist knew what. Typing the missing details into the original note makes the record look contemporaneous when it is not, which is an alteration and damages the credibility of the whole chart. Documentation is completed by the clinician who provided the care, not by an administrator.
  2. The correct answer is D (The agent, concentration, dose given, lot number and client response): local anesthetic is recorded with the agent and concentration, the amount actually administered, the lot number so a product recall can be traced, and how the client responded. A partial entry naming only a trade name, a cartridge count or a site leaves the record unable to answer the two questions that matter later, which are how much drug the client received and which product it came from. The lot number is the element most often omitted and is the one that makes a recall traceable. Any adverse response is recorded in the same entry.
  3. The correct answer is A (An audit trail records who viewed or changed each entry and when): the audit trail is the technical safeguard that makes an electronic record defensible, because it logs every view and every change against a unique user and a time stamp. This is precisely why a saved entry cannot simply be overwritten without leaving evidence, and it is what allows an investigator to see whether a note was written at the time of care or added afterwards. Encryption protects information in transit and at rest but does not track who changed what. Screen locking and offsite backup are useful safeguards that serve confidentiality and continuity rather than accountability for changes.
  4. The correct answer is C (Care that was not documented is treated as care not provided): this is the working rule applied in complaints, college investigations and civil claims, and it is why the chart is the practitioner's main defence. A sincere memory of an appointment eighteen months earlier carries very little weight against a contemporaneous record that is silent. Evidence of a usual routine may be offered but it does not establish what happened at one particular visit for one particular client. Professional standing does not displace the record, and there is no rule that ages a complaint out of chart based assessment.
  5. The correct answer is C (Retain it, because a record tied to an open complaint is never destroyed): a chart connected to a complaint, an investigation or a claim is preserved until the matter is fully resolved, regardless of the routine retention schedule. Destroying it, even in good faith as part of a batch purge, looks like spoliation and would be treated far more seriously than the original allegation. A printed summary is not the record, because the complete entries, the audit trail and the radiographs are all part of it. Giving the only copy to the client leaves the practice unable to answer the complaint at all.
Case C028

A dental hygienist owns an independent dental hygiene practice in Alberta. A long standing client needs four units of scaling delivered over two appointments. The client's plan reimburses only two units per benefit year, and the benefit year rolls over in three weeks. The client asks the hygienist to record the second appointment on a date after the rollover, and also asks whether a different, better paying code could be submitted for that visit.

  1. The correct answer is A (Refuse, and explain that a false treatment date is insurance fraud): reporting a treatment date that differs from the date the service was actually provided misrepresents the claim to the insurer, and that is fraud and professional misconduct even when the treatment is genuinely delivered and the fee is unchanged. The absence of obvious harm does not change the character of the act, because the insurer's benefit year limit is exactly what the false date is designed to defeat. A client cannot consent on the practitioner's behalf to a false statement made to a third party, so a signed note transfers nothing. Omitting the date entirely is still a deliberately misleading submission.
  2. The correct answer is B (Use the code that accurately describes the service actually provided): claims carry the code matching the procedure that was performed, and choosing a better paying code for a service that was not provided is fraud. That another office did it routinely is not a defence and may simply mean that office has not yet been audited. Refunding the difference to the client does not cure the misrepresentation made to the insurer, and it adds a second problem by disguising the real fee. Submitting alternative codes and inviting the insurer to choose passes a decision that belongs to the practitioner onto a third party who cannot see what was done.
  3. The correct answer is D (Submit a predetermination and review the insurer's response with the client): a predetermination sends the proposed treatment and codes to the insurer before care begins so the client learns the coverage position in advance, which is the standard step for treatment spanning several units or visits. It also gives the hygienist a written basis for the conversation that the contract is between the client and the insurer. Billing a first visit to see what happens gives a partial answer only after the client has already incurred a charge. Asking the client to telephone during the appointment wastes chair time, and dropping the fee to the plan maximum simply hides the cost question rather than answering it.
  4. The correct answer is A (The guide suggests fees, and an independent practitioner sets and discloses her own): provincial fee guides are suggested fee schedules, not legally binding maximums, and an independent dental hygienist sets her own fees and discloses them to the client in advance of treatment. Explaining that openly is part of informed financial consent and prevents a dispute after the work is done. The guide is published by a professional association rather than by an insurer, so it does not govern claim adjudication. It also applies to the profession generally rather than only to practices owned by a dentist.
  5. The correct answer is D (It misrepresents the actual fee charged and is professional misconduct): billing the insurer a full fee while quietly waiving the client's co-payment means the fee actually charged is lower than the fee reported, so the claim overstates the true cost of care. The insurer's share is calculated as a proportion of the real fee, so its share is not in fact unchanged. Documenting the waiver in the chart records the misrepresentation rather than curing it. Genuine financial hardship can be addressed honestly, by reducing the fee and billing the reduced amount, or by arranging a payment plan.
Case C029

A dental hygienist begins work at a four operatory clinic. At each chairside she finds a spray bottle of surface disinfectant that was decanted from a large supplier container and marked only with the handwritten word Disinfectant. The safety data sheet binder is kept in the owner's office, which is locked on the evenings the hygienist works. After several full days of hand instrumentation she also reports wrist and neck pain.

  1. The correct answer is C (Product identifier, safe handling information and reference to the safety data sheet): under WHMIS 2015 a workplace label is used when a hazardous product is decanted into a secondary container, and it carries these three elements. It is deliberately simpler than a supplier label because the full hazard detail stays available on the safety data sheet the label points to. Supplier identifiers, signal words and full hazard statements belong on the supplier label that arrives on the original container from the manufacturer. A handwritten word such as Disinfectant identifies nothing specific and gives a worker no route to the hazard information.
  2. The correct answer is B (Sheets must be current and accessible to every worker on every shift): the employer duty under WHMIS 2015 is continuous access, so a binder locked away during the evenings an employee actually works does not meet the requirement. The point of the sheet is immediate use during a spill, a splash or an exposure, when nobody has time to telephone a manager for a key. Sheets are required for every hazardous product in the workplace, not only those bearing the Danger signal word. A supplier label carries a summary of hazards but does not replace the sixteen section document.
  3. The correct answer is A (The right to participate through a health and safety representative or committee): the internal responsibility system gives workers three rights, and the right to participate is the one that supports raising a hazard formally through a representative or a joint health and safety committee rather than accepting that it is management's business alone. Participation is exactly what the owner's comment is denying. The right to refuse unsafe work is reserved for work the worker believes is dangerous and is not the vehicle for correcting a labelling and information gap. The right to know entitles a worker to hazard information and does not require deference to management, and workers compensation is a separate scheme that follows an injury.
  4. The correct answer is B (Sharp instruments with larger diameter handles, alternating with ultrasonic instrumentation): dull instruments demand greater lateral pressure and a tighter grasp, so keeping edges sharp lowers the force the hand must generate on every stroke. Larger diameter, lightweight handles reduce pinch force further, and alternating powered and hand instrumentation varies the loading pattern instead of repeating one stress all day. Narrow handles used with a firmer grasp increase exactly the pinch force she is trying to reduce. Standing over an upright client and working longer without breaks both add static load rather than removing it.
  5. The correct answer is D (Subjective: client reports looseness and bleeding. Objective: depths, bleeding, mobility): subjective findings are what the client reports, and objective findings are what the clinician measures or observes. The sensation of loose teeth and the report of bleeding while brushing come from the client, while the 5 mm probing depths, bleeding on probing and grade 1 mobility on teeth 31 and 41 are measured at the chair. Reversing the two categories misrepresents where the information came from and weakens the reasoning that follows in the assessment. Collapsing everything into one category removes the distinction that supports the dental hygiene diagnosis.
  6. The correct answer is C (What was recommended, the risks explained, and the client's decision to decline): informed refusal is documented to the same standard as informed consent, naming the care that was recommended, the risks and consequences of declining that were explained, and the client's decision. A bare note that the client refused proves nothing about whether the client was actually informed, which is the point a complaint would turn on. A waiver signature does not release a practitioner from professional accountability and is no substitute for the record of the discussion. Referral may be appropriate but does not replace documenting the refusal itself.
  7. The correct answer is A (Draw a single line through it, write the correction, date and initial): a paper record is corrected so that the original entry remains legible, the correction sits beside it, and the date and initials show who changed it and when. Nothing is removed, because the ability to read what was originally written is what keeps the chart credible. Correction fluid and erasure both destroy the original and look like concealment even when the intent was innocent. Waiting until the next appointment leaves an inaccurate probing depth standing in the record in the meantime.
  8. The correct answer is A (The period generally begins when the client reaches the age of majority): retention periods are set provincially and are commonly ten years from the last entry for an adult, but for a client treated as a minor the clock generally starts when that client reaches the age of majority. This protects a young person who may only be able to bring a claim once they are an adult. Starting the period at the first appointment or at the point a parent leaves the practice would allow the record to be destroyed while the client still has recourse. No Canadian jurisdiction leaves the charts of minors without any retention requirement.
  9. The correct answer is B (Contain the breach by retrieving the information or closing the access point): breach response follows a set order, and containment comes first because every hour the information stays exposed increases the harm. Here containment means recalling or having the message deleted, confirming it was not forwarded, and correcting the address list. Only after containment does the practice evaluate the risk and then notify the privacy officer, the affected individuals, and the commissioner and college where legislation requires it. Root cause review and policy change belong to the prevention step at the end, once the exposure has been stopped.
  10. The correct answer is D (Decline, and ask the owner to issue the assistant a unique login): electronic records require unique credentials for every user, because the audit trail is only meaningful if each entry can be tied to the person who actually made it. Lending a login means the hygienist's name is attached to notes she did not write, which she would have to answer for later. Sharing a password, even briefly, breaks the same safeguard and is a reportable privacy failure in most practices. Typing the assistant's observations under her own login is no better, since it still attributes one person's work to another.
  11. The correct answer is C (Three months, as periodontal maintenance matched to the client's risk profile): recall intervals are set by risk rather than by habit, and this client stacks treated periodontitis, poorly controlled diabetes, current smoking, inconsistent plaque control and residual bleeding on probing. That combination supports three month periodontal maintenance, with the reasoning recorded in the chart so the interval can be defended and later revisited. Nine or twelve month intervals suit a low risk client with stable tissues and good self care, which does not describe this client. A fixed six month interval applied because it is standard ignores the risk assessment entirely.
  12. The correct answer is B (A quadrant series of appointments with local anesthetic at each visit): heavy generalized subgingival calculus with 5 to 7 mm depths and marked sensitivity calls for staged quadrant debridement under local anesthetic, which allows thorough instrumentation within the client's tolerance. Appointment number and length follow the extent of deposit, the periodontal diagnosis, client tolerance and the need for anesthesia, and all four point the same way here. Attempting the full mouth in one visit without anesthetic would leave deposit behind and would exceed what this client can tolerate. Polishing or supragingival scaling alone does not address subgingival deposit and leaves active disease untreated.
  13. The correct answer is D (Build turnover time into the schedule so that reprocessing keeps pace): scheduling is part of safe practice, and a schedule with no turnover forces staff to shorten or skip reprocessing steps to keep up. Adding realistic time between clients lets cleaning, drying, packaging and sterilization run to completion without pressure. Extra equipment or extra cassettes may help capacity but neither creates the time the cycle actually needs, and instruments would still be rushed at the same points. Shortening the drying stage is unsafe, because a wet package is a contaminated package once it is handled.
  14. The correct answer is A (Decline to confirm any information without the client's express consent): personal health information is disclosed only with the client's consent or where the law requires it, and paying premiums gives an employer no right of access. Confirming procedure codes discloses clinical information just as surely as describing the treatment in words. Even confirming that the appointment took place reveals that the person is a client of the practice, which is itself personal health information. The correct handling is to say that the practice cannot confirm or deny anything and to invite the caller to speak with the client directly.
  15. The correct answer is C (Each exposure needs a recorded clinical justification, so a fixed schedule fails): radiographs are prescribed for an individual client after assessing need, and the justification is recorded in the chart. A blanket policy that images every client on a calendar interval regardless of caries risk or previous findings cannot be defended, because it exposes low risk clients without any clinical reason. Bitewings remain appropriate imaging when they are justified, so replacing them with a panoramic image would raise dose without answering the caries question. A thyroid collar and other ALARA measures reduce dose but never supply the missing justification for taking the image at all.
  16. The correct answer is D (The owner or employer, who holds these duties as a legal obligation): radiation protection at the facility level is an employer duty under provincial radiation legislation and Health Canada's Safety Code 30, covering registration of equipment, inspection and maintenance, appointment of a responsible user, the written radiation protection plan, shielding, dosimeters where required and operator qualification. The hygienist holds clinical duties such as ALARA technique, justification and receptor holding devices, but she does not carry the facility obligations. Health Canada publishes the safety code rather than inspecting individual offices on a set cycle. The college regulates the individual registrant and does not assume the owner's equipment obligations.
  17. The correct answer is B (Decline, and use a receptor holding device or an alternative technique): no staff member ever holds a receptor or the tube head during an exposure, because it places a worker in the primary beam repeatedly across a career. A lead apron does not make that acceptable and does not shield the hands that are in the beam. Asking a parent or accompanying adult is a last resort considered only for a client who genuinely cannot hold the receptor themselves, and it is not a way around a staff restriction for an adult client. Reducing the exposure setting lowers image quality without removing the person from the beam, so the correct route is a holder, a repositioned receptor, or an extraoral alternative.
  18. The correct answer is C (Record the facts in the chart and complete an internal incident report): a clinical event affecting the client is documented factually in the client's own record, including what separated, that it was recovered intact and that the client was unharmed. The internal incident report is a separate risk management document used to analyze the root cause and prevent recurrence, and it does not replace the chart entry. Omitting the chart entry leaves a gap that would be very difficult to explain if the client later reported a problem. Recording nothing because there was no harm is the least defensible option of all.
  19. The correct answer is A (Participation in the program is a condition of holding her registration): every provincial college runs a quality assurance program combining self-assessment, a learning plan, continuing education and practice or peer assessment, and taking part is a requirement of registration rather than a professional courtesy. Selection is usually random or cyclical and carries no implication of wrongdoing. Continuing education hours are one component of the program, not an alternative to being assessed. Assessment is not triggered by complaints, which run through a separate complaints and discipline process, and it applies to registrants in every practice setting.
  20. The correct answer is B (Replace the expired drugs and the pads, and refill the oxygen cylinder): emergency readiness means every item is usable at the moment it is needed, so expired drugs are replaced, pads approaching their date are changed before they lapse, and a cylinder at one quarter is refilled while there is time. A partially full cylinder can empty during a prolonged emergency, which is when the supply matters most. Fixing only the drugs leaves two known gaps in place. Recording the findings without acting defers the risk to the next client, and withdrawing the entire kit pending an outside audit leaves the office with no emergency capability at all.
  21. The correct answer is D (Product identifier, supplier identifier, pictogram, signal word, hazard and precautionary statements): a WHMIS 2015 supplier label carries these six elements, with the signal word being either Danger or Warning depending on the severity of the hazard. Together they identify the product and its source, show the hazard class at a glance and tell the worker how to handle, store and respond to the product. Expiry dates, lot numbers and storage temperatures may appear on packaging for other reasons but they are not required label elements. First aid measures belong to the safety data sheet rather than to the required label content.
  22. The correct answer is A (A standard sixteen section document, kept current and available to workers): WHMIS 2015 safety data sheets follow a fixed sixteen section format aligned with the Globally Harmonized System, and the employer keeps them current and accessible to every worker. The standard layout is what lets a worker find first aid measures in the same place on every sheet during an emergency. A supplier summary of any length is not a safety data sheet and cannot be substituted for one. Sheets are required for every hazardous product regardless of its signal word, and a marketing brochure summarized by the employer meets none of the requirements.
  23. The correct answer is C (Position the client supine, rotate the client's head, and use indirect vision): neutral neck posture is achieved by moving the client rather than the clinician, so the chair goes supine, the client's head turns toward or away from the operator, and the mirror does the rest. That removes the forward lean and downward head tilt that are producing the pain. Raising the stool changes shoulder position without addressing where the working field sits. Leaning in closer and steadying the head by hand increases the very posture under review, and shortening appointments reduces exposure time without correcting the technique that causes the strain.
  24. The correct answer is C (Choose loupes with a declination angle allowing a neutral head position): the benefit of loupes for musculoskeletal health depends on the declination angle steering the line of sight downward through the optics so the head stays near neutral. A shallow angle that forces the head forward converts a protective device into a source of sustained neck flexion held for the whole appointment. A headrest supports a seated client and does nothing for the operator's own tilted posture. Loupes are worth using when correctly specified, so avoiding them entirely gives up the visual acuity and working distance benefits for no reason.
  25. The correct answer is B (Sterilizer monitoring logs, waterline test results and chart audit summaries): office level quality assurance is the documented evidence that the practice checks its own systems, and these records show mechanical, chemical and biological monitoring, water quality testing and review of documentation quality over time. Radiography quality control and equipment maintenance schedules sit in the same group. Continuing education certificates and registration renewals belong to individual registrant quality assurance rather than to the office systems. Satisfaction surveys, claim approvals, purchase orders and warranties describe the business rather than the safety and documentation systems the assessor is asking about.
  26. The correct answer is A (Document the call, the advice given and the follow-up in the chart): telephone advice is professional care and belongs in the record, including who called, what was reported, what was advised and what follow-up was arranged. If the client later deteriorates, that entry is the only evidence that appropriate advice was given and a review was offered. The place the hygienist happened to be standing when the phone rang has no bearing on the duty to record it. Passing the information on verbally and waiting to see whether the client attends both leave the advice undocumented at the moment it matters most.
  27. The correct answer is D (Cancellations and no-shows are recorded, since they document the client's care): the record includes cancellations, no-shows, telephone advice and referrals, because a pattern of missed appointments in a client with untreated periodontitis is clinically meaningful and shows that care was offered and not received. Deleting those entries removes the evidence that the practice tried to treat the disease. Keeping them only in a financial ledger separates the information from the clinical picture it belongs to. Recording a missed visit only when a fee was charged makes documentation depend on billing rather than on care.
  28. The correct answer is B (Store backups securely offsite and periodically test a full restore procedure): a backup sitting beside the server is lost with the server in a fire, flood or theft, and a backup that has never been restored is only an assumption that data is recoverable. Secure offsite storage plus a tested restore turns both assumptions into verified safeguards. Backing up more often to the same drive in the same room multiplies copies of the same single point of failure. Encrypting the server protects confidentiality but not availability, and printed chart summaries are neither complete nor a practical way to rebuild an electronic record system.
  29. The correct answer is C (Billing for services not rendered is fraud and must not continue): submitting units of scaling that were never performed misrepresents the claim regardless of how the employer characterizes the time, and it is fraud and professional misconduct. Client education is valuable and may be billable under its own appropriate code, but it is not billed as scaling. The hygienist cannot rely on the employer being the one who presses the submit button, because the claims are made in her name and against her services, so she has a duty to raise it and to refuse to let it continue. That the client pays no more does not cure a false statement made to the insurer.
  30. The correct answer is D (Assign and rehearse defined emergency roles, keeping the kit accessible): emergency preparedness is built before the emergency, with named roles for calling emergency services, bringing oxygen and the kit, and managing the client, rehearsed so the team acts without discussion. The failures here were duplicated effort, nobody calling for help and equipment that could not be reached, and all three are fixed by assigned roles plus an unlocked, known location for the kit. A second kit does not help if nobody knows who is meant to fetch it. Repeat certification and a posted protocol are useful supports but neither one allocates the tasks that went undone.
Case C030

Mira is 26 months old and attends a community health clinic in Manitoba with her mother. She is still given a bottle of apple juice at bedtime and falls asleep with it. Her mother reports she snacks on crackers through the day. Chalky white opaque bands are visible along the cervical margins of teeth 51, 52, 61 and 62, while the mandibular primary incisors look sound. The family drinks non fluoridated well water.

  1. The correct answer is B (Subsurface demineralization with the surface layer still intact and not cavitated): A white spot lesion is the first clinical sign of caries, where mineral has been lost beneath a relatively intact surface layer as acid diffused in and calcium and phosphate diffused out. Because the surface is not yet broken, the lesion can still remineralize once fluoride is supplied and the frequency of sugar exposure falls. Erosion is chemical dissolution of non bacterial origin and produces smooth shiny cupped surfaces in a generalized pattern, not localized opaque bands at a plaque retentive cervical site. Nothing in the findings points to cavitation or to a defect formed before eruption.
  2. The correct answer is D (The tongue and sublingual salivary ducts shield the mandibular incisors during feeding): During bottle feeding the tongue lies over the lower incisors and the openings of the submandibular and sublingual ducts flood that area with saliva, so those teeth are both physically covered and continuously cleared. The maxillary incisors sit outside that protection and hold the sweetened liquid against the cervical enamel, which is why they show the first lesions. The mandibular and maxillary primary incisors erupt within a few months of one another, so timing of eruption does not explain the pattern. Posture does not redirect a pooled liquid away from the upper teeth.
  3. The correct answer is A (A rice grain sized smear of fluoridated paste, brushed by an adult): From birth to three years the recommended amount is a rice grain sized smear, applied and brushed by an adult, and it is used once the child has been assessed as at risk, which Mira clearly is. The small quantity keeps the swallowed dose low while still delivering fluoride to the tooth surface where it acts. A pea sized amount belongs to the three to six year age group with supervision and spitting, so it is too much for a child of 26 months. Withholding fluoride entirely in an active early childhood caries case removes the single most useful remineralizing measure available at home.
  4. The correct answer is C (22 600 parts per million fluoride, far higher than any toothpaste sold in Canada): Five percent sodium fluoride varnish delivers 22 600 parts per million fluoride, which is far above any dentifrice available in Canada. The varnish sets on contact with saliva and only a small quantity is used, so the swallowed dose stays low despite that concentration, and this is why it is the agent of choice for very young children. Family toothpaste in Canada runs about 1000 to 1500 parts per million, roughly fifteen times lower, and even prescription paste at 5000 parts per million is about four times lower. Optimally fluoridated drinking water is set at 0.7 mg/L, which is a water supply figure and not a professionally applied product.
  5. The correct answer is D (Soft food and no hot drinks today, with no brushing until tomorrow morning): Varnish works by staying in contact with the tooth surface and releasing fluoride slowly, so the instructions protect that film: soft food for the rest of the day, no hot drinks and no alcohol containing rinses, and no brushing or flossing until the following morning, or at least four to six hours. Rinsing or brushing straight away strips the varnish off and wastes the application. Withholding all food and fluid for six hours is unnecessary and unsafe for a toddler, since eating soft food is permitted. Hot liquids soften and dislodge the varnish, so hot soup works directly against the purpose of the treatment.
Case C031

Mr Dubois is 78 and lives in an assisted living residence in New Brunswick. He takes an antidepressant, a diuretic and an antihistamine daily. He reports his mouth feels dry, especially at night, and he sucks hard candies for relief. Examination shows generalized recession of three to four millimetres, soft leathery lesions on the exposed root surfaces of teeth 34, 35 and 44, and visibly reduced saliva. Arthritis limits his grip.

  1. The correct answer is A (Root surface about 6.2 to 6.7, and enamel about 5.5): The critical pH for root surface dentin and cementum is approximately 6.2 to 6.7, while for enamel it is approximately 5.5. A higher number means less acidity is needed to start mineral loss, so an acid challenge that an intact crown tolerates safely will already be dissolving exposed root surface. That is exactly why Mr Dubois has become vulnerable only now that recession has uncovered cementum and dentin. Reversing the two values, or treating them as one shared threshold, removes the whole basis for his raised risk.
  2. The correct answer is B (Review his medication list with the prescriber and screen for xerostomia causes): His antidepressant, diuretic and antihistamine are all drug groups strongly associated with reduced salivary flow, so the cause is most likely polypharmacy rather than age itself, and a salivary assessment with a medication review through the prescriber is the correct first step. Saliva provides clearance, buffering, a calcium and phosphate reservoir and antimicrobial proteins, so restoring or substituting it addresses the actual driver of his caries. An alcohol containing rinse dries the mucosa further and worsens the problem it is meant to treat. Dismissing dry mouth as inevitable leaves a modifiable risk factor untouched while his lesions progress.
  3. The correct answer is B (Xylitol sweetened gum or lozenges plus water and a saliva substitute): Xylitol is a sugar alcohol that plaque bacteria do not ferment to acid, so gum or lozenges stimulate saliva mechanically and by taste without producing an acid drop, and pairing them with water and a saliva substitute treats the dryness directly. This keeps the behaviour he values while removing the fermentable carbohydrate that his current candies deliver every hour. Sports drinks and diluted juice both supply fermentable carbohydrate along with a low pH, and sipping them spreads the exposure across the whole day, which is the worst possible pattern. Honey, molasses and concentrated fruit juice behave in plaque exactly as refined sucrose does.
  4. The correct answer is C (Prescription 5000 ppm paste twice daily plus varnish two to four times yearly): Prescription 5000 parts per million paste is intended for exactly this profile, a high risk adult with root caries and xerostomia, and pairing it with varnish two to four times a year gives both the frequent low level topical exposure and the periodic professional application that the evidence supports. An adapted or powered brush handle addresses his grip so that the paste is actually delivered. Family strength paste with one varnish a year is the regimen for a low risk client and is too weak for active lesions. Supplements deliver fluoride systemically when the benefit is largely topical, and a rinse alone replaces neither the mechanical disruption nor the higher concentration he needs.
  5. The correct answer is D (Arrested dentin in the treated lesion turns permanently black after application): The silver component is antibacterial and the fluoride remineralizes the lesion surface, but the arrested dentin that results is permanently black, and consent must cover that appearance change before the solution touches the tooth. Sound enamel is not stained, so the darkening is confined to the treated carious tissue rather than spreading to the rest of the mouth. Silver diamine fluoride arrests a lesion and reduces dentinal hypersensitivity; it does not restore form or function, and the tooth still needs review and possible restoration later. Telling the family the tooth will look better misrepresents the single most predictable effect of the material.
Case C032

Kayla is 15 and has worn fixed orthodontic appliances for eight months. She plays competitive volleyball, sips a sports drink through every practice and finishes a large energy drink late while studying most evenings. She brushes once daily, usually in the morning. White demineralized patches surround the brackets on teeth 13, 12, 11, 21, 22 and 23. Her salivary flow is normal and her community water is fluoridated at 0.7 mg/L.

  1. The correct answer is A (Repeated exposures create overlapping acid drops that hold plaque below critical pH): Each carbohydrate exposure produces its own Stephan curve, with plaque pH falling within two to five minutes and taking twenty to sixty minutes to recover, so exposures spaced minutes apart overlap and the pH never climbs back above the critical value. Frequency of exposure therefore matters more than the total quantity consumed, which is why sipping through a practice session does more damage than one can with a meal. The concentration of sugar in a drink does not change according to how it is swallowed. Saliva buffers a single bolus efficiently once the exposure ends, which is precisely what continuous sipping prevents.
  2. The correct answer is C (pH falls sharply within two to five minutes, recovering over twenty to sixty minutes): Resting plaque pH sits near 6.5 to 7.0, and after a carbohydrate exposure bacterial acid production drives it sharply down within two to five minutes, below the critical pH for enamel, before salivary buffering brings it back over roughly twenty to sixty minutes. The area under the critical pH line during that recovery is the demineralization window, which is what Kayla needs to see. Plaque pH does not rise after a sugar exposure, so a curve that begins by climbing describes the opposite of what happens. Reversing the fall and the recovery times misstates why a bedtime exposure with reduced salivary flow is so damaging.
  3. The correct answer is C (The late night drink, because salivary flow and clearance fall sharply during sleep): A carbohydrate exposure taken close to bedtime is the most damaging of the day, because salivary flow falls close to zero during sleep and there is almost no clearance or buffering to bring plaque pH back up. The acid produced therefore sits against the demineralized enamel around her brackets for hours. Exercise does not alter the sugar concentration of a drink, so the practice exposure is a real risk but a lesser one than the nocturnal exposure. Water fluoridated at 0.7 mg/L reduces caries risk but does not neutralize an acid challenge, and treating the two exposures as equivalent ignores the timing that makes one of them worse.
  4. The correct answer is B (Powered brush twice daily, interdental brushes around brackets, and a daily fluoride rinse): A powered brush suits a client with fixed appliances and gives a small but real advantage in biofilm removal, interdental brushes reach around brackets and under the archwire far faster than threaded floss, and a daily fluoride rinse adds the frequent low concentration exposure that her white spot lesions need. The plan is also short enough that she is likely to do it. Chlorhexidine is a short course agent, usually about two weeks and rarely beyond two months, and long term use causes brown staining she would not accept. A firm brush with abrasive paste invites cervical abrasion, and an irrigator flushes rather than disrupts adherent biofilm, so it can never replace brushing.
Case C033

A father brings his two children to a public health clinic in rural Saskatchewan. Jayden is 4 years old and Ella is 4 months old with no erupted teeth. The family well water tests at 0.1 mg/L fluoride, and the father reconstitutes Ella's infant formula with that water. He has read online both that his town should fluoridate and that fluoride causes staining, and he asks the dental hygienist to explain both claims.

  1. The correct answer is D (Fluoride acts mainly topically after eruption, inhibiting demineralization and driving remineralization): Fluoride present in the fluid around an erupted tooth inhibits mineral loss during an acid challenge and accelerates the return of mineral afterwards, forming fluorapatite, which dissolves at a lower pH than hydroxyapatite. That topical action after eruption is the dominant mechanism, which is why a low concentration available frequently outperforms a large infrequent swallowed dose. Pre eruptive incorporation contributes only a small part of the effect and is the reason supplements have fallen out of favour. Inhibition of bacterial enolase does occur, but only at higher concentrations and as a third mechanism rather than the whole explanation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Optimal 0.7 mg/L, with a maximum acceptable concentration of 1.5 mg/L): Health Canada sets the optimal concentration for communities that choose to fluoridate at 0.7 mg/L, and the maximum acceptable concentration for drinking water safety is a separate figure of 1.5 mg/L. The safety limit is a ceiling that must not be exceeded, not a target to aim toward, and confusing the two is the most common misunderstanding in this conversation. Community water fluoridation is the most equitable preventive measure available because it reaches every resident regardless of income or dental attendance. The family well at 0.1 mg/L sits far below the optimal level, so these children get almost no benefit from their water.
  3. The correct answer is D (Supplements are not given below six months of age, and are rarely recommended at all): Fluoride supplements are never given below six months of age, and Ella is 4 months old, so the question of a dose does not arise for her yet. Even beyond that age supplements are rarely recommended now, because they deliver fluoride systemically when the benefit is largely topical, they carry a real fluorosis risk, and long term compliance is poor. Where they are considered at all it is only for a high risk child without a fluoridated supply, and only after every other fluoride source has been audited. Treating drops as the automatic first response to a low fluoride well reverses the current order of preference.
  4. The correct answer is B (Risk runs from birth to about six to eight years while permanent crowns mineralize): Dental fluorosis arises from excess fluoride intake while enamel is still forming, so the window runs from birth to roughly six to eight years while the permanent crowns mineralize, with the anterior teeth most at risk in about the first three years of life. Both children are inside that window, so the answer covers both of them. Once a tooth has erupted it can no longer become fluorotic, which rules out any lifelong risk. In this family the well water at 0.1 mg/L contributes very little, so the sources worth auditing are swallowed toothpaste and any supplement rather than the formula water itself.
  5. The correct answer is C (The acquired pellicle, an acellular film of salivary glycoproteins and enzymes): Within seconds of a surface being cleaned, salivary glycoproteins, phosphoproteins and enzymes adsorb onto the enamel to form the acquired pellicle. It is acellular and bacteria free when it forms, it cannot be rinsed away, and it supplies the receptors that bacteria later bind to. Bacterial colonization follows the pellicle rather than preceding it, and the gram negative anaerobes appear only as the biofilm matures over days. Dental biofilm is an organized microbial community in a polymer matrix, not food debris and not a calcified deposit, which is why mechanical disruption rather than rinsing is what removes it.
  6. The correct answer is A (Mainly gram positive cocci and rods such as Streptococcus sanguinis and Actinomyces): Early colonization within hours is dominated by gram positive cocci and rods, with Streptococcus sanguinis and Actinomyces species the usual examples, because these organisms bind directly to receptors in the pellicle. Filaments and gram negative anaerobes join between one and three days by coaggregation, attaching to organisms already in place rather than to the tooth itself. Motile gram negative species characterize a mature biofilm at seven to fourteen days, not the first hours. Lactobacillus adheres poorly to smooth surfaces and colonizes cavitated lesions and deep fissures, so it drives progression rather than early attachment.
  7. The correct answer is B (It shifts toward gram negative anaerobic and motile species with visible gingival inflammation): By seven to fourteen days undisturbed biofilm is mature and dominated by gram negative anaerobic and motile species, and clinically the gingiva shows redness, edema and bleeding. That is the classic experimental gingivitis finding, and the inflammation resolves once biofilm is removed and returns when it reaccumulates. The microbial shift moves away from the early gram positive cocci rather than back toward them. Biofilm does not become impossible to disrupt at twelve days, and the host response alone does not clear an established attached community, which is why mechanical disruption remains non negotiable.
  8. The correct answer is D (Organisms inside an organized biofilm matrix resist antimicrobials that reach them briefly): Bacteria living inside an organized biofilm matrix are far more resistant to antimicrobials than the same species free in saliva, and a rinse held for thirty seconds cannot penetrate an established mass. Mechanical disruption breaks the structure apart and resets the community toward its early health associated state, which is why every chemotherapeutic agent is an adjunct and never a substitute. Chlorhexidine in particular is substantive and binds to oral surfaces for eight to twelve hours, so the problem is penetration rather than immediate inactivation. Staining is a genuine side effect but it is not the reason a rinse cannot replace interdental cleaning.
  9. The correct answer is D (Streptococcus mutans initiates lesions and Lactobacillus progresses them in cavities and fissures): Streptococcus mutans is most strongly associated with lesion initiation because it adheres well to pellicle, builds extracellular polysaccharide from sucrose, and is both acidogenic and acid tolerant. Lactobacillus adheres poorly to smooth surfaces and is found in cavitated lesions and deep fissures, so it drives progression once a lesion exists rather than starting one. Reversing those roles misreads the adhesion properties of each organism. Actinomyces species are linked more with root surface lesions, and Streptococcus mutans is certainly not restricted to root surfaces.
  10. The correct answer is C (Sucrose is the substrate for extracellular polysaccharide that helps bacteria adhere): Streptococcus mutans uses sucrose to build extracellular polysaccharide, which anchors the organism to the pellicle and thickens the matrix that holds acid against the enamel surface. That structural contribution is what sets sucrose apart, because acid production alone occurs with many fermentable carbohydrates. Glucose, fructose, maltose, dextrose, honey, molasses and concentrated fruit juice are all fermented in plaque, so sucrose is not uniquely metabolized. The critical pH of enamel is a property of the mineral at about 5.5 and no dietary sugar shifts it, and salivary calcium is not depleted by sugar intake.
  11. The correct answer is A (High risk, so shorten the recall interval and intensify fluoride and diet counselling): Any new lesion within twelve months places a client in the high risk category on its own, and here it is joined by white spot lesions and a frequent sipping habit, so the profile is unambiguously high risk. The category then drives the plan: a shorter recall interval, a stronger fluoride regimen and diet counselling aimed at the number and timing of exposures rather than the total quantity. Treating a single new lesion as reassuring inverts the meaning of a disease indicator. Salivary testing and bacterial counts refine an assessment but are not required before a category can be assigned from indicators, risk factors and protective factors.
  12. The correct answer is B (Protective factors, which are weighed against the risk factors to set the category): Fluoride exposure, effective home care and xylitol use are protective factors, and risk assessment works by weighing them against the disease indicators and risk factors to place the client at low, moderate or high risk. That balance is what makes the assessment useful, because two clients with identical lesions may sit in different categories once protection is counted. Disease indicators are findings such as new lesions or white spots that show disease is already active, which none of these three are. Xylitol is a sugar alcohol that plaque bacteria do not ferment to acid, so it is not a sweetened exposure in the cariogenic sense.
  13. The correct answer is C (Caries in any primary tooth of a child under six years of age): Early childhood caries is defined as caries in any primary tooth of a child under six years of age, which is the definition a screening protocol must use if it is to identify children early enough to act. Restricting the definition to the maxillary incisors describes the classic presenting pattern rather than the condition, and would miss occlusal and molar lesions in the same children. Requiring the whole primary dentition to be involved would only capture the most advanced cases. Permanent teeth are outside the definition altogether, since the condition is specific to the primary dentition.
  14. The correct answer is A (Within six months of that first tooth erupting, and by twelve months at latest): The first dental visit should occur within six months of eruption of the first tooth and by twelve months of age at the latest, which for this child means a visit at around twelve months either way. An early visit allows risk assessment, caregiver education on the bedtime bottle and on transmission of cariogenic bacteria, and fluoride advice before lesions appear. Waiting until three or five years means the highest risk period for early childhood caries passes without any professional contact. Waiting for visible discolouration or pain delays care until the disease is already established.
  15. The correct answer is D (Plain water only in the bottle at bedtime, with other drinks given in a cup): Only plain water belongs in a bedtime bottle once teeth have erupted, because salivary flow falls close to zero during sleep and any fermentable carbohydrate held against the teeth produces a prolonged acid challenge with no clearance. Moving all other drinks to an open cup at mealtimes keeps the exposures short and separates them from sleep. Diluting juice lowers the concentration but not the number or duration of exposures, which is what actually drives the lesions. Milk sugars are fermentable and a sweetened electrolyte drink adds both carbohydrate and acidity, so neither is safe overnight.
  16. The correct answer is B (Cariogenic bacteria transfer to the infant, so stop sharing and treat her own caries): Cariogenic flora is acquired from a caregiver, and sharing spoons or cleaning a soother in the adult's mouth transfers those organisms directly to the infant, which is why a caregiver with untreated caries is itself listed as a high risk indicator for the child. The recommendation therefore has two parts: stop the sharing behaviours, and get the caregiver's own lesions treated so the bacterial load falls. Diet supplies the substrate but not the organisms, so the transmission route is real and modifiable. The risk applies from the moment primary teeth erupt, not years later with the permanent dentition.
  17. The correct answer is B (Exposed root surface with a higher critical pH, dry mouth and reduced dexterity): Root caries dominates in older adults because people keep their teeth longer and recession exposes cementum and dentin, which begin to lose mineral at approximately pH 6.2 to 6.7 rather than the 5.5 that intact enamel tolerates. Add dry mouth from polypharmacy, reduced dexterity and a softer more frequently eaten diet, and the risk rises sharply even in a mouth that has never had a restoration. Enamel does not soften with age; if anything it becomes less soluble. Salivary calcium supports remineralization rather than driving mineral out, and recession does not raise plaque pH.
  18. The correct answer is C (Anticholinergics, antidepressants, antihypertensives, diuretics and antihistamines taken daily): Anticholinergics, antidepressants, antihypertensives, diuretics and antihistamines are the drug groups repeatedly implicated in reduced salivary flow, and a client on several of them at once is at compounded risk. Head and neck radiation and Sjogren syndrome are the other major causes worth screening for. Rampant cervical caries in a client with a long medication list calls for a salivary assessment and a medication review with the prescriber, not a lecture about brushing. The other groups listed carry their own oral considerations, such as inhaled corticosteroids and candidiasis, but they are not the principal xerostomia producing medications.
  19. The correct answer is A (Clearance, buffering, a calcium and phosphate reservoir, and antimicrobial proteins): Saliva clears food and substrate from the mouth, buffers the acid that plaque produces, holds a reservoir of calcium and phosphate that supports remineralization, and carries antimicrobial proteins. Losing all four at once explains why caries can accelerate dramatically even when home care is unchanged, and why xerostomia ranks among the strongest caries drivers. Saliva does not scrub biofilm off mechanically, which is why brushing and interdental cleaning are still required. Its buffering acts on plaque acid as much as on dietary acid, and it is not a route for delivering systemic fluoride into erupted enamel.
  20. The correct answer is D (It dissolves at a lower pH than hydroxyapatite, so it resists acid longer): Fluorapatite is less soluble than hydroxyapatite and requires a lower pH before it begins to dissolve, so a tooth surface enriched with fluoride survives acid challenges that would demineralize untreated enamel. Fluoride in the surrounding fluid also inhibits demineralization during the challenge and accelerates remineralization once pH recovers. A higher critical pH would mean the mineral dissolves more readily, which is the opposite of the protective effect and describes root surface rather than fluorapatite. Fluoride does reduce acid production at higher concentrations by inhibiting bacterial enolase, but it does not prevent biofilm from forming.
  21. The correct answer is C (It inhibits bacterial enolase, reducing the acid that the biofilm produces): At higher concentrations fluoride inhibits bacterial enolase, an enzyme in the glycolytic pathway, so the biofilm produces less acid from the same carbohydrate load. This antibacterial effect sits alongside the two mineral mechanisms, inhibition of demineralization during the acid challenge and acceleration of remineralization with formation of fluorapatite afterwards. Fluoride is not itself a buffer, and buffering of plaque acid is a salivary function. Pellicle formation is unaffected by fluoride, and fluoride substitutes for hydroxyl rather than calcium in the apatite lattice, producing a less soluble mineral rather than an inert one.
  22. The correct answer is D (A low concentration available frequently outperforms a large infrequent dose): Because fluoride acts topically on the mineral exchange at the tooth surface, what matters is having fluoride present in the fluid around the tooth whenever an acid challenge occurs, so a low concentration delivered often beats a large dose given rarely. Twice daily fluoridated toothpaste is therefore the foundation of any regimen, with professional applications added on top for clients at higher risk rather than substituted for daily use. No single application creates a reservoir that protects for a year. Systemic dosing after eruption offers little benefit, which is precisely why supplements have fallen out of routine use.
  23. The correct answer is A (Spit out the excess paste and avoid rinsing with water afterwards): Spitting without rinsing leaves a reservoir of fluoride at the tooth surface and in saliva after brushing, which extends the topical effect for far longer than the brushing itself. Rinsing repeatedly with water and then with a fluoride free mouthwash washes almost all of that away, so she is discarding most of the benefit she has already paid for. Using more paste raises the swallowed dose without extending contact time and does not fix the rinsing problem. Brushing more often while still rinsing it all away repeats the same loss, and a weaker paste would reduce the benefit further.
  24. The correct answer is B (Family paste 1000 to 1500 ppm, and prescription paste about 5000 ppm): Toothpaste sold in Canada for general use contains roughly 1000 to 1500 parts per million fluoride, and prescription paste at 5000 parts per million is intended for high risk adults with root caries or xerostomia, which fits her father exactly. Knowing both figures lets a client understand why the prescription product is not simply a stronger version of the same thing. The figure of 22 600 parts per million belongs to 5 percent sodium fluoride varnish, a professionally applied product rather than a paste. The values of 0.7 and 1.5 are drinking water figures expressed in mg/L, which are thousands of times lower than any dentifrice.
  25. The correct answer is D (Use a green pea sized amount with adult supervision and teach her to spit): From three to six years the recommended amount is a green pea sized quantity used with adult supervision, and the child is actively taught to spit rather than swallow. That combination keeps the fluoride benefit while limiting the ingested dose, which matters here because swallowed toothpaste is a leading source of fluorosis during the years when permanent crowns are mineralizing. Withdrawing fluoride toothpaste altogether removes the main preventive measure and is not the answer to poor spitting. The rice grain smear belongs to children under three, and a full brush head raises the swallowed dose in exactly the child who swallows most of it.
  26. The correct answer is C (Dry the teeth, then apply a thin layer to all surfaces including interproximal): The teeth are dried first and a thin layer of varnish is applied to all surfaces, with particular attention to interproximal and cervical areas where lesions start and where a brush reaches least well. The varnish then sets on contact with saliva, and because so little material is used the swallowed dose stays low even in a young child. Flooding wet teeth wastes material and gives uneven coverage. Restricting application to occlusal surfaces leaves the smooth surface white spot lesions untreated, and painting mucosa rather than tooth structure does not deliver fluoride where remineralization is needed.
  27. The correct answer is B (Two to four times per year, alongside daily fluoridated toothpaste at home): High risk clients receive varnish two to four times per year, and the professional applications sit on top of daily fluoridated toothpaste rather than replacing it, because frequent low concentration exposure is what protects the tooth surface between visits. Two new lesions in twelve months place this child clearly in that category. An application every two years or a single lifetime application cannot maintain the raised fluoride availability that the caries balance needs. Fortnightly application for three months has no evidence base and raises the total fluoride dose in a child still within the fluorosis risk window without added benefit.
  28. The correct answer is A (Benefit is largely topical, fluorosis risk is real, and other sources already exist): Supplements deliver fluoride systemically when the benefit is largely topical after eruption, they carry a genuine fluorosis risk in a 5-year-old whose permanent crowns are still mineralizing, and long term compliance is poor. This child already has a daily topical source in her toothpaste and no disease indicators, so there is nothing for a supplement to add. Where supplements are considered at all, it is for a high risk child without a fluoridated supply and only after every other fluoride source has been audited. Supplements do exist and are not withdrawn for kidney toxicity, so those explanations would misinform the parent.
  29. The correct answer is C (Once a tooth has erupted it cannot become fluorotic, so erupted teeth are safe): Dental fluorosis is a defect of enamel formation, so it can only arise while the crown is mineralizing before eruption, and a tooth that is already in the mouth cannot become fluorotic no matter what is applied to it. At 14 her permanent crowns other than third molars are fully formed and erupted, so varnish carries no fluorosis risk for her. Varnish is in fact highly concentrated at 22 600 parts per million, so the reassurance cannot rest on a low dose. Swallowed toothpaste and supplements are among the usual sources during the risk window, so restricting the cause to drinking water is inaccurate.
  30. The correct answer is D (Swallowed toothpaste, supplements and formula water in roughly the first three years): The permanent anterior crowns mineralize earliest, so the appearance of those teeth is most vulnerable to excess intake in roughly the first three years of life, and the usual sources are swallowed toothpaste, fluoride supplements and fluoridated water used to reconstitute infant formula. Controlling the amount of paste and auditing every fluoride source in that period is what protects the visible teeth. Exposures at six to ten years or later fall outside the window for anterior crowns, and by twelve years the risk window has closed for all teeth except third molars. Varnish is applied to erupted teeth, which cannot become fluorotic.
  31. The correct answer is B (70 mg of fluoride, calculated as five milligrams per kilogram of body weight): The probable toxic dose is 5 mg of fluoride per kilogram of body weight, so for a 14 kg child the threshold is 5 multiplied by 14, which is 70 mg of fluoride. At or above that amount, intervention and hospital assessment are indicated rather than observation at home. Knowing the threshold in milligrams lets the clinician convert what was swallowed, since one gram of 1000 parts per million paste holds 1 mg of fluoride. The figure of 210 mg corresponds to 15 mg per kilogram, which marks the next management band rather than the probable toxic dose itself.
  32. The correct answer is A (Give milk or another calcium source and observe the child at home): At about 4.3 mg per kilogram the ingestion sits below the probable toxic dose of 5 mg per kilogram, so management is to give milk or another calcium source, which binds fluoride in the stomach, and to observe the child. Calcium is given even below the threshold because it reduces absorption and settles the early gastric symptoms of nausea and abdominal pain. Inducing vomiting and transporting to hospital belongs to the 5 to 15 mg per kilogram band, and immediate transport with cardiac monitoring belongs above 15 mg per kilogram. Doing nothing until the next recall ignores an ingestion that still warrants observation today.
  33. The correct answer is D (Give calcium by mouth, induce vomiting, and transport the child to hospital): At about 8 mg per kilogram the ingestion falls in the 5 to 15 mg per kilogram band, where management is calcium by mouth, induced vomiting, and transport to hospital for assessment. Calcium binds fluoride in the stomach and limits absorption while emptying the stomach removes what has not yet been absorbed. Milk and observation at home is the response below 5 mg per kilogram, which this ingestion exceeds. Immediate transport with cardiac monitoring and nothing by mouth is reserved for ingestions above 15 mg per kilogram, where hypocalcemia and arrhythmia become the leading concerns.
  34. The correct answer is C (Transport immediately to hospital with cardiac monitoring during the transfer): At about 21 mg per kilogram the ingestion is above 15 mg per kilogram, the band where immediate transport with cardiac monitoring is the priority because severe fluoride poisoning causes hypocalcemia, arrhythmia and death. Drowsiness with repeated vomiting signals systemic effects rather than simple gastric irritation, so nothing should delay transfer. Giving a calcium drink and waiting an hour, or inducing further vomiting in a drowsy child, both waste time and add aspiration risk. Discharging the child home at this dose would leave a genuine medical emergency unmanaged.
  35. The correct answer is B (1 mg of fluoride, since 1000 ppm equals one milligram per gram of paste): Parts per million is milligrams per kilogram, so 1000 parts per million is 1000 mg per kilogram, which is 1 mg of fluoride in every gram of paste. That single conversion is what lets a clinician turn a swallowed quantity into a dose in milligrams and compare it against the probable toxic dose of 5 mg per kilogram of body weight. A tube of paste weighing 100 grams at this concentration therefore holds about 100 mg of fluoride in total. The other figures are out by factors of ten or a thousand and would badly misclassify the severity of an ingestion.
  36. The correct answer is A (Apply 38 percent silver diamine fluoride after consent covering the permanent black staining): Silver diamine fluoride at 38 percent is designed for exactly this situation, root caries in a frail older adult where restoration is difficult, because the silver is antibacterial and the fluoride remineralizes the lesion surface to produce hard arrested dentin without drilling. Consent must cover the permanent black staining of the treated lesions before application, and the substitute decision maker's concern about appearance makes that discussion essential rather than optional. Fluoride varnish supports remineralization but does not reliably arrest soft cavitated root lesions on its own. Deferring all care leaves active disease untreated in someone unlikely ever to tolerate restorative treatment.
Case C034

Priya Raman, age 34, attends for a recall appointment. She brushes twice daily with a medium bristle brush using a horizontal scrubbing motion, and reports she has never used anything between her teeth. Assessment shows generalized bleeding on probing at interproximal sites, probing depths of 2 to 3 mm with no attachment loss, and smooth wedge shaped cervical defects on the facial surfaces of 13, 14, 23 and 24. Gingival margins on those four teeth have receded about 1 mm. She is otherwise healthy and takes no medication.

  1. The correct answer is B (Horizontal scrubbing with a medium bristle brush applied with heavy force): toothbrush abrasion appears as smooth, wedge shaped cervical defects on the facial surfaces of canines and premolars, which is exactly the group of teeth affected here. The client's reported horizontal scrubbing with a medium bristle brush supplies the mechanism, and the associated 1 mm of recession at those same four teeth fits mechanical trauma at the gingival margin. Dietary acid produces broader, shiny, cupped lesions in a generalized distribution rather than sharply wedged notches confined to four teeth. Bacterial demineralization would give opaque or cavitated lesions at plaque retentive sites, not at the site of vigorous brushing.
  2. The correct answer is D (Angle a soft brush 45 degrees into the sulcus with short strokes): this is the Bass technique, which directs the bristle tips apically into the gingival sulcus using short vibratory strokes and light pressure. It addresses the marginal and sulcular biofilm driving her bleeding while removing the horizontal scrubbing that produced the cervical defects. Keeping the same scrubbing motion, or moving to a harder brush, would continue the abrasion and the recession. Shortening brushing time reduces biofilm removal without correcting the damaging stroke, so the tissue response would not improve.
  3. The correct answer is A (Dental floss curved in a C shape against each proximal surface): where the papilla completely fills a type I embrasure and the contact is tight, floss is the aid of choice because it adapts to the curved proximal surface and passes gently beneath the papilla. An interdental brush is preferred only where a space allows one to fit passively, and forcing a large brush through a tight contact traumatizes the tissue. An oral irrigator flushes debris but does not disrupt adherent biofilm, so it cannot be the only interproximal method. Wood sticks are limited to facial access and would not reach these posterior proximal surfaces.
  4. The correct answer is C (Floss the lower right posterior contacts each night before bed): a goal works when it names the behaviour, the site and the time, so the client knows exactly what to do and can tell whether she did it. Limiting the first goal to one sextant makes early success likely, and early success builds self efficacy. Telling her to improve her home care names no behaviour at all and cannot be evaluated. Flossing every contact three times daily is unrealistic for someone who has never flossed, and reading a pamphlet supplies information without changing any behaviour.
  5. The correct answer is A (Bleeding on probing at the lower right interproximal sites since baseline): evaluation compares an objective clinical finding against the recorded baseline at the exact sites the goal targeted, and gingival bleeding responds within weeks to interproximal biofilm removal. Self reported frequency and stated confidence are useful supporting information, but they measure what the client reports rather than how the tissue responded. Cervical abrasion defects are lost tooth structure and do not refill, so they can never show whether the new home care worked.
Case C035

Devon Clarke, age 19, plays competitive hockey and attends as a new client. He sips a sports drink through most practices and training sessions, drinks two energy drinks on game days, and eats a granola bar between classes. Examination shows smooth, shiny, cupped lesions on the occlusal surfaces of 16, 26, 36 and 46 with no cavitation, generalized sensitivity to cold air, and light biofilm with minimal gingival inflammation. He is healthy, takes no medication, and has no history of restorative treatment.

  1. The correct answer is C (Chemical dissolution by dietary acid of non bacterial origin): erosion requires neither biofilm nor fermentable carbohydrate, and it produces smooth, shiny, generalized cupped lesions of exactly this kind. Devon's sports and energy drinks sit near pH 3 and are sipped over long periods, which keeps the surfaces bathed in acid. Bacterial demineralization would give localized opaque or cavitated lesions at plaque retentive sites, which his light biofilm and absence of cavitation argue against. Tooth to tooth wear produces flat matched facets rather than rounded cupping, and brushing wear affects cervical facial surfaces.
  2. The correct answer is B (A food diary over three to five days including one non training day): a multi day diary spanning both a typical and an atypical day captures the number, timing and form of exposures, which is what drives his risk. A single 24 hour recall screens quickly but would miss the swing between his training days and his rest days. Asking him to list the foods he already believes are harmful records his beliefs rather than his intake. A weekly sugar total hides frequency, and frequency of exposure matters more than the total amount consumed.
  3. The correct answer is D (Rinse with water and delay brushing for 30 to 60 minutes): the tooth surface is softened immediately after an acid challenge, so brushing at once removes softened mineral, while water or a bicarbonate rinse raises the pH and lets saliva begin remineralizing. Brushing harder with an abrasive paste adds mechanical loss to the chemical loss already occurring. An acidic rinse repeats the challenge instead of reversing it. Swishing the drink increases both the contact time and the surface area exposed, which is the opposite of what erosion control requires.
  4. The correct answer is D (Drink water during practices and study sessions instead of the sports drink): water carries no fermentable carbohydrate and no acid, so it removes both the caries and the erosion challenge from the exposures that occupy most of his day. Diet cola is sugar free but remains strongly acidic, so erosion would continue unchanged. Unsweetened juice still supplies fruit sugar and citric acid, and it behaves in plaque like any other sugar. Adding a gel increases the number of separate exposures rather than reducing them.
  5. The correct answer is A (A piece of aged cheddar cheese eaten after the afternoon class): aged cheese is anticariogenic because it stimulates salivary flow and supplies calcium, phosphate and casein, so it actively opposes demineralization rather than merely avoiding it. Dried fruit is a concentrated fermentable carbohydrate that clings to the tooth, and eating it slowly creates overlapping acid exposures. Honey behaves in plaque exactly as sucrose does, so a honey sweetened bar is cariogenic. A sipped smoothie combines fruit sugar with acid over a long contact time, which raises both his caries and his erosion risk.
Case C036

Marguerite Chan, age 62, completed non surgical periodontal therapy in two quadrants last week. Her health history lists hypertension controlled with a thiazide diuretic, and she reports a persistently dry mouth. Her dentist prescribed a 0.12 percent chlorhexidine gluconate rinse. She has extensive anterior composite restorations, wears a cast metal framework partial denture in the mandible, and tells the hygienist she has been rinsing with the chlorhexidine straight after brushing each morning and evening.

  1. The correct answer is C (Separate the rinse from brushing by about 30 minutes to avoid inactivation): anionic detergents in toothpaste bind chlorhexidine and block its substantivity, so using the two together wastes the dose. Leaving roughly 30 minutes between brushing and rinsing preserves the slow release over eight to twelve hours that makes the agent effective. Rinsing first does not help, because the detergent still follows and inactivates whatever has bound to the oral surfaces. Diluting the rinse lowers the delivered concentration, and rinsing more often raises the staining and taste effects without adding benefit.
  2. The correct answer is B (Use is short term, usually about two weeks, and is then reviewed): chlorhexidine is indicated for defined short courses such as the period after periodontal therapy, and use rarely extends beyond two months because extrinsic brown staining, altered taste, increased supragingival calculus and mucosal desquamation accumulate with time. Indefinite or year long use would stain her extensive anterior composites heavily for no added benefit once mechanical cleaning is re established. Stopping today is premature, since the period immediately after therapy is exactly when the agent is indicated and staining is managed at a professional appointment rather than assumed.
  3. The correct answer is A (An alcohol free essential oil rinse used daily alongside mechanical cleaning): essential oil rinses reduce plaque and gingivitis without the staining that limits chlorhexidine, which makes them the better option for long term daily use in a client with anterior composites. Choosing an alcohol free formulation matters because alcohol aggravates an already dry mouth. Returning to chlorhexidine reintroduces staining and taste disturbance for a client who no longer has a short term indication. No rinse removes an established biofilm, so a rinse never replaces interdental cleaning.
  4. The correct answer is B (Stop the bleach, use a denture cleanser, and remove it overnight): sodium hypochlorite corrodes a cast metal framework, so it is not an appropriate soak for this appliance, and a denture brush with mild soap or a denture cleanser cleans it without damage. Removing the denture overnight and storing it in water lets the mucosa recover and reduces the risk of denture stomatitis caused by Candida albicans, which nocturnal wear drives. Hot water warps acrylic rather than disinfecting the appliance safely. Regular and whitening toothpastes are both too abrasive for a denture surface.
Case C037

Rashid Osman, age 47, has two implant supported crowns replacing 36 and 46, placed 18 months ago.
