He smokes 12 cigarettes daily and recently started vaping a sweetened nicotine product between cigarettes. Assessment shows bleeding on probing around both implants, probing depths of 4 mm with no radiographic bone loss, generalized extrinsic staining, and biofilm on the lingual surfaces of the implant crowns. He cleans with a manual toothbrush only and uses no interdental aid.

  1. The correct answer is D (A nylon coated wire interdental brush with a low abrasive paste): an exposed metal core scratches a titanium abutment and a scratched surface then accumulates more biofilm, so the wire must be nylon coated. A low abrasive paste protects the implant surface for the same reason. An uncoated steel wire brush is precisely the instrument that causes that damage. A wood stick will not adapt around an implant crown, and a hard brush with an abrasive paste risks both surface damage and further gingival recession.
  2. The correct answer is C (Nicotine causes vasoconstriction, which suppresses visible bleeding despite active inflammation): reduced gingival blood flow in a smoker masks bleeding, so the tissue can look deceptively healthy at home while disease progresses. Smoking in fact delays healing and produces greater attachment and bone loss and higher implant failure, so faster healing is the opposite of what happens. Brushing at the margin does disturb the sulcus, which is why a client with inflammation usually notices bleeding. Bleeding around an implant is a sign of inflammation and is never a normal expected finding.
  3. The correct answer is C (Explain the dry mouth and caries risk, and offer help when ready): vaping is not harmless, and the reported oral effects include dry mouth, mucosal irritation, gingival inflammation and caries risk from sweetened aerosols, which is information he has invited by stating his belief. Linking clear personalized advice to his own findings and leaving the door open matches the advise step of the five A framework, which is what is indicated once the assess step has shown the client is not ready. Agreeing that vaping is harmless would be false. Refusing care is punitive and not indicated, and imposing a quit date on an unwilling client applies the assist step out of order.
  4. The correct answer is A (Assist him by setting a quit date and discussing pharmacotherapy options): in the five A framework, once the hygienist has asked, advised and assessed a willingness to quit now, the next step is to assist, which means agreeing a quit date, offering counselling and providing information about pharmacotherapy. Repeating the advise or ask steps for a client who has already declared readiness wastes the moment when he is most motivated. Deferring the conversation to a future recall abandons him at that same moment, and arranging follow up or a quitline referral comes after assisting rather than instead of it.
  5. The correct answer is B (Replace it now, and normally every three to four months): the standard replacement interval is three to four months, but splayed bristles mean the brush no longer adapts to the tooth surface or reaches the sulcus, so it is replaced whenever that happens, which for this client is today. Waiting a year, or waiting until bristles fall out of the head, leaves her cleaning with a brush that has already lost its effectiveness. Boiling a nylon head does not restore splayed filaments and can distort the head further.
  6. The correct answer is D (Both work well, and powered brushes show a small additional benefit): systematic review evidence shows that manual and powered brushes both remove biofilm effectively when used properly, with a small advantage for powered designs, particularly oscillating rotating ones. Saying that powered brushes offer no benefit at all misstates that evidence, and claiming manual brushes are always superior reverses it. No toothbrush of either type reaches the interproximal surfaces that carry most of the caries and attachment loss, so neither removes the need for an interdental aid.
  7. The correct answer is A (Spit out the excess paste and avoid rinsing with water afterwards): fluoride acts mainly by topical mechanisms at the tooth surface, so leaving a thin film of paste in place after spitting keeps the concentration in the oral fluid raised for longer. Rinsing vigorously with a full cup of water washes that reservoir away and removes much of the benefit of the paste she has just used. A salt water rinse does the same thing while adding nothing. Doubling the brushing time does not compensate for washing the fluoride away immediately afterwards.
  8. The correct answer is D (A sweeping roll toward the occlusal surface at the end): the modified Bass keeps the 45 degree sulcular placement and the short vibratory strokes, then adds a sweep of the bristles occlusally to clean the rest of the crown before the brush is repositioned. A horizontal scrubbing stroke is the motion associated with cervical abrasion and recession, so it forms no part of a recommended technique. A firm circular motion with the teeth closed describes the Fones technique instead. Holding the bristles motionless removes nothing, because it is the vibratory stroke that disrupts the biofilm.
  9. The correct answer is C (A larger interdental brush sized to fill the open embrasure): where the papilla is gone and a type III embrasure is present, a brush large enough to contact both proximal surfaces removes far more biofilm than floss can, and an end tuft brush suits the remaining spaces. Floss passes through the middle of a wide space without contacting much of either proximal surface, so it underperforms badly there. A rubber tip works at the gingival margin rather than through the embrasure. A pre threaded flosser shares the limitation of floss and mainly helps clients with poor dexterity.
  10. The correct answer is B (Change to a small interdental brush sized to pass through passively): current evidence favours interdental brushes over floss for reducing plaque and gingival inflammation wherever the space allows a brush to fit without force, which is the case in this type II embrasure. Floss remains the aid of choice only where the papilla completely fills the space and the contact is tight. An oral irrigator flushes debris but does not disrupt adherent biofilm, so it cannot replace mechanical cleaning. Wood sticks are used from the facial aspect and would not adapt to these proximal surfaces.
  11. The correct answer is B (Curve the floss into a C shape and slide it under the papilla): held straight, floss contacts only a narrow line on the tooth, whereas a C shaped wrap adapts to the curved proximal surface and reaches the biofilm just beneath the gingival margin where inflammation begins. Easing the floss past the contact instead of snapping it also protects the papilla from laceration. Changing to unwaxed floss does not correct the adaptation problem and shreds more readily at tight contacts. Blanching the tissue signals trauma, and reusing one short section carries debris from site to site.
  12. The correct answer is A (A floss holder, which lets the caregiver clean contacts without inserting fingers): floss holders and pre threaded flossers exist for exactly this situation, where one person cleans another person's mouth or where dexterity is limited, and they keep the caregiver's fingers clear of a client who resists. Finger wrapped floss requires both hands inside the mouth, which this client will not tolerate and which risks a bite injury. A rinse cannot remove an established biofilm and so cannot replace interdental cleaning. Wood sticks will not pass through tight contacts and are limited to facial access.
  13. The correct answer is D (The irrigator flushes debris and is used in addition to mechanical cleaning): oral irrigators reduce bleeding and reach pockets and fixed appliances well, but they flush rather than break apart adherent biofilm, so they are prescribed alongside brushing and interdental cleaning and never instead of them. Telling him the device can replace brushing would leave the biofilm around his brackets intact, which his red bleeding margins already demonstrate. Stopping the irrigator discards a device that genuinely helps around appliances. Raising the pressure does not change the mechanism and can traumatize the tissue.
  14. The correct answer is C (It has a modest antiplaque effect and lower substantivity than chlorhexidine): cetylpyridinium chloride at 0.05 to 0.075 percent is sold over the counter and produces a real but modest reduction in plaque, and it is retained on oral surfaces for much less time than chlorhexidine. Claiming that it binds for longer reverses that comparison. It is not a prescription agent and is not restricted to the period after periodontal therapy. No rinse of any kind removes an established biofilm, so nothing about this product justifies reducing her mechanical cleaning.
  15. The correct answer is A (An alcohol free formulation, because alcohol suits neither her history nor xerostomia): alcohol containing rinses are unsuitable for anyone recovering from alcohol dependence, and they aggravate a dry mouth and mucositis, so an alcohol free essential oil rinse meets both needs while avoiding the staining she wants to avoid. A high alcohol content is a vehicle characteristic and is not what produces the antiplaque effect. Chlorhexidine stains regardless of salivary flow and is a short term agent rather than a daily long term one. An acidic rinse would add an erosion risk to an already vulnerable mouth.
  16. The correct answer is C (Have the client rinse for 30 seconds immediately before the appointment): a pre procedural rinse is one of the recognized short term indications for chlorhexidine, and rinsing 15 mL for 30 seconds just before treatment lowers the bacterial load carried in the aerosol. Six months or a year of use goes far beyond the short courses this agent is intended for and would produce extrinsic staining, altered taste and increased supragingival calculus. Chlorhexidine cannot substitute for instrumentation, because an agent applied to an organized biofilm does not remove it and the deposits still have to be disrupted mechanically.
  17. The correct answer is B (Explain these are known reversible effects and review the plan at her visit): altered or reduced taste and extrinsic brown staining of the teeth, restorations and tongue are documented effects of chlorhexidine, and they resolve once the short course ends and the staining is removed professionally. An allergic reaction presents with mucosal swelling, urticaria or breathing difficulty rather than a dull taste and a brown tongue. Doubling the rinse volume would increase the very effects she is reporting. An urgent neurological referral is not warranted when a known and expected drug effect fully explains the findings.
  18. The correct answer is A (Denture stomatitis from Candida albicans, so remove it overnight and clean it): erythema confined to the area covered by the denture base in a client who wears the appliance continuously is the classic presentation, and nocturnal wear is the main driver. Management starts with removing the denture overnight and storing it in water, cleaning it daily with a denture brush and cleanser, and brushing the ridges, palate and tongue. Acrylic allergy is rare and would not follow the denture outline so precisely. Redness matching a denture base is not a normal finding and should not be left unmanaged.
  19. The correct answer is D (Clean them over a basin part filled with water or a folded towel): acrylic fractures readily when it strikes a hard surface, and a partly filled basin or a soft towel cushions a denture dropped by a client with tremor. A hard countertop offers no cushioning when the towel is set beside the denture rather than beneath it. Working over an open dry sink is exactly the situation in which the fracture happens. Holding both dentures at once increases the load on an unsteady hand and makes a drop more likely rather than less.
  20. The correct answer is B (A floss threader carrying tufted floss under the tissue surface of the pontic): the units of a fixed bridge are joined, so floss cannot be passed down through a contact to reach beneath the pontic and has to be threaded from the facial or lingual aspect and drawn along that surface. Tufted floss holds more cleaning material against the broad tissue surface than plain floss does. Standard floss at the abutment contacts cleans the outer proximal surfaces of the abutments and never reaches under the pontic. A rubber tip and an occlusal end tuft brush both work on surfaces other than the inflamed one.
  21. The correct answer is C (Brush twice daily, add an interdental brush around brackets and under wires): fixed appliances add retentive surfaces that a toothbrush alone cannot reach, and a small interdental brush passes under the archwire and around each bracket where the inflammation is arising. Twice daily brushing with a fluoridated toothpaste remains the base of the plan, and a powered brush is a reasonable addition for a client who finds the task awkward. A rinse does not remove an established biofilm and cannot carry the plan alone. Avoiding the brackets or brushing less often would allow white spot lesions to develop.
  22. The correct answer is D (Teach her to scan ingredient lists for names ending in ose): sucrose, glucose, fructose, maltose and dextrose all behave the same way in dental biofilm, along with honey, molasses and concentrated fruit juice, so a client who can read a label can identify the sugars in the products she actually buys. A list of forbidden foods is generic, ignores what she chooses and dates quickly. Fruit derived sugars are fermentable and are not safe for the teeth. Removing table sugar while still drinking three sweetened products daily leaves the frequency of exposure essentially unchanged.
  23. The correct answer is A (Sweetened with a sugar alcohol such as xylitol or sorbitol): sugar alcohols are cariostatic, because xylitol cannot be fermented by oral bacteria at all and sorbitol is fermented only slowly, so neither drops plaque pH to a cariogenic level, and chewing itself stimulates saliva, which clears and buffers the mouth when she cannot brush. Honey is a fermentable carbohydrate and behaves in plaque exactly as sucrose does. Glucose syrup is equally fermentable and would prolong the acid challenge rather than avoid it. Citric acid does stimulate salivary flow, but it also lowers pH directly and adds an erosion risk.
  24. The correct answer is C (The second client is at greater risk because of prolonged repeated exposure): plaque pH falls within minutes of a carbohydrate exposure and takes twenty to sixty minutes to recover, so a drink finished in ten minutes produces one drop while the same drink sipped across an afternoon produces overlapping drops that hold the pH low for hours. Frequency and duration of exposure therefore matter more than the total amount, which is why identical totals do not give identical risk. Saliva does buffer and clear the acid, but it needs an uninterrupted recovery period, and continuous sipping never allows one.
  25. The correct answer is B (Salivary flow falls near zero in sleep, so a bedtime exposure is worst): saliva provides the clearance and buffering that end an acid challenge, and it almost stops during sleep, so fermentable carbohydrate taken after the last brushing sits against the teeth for hours. Chocolate milk carries added sugar, so its dairy content does not make it safe at that time of day. Brushing again afterwards is better than nothing but does not undo the exposure and is unlikely to be done thoroughly by a tired child. Timing is not irrelevant, because frequency and timing outweigh the total daily amount.
  26. The correct answer is A (Intrinsic acid reaching the palatal surfaces from reflux or vomiting): gastric acid pools against the palatal surfaces of the maxillary anterior teeth, which is the classic pattern of intrinsic erosion, and it explains loss confined to those surfaces in a client whose only drinks are water and milk. Dietary acid from citrus or carbonated drinks affects the facial and occlusal surfaces first and would not spare them entirely. Bacterial demineralization requires biofilm and fermentable carbohydrate, neither of which is present, and it produces opaque or cavitated lesions rather than smooth cupping. Toothpaste abrasion affects facial cervical areas instead.
  27. The correct answer is D (Add a self monitoring tool such as a calendar tick or reminder): self monitoring makes the behaviour visible to the client and gives her feedback between appointments, which is exactly what a client who forgets and cannot judge her own progress is missing. A weekly disclosing tablet serves the same purpose by showing her where biofilm remains. Lengthening the recall removes support at the moment she needs more of it. Repeating an explanation adds information she already has, and information alone rarely changes behaviour. Adding four more goals dilutes her attention and makes early success less likely.
  28. The correct answer is B (Build each instruction from that client's own findings and negotiate it): generic advice fails predictably because it ignores what the client values, targets a behaviour that is not that client's main problem, or names an aid that does not suit the mouth, the dexterity or the budget in front of her. Advice drawn from the client's own bleeding sites and negotiated rather than dictated is what changes behaviour. Repeating the same message more firmly or in print does not make it any more relevant to an individual. Shortening or moving the same generic message changes its delivery without changing its content.
  29. The correct answer is C (Within six months of the first tooth, and by twelve months at latest): an early visit lets the caregiver receive a risk assessment together with brushing and feeding advice while the child's habits are still forming, which is when early childhood caries can still be prevented. Waiting for the full primary dentition or for school entry misses several years of the highest risk period. Waiting until decay or pain appears means the first visit happens after disease is established, which is precisely the outcome that an infant oral health visit exists to avoid.
  30. The correct answer is A (Wipe the gum pads daily and put only water in the bedtime bottle): mouth care starts before the first tooth, and wiping the gum pads with a damp cloth familiarizes the infant with a daily routine, while nothing but water should go into a bottle taken to bed because a sweetened liquid pooling overnight drives early childhood caries. Waiting for the first tooth while the formula bottle continues leaves the main risk in place. Toothpaste has no tooth surface to act on before eruption and does nothing about the bottle. Cleaning a soother in the caregiver's mouth transmits cariogenic bacteria to the infant.
  31. The correct answer is C (Offer a chilled teething ring or firm gum massage with a clean finger): teething runs from roughly six to thirty months, and the recommended measures are physical rather than pharmacological, because pressure and cold relieve the tenderness safely. Benzocaine gels are not recommended for infants. Amber necklaces are a strangulation hazard and are not made safe by removing them at night. A sweetened drink in a bottle adds a frequent cariogenic exposure at exactly the age when early childhood caries begins.
  32. The correct answer is D (Reposition her semi supine and tilted to her left side): these symptoms fit supine hypotensive syndrome, in which the uterus compresses the vena cava and reduces venous return, and tilting the client to her left displaces the uterus and restores blood flow. Lowering the chair further makes the compression worse rather than better. Dental hygiene care is safe at any stage of pregnancy, so cancelling all treatment until after delivery is not indicated once she has been repositioned and is comfortable. A sweetened drink treats hypoglycemia, which does not explain symptoms that began the moment she lay flat.
  33. The correct answer is B (Rinse with water or a bicarbonate solution, and delay brushing): gastric acid leaves the enamel surface softened, so brushing at once removes mineral that saliva could otherwise restore, while a water or bicarbonate rinse raises the pH straight away. Delaying brushing for 30 to 60 minutes gives the surface time to recover. Brushing harder with an abrasive whitening paste adds mechanical loss to the chemical loss already occurring. An alcohol containing rinse dries the mouth without neutralizing the acid, and stopping brushing altogether would let biofilm accumulate during a period when pregnancy gingivitis is already likely.
  34. The correct answer is A (Saliva substitutes, xylitol gum, an adapted handle and shorter recall intervals): this client has both a salivary problem and a dexterity problem, so the plan has to restore clearance and buffering, make the brush usable, and bring her back more often because exposed root surfaces lose mineral at a higher critical pH than enamel does. A hard brush used with vigorous scrubbing would abrade those exposed root surfaces, and an alcohol containing rinse worsens the dryness. Disclosing alone assesses without changing anything. A dental hygienist does not direct a client to stop prescribed medication, and the conditions treated still need control.
  35. The correct answer is D (Educate the care staff on positioning, aids and a daily brushing routine): when a client can no longer initiate her own care, the effective intervention is directed at the people who provide it, covering how to position themselves, which aids to use, how to approach resistance and how to record what was done. A rinse cannot remove an established biofilm and does not substitute for brushing, quite apart from the swallowing risk in this population. Teaching a technique to a client with moderate dementia will not carry over between appointments. Discharging her removes the professional support the situation requires.
  36. The correct answer is C (Defer elective treatment and rebook him when he is not intoxicated): recent cannabis use causes tachycardia and anxiety, and a local anesthetic containing a vasoconstrictor can compound that cardiovascular effect, so elective treatment is postponed until the client presents unimpaired. Informed consent is also questionable in an intoxicated client. Proceeding as planned ignores a recognized interaction, and increasing the vasoconstrictor makes the cardiovascular risk considerably worse. Cannabis does not provide reliable analgesia for instrumentation, so treating without anesthetic would simply leave the client in pain.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

Case C038

Maple Bay is a rural community of about 4,200 people with no resident dentist or dental hygienist. Most employment is seasonal fishing and forestry work without benefits. Households draw water from private wells that are not fluoridated. The public health unit screened all grade two students this year and found that 46 percent had untreated decay, compared with 19 percent provincially. Many parents told the screening team that the nearest clinic is a two hour drive and that they cannot take unpaid time off work.

  1. The correct answer is C (Describe income, employment, geography and water fluoride as structural causes): oral disease is socially patterned, and the conditions described in Maple Bay are the recognized social determinants of oral health acting together. Seasonal work without benefits removes coverage, low income removes the ability to pay, distance removes supply, and unfluoridated well water removes a population level protection. Blaming parental motivation mistakes an outcome of structural disadvantage for its cause, and it would steer the board toward another brushing campaign that cannot touch any of these conditions. When a finding describes a structural barrier, the answer is rarely more education about brushing.
  2. The correct answer is A (A population health survey collecting self reported insurance status and cost barriers): the new question is about reported access and coverage, not about disease levels. Population health surveys such as the Canadian Community Health Survey collect self reported oral health, insurance status and cost as a reason for avoiding care, which is exactly what the committee needs. A clinical survey measuring DMFT and edentulism would repeat what the screening already established about disease and would still say nothing about insurance. Use a clinical survey for disease levels and a population health survey for reported access barriers.
  3. The correct answer is D (Seeking provincial funding for a resident hygienist and school sealant program): upstream work acts on policy and the conditions that produce disease rather than on individuals already affected. Securing funding that changes workforce supply and puts a program into the schools alters the environment for every child in Maple Bay, whether or not any family seeks care. Diet counselling with individual parents is midstream because it targets individual behaviour, while referral for treatment and chairside varnish for identified high risk children are downstream and midstream responses to disease that already exists.
  4. The correct answer is B (Distance, unpaid time off work and few billing providers block attendance): coverage on paper is only one component of access, which also depends on provider supply, travel, and appointment times that fit how people actually work. A two hour drive, lost unpaid wages, and only two offices willing to bill a program that pays below the fee guide together explain the gap without any assumption about parental knowledge. Suggesting that families do not know primary teeth need treatment ignores what the parents themselves reported to the screening team as their reason.
  5. The correct answer is D (Outcome evaluation, because long term change in disease status is measured): outcome evaluation measures the long term change in health status the program existed to achieve, and a caries increment compared across cohorts three years apart is exactly that. Process evaluation is the separate annual reporting of sessions delivered and children seen, which shows whether the program ran as planned but not whether disease changed. Formative work happens before launch, and impact evaluation captures immediate effects such as sealants placed or knowledge gained. A program can pass process evaluation and still fail on outcome, which is why both are done.
Case C039

Cedarwood Manor is a 92 bed long term care home. Most residents now keep many of their natural teeth. The average resident takes six medications, several of which cause xerostomia. Personal support workers provide daily care but have had no oral health training, and the home has no written oral care policy. A dental hygienist contracted to visit twice a year finds widespread root caries, denture stomatitis and heavy biofilm. The director of care asks the hygienist for advice on reducing aspiration pneumonia in the home.

  1. The correct answer is A (An oral care policy, admission assessment, bedside supplies and ongoing training): daily mouth care in long term care changes through structural measures, not through a single teaching session. A written policy makes the care an expectation rather than an option, an admission assessment identifies need, supplies at the bedside remove the practical obstacle, and repeated training reaches new staff as turnover occurs. More frequent hygienist visits still leave the other 363 days uncovered, a pamphlet reaches residents who depend on staff for care anyway, and shifting responsibility to families abandons residents who have no family visiting.
  2. The correct answer is B (Build healthy public policy, since decision makers set a binding requirement): this action area puts health on the agenda of decision makers, in this instance a facility board and a provincial standards review, so that the requirement applies to everyone regardless of individual effort. Developing personal skills would describe teaching a resident or a family member, which is not what happened here. Reorienting health services describes moving the system from treatment toward prevention, and strengthening community action describes a community setting its own priorities rather than a board adopting a mandatory standard.
  3. The correct answer is C (Raise the documented gap with the director of care and request a plan): client level advocacy means acting for the person in front of you, and an eleven day gap in documented mouth care alongside an ulcerated palate is a care failure that belongs with the person accountable for care in the home. Requesting a plan converts a complaint into a corrective action that can be followed up at the next visit. Charting the finding and simply waiting leaves the resident at continued risk, handing the task to the family transfers a facility duty onto visitors, and advising a move offers no realistic remedy.
  4. The correct answer is B (Silver diamine fluoride is secondary prevention, maintenance is tertiary prevention): secondary prevention detects and treats disease early to limit damage, and arresting an early root caries lesion found on screening fits that definition precisely. Tertiary prevention limits disability and preserves function once disease is established, which is what periodontal maintenance therapy does for residents with existing attachment loss. Primary prevention acts before any disease occurs, through measures such as water fluoridation or sealants on sound surfaces, so neither activity in this question is primary.
  5. The correct answer is D (A plaque index, because biofilm scores fall when daily care improves): reversible indices measure current conditions and therefore improve when care improves, which makes them suitable for evaluating a program over six months. DMFT records lifetime caries experience, is cumulative and irreversible, and can only rise, so it can never demonstrate that anything got better. The lower case dmft index applies to the primary dentition and has no place in a seniors residence, and counting edentulous residents measures a permanent past state rather than the effect of a new policy.
Case C040

A community health centre serves a neighbourhood with many recent arrivals. A government assisted refugee family arrived in Canada four months ago. The parents worked as an engineer and a teacher before arriving, and neither credential has been recognized, so both now hold part time jobs with no benefits. They speak limited English. The mother has pain from a broken molar and says she has been avoiding care because she expects a bill she cannot pay. The two children have never seen a dental provider.

  1. The correct answer is A (Temporary federal coverage may fund limited urgent dental care for a period): government assisted refugees and protected persons may hold temporary federal health coverage that includes a limited range of urgent dental services, and it ends once other coverage begins. The Canada Health Act covers medically necessary physician and hospital services and does not cover routine or urgent office based dental treatment, so pain alone does not bring her under it. Describing the coverage as full and comprehensive for five years overstates it, and telling her no coverage exists would wrongly leave her paying privately.
  2. The correct answer is C (Language and cultural safety barriers operating despite the family holding coverage): the family had both an appointment and a paying source of coverage, so cost and supply were not what stopped them. What stopped them was being unable to communicate in their own language and being left uncertain about what they would owe, which is a language and cultural safety failure by the receiving office. Calling this a financial barrier misreads the situation, because the fear of a bill arose from the absence of interpretation rather than from an absence of coverage.
  3. The correct answer is C (Arrange an interpreter and a provider who bills the federal program directly): client level advocacy acts for the person in front of you, using the resources at hand to remove the specific obstacles that stopped this family. Booking interpretation and matching them with an office that bills directly addresses both the language barrier and the fear of an unexpected bill. Submitting a brief to a provincial consultation, presenting data to a council and writing to a member of Parliament are all policy level advocacy, which is worthwhile but does not get this mother's molar treated.
  4. The correct answer is B (It is income tested and intended for people without private dental insurance): the federal plan is structured around household income and the absence of private coverage, and applicants must have filed a tax return. It does not replace provincial dental programs or private plans, so describing it as a full replacement is wrong. Eligibility is not open to everyone regardless of income or insurance, and enrolment was phased in by group over several years rather than opening to every age group at once. Direct the family to the current official source for ages, ceilings and copayments, since those details have changed.
Case C041

A dental hygienist joins an outreach team that visits a downtown shelter. Guests are mostly adults experiencing homelessness. Many report untreated pain, and clinical findings include rampant caries, generalized periodontitis and heavy xerostomia. Most guests use tobacco, several use stimulants, and many take medications with drying side effects. The shelter has a small room, a portable unit and a sink. Guests have no fixed address and rarely carry identification. An earlier program offering scheduled appointments at a hospital clinic had an attendance rate below ten percent.

  1. The correct answer is D (Drop in sessions at the shelter accepting walk ins without a fixed address): outreach into shelters and drop in centres that accepts walk ins and requires no fixed address is the model that reaches people experiencing homelessness, and the earlier attendance rate below ten percent shows what a fixed appointment model achieves with this population. Reminder calls fail when guests have no phone, proof of address excludes the very people the program exists to serve, and handing out referral letters simply moves the same appointment barrier to another building.
  2. The correct answer is A (Apply silver diamine fluoride to arrest lesions where restorative access is limited): silver diamine fluoride arrests active lesions in a single application, needs no anesthesia or handpiece, and suits settings where restorative care and reliable follow up are both unavailable. Placing interim restorations on every carious surface is unrealistic within one portable session for a mouth with rampant caries. A daily supplement depends on year long compliance and access to the prescription, and booking a hospital debridement repeats the model that already failed to bring guests through the door.
  3. The correct answer is B (Offer care in an upright position and let him control the pace): discrimination is itself a determinant of health, and whether a person returns to a service depends heavily on how he was treated the last time. Giving him control over position and pace restores safety without giving up the clinical encounter, and much preventive care can be delivered upright. Insisting on a reclined position turns a preference into a condition of care, rescheduling delays help he needs today, and handing him to another team member signals that his response was the problem.
  4. The correct answer is D (Those are process data, so outcome data on disease are still needed): counts of sessions, guests seen and referrals made are process measures showing that the program was delivered as planned. Whether untreated decay has fallen is an outcome question about health status and needs its own clinical measurement over time. A program can pass process evaluation and still fail on outcome, which is precisely why both are done. Rising attendance and referral counts describe activity, not disease change, so neither figure answers what the funder asked.
  5. The correct answer is C (The Act covers medically necessary physician and hospital care, not routine dental): the Canada Health Act sets the conditions provinces must meet for federal health transfers and applies to medically necessary hospital and physician services. Routine examinations, scaling, restorations, office extractions, dentures and orthodontics are not insured services under it, which is why dental care in Canada is financed mostly privately through employer plans and out of pocket payment. Dental care was not insured and then removed, and filing a tax return relates to the separate federal dental plan rather than to the Act.
  6. The correct answer is B (Maxillofacial surgery that must be performed in a hospital operating room): the narrow exception under the Canada Health Act is surgical dental treatment that must be carried out in a hospital setting, including care under general anesthesia when a client's medical condition requires it. Once that procedure is delivered as a hospital service it falls within medically necessary hospital care. Routine scaling, dentures for a person on social assistance and orthodontic treatment are all outside the Act, and where they are funded at all it is through a provincial program, a federal plan or private payment.
  7. The correct answer is A (Most private dental insurance comes through employment, so precarious work means none): employment matters twice in oral health, because work pays for care and because most private dental coverage in Canada is attached to a job. Part time, contract and gig arrangements rarely carry benefits, so the people with the least income also hold the least coverage, which is one mechanism producing the social gradient in oral disease. Insurers do not bar workers with several employers, no law prevents individual purchase, and provincial programs are targeted rather than automatic for anyone without a workplace plan.
  8. The correct answer is D (A social gradient, since disease increases stepwise across every income band): a gradient means the relationship runs across the entire income range rather than appearing only below a threshold, which is exactly what a stepwise rise through every band describes. The prevention paradox is a different idea, about most cases arising from the many people at moderate risk. A sampling artefact would not produce an orderly rise across all bands, and a high risk strategy is an intervention approach rather than a description of how disease is distributed in a population.
  9. The correct answer is D (Non Insured Health Benefits predetermination, administered by Indigenous Services Canada): the Non Insured Health Benefits program covers a defined range of dental services for eligible registered First Nations people and recognized Inuit, and eligibility is not limited to living on reserve. Many services require predetermination, where the provider submits a treatment plan for approval before proceeding, which is a recognized source of delay in care. The federal dental plan and employer insurance are different programs with different administrators, and no provincial ministry administers this federal benefit.
  10. The correct answer is C (First Nations and Inuit clients may qualify, Metis clients generally do not): the Non Insured Health Benefits program is for registered First Nations people and recognized Inuit, while Metis and non status First Nations people are generally not covered by it. Saying that all Indigenous people qualify would set up a Metis client for a refused claim, and saying that Inuit hold provincial coverage instead is simply wrong. Eligibility does not depend on living on reserve, so an off reserve address does not exclude anyone. Each client should still confirm current status and coverage directly.
  11. The correct answer is B (Partner with the community to set priorities before any plan is written): cultural safety and community partnership are the conditions under which any program with a First Nations community will work, and the caries burden carried by Indigenous populations traces to colonization and residential schools rather than to a shortage of pamphlets. Strengthening community action means the hygienist works with a community advisory process rather than arriving with a finished plan. Delivering a completed plan, mailing materials, or measuring satisfaction after the fact all keep decision making outside the community.
  12. The correct answer is A (High untreated caries, periodontitis and tooth loss, with heavy xerostomia): Canadian findings for adults experiencing homelessness show very high levels of untreated decay, periodontitis and tooth loss, alongside tobacco use, substance use and medication induced dry mouth that accelerates caries. Describing disease levels as similar to the general population would understate the need in a funding proposal. Caries is not low in this group, since cheap shelf stable food is often high in fermentable carbohydrate, and restoration counts are low precisely because treatment access is so poor.
  13. The correct answer is C (Structural barriers of physical access and inadequate appointment length): both reports describe features of the service rather than of the client, since the operatories could not manage a transfer and the appointment times offered were too short for the care she needs. These are recognized access barriers for people with disabilities, along with transfer equipment, sedation availability and provider discomfort. Nothing in the scenario points to income or insurance, she did not decline care but was unable to obtain it, and distance was never raised as the obstacle.
  14. The correct answer is B (Train group home staff and add documented mouth care to his routine): when a client depends on caregivers for daily oral care, the intervention has to reach the caregivers and be built into the daily routine so that it survives staff turnover and busy shifts. Adults with intellectual and developmental disabilities carry more untreated disease than the general population largely because this daily link is missing. Teaching a brushing technique to a client who cannot perform it, adding recalls, or leaving a pamphlet at the home all leave the daily care unchanged.
  15. The correct answer is D (It addresses workforce distribution, a supply barrier separate from coverage): the problem in communities with no resident provider is where the workforce is located, and independent or self initiated dental hygiene practice exists in several provinces in part to reach exactly these gaps. Portable equipment and travel put a provider where none was available. It does not create coverage for uninsured residents, it does not remove the need for referral when restorative treatment is required, and it does nothing by itself about language or cultural safety, which need separate attention.
  16. The correct answer is A (Geographic access and the distribution of the dental workforce): portable equipment, rotating clinics and teledentistry are the standard responses to remoteness and to a workforce concentrated in urban centres, because each one shortens the distance between a provider and a community. None of these measures alters income testing within a federal plan, none shortens a predetermination review at a federal benefits program, and none affects whether local offices are willing to bill a provincial fee schedule, since in this community there are no local offices at all.
  17. The correct answer is B (Coverage on paper does not guarantee access when providers decline participation): provincial and territorial programs often pay below the provincial fee guide, so some offices decline to bill them, and eligible clients are turned away despite holding coverage. This is one of the clearest examples of why coverage and access are not the same thing. There is no basis for assuming the client misunderstood her own enrolment, provincial programs are not obliged to match the fee guide, and an office choosing which plans it accepts is not in itself a breach of practice standards.
  18. The correct answer is C (Provider participation is voluntary, so she should confirm before booking care): participation in the federal dental plan is left to the provider, so a client should check that an office is participating before making an appointment. No regulatory body requires an office to accept the plan as a condition of registration, and billing is not limited to community health centres. Provincial colleges regulate practice, they do not decide participation in a federal benefit plan. Keeping the answer at this structural level avoids quoting figures that have changed during the phase in.
  19. The correct answer is D (Those details changed during phase in, so direct clients to official sources): eligibility ages, income ceilings, copayment tiers and covered procedures for the federal dental plan have all changed during the phased rollout and can change again, so a handout written from memory of last year's news will misinform clients about what they qualify for. Describing the structure of the plan is safe, quoting its numbers from memory is not. Nothing prevents a hygienist from discussing federal programs or copayments with clients, and no college approval is needed to hand out accurate information.
  20. The correct answer is A (Downstream treatment, midstream behaviour change, and upstream policy action respectively): the upstream metaphor places rescue of people already affected downstream, individual behaviour change midstream, and action on the social and policy conditions upstream. Restoring carious teeth treats disease that has already occurred, diet counselling aims at individual behaviour, and a municipal fluoridation bylaw changes the environment for everyone connected to the supply without requiring any individual effort or purchase. Grouping all three as midstream loses the distinction that makes the metaphor useful for planning.
  21. The correct answer is D (A small shift across everyone prevents more total cases than targeting few): this is the prevention paradox described by Geoffrey Rose. When most cases arise from the many people at moderate risk rather than the few at extreme risk, shifting the whole distribution slightly prevents more total disease than a large improvement in a small identified group, even though any one participant sees little visible benefit. The high risk strategy needs a screening step, reaches only those identified, and leaves the underlying cause untouched. Population measures also tend to narrow inequities rather than widen them.
  22. The correct answer is B (Toothpaste must be bought and used daily, which disadvantaged families sustain least): the equity argument is the strongest response to this objection, because fluoridation reaches everyone connected to the supply with no appointment, no purchase and no daily compliance, and its benefit is generally largest in the lowest income groups. Toothpaste does carry a much higher fluoride concentration than water, but a concentration comparison says nothing about equity and the added claim that toothpaste therefore works better ignores that its benefit depends on being bought and used every day. Fluoridation works mainly through a topical effect after eruption rather than only before it, and toothpaste retains a real benefit in fluoridated communities.
  23. The correct answer is C (0.7 mg/L is recommended, which sits well below the separate safety maximum): Health Canada recommends an optimal concentration of 0.7 mg/L for community water fluoridation, and a separate and considerably higher maximum acceptable concentration is set for safety. Confusing the two figures is a common error at public meetings and would suggest, wrongly, that the recommended level sits at the safety ceiling. The recommended level is national guidance rather than a figure that each province sets independently, and 1.5 mg/L is not the recommended operating concentration.
  24. The correct answer is A (Caries experience and treatment need generally rose relative to communities continuing): studies of communities in Canada and elsewhere that stopped fluoridating have generally shown a later rise in caries experience and treatment need in children compared with communities that continued, and several of those communities subsequently voted to resume. Cessation studies therefore give some of the clearest natural evidence available, so claiming none were conducted is false. Toothpaste has not replaced the benefit, and no consistent body of evidence shows caries falling after fluoridation stops.
  25. The correct answer is B (Fluorosis at that level is mostly very mild or mild, and aesthetic): an honest answer acknowledges that fluorosis does occur and then describes what is actually seen at the recommended concentration, which is predominantly very mild or mild fluorosis appearing as faint white flecks, an aesthetic finding rather than a functional defect. Denying that fluorosis occurs at all is inaccurate and damages credibility with an audience that can check. Moderate fluorosis is not the common presentation at this level, and Canadian data on fluorosis do exist.
  26. The correct answer is D (School varnish or mouthrinse programs, with supplements only after assessing sources): where fluoridation is unavailable, school based varnish and mouthrinse programs deliver a population level benefit, and supplements are prescribed only after all fluoride sources have been assessed, including the natural fluoride in each well. Prescribing supplements without testing the water risks excess intake in households whose wells already carry appreciable fluoride. High concentration home gel is not a general population measure for schoolchildren, and toothpaste alone leaves the group with the highest need least protected.
  27. The correct answer is C (Target whole schools selected by neighbourhood disadvantage rather than individual screening): targeting whole schools by neighbourhood disadvantage reaches high risk children more efficiently than screening every child, because it avoids the cost of screening the whole region and does not lose children at the referral and consent steps that individual invitation depends on. Screening every child then inviting the highest risk children adds a filtering stage that leaks participants. First come invitations favour the best organized schools, and dividing placements evenly ignores where the disease actually is.
  28. The correct answer is A (The screening indicates a probable problem and a full assessment is needed): screening is not diagnosis. A screening is a brief examination of an apparently well population, usually without radiographs and in poor lighting, and it produces a referral rather than a diagnosis. The parent must be told that plainly so she neither dismisses the note nor believes treatment has already been decided. Telling her decay is confirmed overstates what a classroom look can establish, calling the note a formality would discourage follow up, and waiting a year abandons a child who may need care now.
  29. The correct answer is A (Screening helps only when the referral pathway leads to accessible care): a screening program can perform well as a detection tool and still change nothing, because detection only helps when the people identified can actually reach care. With four in five referred families never seeing a provider, the failure sits in the referral pathway rather than in the screening instrument. Poor specificity would produce referrals for children without disease, which is not what was reported, and reducing the detection rate would simply find fewer of the children who need help.
  30. The correct answer is D (Use the clinical measures survey for disease, the health survey for barriers): national clinical measurements of DMFT, edentulism and periodontal condition come from a survey with a clinical examination component, while self reported insurance status and cost as a reason for avoiding care come from a national population health survey. Matching each question to the right instrument is the point. A population health survey cannot supply clinically examined disease data, provincial school screening produces local rather than national figures, and administrative claims describe billed services rather than disease or unmet need.
  31. The correct answer is C (Prevalence rises with age because past disease stays counted, while incidence fell): prevalence is the proportion with a condition at a point in time and it accumulates, because caries experience never leaves the count once it has occurred, so an older cohort will show higher prevalence regardless of what the program achieved. Incidence, seen here as the caries increment, is the number of new cases over a defined period, and it is what a preventive program actually changes. The two figures are therefore consistent rather than contradictory, and no repeat evaluation is needed.
  32. The correct answer is B (DMFT is cumulative and irreversible, so the score can only rise over time): DMFT records lifetime caries experience, counts each tooth once in the most severe applicable category, and can never fall, so it cannot demonstrate improvement from any program. A reversible index such as a plaque or gingival index is the right choice when the target is biofilm and inflammation. DMFT applies to the permanent dentition and so is appropriate for adults, the F component counts filled teeth rather than excluding them, and it can be scored without radiographs.
  33. The correct answer is A (The first is client level advocacy, the second is policy level advocacy): client level advocacy acts for the person in front of you, and helping someone complete an application to a public plan is a clear example. Policy level advocacy acts on the conditions that produced the problem, which is what submitting to a provincial standards review does, since a change there would affect every resident in the province rather than one client. The two levels are complementary, and the Ottawa Charter names advocacy as one of its core strategies, so both belong to the profession.
  34. The correct answer is D (Advocacy partners with those affected rather than speaking on their behalf): effective advocacy is evidence informed, partners with the community, and respects the priorities of the people affected instead of substituting the clinician's own judgment about what they need. Meeting the residents and their families first would make her submission both more accurate and more legitimate. No approval from the facility is required to speak publicly, holding a service contract does not disqualify a hygienist from advocacy, and individuals as well as associations may speak on care standards.
  35. The correct answer is C (Policy change is health promotion, the demonstration education, sealants prevention): the three terms are not synonyms. Health promotion is the broader process of enabling people to increase control over their health, and it includes policy and environmental change such as removing sugary drinks from vending machines. Health education is planned learning activity that builds knowledge and skills, which is what a brushing demonstration provides. Disease prevention is action aimed at reducing a specific disease, and sealants prevent caries. Grouping all three under one label loses the distinction that determines what a program should do.
  36. The correct answer is B (The Charter names advocate as one of its three core strategies): the Ottawa Charter for Health Promotion, adopted in Ottawa in 1986, names advocate, enable and mediate as its three strategies alongside five action areas, so speaking to decision makers is part of the professional role rather than outside it. Presenting local screening data to a council debating fluoridation is a recognized example of policy level advocacy for a hygienist. The Charter does not list five strategies, does not require elected office, and places no restriction limiting advocacy to national associations.
Case C042

A public health dental hygienist is assigned to a school district where the local health unit screening data show grade two students have almost twice the untreated caries of the regional average. The neighbourhood has a high proportion of families on social assistance, the municipal water supply is not fluoridated, and no school based preventive program currently exists. The hygienist has a small budget and one dental assistant for two days a week.

  1. The correct answer is B (Reviewing the health unit screening data and consulting the school community): needs assessment is the first stage of the planning cycle and gathers information before any decision is taken, using surveillance data, screening surveys, sociodemographic profiles, an inventory of existing services and consultation with the community. Ranking problems against staffing and budget is priority setting, which can only happen once the needs are known. Writing a percentage target with a deadline is an objective, which comes later still. Counting sessions delivered is process evaluation and cannot occur before the program runs.
  2. The correct answer is D (Normative need from the professional report and felt need from the parents): normative need is defined by professionals against a standard, so the health unit report ranking untreated caries highest is normative need. Felt need is what the community itself says it wants, so the parents naming cost is felt need. Expressed need is felt need turned into demand, such as a waiting list or requests for appointments. Comparative need compares this population against a similar one, which neither source here does.
  3. The correct answer is A (By June 2028, 70 percent of grade two students will have sealants placed): an objective must be measurable and time limited, stating who, what, how much and by when, and this statement names the population, the intervention, the level to be reached and the deadline. Improving overall oral health across a district is a goal, a broad statement of intent with no measure attached. Increasing awareness has no target figure and no date, so no reviewer could judge whether it was met. Delivering education as staffing allows sets no standard at all.
  4. The correct answer is C (Size and seriousness of the problem, intervention effectiveness, cost and feasibility): priority setting is the stage that ranks identified needs against available resources, and these are the criteria it uses. Sensitivity and specificity judge a screening instrument, not a list of competing problems. Counting delivery and attendance is process evaluation, which cannot happen before the program has run. Comparing what the community felt with what professionals judged describes the types of need collected during the needs assessment, not the ranking criteria.
  5. The correct answer is D (Formative evaluation, because it tests materials while the program is still developing): formative evaluation takes place during development, testing materials and procedures before launch so they can be improved. Nothing has reached a student yet, so no delivery can be assessed. Checking whether the program ran as planned and counting attendance is process evaluation, and measuring an immediate effect such as improved parent knowledge is impact evaluation. Measuring caries experience years later is outcome evaluation.
Case C043

A municipal council is holding a public meeting on whether to continue community water fluoridation, and a dental hygienist has been invited to present. Local screening data show that grade two children in the neighbouring town, which is not fluoridated, have a mean dmft almost one third higher. Speakers at the meeting raise mass medication, argue that fluoride toothpaste has made fluoridation unnecessary, and express concern that children will develop fluorosis.

  1. The correct answer is A (Fluoridation reaches everyone on the supply regardless of income, insurance or attendance): the equity argument rests on the fact that fluoridation needs no appointment, no purchase and no daily compliance, so its benefit is largest in the lowest income groups and it narrows the social gradient. Fluoridation does not remove the need for fluoride toothpaste, which remains a recommended daily measure. A cost comparison with a sealant program is a budget argument rather than an equity one. The claim that higher income families gain most reverses the actual pattern of benefit.
  2. The correct answer is C (Build healthy public policy, since the bylaw commits decision makers outside health): building healthy public policy puts health on the agenda of decision makers outside the health sector, and a municipal fluoridation bylaw is the standard example, alongside sugar taxation and a required oral care policy in long term care. Developing personal skills covers information and life skills for individual choice, which is where the presentation itself sits. Strengthening community action means supporting a community to set its own priorities. Reorienting health services shifts the care system toward prevention.
  3. The correct answer is B (Explain that fluorosis at this concentration is mostly very mild, faint white flecking): an honest answer acknowledges that fluorosis does occur and states that at the optimal concentration it is predominantly very mild or mild, appearing as faint white flecks that are an aesthetic finding rather than a functional defect. Claiming it never occurs is false and destroys credibility with an audience that can check. Calling it a functional defect needing restorative care overstates what is seen at this concentration. Avoiding the question leaves the concern to be answered by someone with no evidence.
  4. The correct answer is C (Caries experience in children generally rose relative to communities that continued fluoridating): studies of communities in Canada and elsewhere that discontinued fluoridation have generally shown a rise in childhood caries experience compared with communities that kept it, and several of those communities later voted to resume. The claim that nothing changed is not what the discontinuation literature shows. Increased toothpaste use has not been found to compensate for the loss. Resumption after a discontinuation vote has happened in more than one community.
  5. The correct answer is A (Fluoride varnish and school mouthrinse programs targeted at children at elevated risk): where fluoridation is absent the recognized alternatives are varnish programs, school mouthrinse programs, supplements prescribed only after every fluoride source has been assessed, and silver diamine fluoride where restorative access is limited. Prescribing supplements to every child without assessing well water and other sources risks excess intake. Telling families to buy bottled water shifts cost onto the households least able to pay, the opposite of an equity measure. Pausing prevention until a political decision changes abandons the children who are at risk now.
Case C044

A dental hygienist contracted by a long term care home finds that most residents keep many natural teeth, take several xerostomic medications, and depend on personal support workers for daily mouth care. Rampant root caries and denture stomatitis are common. The home has no written oral care policy and no admission oral assessment, and staff report that they were never trained in mouth care and have no time for it in the morning routine.

  1. The correct answer is D (Getting a written oral care policy and an admission assessment into the home): upstream work acts on the conditions producing the problem, and a policy plus a mandatory admission assessment changes the system for every current and future resident rather than for one person. Restoring existing lesions is downstream treatment of people already affected. An annual brushing demonstration is a midstream attempt at individual behaviour that leaves the daily routine unchanged. Fluoride varnish for residents with active lesions is useful but reaches only those already identified.
  2. The correct answer is B (Providing periodontal maintenance therapy for a resident with established treated periodontitis): tertiary prevention limits disability and restores function once disease is established, and periodontal maintenance therapy after active treatment is a standard example alongside dentures and complex restorative care. Quarterly varnish for residents with no lesions acts before disease occurs, so it is primary prevention. Admission screening detects disease early to limit damage, which is secondary prevention. Changing the beverage cart alters the environment before disease occurs, so it is primary prevention as well.
  3. The correct answer is D (Create supportive environments, because the daily setting itself is being changed): creating supportive environments changes the settings where people live and work, and stocking supplies at every bedside while writing mouth care into the unit routine changes the residents daily environment directly. Building healthy public policy would mean a policy adopted by decision makers such as the regional authority, not the supply of equipment. Reorienting health services shifts the care system toward prevention rather than toward more restorative treatment. Strengthening community action would require the residents themselves to set the priorities.
  4. The correct answer is A (Impact evaluation, because it measures an immediate measurable effect of the program): impact evaluation measures the immediate effects of a program, such as improved knowledge, sealants placed or reduced plaque scores. Plaque score is a reversible measure that responds within months, so it sits at the impact level. Counting how many training sessions were delivered would be process evaluation, which asks only whether the program ran as planned. Outcome evaluation measures long term change in health status, for example a reduced root caries increment several years later.
Case C045

A dental hygienist screens grade two students in a school where most families have no private dental insurance. One student is six years old. Four primary teeth have untreated cavitated lesions, three primary teeth have restorations placed because of caries, and two primary molars were extracted because of caries. Both lower primary central incisors exfoliated naturally and their permanent successors are erupting. The permanent first molars are all sound and unsealed.

  1. The correct answer is B (9, counting four decayed, two extracted for caries and three filled teeth): the dmft index sums decayed, missing and filled primary teeth, so four decayed plus two extracted because of caries plus three filled gives nine. A primary tooth counts in the missing component only when it was extracted because of caries and only in a child young enough that natural exfoliation is not expected, so the two incisors that shed naturally with successors erupting are excluded. Counting only decayed and filled teeth drops the two caries extractions. The permanent first molars belong to the separate permanent index and are sound, so they add nothing.
  2. The correct answer is D (The screening suggests a problem and a full dental examination is needed): a screening is a brief examination of an apparently well population to find people who probably have a condition, and it produces a referral rather than a diagnosis. The parent must be told this plainly so the family does not assume the child has been fully assessed or treated. Saying the child has been diagnosed misrepresents what a screening can do and may delay real care. Telling the parent that no examination is needed this year, or that the result cannot be shared, would both break the referral pathway that makes screening worthwhile.
  3. The correct answer is C (Whole school sealants in disadvantaged neighbourhoods, because that reaches high risk children efficiently): targeting whole schools by neighbourhood level disadvantage reaches high risk children more efficiently than screening every child, because it removes the screening step, the consent attrition and the cost of examining a whole region. Screening every child across a region is a high risk strategy rather than a population strategy, and it reaches only those identified who then attend. Sealants are primary prevention, since they act before caries occurs, and portable equipment places them successfully outside dental offices every day.
  4. The correct answer is A (The Simplified Oral Hygiene Index, because it is reversible and improves after treatment): reversible indices such as the OHI-S, the Plaque Index and the Gingival Index improve after treatment, which makes them usable for evaluating a program over time. DMFT is cumulative and irreversible, so a group mean cannot fall to demonstrate success. A falling dmft caused by exfoliation reflects normal development rather than any program effect. The Community Periodontal Index cannot describe attachment loss, since it records only the worst site in each sextant.
  5. The correct answer is D (Health promotion, because it changes the environment rather than only informing people): health promotion is the process of enabling people to increase control over their health, and it covers policy and environmental change rather than information alone, so removing sugary drinks from schools is health promotion. The classroom talk was health education, a planned learning activity that builds knowledge and skills. Secondary prevention detects and treats disease early, and tertiary prevention limits disability once disease is established, so neither describes changing what is sold in a school.
  6. The correct answer is B (The process of enabling people to increase control over their own health): this is the World Health Organization definition of health promotion, and it is broader than education or prevention because it adds policy, environments and community power. Planned learning activity that improves knowledge and skills defines health education instead. Action aimed at reducing the occurrence or progression of a specific disease defines disease prevention. Clinical treatment to restore function after disease is established belongs to tertiary care rather than to promotion.
  7. The correct answer is A (Secondary prevention, because early disease is detected and its damage is limited): secondary prevention detects disease early and limits the damage, and remineralizing a white spot lesion with fluoride varnish is a standard example alongside radiographic caries detection and scaling for early gingivitis. Disease is already present, so this cannot be primary prevention, which acts before any disease occurs. Tertiary prevention restores function after established disease, such as a denture or periodontal maintenance therapy. The fact that varnish is a therapeutic agent does not remove the activity from the prevention framework.
  8. The correct answer is C (A mouthguard program for a community youth hockey league before the season): primary prevention acts before disease or injury occurs, and mouthguards sit there alongside community water fluoridation, sealants and tobacco prevention. Radiographic detection of an interproximal lesion finds existing disease early, which is secondary prevention. A complete denture restores function after tooth loss and periodontal maintenance follows established periodontitis, so both are tertiary prevention. Only the mouthguard program acts before anything has happened.
  9. The correct answer is B (DMFT cannot fall, so a reduction in the group mean is not possible): DMFT is cumulative and irreversible, because a decayed tooth that is later restored simply moves from the decayed component to the filled component and stays in the total. The index measures lifetime caries experience rather than current activity, so a person who is caries free today still carries every past lesion in the score. Sealing a sound tooth removes nothing from the index either. The correct evaluation measure is the caries increment, meaning the new disease appearing during the program.
  10. The correct answer is D (11, counting three decayed, two missing from caries and six filled teeth): DMFT sums decayed, missing and filled permanent teeth, giving three plus two plus six, which is eleven. A tooth lost to trauma is not counted in the missing component, because only teeth missing because of caries qualify there. A sealant is not a restoration placed for caries, so a sound sealed tooth adds nothing to the filled component. Counting only decayed and filled teeth drops the two caries extractions and understates the score.
  11. The correct answer is A (28, one point for each tooth when the third molars are excluded): DMFT scores one point per tooth and counts each tooth once only, in the most severe applicable category, so the ceiling equals the number of teeth examined. Excluding third molars leaves 28 teeth and therefore a maximum of 28. The figure of 32 applies only when third molars are included in the survey. Surface based counting belongs to DMFS, a separate and more sensitive index, and the number of erupted teeth does not set a fixed ceiling of 20.
  12. The correct answer is C (Do not score it, because it exfoliated naturally and was not lost to caries): the missing component of dmft counts a primary tooth only when it was extracted because of caries and only in a child young enough that natural exfoliation is not expected. A lower primary first molar exfoliates at about nine to eleven years, so at ten years this loss falls within the normal range, and the erupting first premolar confirms that is what happened. Scoring it as missing would inflate the caries experience of the whole survey, which is why many surveys report dft instead. It cannot go into the permanent index either, since it is a primary tooth.
  13. The correct answer is D (Report the two indices separately, upper case permanent and lower case primary): mixed dentition is scored with both indices, DMFT in upper case for the permanent teeth and dmft in lower case for the primary teeth, and the two are never added into a single number. Adding them would combine dentitions with different tooth counts and different rules for the missing component, producing a figure that means nothing. Reporting only one of them discards real caries experience in the other dentition and makes the survey incomparable with published data.
  14. The correct answer is B (The first figure is prevalence and the second describes incidence over time): prevalence is the proportion of a population with a condition at a point in time, which is what the screening day figure gives. Incidence is the number of new cases arising over a defined period, which is what the 84 new lesions over twelve months describe. Prevalence answers how much disease exists now, while incidence answers how fast disease is appearing. A preventive program changes incidence, so the second figure is the one a program evaluation follows.
  15. The correct answer is C (36 percent, counting the ninety students with untreated cavitated lesions that day): prevalence is the number with the condition divided by the population examined at that point in time, so 90 divided by 250 gives 0.36, which is 36 percent. Adding the 40 students who have restorations but no untreated lesion gives 130 of 250, or 52 percent, which is the prevalence of any caries experience rather than of untreated caries. Restorations alone give 40 of 250, or 16 percent. The 160 students free of untreated caries give 64 percent, which is the complement of the answer rather than the answer.
  16. The correct answer is A (Past caries experience never leaves the count, so the index only accumulates): DMFT is cumulative and irreversible, so every lesion a person has ever had stays in the score, and the prevalence of caries experience therefore rises with each older age group even where prevention is working well. The rise is not evidence that prevention has failed. The measure a program actually changes is incidence, the rate at which new lesions appear. Prevalence counts all existing experience rather than only new lesions, so it cannot fall in the same cohort.
  17. The correct answer is D (Incidence, because the program reduces the rate at which new lesions appear): incidence counts new cases arising over a defined period, so a fall in the caries increment is what a successful preventive program produces and what an evaluation should measure. Prevalence of caries experience cannot fall in the same cohort, because past experience stays in the count permanently. Mean DMFT is cumulative and irreversible, and placing a sealant removes nothing from it. Prevention does not reverse tooth loss, so the edentulism rate cannot improve within the same population.
  18. The correct answer is C (2.0, the total of the four surface scores divided by the four surfaces): the Gingival Index of Loe and Silness scores four surfaces per tooth from 0 to 3, and the value for a tooth is the mean of those four scores. Here 2 plus 3 plus 2 plus 1 gives 8, and 8 divided by 4 gives 2.0. The raw total of 8 is not an index value, since the scale runs only from 0 to 3. Taking the highest single score is the rule for a sextant in the Community Periodontal Index rather than for this index, and four surfaces are examined per tooth rather than five.
  19. The correct answer is B (1.5, the sum of the tooth values divided by the six teeth examined): the whole mouth Gingival Index is the mean of the individual tooth values, so 2.0 plus 1.5 plus 1.0 plus 2.0 plus 1.5 plus 1.0 gives 9.0, and 9.0 divided by 6 gives 1.5, which sits in the moderate inflammation range. The raw sum of 9.0 falls outside the 0 to 3 scale and is not an index value. Dividing again by the 24 individual surfaces would repeat the averaging already done at tooth level. Taking the worst tooth value is not the rule for this index.
  20. The correct answer is A (Redness and edema with bleeding on probing at that gingival surface): the Gingival Index of Loe and Silness runs from 0 to 3, and a score of 2 is moderate inflammation with redness, edema and bleeding on probing. A score of 1 is mild colour change with no bleeding when the surface is probed. A score of 3 is severe inflammation with a tendency to spontaneous bleeding. A score of 0 is healthy gingiva with no change in colour, contour or texture.
  21. The correct answer is C (Score 1, because biofilm was detected only by running a probe across): the Plaque Index of Silness and Loe scores biofilm at the gingival margin from 0 to 3, and a score of 1 means no biofilm is visible but soft material is picked up when a probe is drawn across the surface. A score of 0 requires that no material be found even on probing, which is not what happened here. A score of 2 is a moderate accumulation visible to the unaided eye. A score of 3 is an abundance of soft matter filling the sulcus and covering the marginal area.
  22.
