The correct answer is D (3.0, a debris score of 2.0 added to a calculus score of 1.0): the OHI-S is the sum of two halves, the Debris Index Simplified and the Calculus Index Simplified, each the mean of six surface scores. The debris scores total 12, and 12 divided by 6 gives 2.0. The calculus scores total 6, and 6 divided by 6 gives 1.0. Adding the two components gives 3.0, and neither a raw total of all twelve scores nor a single component on its own produces a valid OHI-S value.
  23. The correct answer is B (0 to 6, because two components each scored zero to three are added): the OHI-S of Greene and Vermillion adds the Debris Index Simplified and the Calculus Index Simplified, each scored from 0 to 3 across six surfaces, so the combined index runs from 0 to 6. A range of 0 to 3 would apply to either component taken on its own. The six surfaces are the sites examined rather than a multiplier for the maximum, so 12 is not the ceiling. A maximum of 28 belongs to the DMFT and to no oral hygiene index.
  24. The correct answer is C (4, the highest code found, since the worst site represents the sextant): the Community Periodontal Index records one code per sextant and that code is the highest found at any site examined, so the code of 4 governs here. Averaging the site codes to 2.4 is not permitted, because these are ordered categories rather than a continuous scale. Taking the most common code or the lowest code would hide the deepest pocket, which is the finding the survey exists to capture. Recording only the worst site is also the main limitation of the index.
  25. The correct answer is A (It records only the worst site and cannot describe attachment loss): the Community Periodontal Index was built for treatment need surveys, so it records one code per sextant taken from the worst site, ignores recession and cannot describe attachment loss. Later World Health Organization methodology added a separate loss of attachment score for exactly this reason. The index examines selected index teeth rather than probing six sites on every tooth. Scoring inflammation on four surfaces of each tooth describes the Gingival Index, and calculus is captured by code 2 of this index.
  26. The correct answer is D (Code 3, with code 4 reserved for pockets of six millimetres or more): the Community Periodontal Index codes run from 0 for healthy, 1 for bleeding on probing, 2 for calculus or a defective margin, 3 for a pocket of four to five millimetres, to 4 for a pocket of six millimetres or more. A pocket of 5 mm therefore scores 3. Code 2 marks calculus or a defective restoration margin rather than any pocket. Code 1 marks bleeding on probing alone, with no calculus and no pocketing.
  27. The correct answer is B (High sensitivity, because the tool must detect the children who have the disease): sensitivity is the ability of a screening tool to detect the people who truly have the condition, and a program willing to accept some referrals of disease free children is deliberately favouring sensitivity over specificity. Specificity is the ability to exclude those without disease, which is what is being traded away here. Prevalence describes how much disease exists in the population rather than any property of the tool. Reliability describes agreement between examiners, which matters but is not what this choice turns on.
  28. The correct answer is A (Link families to funding programs and follow up on each referral made): screening helps only if the referral pathway leads to accessible care, so a program with high referral and near zero attendance has a pathway problem rather than a detection problem. Connecting families to provincial funding programs and the federal plan, and following up each referral, addresses the cost barrier that is actually stopping them. Raising the referral threshold hides the problem by referring fewer children who still need care. Repeating the screening or replacing it with a lecture repeats detection and information while the cost barrier stays in place.
  29. The correct answer is C (Targeting whole schools by neighbourhood disadvantage reaches high risk children efficiently): selecting whole schools by neighbourhood level disadvantage reaches high risk children more efficiently than screening every child individually, because it avoids the cost of the screening step and the loss of children at each stage of referral. School sealant programs are among the most cost effective preventive measures for children at elevated risk. Sealants are primary prevention, since they act before caries occurs rather than arresting existing lesions. Portable equipment is used routinely to place sealants in schools, and requiring a full examination first would defeat the outreach model.
  30. The correct answer is D (Permanent first and second molars, because their pits and fissures retain biofilm): school sealant programs target the permanent first and second molars, whose deep pits and fissures retain biofilm and account for most caries in school aged children. Permanent first molars erupt around age six and second molars around age twelve, which matches the age range of the program. Primary second molars exfoliate and are not the target of a program built around permanent teeth. Central incisors rarely carry retentive fissures, and sealing every permanent tooth is neither indicated nor affordable.
  31. The correct answer is B (The prevention paradox, where most cases arise from the many at moderate risk): the prevention paradox explains why a population strategy prevents more total disease than a high risk strategy, because most cases arise among the many people at moderate risk rather than the few at high risk, even though each individual gains very little. Choosing the high risk strategy for a larger individual benefit is the alternative being weighed and rejected here. Sensitivity falling as disease becomes rarer is a screening property with a different meaning. Comparative need is a category of need used during a needs assessment.
  32. The correct answer is A (It needs a screening step and leaves the underlying cause untouched): the high risk strategy is efficient for each individual it reaches, but it requires a screening step to find those individuals, reaches only the people actually identified, and does nothing about the conditions that produced the risk. It is usually cheaper per person helped than a whole population measure, so cost per person is not the weakness. It suits clinical practice well rather than being unusable there. Measures depending on individual effort and money tend to widen inequities rather than narrow them.
  33. The correct answer is D (Fluoridation narrows the gradient, since it needs no application or purchase): population measures that require no appointment, no purchase and no compliance narrow inequities, because they reach everyone connected to the supply and their benefit is largest in the lowest income groups. A voucher claimed through an online application depends on internet access, literacy, identification and time, so uptake is lowest among the families with the greatest need and the gap tends to widen. The two proposals therefore do not act equally on the gradient. Oral health is socially patterned rather than determined by biology alone.
  34. The correct answer is C (Restoration downstream, counselling midstream and the bylaw upstream in that order): downstream work rescues those already affected, so restoring a carious tooth is downstream treatment. Midstream work aims at individual behaviour, so chairside diet counselling is midstream. Upstream work addresses the policies and social conditions creating the risk, so helping pass a municipal fluoridation bylaw is upstream. Delivery by a hygienist does not make an activity downstream, since the level is defined by what the activity acts on rather than by who performs it.
  35. The correct answer is B (The Canadian Health Measures Survey, which included a clinical oral health component): the Canadian Health Measures Survey included a clinical oral health component that produced the first national clinical oral health data in decades, covering caries experience, edentulism and periodontal condition. The Canadian Community Health Survey collects self reported oral health, insurance status and cost barriers rather than examination findings. The Canadian Institute for Health Information reports health spending, including the private share of dental spending, and examines no one. The Canada Health Act is legislation rather than a data source, and dental care sits largely outside it.
  36. The correct answer is C (The Canadian Community Health Survey, because it collects self reported barriers and insurance): the Canadian Community Health Survey collects self reported oral health, dental insurance status and avoidance of care because of cost, which is precisely what these figures require. The Canadian Health Measures Survey provides clinically measured disease levels rather than reported barriers. Provincial school screening data are clinical and local, and they do not capture household insurance across the country. Enrolment counts for a single federal plan describe that plan only and miss everyone holding private or provincial coverage.
Case C046

Marc, aged 61, attends after two years away from dental care. He has smoked about 25 cigarettes a day since he was 20 and drinks five to six beers most evenings. He reports a sore spot under his tongue that has not settled for about seven weeks. Extraoral examination finds a firm, fixed, non-tender left submandibular node measuring about 2 cm. Intraorally there is a 14 mm indurated ulcer with rolled borders on the left lateral border of the tongue, beside 36 and 37.

  1. The correct answer is B (Grasping the tongue tip with gauze and drawing it forward and sideways): the lateral borders of the tongue are among the highest risk sites for oral squamous cell carcinoma and are also the most commonly under-examined, because the posterior lateral surface rolls out of view unless the tongue is held. Gauze gives the grip needed to draw the tongue forward and to each side so the whole lateral border can be seen and palpated. Simply asking the client to protrude the tongue leaves the posterior third hidden. Air drying the dorsum and bimanual palpation of the floor of the mouth both examine different surfaces.
  2. The correct answer is D (A node with malignant features requiring prompt referral for investigation): a node that is hard, fixed, non-tender, unilateral and persistent carries the classic profile of malignant involvement. A node reacting to infection is typically soft, tender, mobile and often bilateral, and Marc reports no recent infection at all. Healthy cervical and submandibular nodes are not normally palpable, so calling this a normal finding would be wrong. The node together with a seven week indurated tongue ulcer makes prompt referral the only defensible course.
  3. The correct answer is A (Colour, surface, consistency, border definition and whether it is sessile): a soft tissue lesion is described by location and side, size in millimetres, colour, surface texture, consistency, border definition and attachment, together with number and duration. Those descriptors let a receiving clinician picture the lesion without seeing it and allow accurate comparison later. Probing depths, mobility, plaque index and calculus distribution describe the periodontium rather than the lesion. Pain score, appetite and weight change are useful history but do not describe the lesion itself.
  4. The correct answer is C (Refer now for definitive diagnosis and biopsy without a further wait): the two week rule applies to removing an identified local cause and then re-evaluating. Here no local cause exists, the ulcer is indurated with rolled borders, and it has already persisted about seven weeks, far beyond that window. Smoothing 37 and waiting another two weeks or trialling a rinse only delays diagnosis of a lesion that already meets every referral criterion. Adjunctive light devices cannot rule out malignancy and never substitute for biopsy.
  5. The correct answer is C (Used together they raise risk by more than the two added separately): tobacco and alcohol act synergistically, so combined exposure produces risk greater than the sum of each exposure alone. Alcohol is an independent risk factor in its own right and also increases mucosal permeability to tobacco carcinogens, so calling it neutral is wrong. Nothing about alcohol reduces the carcinogenic effect of smoking. Marc's daily drinking on top of forty years of smoking places him in a markedly elevated risk group.
Case C047

Priya, aged 58, attends for a recall appointment. Her health history lists type 2 diabetes, hypertension and depression, and she takes metformin, amlodipine, hydrochlorothiazide and amitriptyline. Her most recent HbA1c was 9.4 percent and she wakes at night with a dry mouth. Blood pressure today is 186/104 mmHg in the right arm after five minutes of rest, repeated at 184/106 mmHg. Pulse is 88 beats per minute and regular. She has no chest pain, headache or visual change. Root surfaces are exposed on 36 and 46.

  1. The correct answer is A (Defer elective care today and refer her for prompt medical assessment): a repeated systolic reading at or above 180 mmHg, or a repeated diastolic reading at or above 110 mmHg, means elective dental hygiene care is deferred and the client is referred for prompt medical assessment, and Priya meets that threshold on her systolic reading of 184 mmHg. Because Priya has no chest pain, severe headache, visual change or neurological signs, this is not a hypertensive emergency, so summoning emergency medical services is not indicated. Proceeding with scaling, with or without anesthetic, exposes her to avoidable cardiovascular risk. A vague suggestion to see her physician at some point does not match the urgency of the reading.
  2. The correct answer is D (ASA III, so stress reduction and medical consultation are both indicated): ASA III describes severe systemic disease with substantive functional limitation, which fits poorly controlled diabetes together with markedly uncontrolled hypertension and breathlessness on one flight of stairs. ASA I is a healthy client, and ASA II is mild disease without functional limitation, so neither describes her. ASA IV means a constant threat to life such as unstable angina, and it defers elective care rather than refusing emergency treatment. Management at ASA III means shorter appointments, stress reduction and medical consultation, not a shorter appointment alone.
  3. The correct answer is B (Amitriptyline, a tricyclic antidepressant with strong anticholinergic activity): tricyclic antidepressants block muscarinic receptors at the salivary glands and are among the most strongly xerostomic drugs in common use. Metformin has no meaningful anticholinergic action and does not act on salivary secretion. Calcium channel blockers are associated with gingival enlargement rather than with duct closure, which is not a real mechanism. Thiazide diuretics can contribute to dry mouth through fluid loss, but they do not block acetylcholine receptors, so that stated mechanism is wrong.
  4. The correct answer is A (Risk of root caries related to xerostomia, evidenced by exposed roots): a dental hygiene diagnosis names an actual or potential problem within hygiene scope, states the related cause, and cites the evidence supporting it. Priya's exposed root surfaces on 36 and 46 combined with drug induced xerostomia make root caries a genuine potential problem the hygienist can prevent and monitor. Naming recurrent caries requiring a restoration is a dental diagnosis belonging to the dentist. Saying the client needs scaling and varnish names interventions, and restating her HbA1c is a medical finding rather than a hygiene diagnosis.
  5. The correct answer is C (Three to four months, because her caries and periodontal risk are high): recall intervals follow documented risk, and high risk clients, including those with poorly controlled diabetes, are seen every three to four months. Priya carries poor glycemic control, xerostomia, exposed root surfaces and widespread bleeding, which places her clearly in the high risk group. Six months is the moderate risk default and does not reflect her findings. Nine to twelve months suits low risk clients with stable tissue, and appointment convenience is not a clinical rationale.
Case C048

Devon, aged 34, is a new client. He reports a clicking jaw and morning tightness in his cheeks, and he grinds his teeth at night. Extraoral examination finds tenderness of both masseter muscles, a click on the right side at about 20 mm of opening, and maximum comfortable opening of 33 mm. The mandible deviates to the right during opening and then returns to the midline at full opening. Flat, shiny wear facets are present on the incisal edges of 13, 23, 33 and 43.

  1. The correct answer is B (Restricted opening, since adult range is roughly 40 to 55 mm): maximum opening in adults sits around 40 to 55 mm, and anything under 40 mm is recorded as restricted. Devon's 33 mm therefore falls below the normal range and, with bilateral masseter tenderness, points to a muscular component that must be documented. Nothing here suggests hypermobility, which would present as excessive opening rather than limitation. Opening is a routine clinical measurement and does not require imaging before it can be interpreted.
  2. The correct answer is B (Deviation to the right, the mandible returning to the midline): deviation describes a mandible that moves off the midline during opening and then comes back to it at full opening, which is exactly what Devon shows. Deflection describes a mandible that moves off the midline and stays there, and it carries different significance. Subluxation refers to the condyle translating beyond the articular eminence, which is not described here. Crepitus is a grating joint sound rather than a movement pattern.
  3. The correct answer is D (Attrition from tooth against tooth contact, accelerated by bruxism): attrition produces flat, shiny wear facets on occlusal and incisal surfaces that match precisely against the opposing tooth, exactly the pattern seen on 13, 23, 33 and 43. Night grinding accelerates the process. Abrasion from a toothbrush produces a horizontal cervical notch wider than it is deep, not incisal facets. Erosion produces smooth cupped surfaces and typically begins palatally when the acid is intrinsic, and abfraction produces a sharp wedge-shaped cervical defect.
  4. The correct answer is A (Document the findings, give self care advice and refer to the dentist): the restricted opening, bilateral muscle tenderness, right sided click and wear facets are recorded, and Devon receives advice on soft diet, jaw rest and parafunction awareness before referral. Appliance therapy sits outside her authorized scope in this province, so fabricating one would be practising beyond scope. Telling him joint sounds are always harmless ignores the restricted opening and the muscle findings. Periodontal care has no clinical reason to be delayed by his joint symptoms.
  5. The correct answer is C (Evaluation, because outcomes are being measured against the agreed goal): evaluation compares the client's current status with the goals set during planning and determines whether therapy achieved them. Re-measuring bleeding on probing at six weeks and comparing it with the agreed target is precisely that comparison. Assessment gathers baseline data, and although data are collected here, the purpose is outcome measurement rather than a fresh baseline. Implementation was the earlier delivery of instrumentation, and planning is the step that set the goal she is now measuring against.
  6. The correct answer is D (The hygienist records the objective findings and refers for dental diagnosis): naming a disease of the tooth such as caries requiring restoration or irreversible pulpitis is a dental diagnosis, and only a dentist makes it. The hygienist records what she observed, a deep occlusal lesion in 26 with a dentin shadow plus reported lingering cold sensitivity, and refers for diagnosis. Recording nothing until the dentist attends would leave a real finding undocumented, which is indefensible. Prescribing endodontic treatment sits well outside dental hygiene scope in every province.
  7. The correct answer is A (Review the history with him verbally, update it and have it signed): the health history is reviewed, updated and re-signed at every recall appointment, before any instrument enters the mouth. A verbal review often surfaces new medications or conditions the client did not think worth mentioning. Starting treatment first defeats the purpose of the review, which is to identify risk before care begins. Copying an entry forward documents a review that never happened, and treating a full history as an annual-only requirement is not accepted practice.
  8. The correct answer is A (Different reactions carry different risk and change the drug choices available): a delayed rash and airway swelling sit in different risk categories and lead to different decisions about analgesics, antibiotics and emergency preparedness. Recording the exact reaction lets the next clinician judge that risk rather than guess at it. The word allergy is legally recognized but clinically uninformative, so dismissing its legal standing misses the real point. A delayed rash is still a genuine adverse reaction and does not prove tolerance, and documentation never shifts responsibility for drug selection onto the client.
  9. The correct answer is B (Phenytoin, a long established anticonvulsant linked to gingival overgrowth): phenytoin is the classic anticonvulsant associated with firm, fibrotic gingival enlargement beginning at the interdental papillae and most visible in the anterior segments. Cyclosporine and nifedipine produce a similar picture but are not seizure medications, so they do not fit the history given. Amoxicillin, salbutamol and levothyroxine have no recognized association with gingival overgrowth. Meticulous biofilm control reduces the severity of the enlargement but does not prevent it entirely.
  10. The correct answer is C (Take a reading today, since anesthetic and an invasive procedure are planned): blood pressure is measured at the first appointment, at recall, before local anesthetic or any invasive procedure, whenever the health history changes and whenever symptoms are reported. Quadrant scaling under anesthetic meets that trigger at every appointment in the series. Relying on a baseline recorded weeks earlier misses any change that has occurred since. Waiting for symptoms or for the final appointment defeats the purpose of measuring before care rather than after it.
  11. The correct answer is D (Proceed with stress reduction, and refer him for medical follow up): readings from 140/90 to 179/109 mmHg usually allow care to proceed after the reading is repeated following rest, with stress reduction applied and a medical referral made. His repeated reading of 148/92 mmHg sits in that band and he is entirely asymptomatic. Emergency department referral belongs to a hypertensive emergency with symptoms, which he does not have. Saying nothing wastes a genuine screening opportunity, and deferring routine scaling for two weeks is not required at this level.
  12. The correct answer is B (ASA II, so care proceeds normally with routine monitoring only): ASA II covers mild systemic disease without functional limitation, and well controlled type 2 diabetes together with current smoking is the textbook example. ASA I is reserved for a healthy client with no systemic disease, so a controlled condition still moves the client out of that grade. ASA III requires substantive functional limitation, such as poor glycemic control or stable angina, which is absent here. ASA IV means a constant threat to life and would defer elective care altogether.
  13. The correct answer is C (Finger pads in gentle circular motions, comparing both sides): lymph nodes are palpated bilaterally with the finger pads using gentle circular pressure, which detects small nodes without causing pain or pushing them away. Comparing the two sides at once is what makes a unilateral finding stand out. Firm thumb pressure on one side at a time loses that comparison and is uncomfortable for the client. Sharp inward pressure toward the vertebrae risks injury, and a flat sweeping palm lacks the sensitivity to feel a small node.
  14. The correct answer is A (The Stensen duct opening opposite 17 and 27, draining the parotid gland): the parotid duct opens on a small papilla on the buccal mucosa opposite the maxillary second molars, which in FDI notation are 17 and 27. The Wharton duct drains the submandibular gland and opens in the floor of the mouth beside the lingual frenum, not on the cheek. The sublingual gland empties through several small ducts in the floor of the mouth. A normal papilla in this position is not a lesion and needs no referral.
  15. The correct answer is D (Floor of mouth, ventral and lateral tongue, and soft palate complex): these are the recognized highest risk sites for oral squamous cell carcinoma, and the soft palate complex includes the anterior tonsillar pillar and retromolar area. They also demand deliberate retraction, since they are the areas most often skimmed during a rushed examination. The dorsal tongue, hard palate and attached gingiva are comparatively low risk sites. The vermilion border and labial mucosa deserve examination but are not where most intraoral carcinomas arise.
  16. The correct answer is C (High risk human papillomavirus, especially type 16, drives oropharyngeal cancer): high risk HPV, and type 16 in particular, causes a growing share of cancers of the tonsil and base of tongue, frequently in younger clients with little or no tobacco history. That is exactly why screening continues for clients without the traditional exposures. Denture irritation and poor oral hygiene are not established primary causes of tonsillar or oropharyngeal carcinoma. Water fluoridation has no association with cancer risk at any site.
  17. The correct answer is B (The result does not change management, so referral for biopsy proceeds): adjunctive screening devices supplement the conventional examination and never replace biopsy or override a clinical finding. A three week ulcer on the floor of the mouth, one of the highest risk sites, has already outlasted the two week window and requires referral whatever the device shows. Treating a negative device result as proof of benignity is the exact misuse these devices invite. Operator certification does not change what the technology can and cannot rule out.
  18. The correct answer is D (Reassess in two weeks and refer if the ulcer has not resolved): where a plausible local cause is identified, the correct sequence is to remove the cause and then re-evaluate after two weeks. An ulcer that resolves confirms the cause, and one persisting beyond two weeks is referred for definitive diagnosis and biopsy. Smoothing the fractured cusp and then taking no further action leaves the outcome unverified. Size alone does not determine urgency, and a six month review is far too long a delay for an oral ulcer.
  19. The correct answer is A (Indurated, meaning the tissue feels hardened on palpation): induration describes abnormally firm, hardened tissue detected on palpation, and it is a concerning feature beneath a white patch on a high risk site such as the lateral tongue. Sessile and pedunculated describe how a lesion attaches to the underlying tissue rather than how it feels. Diffuse describes a border that blends into surrounding tissue. Consistency, attachment and border are separate descriptors, and each is recorded on its own line in the chart.
  20. The correct answer is B (3 mm round ulcer, yellow base, red halo, labial mucosa, 4 days): a defensible record states what was observed, with site, size, appearance and duration, so that another clinician can compare the lesion at a later visit. Writing a diagnostic label such as aphthous ulcer records a conclusion rather than an observation and cannot be checked against anything. Describing it as a small sore that appears insignificant is vague and dismissive. Recording possible early carcinoma states an unsupported conclusion that is neither observed nor within hygiene scope to diagnose.
  21. The correct answer is C (Just anterior to the tragus of each ear, bilaterally): the lateral pole of the condyle lies immediately anterior to the tragus, and palpating there on both sides through opening and closing detects clicking, popping, crepitus and tenderness. Palpating bilaterally allows the two joints to be compared directly. Placing fingers inside the ear canal is unnecessary, uncomfortable and no longer taught as routine. The angle of the mandible and the anterior border of the masseter are muscle landmarks rather than the joint itself.
  22. The correct answer is A (A fixed sequence makes it unlikely that any region is missed): a standard order for the extraoral examination, running from general appearance and skin through head, face, eyes, ears, nose, lips, joints, muscles, nodes, thyroid and salivary glands, is what stops a region being skipped when the clinician is rushed or distracted. Consistency also makes findings comparable between visits. No national body dictates a specific order as an examination requirement. A thorough extraoral examination takes minutes rather than seconds, and insurance reporting does not depend on the sequence used.
  23. The correct answer is D (Proceed with staged scaling and use local measures to control bleeding): a client whose INR is stable within the therapeutic range can receive non-surgical periodontal therapy without altering the anticoagulant, using staged treatment and local measures such as pressure, gauze and atraumatic technique. Stopping warfarin exposes him to thromboembolic risk far exceeding the bleeding risk of scaling. Requiring an INR below 1.5 would mean abandoning therapeutic anticoagulation altogether. Proceeding while ignoring any bleeding abandons the local control that makes the treatment safe.
  24. The correct answer is C (Oral candidiasis, favoured by local immunosuppression of the mucosa): inhaled corticosteroid deposited on the oral mucosa suppresses local immunity and predisposes to candidiasis, which commonly appears as palatal erythema with angular cheilitis, and rinsing after each dose reduces it. Gingival enlargement is linked to phenytoin, cyclosporine and nifedipine rather than to corticosteroids. Corticosteroids do not cause intrinsic enamel staining. Some inhalers are mildly acidic, but acid would produce smooth palatal erosion rather than the red sore patches and angular involvement described.
  25. The correct answer is B (Record the history and consult the physician before deciding on prophylaxis): current guidance does not support routine antibiotic prophylaxis for clients with prosthetic joints, but the decision rests with the physician or surgeon who knows the client's full circumstances, and that advice is documented in the record. Overruling the surgeon's earlier instruction on the spot is neither the hygienist's decision nor a defensible one. Dental hygienists do not supply antibiotics from an office stock in this situation. A prosthetic joint is not a reason to withhold dental hygiene care.
  26. The correct answer is A (Non-surgical hygiene care proceeds, while extraction of 47 carries higher risk): medication related osteonecrosis of the jaw is associated chiefly with procedures that expose bone, so extraction of 47 is the higher risk event and the one the dentist must weigh with her physician. Debridement and oral hygiene instruction are safe and protective, because good periodontal health reduces the chance of needing extractions later. Withholding hygiene care would raise her overall risk rather than lower it. Six years of oral therapy does add risk, so describing it as risk free is wrong.
  27. The correct answer is D (Bimanual palpation with a finger intraorally and one hand extraorally): the floor of the mouth is examined bimanually, with a gloved finger inside pressing down toward the fingers of the other hand placed under the chin, which is the only way to feel a firm submucosal mass or an enlarged submandibular gland. The floor of the mouth is a highest risk site in a long term smoker, so palpation is not optional. Visual inspection alone detects surface change only. Probing the mucosa and transillumination give no useful information about deep consistency.
  28. The correct answer is B (Book in the morning after he has eaten and taken his usual dose): a morning appointment following a normal meal and his usual insulin dose keeps blood glucose in a predictable range through a long procedure, and hypoglycemia is the risk being managed. Telling a client to omit insulin is a medication change outside dental hygiene scope and destabilizes his control. A late appointment after a missed meal is exactly the scenario that produces a hypoglycemic episode. Sugar is given to treat hypoglycemia, not routinely at the start of care.
  29. The correct answer is C (Provide care now, as the second trimester is comfortable and safe): dental hygiene care during pregnancy is safe, and the second trimester is generally the most comfortable period for longer appointments. Her bleeding gingiva is consistent with pregnancy associated gingivitis, which responds to debridement and improved biofilm control, so delay would allow the inflammation to worsen. Scaling does not trigger labour. Offering advice without instrumentation leaves treatable inflammation untreated, and the third trimester brings supine discomfort rather than any added safety.
  30. The correct answer is D (Generalized gingivitis related to inadequate biofilm removal, evidenced by bleeding): a dental hygiene diagnosis names a problem within hygiene scope, links it to a cause, and cites the evidence supporting it, which this entry does completely. Saying the client requires scaling and root planing names an intervention rather than the problem it treats. A chronic periodontal abscess associated with a root is a dental diagnosis made by the dentist. An instruction to refer for a restoration records an action taken, not a diagnosis.
Case C049

Priya is 29 and attends for a new client appointment. She reports acid reflux most nights for the past four years and takes a proton pump inhibitor only irregularly. She brushes twice daily with a firm brush and a whitening dentifrice, holding the brush horizontally and scrubbing hard. Examination shows smooth glossy cupping on the palatal surfaces of 11, 12, 21 and 22, shallow horizontal notches at the cervical of the buccal of 13, 14 and 15, and flat shiny facets on the incisal edges of 33 and 43. She reports sharp pain to cold air.

  1. The correct answer is C (Erosion from intrinsic acid reaching the palatal surfaces at night): erosion is chemical dissolution of enamel by acid of non-bacterial origin, and intrinsic acid from nocturnal reflux pools against the palatal surfaces of the maxillary incisors. The resulting surfaces are smooth, glossy and cupped rather than notched or facetted. Tooth against tooth wear would produce flat matching facets that key exactly into the opposing tooth, not shallow cupping in the palatal concavity where no opposing tooth contacts. A toothbrush cannot reach the palatal surfaces with the horizontal scrubbing action she describes.
  2. The correct answer is A (Toothbrush abrasion from horizontal scrubbing with a firm brush and abrasive paste): abrasion is loss caused by friction from a foreign object, and its classic sign is a horizontal cervical notch that is wider than it is deep with a smooth floor. Canines and premolars are hit hardest because they sit most prominently in the arch. A cervical lesion attributed to occlusal loading would instead be deep, narrow and wedge-shaped with a sharp internal line angle. Acid erosion produces broad shallow saucering rather than a defined horizontal notch.
  3. The correct answer is D (Fluid movement in patent tubules stimulating A-delta fibres near the pulp): the hydrodynamic theory is the accepted explanation of dentin hypersensitivity. A thermal, evaporative, tactile, osmotic or chemical stimulus moves fluid rapidly within open dentinal tubules, which distorts the odontoblast layer and fires the fast conducting A-delta fibres, giving a short sharp pain that stops when the stimulus stops. Nerve fibres do not run through enamel, so direct enamel stimulation is not possible. The odontoblast receptor idea has been tested and is not supported, and pulpal inflammation would give a lingering ache rather than a brief response.
  4. The correct answer is B (Delay brushing after a reflux episode and rinse with water first): enamel softened by acid is far more vulnerable to mechanical removal, so brushing immediately after an acid challenge strips softened mineral that saliva could otherwise have helped replace. Rinsing with water or a neutral rinse and waiting before brushing lets remineralization begin. Brushing more often or choosing a more abrasive whitening paste increases the mechanical loss rather than reducing it. Telling her to stop cleaning the palatal surfaces altogether trades erosion risk for biofilm accumulation and gingival inflammation.
  5. The correct answer is D (Dentin hypersensitivity at 13 to 15 related to tooth surface loss, evidenced by air response): a dental hygiene diagnosis names a problem within hygiene scope, states the cause, and gives the evidence, which is exactly the structure of this statement. Naming pulpitis is a dental diagnosis that only a dentist makes. Stating that the client needs a desensitizing agent names an intervention rather than a problem. Naming the reflux disease itself is a medical diagnosis outside dental hygiene scope, although the reflux is properly recorded as a contributing cause within the hygiene diagnosis.
Case C050

Marcus is 14 and attends with his mother for a routine appointment. He has a full permanent dentition apart from the third molars. On examination the mesiobuccal cusp of 16 occludes mesial to the buccal groove of 46, and 13 sits distal to the embrasure between 43 and 44. The same relationship is present on the left side. Overjet measures 7 mm with proclined maxillary central incisors, and overbite measures 5 mm. The mandibular midline sits 2 mm to the left of the maxillary midline. There is no crossbite and no open bite.

  1. The correct answer is A (Angle Class II Division 1 molar relationship with an increased overjet): when the mesiobuccal cusp of the maxillary first molar occludes mesial to the buccal groove of the mandibular first molar, the mandibular molar sits distal to the Class I position, which is Class II. Proclined maxillary central incisors with a large overjet place this in Division 1, while Division 2 has retroclined maxillary central incisors. A Class III relationship would place the maxillary molar cusp distal to the buccal groove. Class I would place that cusp directly in the groove.
  2. The correct answer is B (Overbite is vertical incisor overlap and Angle class is an anteroposterior molar relationship): these are two entirely separate measurements recorded separately in the chart. Angle classification compares the permanent first molars and canines in the front to back dimension, while overbite measures how far the maxillary incisor vertically covers the mandibular incisor. A client can have a Class I molar relationship together with a 7 mm overbite, so a deep overbite never confirms a Class II relationship on its own. Angle class is read at the molars and canines, not at the incisors.
  3. The correct answer is C (Horizontal distance from mandibular incisor labial to maxillary incisal edge, usually 2 to 3 mm): overjet is the horizontal measurement taken from the labial surface of the mandibular incisor out to the incisal edge of the maxillary incisor, and 2 to 3 mm is the usual adult figure. The vertical measure of how far the maxillary incisor covers the mandibular incisor is overbite, normally 1 to 2 mm, which is a different entry in the chart. Canine tip separation and midline separation are neither of these measurements and are recorded under their own headings.
  4. The correct answer is A (Mandibular midline 2 mm left of maxillary midline, no crossbite or open bite): a usable midline entry states which arch is displaced, the amount in millimetres, and the direction, so any clinician can repeat the measurement and detect change. Recording only that a discrepancy exists gives nothing to compare against later. Attributing the shift to the overjet is an interpretation rather than an observation and is not supported by the data collected. A comment about appearance on smiling records neither the amount nor the direction.
  5. The correct answer is D (A custom fitted mouthguard, since increased overjet raises the risk of incisor trauma): proclined maxillary incisors with an overjet of this size are poorly protected by the lip and are among the strongest predictors of traumatic dental injury in contact sport. A custom fitted guard follows directly from the occlusal findings and is within dental hygiene preventive counselling everywhere in Canada. A fluoride rinse addresses caries risk and a chlorhexidine rinse addresses biofilm, neither of which is the risk identified here. Reducing brushing force addresses abrasion, which was not among his findings.
Case C051

Elena is 47, smokes 15 cigarettes daily and has type 2 diabetes with a recent HbA1c of 8.4 percent. She last attended a dental office four years ago. Examination shows heavy white chalky deposit on the lingual of 33 to 43 and on the buccal of 16 and 26, dark flint-like deposit felt subgingivally at 36 and 46, and generalized red, rolled, shiny marginal gingiva with loss of stippling. Periodontal screening codes are 2, 3, 4, 2, 3 and 3, with an asterisk recorded in the lower right sextant.

  1. The correct answer is B (It is supragingival calculus taking its mineral from saliva at the duct openings): supragingival calculus is white to yellow and chalky, draws its mineral from saliva, and is heaviest on the lingual of the mandibular anteriors near the sublingual and submandibular duct openings and on the buccal of 16 and 26 opposite the Stensen duct. That is precisely the distribution described. Subgingival calculus is dark, dense and mineralized from crevicular fluid, so it does not match a white coronal deposit. Materia alba washes away with a water spray and pellicle is a thin invisible film, so neither forms a heavy chalky mass.
  2. The correct answer is B (Penetration into areas of cemental resorption and into small cemental tears): calculus attaches by adhesion to pellicle, by mechanical interlocking into surface irregularities, by direct contact between calculus crystals and tooth crystals, and by penetration into cemental resorption defects. Penetration is the hardest to resolve because the deposit lies within the cementum rather than on it, so complete removal means removing tooth structure. Attachment to pellicle is the most superficial mode and yields readily to instrumentation. Polishing grooves are not a recognized attachment mechanism, and the electrostatic description confuses pellicle formation with calculus attachment.
  3. The correct answer is C (Bleeding on gentle probing, the most reliable single objective sign of inflammation): bleeding elicited by gentle probing is the most dependable objective indicator that inflammation is active at a site, and it is measurable and repeatable across appointments. Colour, contour and texture changes support the picture but are subjective and vary between clinicians. Stippling is a normal variant that may be absent in perfectly healthy tissue, so its loss alone proves nothing. Rolled margins and a shiny surface are consistent with inflammation but do not carry the same objective weight as bleeding.
  4. The correct answer is A (Complete six-point periodontal charting, since a code 4 and an asterisk are present): screening is triage and is never a substitute for full assessment. Complete charting is indicated when code 3 appears in two or more sextants, when any sextant scores code 4, when any asterisk is recorded, and for any client with a history of periodontitis. Elena meets three of those triggers at once. Charting only the asterisked sextant leaves the code 4 sextant unassessed, and screening codes give a range for the worst site in a sextant rather than site specific depths, so they cannot substitute for charting before treatment.
  5. The correct answer is D (36, taking quadrant 3 as the lower left and counting back from the midline): FDI two-digit notation numbers the quadrants 1 for upper right, 2 for upper left, 3 for lower left and 4 for lower right, then counts each tooth from the midline back, so the lower left first permanent molar is 36. Quadrant 4 is the lower right, which makes 46 the wrong side of the mouth. The numbers 19 and 30 come from the American Universal system, in which 19 is the lower left first molar and 30 the lower right first molar, and that system is not used in Canadian charting and must not be mixed with FDI in a record.
  6. The correct answer is C (Do not force the explorer, since the tip can cavitate a remineralizable lesion): a sharp explorer forced into a questionable fissure can fracture the intact but demineralized surface of a non-cavitated lesion and convert it into a true cavity, and it can carry cariogenic bacteria from one site to another. It adds nothing to diagnostic accuracy over careful visual examination. Caries detection is primarily visual on a clean, dry, well lit tooth, supported by radiographs and by a blunt probe if tactile confirmation is genuinely needed. Rubber dam isolation does not make a sharp tip safe in an intact fissure.
  7. The correct answer is A (Code 2, managed non-surgically with fluoride, biofilm control and monitoring): under the International Caries Detection and Assessment System, code 1 is an enamel change visible only after air drying while code 2 is a distinct enamel change visible on the wet tooth with no breakdown, which matches this lesion. Codes 1 and 2 are managed non-surgically because the surface is intact and the lesion can still remineralize. A dark shadow from dentin would be code 4 and visible dentin in a cavity would be code 5, neither of which is present. Recording it as sound would ignore a clearly visible enamel change.
  8. The correct answer is B (Defective restoration margin at distal 37 acting as a biofilm retentive factor): an overhang is a plaque retentive factor that makes the site impossible for the client to clean, and shredding floss with a red bleeding papilla is the expected consequence. Recording the finding this way names the observable cause and points to the correct action, which is referral for correction of the margin. Blaming the client for not flossing ignores the physical obstruction that is defeating the floss. Recurrent caries has not been confirmed here, and calling a 1 mm overhang normal contact anatomy misses a genuine risk factor.
  9. The correct answer is D (Attrition from tooth against tooth contact, accelerated by his bruxism): attrition produces flat, shiny wear facets on occlusal and incisal surfaces that match exactly against the opposing tooth, and morning jaw muscle soreness points to nocturnal grinding as the accelerating factor. Erosion gives smooth, glossy, cupped surfaces without precisely matching opposing facets. Abrasion from a habitual object produces a localized notch shaped like the object rather than broad matched facets across six teeth. Occlusal loading lesions are cervical and wedge-shaped, not incisal.
  10. The correct answer is A (Wedge-shaped cervical lesion, etiology likely multifactorial, abfraction proposed but contested): the deep, narrow, wedge shape with a sharp internal line angle is the classic description attributed to abfraction, but the theory that occlusal loading flexes cervical enamel is contested and most cervical lesions are now regarded as multifactorial. Recording the shape and noting the uncertainty is honest and defensible. Naming toothbrush abrasion as confirmed contradicts a soft brush used with light pressure and the sharp internal angle. Erosion is not supported without any acid source, and a hard wedge defect is not root caries.
  11. The correct answer is C (The first indicates intrinsic acid and the second indicates extrinsic dietary acid): intrinsic acid from reflux, vomiting or an eating disorder reaches the palatal surfaces of 11, 12, 21 and 22 first, producing the localized palatal pattern described for the first client. Extrinsic acid from citrus, soft drinks, sports drinks or wine bathes the facial and occlusal surfaces more widely, matching the second client. Reversing the two would send the wrong client for medical assessment. The pattern is genuinely informative, so treating both as indistinguishable discards useful assessment data.
  12. The correct answer is D (Lesion localization exposes dentin, but lesion initiation requires tubules to stay patent): dentin hypersensitivity needs both conditions met at once. Localization means dentin is exposed by recession or by loss of enamel, and initiation means the tubules remain open to the pulp so fluid can move. A client with deep recession whose tubules have sclerosed or become covered by a smear layer feels nothing, while a client with shallow exposure and patent tubules can be very sensitive. That is why the amount of recession alone predicts sensitivity poorly. Caries is excluded before hypersensitivity is diagnosed, so it is not required for the response.
  13. The correct answer is B (Dental fluorosis, an intrinsic developmental change from fluoride during tooth formation): symmetrical, diffuse white flecking and faint striations affecting teeth that mineralized over the same period, in a child with lifelong fluoride exposure from water and dentifrice, is the classic picture of mild fluorosis. It is intrinsic and does not respond to polishing. Hypoplasia from a febrile illness affects only the teeth mineralizing at that time and usually appears as pitting or a defined band. White spot lesions of active demineralization cluster around biofilm stagnation areas rather than appearing symmetrically, and inherited enamel disorders affect the whole dentition far more severely.
  14. The correct answer is A (Grade III, more than 1 mm horizontally with vertical depressibility into the socket): mobility grading runs from grade 0 for physiologic movement only, grade I for up to about 1 mm horizontally, grade II for more than 1 mm horizontally, and grade III for more than 1 mm horizontally together with depressibility of the tooth in its socket. The vertical component is what separates grade III from grade II, and it is present here. Using two rigid instrument handles rather than a fingertip is the correct technique, since a finger pad compresses and masks real movement.
  15. The correct answer is C (Fremitus at 11 and 21, palpable vibration in function suggesting occlusal trauma): fremitus is vibration felt with a fingertip on the facial surfaces of the maxillary teeth while the client taps together and moves into excursions, and it points to occlusal trauma. Mobility is a different measurement made with the teeth apart using two rigid instrument handles, and these teeth were stable when tested that way. Recording a mobility grade would therefore misstate the finding. Dismissing palpable vibration as physiologic movement loses a finding that changes the occlusal component of the care plan.
  16. The correct answer is B (Class I canine relationship, recorded separately because molar class is charted on its own): a Class I canine relationship exists when the maxillary canine occludes in the embrasure between the mandibular canine and first premolar, which is exactly the relationship of 13 to the 43 and 44 embrasure. Canine and molar classifications are recorded as separate entries because they do not always agree, so a Class I canine relationship does not by itself establish the molar class. Class II places the maxillary canine mesial to that embrasure and Class III places it distal. Overjet and overbite are separate measurements and are not needed to classify the canines.
  17. The correct answer is D (Angle Class I molar relationship bilaterally, with an anterior crossbite at 22 and 32): the mesiobuccal cusp of the maxillary first molar occluding in the buccal groove of the mandibular first molar defines Class I, and that holds on both sides here. A single maxillary incisor sitting lingual to its mandibular counterpart is an anterior crossbite, which is recorded separately and does not change the Angle class. A crossbite is not the same finding as a Class III relationship, and an incisor sitting behind its counterpart is not evidence of Class II. Open bite means no vertical overlap at all, which is not what is described.
  18. The correct answer is C (Anterior open bite, since the opposing incisors have no vertical overlap): open bite is recorded when opposing teeth fail to overlap vertically in maximum intercuspation, and here the incisors do not overlap or touch while the posterior teeth occlude fully. Deep overbite is the opposite finding and describes excessive vertical overlap. An edge to edge relationship still involves incisal contact and would not be recorded as open bite. Posterior crossbite describes a transverse relationship of posterior teeth and has nothing to do with the anterior vertical finding described.
  19. The correct answer is A (Acquired pellicle, a salivary glycoprotein film that bacteria then attach to): the acquired pellicle is a thin, acellular, bacteria-free film of salivary glycoproteins that deposits on a cleaned tooth surface within minutes and cannot be rinsed away. It matters clinically because it protects enamel against acid while also supplying the receptors that oral bacteria use to attach, so it is the first step in biofilm formation. Dental biofilm by definition contains bacteria, which this film does not. Materia alba is displaced by a water spray and calculus takes days to mineralize, so neither forms in minutes on a polished surface.
  20. The correct answer is B (Materia alba, an unstructured accumulation of bacteria, proteins, cells and debris): materia alba is a soft white mass of bacteria, salivary proteins, desquamated epithelial cells and food debris that lacks the organized structure of biofilm, and its defining clinical feature is that a firm water spray displaces it. Dental biofilm is a structured community held in an extracellular polymeric matrix and is not removed by rinsing or spraying. Pellicle is a thin transparent film rather than a visible white accumulation. Calculus is mineralized and hard, so it cannot be washed off a tooth.
  21. The correct answer is D (It draws its mineral from gingival crevicular fluid and can occur at any subgingival site): subgingival calculus is dark brown to black, dense and flint-like, mineralizes from gingival crevicular fluid rather than saliva, and can form at any subgingival location independent of the pattern of supragingival deposit. That is why probing and careful subgingival exploration are needed rather than relying on what is visible. Saliva is the mineral source for supragingival calculus, which is white and chalky and clusters opposite the duct openings. The dark colour comes from blood breakdown products in the pocket and is not a drying artifact.
  22. The correct answer is A (Polishing removes the tea stain, but the darkened 11 is intrinsic and will not respond): extrinsic stain sits on the outer tooth surface within pellicle and biofilm, so tea, coffee and tobacco staining responds to polishing. Intrinsic stain lies within the tooth structure, and a single dark tooth after trauma reflects pulpal necrosis with blood breakdown products inside the dentin, which no amount of surface polishing will lift. Managing that tooth needs a dental assessment and possibly internal bleaching or restorative treatment. Telling the client that polishing will lighten the traumatized incisor sets an expectation that cannot be met.
  23. The correct answer is C (Black line stain from chromogenic bacteria, common in otherwise clean mouths): black line stain is a thin dark line following the gingival margin, produced by chromogenic bacteria, and it is characteristically seen in children and adults with good oral hygiene and low caries experience. It is extrinsic, so it responds to polishing, though it commonly recurs. Chlorhexidine staining is diffuse and generalized rather than a fine marginal line, and a rinse would be unusual in a 10-year-old. Root caries would be soft and cavitated rather than a smooth line, and tetracycline banding is intrinsic and runs horizontally across the crown.
  24. The correct answer is D (Record as physiologic melanin pigmentation, a normal variant with no disease meaning): diffuse, symmetrical brown pigmentation of the attached gingiva present since childhood in otherwise healthy tissue is physiologic melanin pigmentation, a normal variant seen commonly in people with darker skin. It is recorded as a baseline finding so that any future change can be recognized. It is not inflammation, since the margins are knife-edged, the tissue is firm and there is no bleeding on probing. Smoking related melanosis develops after smoking starts rather than in childhood, and biopsy is reserved for pigmented lesions that are new, focal, changing or unexplained.
  25. The correct answer is B (Long-standing fibrotic tissue can feel firm and still be diseased, so read bleeding alongside it): chronic inflammation lays down fibrous tissue, so gingiva can feel firm and look deceptively pale while disease is fully active underneath. Smoking compounds the problem because nicotine induced vasoconstriction reduces marginal redness and bleeding, masking the usual visual signs. Consistency must therefore be interpreted together with bleeding on probing, probing depths and attachment levels, all of which point to active disease here. Bleeding at 38 percent of sites alongside 5 mm pockets is not explained away by probing force or dismissed as historic disease.
  26. The correct answer is A (Code 3 for the sextant, since only the highest code per sextant is recorded): periodontal screening records one code per sextant, and that code is the highest found anywhere in the sextant, so a single site driving a higher code sets the score for the whole sextant. A partially visible colour band corresponds to code 3 and indicates a probing depth of 3.5 to 5.5 mm, since the band itself spans that range. Screening never records a code per tooth, which is what full charting is for. Code 4 requires the band to disappear completely, and code 2 reflects calculus or a defective margin with the band still fully visible.
  27. The correct answer is C (Complete six-point charting is indicated, because code 3 appears in two separate sextants): code 3 in two or more sextants is one of the standard triggers for full periodontal charting, alongside any code 4, any asterisk, a history of periodontitis, and any situation where a periodontal diagnosis with staging and grading is required. Two sextants at code 3 meets that trigger here. Waiting for a code 4 delays assessment of sites already at 4 to 5 mm. The absence of a previous diagnosis is not a reason to skip charting, and postponing to see the response to biofilm control leaves treatment planned on incomplete data.
  28. The correct answer is B (The two new carious lesions restored within the previous three year period): caries risk assessment separates disease indicators, risk factors and protective factors. Disease indicators are evidence that disease is already present or has been recently active, namely existing cavitated lesions, new lesions within the last three years, and white spot lesions. Recent restorations for new lesions fall squarely into that group. Exposed root surfaces and drug induced xerostomia are risk factors, meaning they raise the chance of future disease rather than evidencing current activity. Fluoride dentifrice is a protective factor that lowers risk.
  29. The correct answer is D (Current smoking, glycemic control, percentage of bleeding sites and residual deep sites): periodontal risk assessment weighs smoking, glycemic control in diabetes with HbA1c as the marker, previous attachment and bone loss relative to age, the percentage of sites bleeding on probing, the number of sites at 5 mm or more, tooth loss from periodontitis, and family history. Those variables predict further attachment loss and therefore set the supportive care interval. Appointment preference and travel are scheduling matters that do not alter biological risk. Toothbrush brand and restorative history are not established predictors of periodontal breakdown.
  30. The correct answer is C (Nine to twelve months, documented as a clinical judgment based on her low risk): recall frequency follows assessed risk, with low risk clients who have stable tissue typically seen at 9 to 12 months, moderate risk at 6 months, and high risk clients including treated periodontitis, smokers and those with poorly controlled diabetes at 3 to 4 months. This client meets every low risk marker, and the interval is recorded as a documented clinical judgment with the reasoning behind it. A three month interval applied to everyone is habit rather than assessment, a universal six month rule ignores individual risk, and recall frequency does affect outcomes for higher risk clients.
Case C052

A 52-year-old client attends for assessment after five years without dental care. He smokes 15 cigarettes daily and has type 2 diabetes with a recent glycated hemoglobin of 8.2 percent. Probing depths are 5 to 7 mm at more than half of the sites, interdental clinical attachment loss at the worst site is 6 mm, and radiographic bone loss reaches the middle third of the roots at 45 percent of root length at the worst site. Three teeth have been lost to periodontitis and tooth 46 has a Class II furcation. Bleeding on probing is recorded at 12 percent of sites.

  1. The correct answer is B (Stage III, because attachment loss, middle third bone loss and Class II furcation coincide): interdental clinical attachment loss of 5 mm or more, radiographic bone loss reaching the middle third of the root, and four or fewer teeth lost to periodontitis together define Stage III. The Class II furcation and the probing depths of 6 mm or more are complexity factors that also set Stage III, and complexity factors can raise a stage but never lower it. Stage IV would require five or more teeth lost to periodontitis, or findings such as masticatory dysfunction, bite collapse, drifting or fewer than 20 remaining teeth, and only three teeth have been lost here.
  2. The correct answer is D (Grade C, because smoking 15 daily and glycated hemoglobin of 8.2 percent modify upward): with no earlier radiographs the indirect method divides percentage bone loss at the worst site by age, and 45 divided by 52 is about 0.87, which on its own would give Grade B. Smoking and diabetes are grade modifiers and they can only raise a grade, never lower it. Smoking 10 or more cigarettes daily sets Grade C, and so does a glycated hemoglobin of 7.0 percent or more, so this client meets the Grade C threshold twice over.
  3. The correct answer is A (Ten millimetres, because probing depth and recession are added together at this site): when the gingival margin lies apical to the cementoenamel junction, clinical attachment loss equals probing depth plus recession, so 7 mm plus 3 mm gives 10 mm. Probing depth alone understates the destruction because it is measured from a margin that has already migrated apically. Subtracting the recession would badly underestimate the attachment lost at a molar that already carries a Class II furcation.
  4. The correct answer is C (Nicotine driven vasoconstriction suppresses bleeding, so the low score cannot indicate health): smoking causes vasoconstriction and gingival fibrosis, so the tissues can look tight and pale while attachment and bone loss continue underneath. A low bleeding score in a smoker is not evidence of stability, and this client already has 5 to 7 mm pockets, 6 mm of interdental attachment loss and a Class II furcation. Deferring active therapy on the strength of that score would allow further destruction.
  5. The correct answer is C (Debride thoroughly, reassess at four to six weeks, and refer for the furcation): non surgical therapy comes first, and reassessment at four to six weeks allows connective tissue to mature while the biofilm has not yet recolonized. Stage III disease with a Class II furcation and pockets of 6 mm or more persisting with bleeding is a recognized indication for periodontist referral. Placing a local delivery antimicrobial in every pocket at the first visit is not supported, and withholding therapy until smoking stops leaves active disease untreated.
Case C053

A 34-year-old client has implant supported crowns replacing teeth 36 and 46, both placed four years ago at another practice, and no baseline radiographs or probing records were transferred. She reports dull soreness around the left implant. At the 36 implant, probing depths are 7 mm mesially and distally, with bleeding on probing and a trace of suppuration, and current radiographs show 4 mm of bone loss around the fixture. At the 46 implant, probing depths are 4 mm with bleeding but no bone loss beyond expected initial remodelling.

  1. The correct answer is A (Peri-implantitis, since bone loss of 3 mm or more accompanies deep bleeding pockets): peri-implantitis requires bleeding or suppuration, increased probing depth and radiographic bone loss beyond initial remodelling. Where no baseline records exist, bone loss of 3 mm or more together with probing depths of 6 mm or more and bleeding supports the diagnosis, and this site shows 4 mm of bone loss with 7 mm pockets and suppuration. Peri-implant mucositis shows inflammation without progressive bone loss, so it does not fit.
  2. The correct answer is D (This is peri-implant mucositis, which is reversible with thorough biofilm control): mucositis is bleeding on probing with inflammation at an implant that has no bone loss beyond expected initial remodelling. It is the peri-implant counterpart of gingivitis and resolves once the biofilm is controlled, so the honest answer to the client is that the site can return to health. Bleeding around an implant is never a normal finding and should not be dismissed as expected.
  3. The correct answer is B (A plastic or carbon fibre sheathed tip with glycine or erythritol powder): metal powered scaler tips scratch and roughen titanium, so plastic, silicone or carbon fibre sheathed tips are used on implant surfaces. Sodium bicarbonate powder is moderately abrasive at roughly 40 to 65 micrometres and is limited to supragingival enamel stain, while glycine at about 25 micrometres and erythritol at about 14 micrometres are low abrasive and safe around implants and exposed roots. A stainless steel sickle would gouge the titanium collar just as a metal ultrasonic tip would.
  4. The correct answer is A (Progressive radiographic bone loss beyond the expected initial remodelling of the crest): both peri-implant conditions show bleeding on probing, so bleeding alone cannot separate them. Peri-implant mucositis is inflammation confined to the soft tissue with no progressive bone loss, while peri-implantitis adds radiographic bone loss beyond the remodelling expected after placement, usually with increased probing depth and sometimes suppuration. Probing depth and reported discomfort are supporting findings rather than the deciding one.
  5. The correct answer is B (Debride with implant safe instruments and refer for specialist peri-implantitis management): peri-implantitis with 7 mm pockets, suppuration and 4 mm of bone loss is a recognized referral indication, because non surgical debridement alone rarely resolves established peri-implant bone loss. Biofilm disruption with implant safe instruments and reinforced home care remain part of care while the referral proceeds. Reviewing in a year without radiographs would allow further bone loss, and systemic antibiotics are not definitive treatment for this condition.
Case C054

A 22-year-old university student attends without an appointment during examination week. She reports severe gingival pain that began two days ago, bleeding when she eats, and a foul taste. She smokes and has slept poorly for a week. Intraorally the interdental papillae of the lower anterior teeth are ulcerated and punched out, covered by a greyish pseudomembrane, and the breath odour is strongly fetid. She is afebrile, has no palpable lymphadenopathy, and probing shows no loss of clinical attachment.

  1. The correct answer is C (Necrotizing gingivitis, because necrosis is confined to gingiva with no attachment loss): rapid onset pain, punched out ulcerated papillae, a grey pseudomembrane and a fetid odour in a stressed, sleep deprived smoker are the classic features. Because probing shows no clinical attachment loss, the necrosis has not extended beyond the gingiva. Primary herpetic gingivostomatitis typically presents with fever and vesicles that coalesce into ulcers on several mucosal surfaces, and this client is afebrile.
  2. The correct answer is D (Gentle debridement, chlorhexidine rinse, analgesia and self care advice, without systemic antimicrobials): management of necrotizing gingivitis is gentle mechanical debridement, an antiseptic rinse and pain control, with smoking, stress and rest addressed as predisposing factors. Systemic metronidazole is reserved for fever, lymphadenopathy or malaise, and this client has none of those. High power full mouth ultrasonic debridement would be intolerable in acutely necrotic tissue, and delaying all care for a week leaves severe pain untreated.
  3. The correct answer is D (Fever, malaise or palpable lymphadenopathy develops alongside the gingival necrosis): systemic antimicrobials in necrotizing periodontal disease are indicated only when there is systemic involvement, signalled by fever, lymphadenopathy or general malaise. Persistent local findings such as continued bleeding, a lingering odour or sloughing of the pseudomembrane reflect the local healing process and are managed with continued mechanical debridement and improved biofilm control. Prescribing on local findings alone exposes the client to unnecessary antimicrobial use.
  4. The correct answer is A (Probing shows no clinical attachment loss anywhere in the affected anterior region): the necrotizing conditions are separated by how deep the necrosis extends, not by how painful or how sudden the onset is. Necrotizing periodontitis shows the same papillary necrosis but with clinical attachment loss and often crestal bone destruction, whereas necrotizing gingivitis stops at the gingiva. Fever and lymphadenopathy indicate systemic involvement rather than the depth of destruction, and a pseudomembrane occurs in both conditions.
  5. The correct answer is B (The crown margin has invaded the supracrestal tissue attachment below the sulcus): the supracrestal tissue attachment, historically called biologic width, is the junctional epithelium plus the connective tissue attachment beneath it, roughly 2 mm in total. A restorative margin placed within that zone produces persistent localized inflammation and eventually bone loss that does not respond to home care, which explains why the rest of the mouth stays healthy. The junctional epithelium does not migrate coronally to seal against crown material.
  6. The correct answer is C (Inflamed connective tissue let the probe pass beyond the junctional epithelium): in health the probe tip stops within the junctional epithelium, but where collagen beneath has been degraded by inflammation the tip passes into connective tissue, so the recorded depth overstates the histologic sulcus. As inflammation resolves the tissue resists the probe again and the recorded depth falls without any true attachment gain. Calculus deflection and probe angulation are real sources of error, but neither would correct itself simply because inflammation settled.
  7. The correct answer is A (Four millimetres, because the coronal margin distance is subtracted from probing depth): when the gingival margin sits coronal to the cementoenamel junction, as it does with drug influenced enlargement, clinical attachment loss equals probing depth minus the distance from the junction to the margin, so 6 mm minus 2 mm gives 4 mm. Recording the 6 mm probing depth as attachment loss would exaggerate the destruction, because part of that depth is swollen tissue sitting above the junction. Adding the 2 mm would exaggerate it further still.
  8. The correct answer is C (The ratio of RANKL to osteoprotegerin governing osteoclast maturation in the host): host cells express RANKL, which binds RANK on osteoclast precursors and drives them to mature, while osteoprotegerin acts as a decoy receptor and blocks that signal. Resorption tracks the balance between these two molecules rather than the quantity of biofilm present, which is why two clients with similar deposits can lose very different amounts of bone. Bacteria do not dissolve bone mineral directly, and matrix metalloproteinases are host enzymes that degrade collagen.
  9. The correct answer is B (Dysbiosis of the whole community initiates disease, but host response determines destruction): current understanding describes a shift in the balance of the entire subgingival community rather than the arrival of one pathogen, with the mature biofilm moving from gram positive aerobes toward gram negative anaerobes. Biofilm is necessary but not sufficient, because how much tissue is destroyed depends on the host inflammatory response shaped by genetics, systemic disease and behaviour. Removing a single species would therefore not cure the disease.
  10. The correct answer is D (Impaired neutrophil function and reduced fibroblast attachment to roots limit healing): smoking impairs neutrophil chemotaxis and phagocytosis and reduces the ability of fibroblasts to attach to instrumented root surfaces, so pocket reduction and attachment gain after debridement are consistently smaller in smokers. Surgical outcomes and implant survival are likewise poorer. Smoking does not thicken cementum, and endotoxin is superficially adherent and removable with light instrumentation rather than locked deep within the root.
  11. The correct answer is C (Glycemic control, not the diagnosis alone, modifies periodontal risk in both directions): poor control brings advanced glycation end products and impaired collagen turnover, while periodontal inflammation raises insulin resistance, so the relationship runs both ways. A well controlled client at 6.4 percent carries much less periodontal risk than one at 9.1 percent with the same diagnosis and the same duration. This is also why a glycated hemoglobin of 7.0 percent or more acts as a grade modifier rather than the diagnosis itself.
  12. The correct answer is B (Amlodipine, a calcium channel blocker associated with gingival enlargement in some clients): drug influenced gingival enlargement is associated with phenytoin, cyclosporine and the calcium channel blockers, particularly nifedipine and amlodipine, and it typically appears within the first year of use as firm fibrous papillary overgrowth. Biofilm worsens the appearance but does not by itself explain firm fibrous enlargement across both arches. Thiazide diuretics, statins and angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors are not recognized causes of this change.
  13. The correct answer is D (Chronic and aggressive periodontitis were retired, replaced by a stage and grade): the 2017 World Workshop classification removed the chronic and aggressive split because no reliable biological distinction between the two could be demonstrated. Periodontitis is now recorded as a single disease described by a stage from I to IV, an extent, and a grade from A to C. Her older report was reasonable for its time, but her current diagnosis should be restated in staging and grading language.
  14. The correct answer is A (Yes, interdental attachment loss at two or more non adjacent teeth defines periodontitis): the 2017 case definition is met by detectable interdental clinical attachment loss at two or more non adjacent teeth, and teeth 16 and 46 sit in different quadrants and are therefore not adjacent. The alternative route to the case definition is buccal or lingual attachment loss of 3 mm or more with pocketing greater than 3 mm at two or more teeth. There is no requirement for 5 mm of loss or for four affected teeth.
  15. The correct answer is D (Nine millimetres, found by adding the probing depth to the measured recession): with the gingival margin apical to the cementoenamel junction, clinical attachment loss equals probing depth plus recession, so 5 mm plus 4 mm gives 9 mm. Recording the 5 mm probing depth alone would hide well over half of the destruction, because the margin has already migrated 4 mm apically. Recession on its own also understates the loss, because it ignores the pocket lying below the margin.
  16. The correct answer is C (Attachment loss, bone loss and tooth loss set stage; smoking and diabetes modify grade): stage describes severity and complexity at presentation and is built from interdental clinical attachment loss at the worst site, radiographic bone loss and teeth lost to periodontitis, with complexity factors such as deep pockets and furcation able to raise it. Grade describes the rate of progression, taken from direct evidence over five years or from percentage bone loss divided by age. Smoking and glycated hemoglobin are grade modifiers that can only raise a grade.
  17. The correct answer is A (Stage IV, because bite collapse, drifting and fewer than 20 teeth raise complexity): the severity findings alone would place this client at Stage III, since attachment loss is 5 mm or more with bone loss into the middle third and fewer than five teeth lost to periodontitis. Stage IV is assigned when complexity factors such as masticatory dysfunction, bite collapse, drifting and flaring, severe ridge defects or fewer than 20 remaining teeth are present. Complexity factors raise a stage and can never lower it.
  18. The correct answer is A (Grade C, because bone loss divided by age exceeds 1.0 at the worst site): with no earlier radiographs the indirect method divides percentage radiographic bone loss at the worst site by age, and 42 divided by 35 gives 1.2. A ratio above 1.0 is Grade C, a ratio of 0.25 to 1.0 is Grade B, and below 0.25 is Grade A. The absence of smoking and diabetes means no modifier applies, but modifiers only raise a grade and could not pull this case back down.
  19. The correct answer is B (Localized, because fewer than 30 percent of the teeth show attachment loss): extent is calculated as the proportion of teeth affected, and seven of 28 teeth is 25 percent, which sits below the 30 percent threshold for generalized disease.
