The molar incisor pattern descriptor is reserved for loss confined to first molars and incisors, which does not describe a distribution spread across all four quadrants. The number of quadrants involved does not determine extent.
  20. The correct answer is D (Yes, this is health on a reduced periodontium; she remains a periodontitis client): periodontal health can be recorded on a reduced periodontium in a stable treated periodontitis client, defined by probing depths of 3 mm or less, bleeding at fewer than 10 percent of sites, and no progression. The diagnosis does not revert to health on an intact periodontium, because the attachment and bone already lost do not return and the risk does not reset. She therefore stays in periodontitis specific supportive therapy for life.
  21. The correct answer is C (Five millimetres, because attachment loss equals probing depth when the margin is level): clinical attachment loss is measured from the cementoenamel junction to the base of the pocket, so when the gingival margin sits exactly at the junction the two measurements coincide and a 5 mm probing depth is also 5 mm of attachment loss. Recording zero would ignore the pocket entirely, and subtracting a notional healthy sulcus depth is not part of the calculation. Probing depth is never doubled to obtain attachment loss.
  22. The correct answer is B (Localized biofilm induced gingivitis, because 10 to 30 percent of sites bleed): gingivitis is inflammation confined to the gingiva with no attachment loss, and it is classed as localized when 10 to 30 percent of sites bleed on probing and generalized above 30 percent. At 22 percent this client falls within the localized band. Periodontal health would require bleeding at fewer than 10 percent of sites, and periodontitis cannot be recorded because there is no interdental clinical attachment loss.
  23. The correct answer is A (A non biofilm induced gingival lesion such as lichen planus needing referral): lacy white striae with desquamative erythematous gingiva that persists despite excellent biofilm control and thorough debridement points to a non biofilm induced gingival disease rather than plaque induced gingivitis. That group covers genetic, infectious, immune, reactive, neoplastic, nutritional, endocrine and traumatic conditions, and none of them resolves with biofilm control alone. This client needs referral for definitive diagnosis, including biopsy where indicated.
  24. The correct answer is D (Repeated absence of bleeding predicts periodontal stability with high negative predictive value): the value of bleeding on probing is asymmetric. A single bleeding site is a poor predictor of future attachment loss, with a positive predictive value near 30 percent, whereas repeated absence of bleeding across successive visits predicts continued stability at close to 98 percent. Absence of bleeding does not prove that every deposit has been removed, and it must still be interpreted cautiously in smokers.
  25. The correct answer is C (Class III, since the probe passes through and through beneath intact gingiva): furcation involvement is graded by horizontal probing with a curved Nabers probe, and Class III describes complete through and through penetration while the furcation remains covered by gingiva. Class IV is the same through and through penetration but with the furcation clinically visible because of recession. Class I is incipient loss within the flute, and Class II is partial penetration into a cul de sac.
  26. The correct answer is C (Option C becomes: "Three entrances: the mesial approached palatally, the distal from either aspect, and the buccal buccally". The explanation becomes: "The correct answer is C (Three entrances: the mesial approached palatally, the distal from either aspect, and the buccal buccally): the maxillary first molar has three roots and therefore three furcation entrances. The mesial entrance sits about two thirds of the way toward the palate because the mesiobuccal root is broad, so it is reached from the palatal aspect, the distal entrance sits midway between buccal and palatal and can be reached from either aspect, and the buccal entrance is reached from the buccal. The mandibular first molar has two roots and two entrances, buccal and lingual. Attempting to reach every maxillary entrance from the buccal aspect would miss the mesial involvement entirely."): the maxillary first molar has three roots and therefore three furcation entrances, with the mesial and distal entrances reached from the palatal aspect and the buccal entrance reached from the buccal. The mandibular first molar has two roots and two entrances, buccal and lingual. Attempting to reach all three maxillary entrances from the buccal aspect would miss the mesial and distal involvement entirely.
  27. The correct answer is B (Grade 2 mobility with primary occlusal trauma on an intact periodontium): horizontal movement of more than 1 mm is Grade 2, while movement up to 1 mm is Grade 1 and vertical depressibility is required for Grade 3. Occlusal trauma is primary when excessive force acts on a periodontium with normal attachment and the ligament space widens without attachment loss, which fits a client who grinds heavily. Secondary occlusal trauma requires a periodontium already reduced by attachment loss.
  28. The correct answer is A (Five millimetres, since the coronal margin distance is subtracted from probing depth): when swelling places the gingival margin coronal to the cementoenamel junction, clinical attachment loss equals probing depth minus the distance from the junction to the margin, so 8 mm minus 3 mm gives 5 mm. Recording 8 mm would count swollen tissue sitting above the junction as destroyed attachment. Pregnancy exaggerates the inflammatory response to biofilm but does not change how attachment loss is calculated.
  29. The correct answer is D (Reduce probing force to roughly 0.20 to 0.25 N and walk the probe): excessive force is the commonest cause of readings that are too deep and of immediate heavy bleeding, and the target is about 0.20 to 0.25 N, roughly the pressure that blanches a fingernail. The probe is inserted parallel to the long axis of the root and walked in 1 mm steps around the full circumference. Spot readings miss the deepest point, and a larger tip diameter alters readings without correcting the underlying force problem.
  30. The correct answer is B (Subgingival calculus deflected the probe tip short of the true pocket base): an abrupt hard stop above the base of the pocket is characteristic of a ledge of calculus, and once the deposit is removed the probe reaches the true base and the recorded depth increases. Recognizing this prevents the later deeper reading being misread as new disease. Excessive force and inflamed tissue both produce readings that are too deep, which is the opposite of what happened at the first visit.
Case C055

Nadia is 47 and attends for periodontal therapy. She smokes eight cigarettes daily and takes no medications. Charting shows generalized probing depths of 5 to 7 mm with bleeding at 62 percent of sites, moderate subgingival calculus in all quadrants, and radiographic bone loss reaching the middle third of the roots. Teeth 16 and 26 have Class II buccal furcation involvement. She has never had periodontal therapy and asks how her appointments will be organized.

  1. The correct answer is B (Both approaches give clinically similar outcomes, so appointment length and client tolerance decide): full mouth debridement within 24 hours was proposed to stop untreated sites reinfecting treated ones, but trials show the full mouth and quadrant approaches produce clinically similar probing depth and attachment outcomes. The decision therefore rests on practical factors such as appointment length, client tolerance and case complexity, all of which Nadia can weigh with the hygienist. Claiming that one long visit reliably produces greater attachment gain overstates the evidence and would push clients into appointments they may not tolerate. Furcation involvement and a high bleeding score change the difficulty of the case, not the choice between the two scheduling approaches.
  2. The correct answer is D (Remove biofilm and calculus while conserving root structure, aiming for a clean surface): current practice has moved away from deliberate cementum removal because endotoxin is superficially adherent and comes away with light instrumentation. The endpoint is a biologically clean root rather than a maximally smoothed one, so slight residual roughness with no detectable deposit is acceptable. Planing to a glassy hard surface removes sound tooth structure needlessly and is a common cause of post therapy dentin hypersensitivity. Leaving deposit and relying on a local antimicrobial is also wrong, since chemical adjuncts never substitute for mechanical debridement.
  3. The correct answer is A (The lower shank is parallel to the distal surface being instrumented): an area specific Gracey is adapted by making the lower or terminal shank parallel to the surface being worked, which automatically brings the offset face to the correct working angulation against that surface. The lower shank is the reference point for both Gracey selection and adaptation, which is why the hygienist checks it before activating. A perpendicular shank would swing the blade away from the root and open the angulation past the useful range. The face itself is never held parallel to the tooth, since a closed face slides over deposit instead of engaging it, and handle position relative to the occlusal plane says nothing about blade adaptation.
  4. The correct answer is C (70 to 80 degrees, which lets the cutting edge engage the calculus ledge): the blade is inserted with the face nearly closed at 0 to 10 degrees so the working end can slip to the base of the pocket, then opened to a working angulation of 70 to 80 degrees for calculus removal. Below about 45 degrees the cutting edge slides over the deposit and burnishes it into a smooth layer that is then hard to detect and harder to remove. Above 90 degrees the cutting edge turns away from the root and gouges the soft tissue wall of the pocket. Keeping the insertion angulation for the working stroke would simply carry the blade over the ledge without engaging it.
  5. The correct answer is C (A slim curved tip at low power, moving the lateral surface in light sweeps): slim and curved tips at low power are designed for subgingival deposit, deep pockets and furcation entrances that a curette blade cannot enter, and the curve follows the furcation anatomy of tooth 16. The lateral surface of the tip does the work, so the end is never pointed into the root, and continuous light movement prevents the heat build up and root etching that a stationary tip causes. A standard beavertail tip at high power is a supragingival design and is far too bulky for a furcation entrance. A plastic sheathed tip belongs on titanium implant surfaces, and reducing water flow on a magnetostrictive unit removes the cooling those units particularly need.
Case C056

Marcus is 62 and returns for supportive periodontal therapy. He has an implant supported crown replacing tooth 46, glass ionomer restorations on the exposed roots of teeth 34 and 35, and generalized recession with brown extrinsic stain on the mandibular anteriors. His medical history includes hypertension treated with a thiazide diuretic and a sodium restricted diet. He reports sharp, brief pain when cold air reaches the buccal surfaces of teeth 34 and 35.

  1. The correct answer is A (Glycine powder, which is low abrasive and safe on roots and implants): glycine at roughly 25 micrometres is a low abrasive powder that can be used supragingivally and subgingivally, on exposed root surfaces, around implants and on restorative margins with minimal substance loss. That profile fits Marcus, who has generalized recession, glass ionomer restorations on exposed roots and an implant crown at site 46. Sodium bicarbonate at roughly 40 to 65 micrometres is moderately abrasive and damages cementum, dentin, composite and glass ionomer, and its sodium load also rules it out for a client on a sodium restricted diet, whether or not the pressure is reduced. Pumice in a rubber cup is likewise too abrasive for the exposed roots and the glass ionomer margins.
  2. The correct answer is D (Use a plastic or carbon fibre sheathed tip, keeping metal tips off both surfaces): metal ultrasonic tips scratch titanium implant surfaces and can damage or dislodge glass ionomer, composite and porcelain, so plastic, silicone or carbon fibre sheathed tips are used on these surfaces. Low power settings and extra water reduce heat but do not stop a metal tip from abrading titanium or a restorative margin. Ruling out powered scaling altogether for any client with an implant is unnecessary, since the correct tip material allows safe debridement of both the implant crown and the natural teeth. The precaution is about the material contacting the surface, not about avoiding the technology.
  3. The correct answer is B (Fluid movement in open dentinal tubules activates A delta fibres in the pulp): the hydrodynamic theory explains dentin hypersensitivity, where thermal, evaporative, tactile or osmotic stimuli move fluid within open tubules on an exposed root surface and stimulate fast conducting A delta fibres. Those fibres produce exactly the sharp, brief, stimulus bound pain Marcus describes, and the pain stops when the stimulus is withdrawn. Irreversible pulpitis produces lingering pain that outlasts the stimulus and often disturbs sleep, which does not match his history, and it has already been excluded on examination. C fibre activity gives dull aching pain rather than a sharp response to cold air, and ligament proprioceptors respond to pressure rather than to a thermal stimulus.
  4. The correct answer is A (It reduces nerve excitability and needs consistent daily use over two to four weeks): potassium nitrate works by nerve desensitization, with potassium ions raising the threshold at which the pulpal nerve fibres depolarize. That effect builds up only with regular daily use, so clients should be told to expect a gradual improvement over two to four weeks and to keep using the paste rather than judging it after a few days. Agents that occlude the tubules directly, such as stannous fluoride, arginine with calcium carbonate or a fluoride varnish, act more quickly, but potassium nitrate is not one of them. It has no antibacterial or remineralizing mechanism relevant to the sensitivity.
  5. The correct answer is B (Three to six months, because he remains a periodontitis client for life): a treated periodontitis client who now has shallow depths and low bleeding has periodontal health on a reduced periodontium, but the diagnosis and the risk do not reset. Supportive periodontal therapy intervals run from three to six months and are set by risk factors such as stage, grade, smoking, glycemic control, bleeding scores and residual pockets. Three months is the default for treated periodontitis, because subgingival biofilm returns toward baseline in roughly nine to eleven weeks. Stretching Marcus to a nine or twelve month interval on the basis of a single stable reassessment would allow recolonization and recurrence to go undetected, and a two month interval is shorter than any evidence supports.
Case C057

Priya is 39 and returns six weeks after completing non surgical periodontal therapy for generalized Stage III Grade C periodontitis. She does not smoke, and her glycated hemoglobin is 8.1 percent. Most sites now probe 4 mm or less without bleeding, but the distal of tooth 17 probes 7 mm with bleeding and suppuration, and the mesial of tooth 37 probes 5 mm with bleeding. Her self care is thorough and she has not missed an appointment.

  1. The correct answer is C (Connective tissue healing needs several weeks, so earlier readings understate the response): reassessment is scheduled four to six weeks after non surgical therapy because the connective tissue has not matured before that, so probing at two weeks passes into tissue that is still healing and makes the result look worse than it is. Delaying much further is also unhelpful, because subgingival biofilm steadily recolonizes over the following weeks and any residual disease is left untreated in the meantime. Local anesthetic has no tissue effect lasting weeks. Radiographic bone change is far too slow to be the reason for this interval, and it is not part of a routine four to six week reassessment.
  2. The correct answer is A (It is an adjunct to repeated mechanical debridement of this isolated residual site): local drug delivery places an antimicrobial such as minocycline microspheres, a chlorhexidine chip or doxycycline gel into a single pocket, and it is indicated exactly where Priya is now, at an isolated residual site of 5 mm or more that still bleeds at reassessment. It is always used alongside further mechanical debridement of that site, never instead of it, because the calcified deposit and the biofilm structure must still be disrupted physically. Placing an agent in every pocket that was deep at baseline treats sites that have already responded and is not supported. Reassessment is the point at which these agents become appropriate, not a point at which they are ruled out.
  3. The correct answer is D (Arrange referral to a periodontist, since deep bleeding sites persist after therapy): a site of 6 mm or more that still bleeds and suppurates after thorough non surgical therapy in an adherent client meets the criteria for periodontal referral, and Grade C progression and Stage III disease strengthen that indication. Priya's self care and attendance are good, so the residual defect reflects anatomy and disease severity rather than a correctable behaviour. Repeating the same non surgical approach every four weeks for six months delays definitive care while attachment continues to be lost. Placing a chlorhexidine chip and then waiting a year, or simply recording the finding until the next recall, both leave an actively suppurating deep site unmanaged.
  4. The correct answer is C (They are an adjunct in Grade C disease, not a substitute for debridement): systemic antimicrobials have evidence as an adjunct in Grade C or molar incisor pattern periodontitis in younger clients, where combined amoxicillin and metronidazole is the usual regimen, but they never replace mechanical disruption of the biofilm. Priya is 39 with generalized Stage III Grade C disease, so the discussion is reasonable, yet the deep site at tooth 17 still needs instrumentation and probably periodontal referral. Routine prescribing whenever a site bleeds at reassessment is not supported and drives antimicrobial resistance. Antibiotics are not an alternative to surgical management, and raised glycated hemoglobin is a grade modifier rather than a contraindication to antimicrobial therapy.
  5. The correct answer is D (Probing depths of 4 mm or less, no bleeding, and sustainable daily self care): the recognized endpoints of non surgical periodontal therapy are probing depths of 4 mm or less, absence of bleeding on probing, no suppuration, stable or improved clinical attachment, and self care the client can actually maintain. These are measurable at a four to six week reassessment and together indicate that the inflammation has resolved and the result can be held. Depths of 6 mm with bleeding at half the sites describe a case that has not responded and needs further therapy or referral. Sterilizing the root surface is not achievable or necessary, and radiographic bone regeneration is not an expected outcome of non surgical therapy alone.
  6. The correct answer is B (The lower shank, which is the reference for adaptation and for Gracey selection): the lower or terminal shank is the portion immediately adjacent to the blade, and it is the landmark clinicians use to judge adaptation and to choose the correct area specific Gracey for a surface. Making the lower shank parallel to the tooth surface establishes the correct relationship between the offset blade face and the root. The functional shank is the whole length of shank above the working end, which is a broader term and not the local reference point being indicated. The lateral surface is part of the blade rather than the shank, and a complex shank describes the number of bends for posterior access, not a sharpness reference.
  7. The correct answer is A (A sickle scaler suits supragingival deposit, since its pointed back can traumatize tissue): a triangular cross section with two cutting edges, a pointed back and a pointed tip identifies a sickle scaler, which is designed for heavy supragingival calculus and for deposit just under the gingival margin. The pointed back and tip would lacerate the sulcular epithelium if the instrument were carried deep into a pocket, so its use is deliberately limited, and this client's depths of 3 mm or less make it a suitable choice. Deep subgingival work calls for a curette with a rounded back and toe instead. The instrument described cannot be a universal curette or a Gracey, since both of those have a semicircular cross section with a rounded back.
  8. The correct answer is D (The universal face meets the lower shank at 90 degrees, the Gracey obliquely): the defining difference between the two designs is the angle between the blade face and the lower shank. A universal curette has a face perpendicular to the lower shank at 90 degrees, so both cutting edges are usable and one instrument can be rotated to serve the whole mouth. An area specific Gracey has an offset face meeting the lower shank at 60 to 70 degrees, which is why only its longer outer cutting edge is functional. The number of cutting edges follows from that offset rather than distinguishing it in the way described, shank rigidity is available in both designs, and the offset belongs to the Gracey and not to the universal.
  9. The correct answer is C (The longer outer edge, because the offset face makes only that edge functional): the Gracey face is offset, meeting the lower shank at 60 to 70 degrees, and that offset leaves only the lower or outer cutting edge, the longer and more curved of the two, in a position to adapt to the tooth at a usable working angulation. Using the shorter inner edge would place the blade at an angle that cannot engage deposit and would direct the cutting edge toward the soft tissue. Rotating a Gracey to use either edge is the behaviour of a universal curette, whose face is perpendicular to the lower shank. Proximity to the toe does not determine which edge is used, since adaptation is judged from the lower shank.
  10. The correct answer is B (Relax to a modified pen grasp, with the middle finger resting on the shank): the modified pen grasp places the thumb and index finger pads opposite one another on the handle while the middle finger rests lightly on the shank, where it senses the vibrations that signal subgingival calculus. A grasp that is too tight causes hand and forearm fatigue and dampens exactly that tactile feedback, which explains both of her problems. Increasing grip pressure would worsen the fatigue and the loss of sensitivity. A palm and thumb grasp is reserved for tasks such as sharpening rather than for detection, and moving the middle finger onto the handle removes the shank contact that transmits the vibration.
  11. The correct answer is D (An extraoral palm up fulcrum, resting on the lateral border of the mandible): extraoral fulcrums are specifically indicated for the distal surfaces of maxillary second and third molars, where an intraoral rest cannot give the reach or the wrist position needed. Resting the palm and fingers on the lateral border of the mandible on the working side stabilizes the whole hand and lets the wrist and forearm activate the stroke properly. A cross arch or opposite arch fulcrum extends reach within the mouth but still leaves the wrist compromised at this particular surface. A finger on finger fulcrum is a useful modification in other situations, and a rest in the opposite arch does nothing for maxillary posterior access.
  12. The correct answer is A (The toe of the blade was turned away from the tooth into the soft tissue): adaptation means keeping the lower third of the cutting edge, the portion nearest the toe, against the tooth surface as the blade travels over the changing root contour. When the toe swings away from the root it presses into the pocket wall, which produces the scratching sensation and the laceration at the margin. A working angulation of 75 degrees is within the correct 70 to 80 degree range, so the angulation is not the fault here. Keeping the lower third adapted is the correct technique rather than an error, light lateral pressure does not cut tissue, and a closed face on insertion is exactly what should happen before the stroke.
  13. The correct answer is C (A dull instrument used at an angulation closer to 45 degrees than to 80): burnished calculus is a smooth, hard, sheet like remnant that is difficult to detect with an explorer, and it is produced when the cutting edge slides over a deposit instead of engaging it. Both a dull edge and an angulation that has closed toward 45 degrees cause that sliding, and the sensation of the blade skating over the deposit is the classic warning sign. A sharp instrument at 80 degrees with firm controlled pressure is the correct technique for calculus removal and would have engaged the ledge. Angulations at or beyond 90 degrees turn the edge toward the tissue and cause trauma rather than burnishing.
  14. The correct answer is B (Firm controlled pressure to remove the calculus, then light pressure for final debridement): lateral pressure is matched to the task, with very light pressure for assessment and detection strokes, firm and controlled pressure to engage and remove calculus, and light pressure again for the final debridement strokes that leave a clean root without removing sound structure. That graded sequence protects the root surface while still lifting the deposit at tooth 46. Reversing the sequence and pressing hard for detection destroys tactile sensitivity and can gouge the root. Very light pressure throughout leaves the ledge in place, and unchanged heavy pressure through final debridement removes cementum needlessly and drives post therapy sensitivity.
  15. The correct answer is D (Activate by rotating wrist and forearm, using short overlapping strokes including horizontal ones): working strokes are generated by rotating the wrist and forearm as a unit rather than by flexing and extending the fingers, which fatigues the hand and produces uncontrolled movement. Strokes should be short, overlapping and controlled, and directed in vertical, oblique and horizontal directions, with horizontal strokes particularly valuable at line angles and in furcations, which is where this hygienist is leaving deposit. Continuing to activate from the fingers reproduces the original problem. Shoulder driven circular strokes give no control, and push strokes directed toward the base of the pocket risk driving deposit and debris into the tissue, since working strokes are pull strokes away from the pocket base.
  16. The correct answer is C (At 100 to 110 degrees to the face, finishing with a down stroke): with the instrument held stationary and a flat stone moved against it, the stone is angled at 100 to 110 degrees to the blade face, which preserves the internal 70 to 80 degree angle between the face and the lateral surface and keeps the original design of the blade. Finishing on a down stroke reduces the wire edge, and carrying the stone around the toe keeps the toe rounded on a curette. Holding the stone at 70 to 80 degrees or at 90 degrees alters the blade geometry and flattens the design. Finishing on an upward stroke leaves a wire edge that fractures off during use.
  17. The correct answer is A (Deposit is burnished into a smooth layer that is hard to detect and remove): a dull cutting edge slides over calculus instead of engaging it, smoothing the deposit into a burnished layer that an explorer struggles to find and that later instrumentation struggles to lift. That hidden deposit keeps the site inflamed while the chart records the surface as treated, which is why it matters most for the client's later care. The heavier lateral pressure and tighter grasp that a dull instrument forces also increase fatigue and the chance of slipping. Dull instruments remove less tooth structure rather than more, blades do not commonly fracture from dullness, and sharpening changes the cutting edge rather than permanently resetting the face to shank relationship.
  18. The correct answer is B (Magnetostrictive tips move elliptically, so all surfaces of the tip are active): magnetostrictive units convert energy through a metal stack or ferrite rod at roughly 18,000 to 45,000 cycles per second and move the tip in an elliptical orbit, which makes every surface of the tip active and gives the operator more freedom in adaptation. They also generate more heat, so adequate water flow matters. Piezoelectric units drive ceramic crystals at roughly 25,000 to 50,000 cycles per second and produce linear motion, so the lateral surfaces of the tip do most of the work and they run cooler. The two motion patterns are therefore reversed in the options that assign linear movement to magnetostrictive or elliptical movement to piezoelectric.
  19. The correct answer is B (Keep the tip at 0 to 15 degrees and keep it moving with light sweeps): powered scaler tips are adapted almost parallel to the tooth at 0 to 15 degrees so that the lateral surface contacts the deposit, and the tip is kept moving in light overlapping sweeps. A stationary tip concentrates energy on one spot, generates the heat this client is feeling and etches or grooves the root surface. Increasing the angulation toward 45 or 60 degrees begins to direct energy into the root, and 70 to 80 degrees belongs to hand instrumentation, where the blade face must engage calculus, not to a vibrating tip. Pointing the end of the tip into the root with firm pressure is the most damaging option of all.
  20. The correct answer is D (Defer the appointment, since aerosol generation is contraindicated with communicable respiratory infection): an active communicable respiratory infection rules out aerosol generating procedures such as ultrasonic scaling and air polishing, both to protect the dental team and other clients and because the client is unwell and unlikely to tolerate the appointment. Deferring and rebooking once she has recovered is the appropriate response, with a clear explanation of why. Switching to a piezoelectric unit does not remove the aerosol, and low power with high volume evacuation reduces but does not eliminate it. Air polishing produces its own powder laden aerosol and is contraindicated for the same reason, so it is not a safer substitute.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Consult the cardiologist, and use piezoelectric or hand instrumentation in the meantime): the historical concern was electromagnetic interference between magnetostrictive units and older unshielded pacemakers. Contemporary devices are shielded and the risk is minimal, but this pacemaker is 15 years old and its shielding is unknown, so consulting the cardiologist is the reasonable step while proceeding with piezoelectric or hand instrumentation, neither of which raises the same concern. Assuming that all devices are now shielded ignores the age of this one. Low power does not remove electromagnetic emission from a magnetostrictive unit, and withholding all dental hygiene care would leave periodontal disease untreated when safe alternatives are immediately available.
  2. The correct answer is A (Erythritol at about 14 micrometres, which is low abrasive and safe subgingivally): erythritol at roughly 14 micrometres and glycine at roughly 25 micrometres are the low abrasive powders validated for subgingival biofilm removal, exposed root surfaces, implant surfaces and restorative margins, with far less substance loss than traditional powders. That makes erythritol appropriate for residual 5 mm pockets at teeth 36 and 37 during supportive therapy. Sodium bicarbonate is roughly 40 to 65 micrometres and moderately abrasive, so it is limited to supragingival enamel stain and damages cementum and dentin, whatever particle size within that range is quoted. Pumice is far coarser again and has no subgingival application.
  3. The correct answer is D (Never aim the nozzle into an open wound or steeply into the sulcus): air polishing delivers powder in a pressurized air and water stream, and directing that stream into an open surgical wound or steeply into the sulcus can force air into the connective tissue and cause subcutaneous emphysema. Keeping the nozzle angled away from the tissue and away from any wound is the precaution that directly prevents this complication. Lowering the powder flow reduces abrasion but does nothing about the air pressure that drives the emphysema. Glycine is low abrasive but is still delivered under the same pressure, and holding the nozzle very close to the gingival margin increases rather than decreases the risk.
  4. The correct answer is C (It inhibits matrix metalloproteinases at a dose too low to affect bacteria): sub-antimicrobial dose doxycycline at 20 mg twice daily is host modulation therapy, inhibiting the matrix metalloproteinases that degrade gingival and periodontal ligament collagen while staying below the concentration that suppresses bacteria, which is why it does not select for resistance. It is used exactly in this situation, where destruction is out of proportion to the biofilm present and the response to debridement has been incomplete. The dose is deliberately not bactericidal, so any option describing an antibacterial effect misses the point of the therapy. It does not occlude dentinal tubules, and blocking RANKL as a decoy receptor describes osteoprotegerin rather than doxycycline.
  5. The correct answer is B (Gentle debridement, chlorhexidine rinsing, pain control, and referral for systemic metronidazole): punched out ulcerated papillae, a grey pseudomembrane, severe pain and a fetid odour describe necrotizing gingivitis, and management is gentle debridement, chlorhexidine rinsing and pain control, with review over the following days. Systemic metronidazole is added when there is systemic involvement, and this client has both fever and lymphadenopathy, so the hygienist arranges for prescribing according to the scope of practice in her province. Aggressive full mouth debridement in one visit is intolerable in acutely painful necrotizing tissue and would not address the systemic signs. Deferring all debridement leaves the causative biofilm untouched, and local drug delivery is not a treatment for necrotizing disease.
  6. The correct answer is A (Local delivery suits isolated residual sites still bleeding at reassessment, not routine placement): local drug delivery is an adjunct for the small number of sites of 5 mm or more that still bleed once thorough mechanical therapy has been completed and healing has been assessed at four to six weeks. Placing it in every baseline deep pocket on the day of debridement treats sites that will resolve on their own, adds cost and exposes the client to an antimicrobial with no benefit. Chemical agents also work best once the biofilm has been mechanically disrupted, so placing them beforehand is the wrong order. Local delivery never removes the need for reassessment, and Stage II periodontitis generally responds well to non surgical therapy.
  7. The correct answer is C (Refer to a periodontist, since these defects may need surgical or regenerative care): a furcation that a Nabers probe passes through and through is a Class III involvement, and a 4 mm vertical intrabony defect is a lesion that may be suited to regenerative treatment. Both are recognized indications for periodontal referral, even when the rest of the mouth has responded well to non surgical therapy. Continuing routine supportive care alone leaves two defects that hand and powered instrumentation cannot fully reach. Local drug delivery does not resolve an anatomical defect of this size, and extraction is premature, since a Class III furcation can often be managed surgically and the intrabony defect may respond to regeneration.
  8. The correct answer is D (Subgingival biofilm returns toward baseline in roughly nine to eleven weeks): three months is the default supportive periodontal therapy interval for treated periodontitis because the subgingival microbial community recolonizes toward pretreatment levels within about nine to eleven weeks, so a visit at three months interrupts that regrowth before it can drive renewed attachment loss. This client also smokes and has Grade C disease, both of which argue for the shorter end of the three to six month range. Intervals are set by risk and by clinical findings rather than by insurance rules. Attachment can be measured accurately from four weeks onward, and calculus mineralization has no fixed three month timetable.
  9. The correct answer is B (Rule out caries, cracks, defective restorations and pulpitis, since the diagnosis is exclusionary): dentin hypersensitivity is a diagnosis of exclusion, so caries, a cracked tooth, a defective restoration and pulpitis must all be ruled out before the sharp cold response at tooth 15 is attributed to open dentinal tubules. Treating with a desensitizing agent first would mask the symptom of a condition that needs restorative or endodontic care and delay that treatment. Fluoride varnish gives symptom relief in several conditions, so a response to it confirms nothing diagnostically. Radiographs support the exclusion process but cannot show hypersensitivity, and a vital pulp is consistent with several diagnoses rather than confirming this one.
  10. The correct answer is A (Delay brushing after acidic drinks, reduce dietary acid, and brush gently with soft bristles): behavioural change is part of managing dentin hypersensitivity alongside the desensitizing agent, because dietary acid opens and keeps open the dentinal tubules while forceful brushing with a hard brush abrades softened dentin and drives further recession at teeth 13 and 23. Reducing the acid, waiting before brushing after acidic intake and switching to gentle brushing with soft bristles all address the cause rather than the symptom. Brushing immediately after juice with a hard brush removes softened surface dentin and is the most damaging option offered. An acidic mouthwash adds to the acid challenge, and stopping brushing entirely allows biofilm to accumulate on already exposed roots.
Case C058

A dental hygienist works alone in a rural clinic with a wall mounted intraoral x-ray unit in a small operatory. There is no fixed lead barrier, and the exposure switch hangs on a short coiled cord. A 7-year-old client with a history of proximal caries is scheduled for two posterior bitewings. The child is anxious and fidgety, and the accompanying parent has offered to help in any way that is needed.

  1. The correct answer is B (At least two metres from the tube head, at 90 to 135 degrees to the beam): distance is the cheapest and most effective protection an operator has, because intensity falls with the square of the distance from the source. The zone between 90 and 135 degrees to the primary beam is the region where scattered radiation is lowest. Standing one metre behind the tube head keeps her far too close to the source and is not an accepted substitute. Steadying the receptor with a finger places her hand in the primary beam and is never done.
  2. The correct answer is C (The parent, if not pregnant, holds the receptor while wearing lead protection): when a holding device has genuinely failed, a non pregnant accompanying adult may support the receptor while wearing a lead apron and thyroid collar. The operator never holds a receptor in a client's mouth, because she would be exposed repeatedly across every client of her career while the parent is exposed once. Taping the receptor to the cheek cannot position it against the teeth and would produce an unreadable image. Steadying the tube head by hand is prohibited for the same cumulative exposure reason.
  3. The correct answer is A (Arrange a longer cord so exposures are made from at least two metres): the equipment must let the operator reach the required protective position, so the deficiency is in the cord rather than in her technique. Buying a longer cord or a remote switch is a straightforward fix that restores the two metre standing distance. Dental scatter at 1.2 metres is measurable and accumulates across a working life, so continuing as before is not defensible. A lead apron on the operator does not replace distance, and lowering the milliamperage would only produce underexposed images that must be retaken.
  4. The correct answer is C (She should obtain a personal dosimeter and wear it as her province requires): personal dosimetry is required for dental x-ray workers in most Canadian jurisdictions and is the only way occupational dose is actually documented rather than assumed. The badge is worn at trunk or collar level on the outside of any protective apron, so that it records what reaches the wearer. Placing it under an apron would record an artificially low reading and defeat the purpose of monitoring. A low weekly image count does not exempt an operator, and a badge measures dose rather than preventing it, so distance and barriers still apply.
  5. The correct answer is D (The child's caries risk together with the history and clinical findings): radiographs are prescribed for an individual after a history and clinical examination, never on a fixed calendar. This child has a documented history of proximal caries, so his individual risk is what supports exposing bitewings today. A blanket clinic policy of images at every recall exposes low risk children to radiation that will not change their care and is a standards violation in most provinces. Elapsed time and a parent's request are both considered, but neither one on its own justifies an exposure.
Case C059

A 34-year-old client transfers to a new practice. He has no restorations, no probing depth over 3 mm, no bleeding on probing, a low sugar diet and fluoridated drinking water. His last bitewings were taken 18 months ago at his previous office and are held there electronically. The new practice has a written policy of four bitewings for every adult every twelve months. He asks the hygienist whether he really needs images today.

  1. The correct answer is B (His caries risk, assessed from the health history and clinical examination): selection criteria require the prescribing decision to follow an individual assessment, and this client shows no caries, no periodontal breakdown and a low risk dietary and fluoride profile. Exposing four bitewings on a client like this is unlikely to change his care. A written policy applied to every adult regardless of findings is exactly the routine time based prescribing that cannot be justified. An insurance allowance describes what will be paid for and never establishes that an exposure is clinically warranted.
  2. The correct answer is D (The previous images are requested and reviewed before any new exposure): justification means the exposure must do more good than harm for this individual, and an exposure that duplicates an image already available fails that test. Existing images from another office are part of his health record and are obtained with his signed release. Reviewing them may answer the clinical question outright or may show a change that supports new images. Refusing to accept outside images or making new exposures conditional on payment both put convenience and revenue ahead of the client's dose.
  3. The correct answer is A (As guidance applied to this client, not an automatic schedule to follow): published selection criteria give intervals by age and risk category, and they are written as guidance for a clinician to apply to a particular case. Risk can change between visits, so an interval that suited a client two years ago may be wrong today. Treating a published interval as a fixed rule reproduces the same calendar based prescribing that selection criteria were designed to replace. Reimbursement rules come from the carrier and have no bearing on whether an exposure is clinically justified.
  4. The correct answer is A (His caries risk has risen, so a shorter bitewing interval is justified): two new cavitated proximal lesions, a high frequency sugar habit and visible plaque move him from low to high caries risk, and selection criteria shorten the bitewing interval as risk rises. The prescribing decision is driven by the current assessment, not by the interval that applied when he was caries free. Age alone never sets the interval. A panoramic image has too little resolution for interproximal caries, and cone beam imaging is not used for caries detection and carries a much higher dose.
  5. The correct answer is C (Less than one day of the natural background radiation Canadians receive): four bitewings with a digital receptor and rectangular collimation deliver roughly 3 to 5 microsieverts, while natural background radiation in Canada averages about 6 microsieverts each day. Framing the dose against everyday background gives the client a figure he can actually weigh. A chest computed tomography scan delivers a dose several thousand times larger, so that comparison would badly mislead him. Digital sensors reduce dose substantially but they still use ionizing radiation, so claiming no exposure at all is false.
Case C060

A practice has replaced D speed film with direct digital sensors and has fitted a rectangular collimator to its intraoral unit. A 52-year-old client with a strong gag reflex cannot tolerate the rigid sensor in the posterior maxilla. Later the same day, the office manager asks the hygienist to edit one of this client's bitewings so that an open margin on an existing restoration is no longer visible, before the image is submitted to the insurance carrier.

  1. The correct answer is D (A photostimulable phosphor plate, which is thin, flexible and film sized): a phosphor plate has the dimensions and flexibility of a film packet, so it seats in the palate far more comfortably than a rigid sensor and is the standard solution for a client who cannot tolerate one. The trade off is a scanning step and the need to protect the plate from light. D speed film would solve the comfort problem but at the highest client dose of any available receptor. An occlusal receptor cannot show open proximal contacts, and a panoramic image lacks the resolution for interproximal caries.
  2. The correct answer is B (Both reduce dose, by shrinking the exposed area and needing less exposure): rectangular collimation shapes the beam to the receptor and roughly halves the area of tissue irradiated, while a digital sensor is a faster receptor that needs substantially less exposure than D speed film. The two changes act on different parts of the problem and their benefits add together. Kilovoltage controls penetration and contrast, so leaving it unchanged does not cancel either dose saving. Rectangular collimation does demand more careful alignment, but with an aiming device the retake rate stays low and the net effect on dose remains a large reduction.
  3. The correct answer is C (Refuse, because altering an image to misrepresent findings to a payer is fraud): reasonable adjustment of brightness, contrast and magnification is acceptable, but editing an image so that a real finding disappears misrepresents the client's condition and is fraud when it is submitted to a third party payer. The purpose of the edit, not the software function used, is what makes it wrong here. Keeping the original file or noting who asked for the change does not cure a deliberate misrepresentation. The correct route is to submit the image as exposed and to appeal the claim on its merits.
  4. The correct answer is A (Cover it with a purpose made sheath, then disinfect at intermediate level): solid state sensors contain electronics and cannot be heat sterilized, so a single use barrier sheath is placed for every client and the sensor is then cleaned with an intermediate level disinfectant that the manufacturer approves. Autoclaving would destroy the sensor, even though the holders it attaches to are heat sterilized routinely. Immersion in liquid disinfectant would let fluid reach the electronics and the cable seal. An alcohol wipe alone is inadequate because sheaths leak often enough that a disinfection step is always required.
  5. The correct answer is D (About one percent becomes x-ray photons and the remainder becomes heat): x-ray production at the anode is extremely inefficient, and roughly ninety nine percent of the kinetic energy of the electrons is converted to heat in the tungsten target. That is why tube heads are oil filled, why the target is tungsten with its very high melting point, and why duty cycle limits exist. The filament current heats the cathode and liberates electrons, but it is not the source of the heat felt in the housing after a series. No dental tube converts anything close to half or all of the electron energy into photons.
  6. The correct answer is B (Raise the kilovoltage to 90 kVp, which gives a longer scale of contrast): kilovoltage controls beam penetration and therefore the scale of contrast, and a higher setting produces many intermediate greys rather than mostly black and white. That long scale is what allows subtle differences in crestal bone density to be appreciated in a periodontal client. Dropping to 60 kVp does the opposite and produces a short scale with high contrast, which suits caries detection instead. Milliamperage and exposure time govern the quantity of radiation and therefore density, so changing them alters darkness and not the range of greys.
  7. The correct answer is B (Reduce the exposure time or the milliamperage, which together control density): density is the overall darkness of the image and is governed by the quantity of radiation delivered, which is the product of milliamperage and time. An image that is uniformly too dark with acceptable contrast is an overexposure, so reducing milliampere seconds corrects it while leaving the scale of greys untouched. Changing kilovoltage would alter penetration and contrast as well as darkness, so it is the wrong control for an isolated density fault. Raising kilovoltage would in fact add density rather than remove it.
  8. The correct answer is D (4 units, because intensity falls to one ninth of the original value): the inverse square law states that new intensity equals old intensity multiplied by the square of the ratio of the old distance to the new distance. Here the ratio is 20 divided by 60, which is one third, and one third squared is one ninth. Multiplying 36 units by one ninth gives 4 units. Intensity does not fall in direct proportion to distance, which is the error behind the larger figures offered, and tripling the distance reduces intensity by far more than a half or a quarter.
  9. The correct answer is A (0.80 seconds, because four times the exposure is needed at twice the distance): the receptor now sits twice as far from the focal spot, so the beam reaching it carries one quarter of the previous intensity and the exposure time must rise by a factor of four to restore the same density. The calculation is 0.20 seconds multiplied by the square of 40 divided by 20, which is 0.20 multiplied by 4, giving 0.80 seconds. Doubling the time to 0.40 seconds treats the relationship as directly proportional and would leave the image far too light. Shortening the time moves in the wrong direction entirely.
  10. The correct answer is C (0.16 seconds, because the shorter distance needs four ninths of the time): moving from a 30 cm device to a 20 cm device brings the receptor closer to the source, so intensity rises and the exposure time must fall. The factor is the square of 20 divided by 30, which is four ninths, and 0.36 seconds multiplied by four ninths gives 0.16 seconds. Scaling the time in direct proportion to device length would give 0.24 seconds and leave the image too dark. A shorter device needs less exposure rather than more, so a longer time is the opposite of what is required.
  11. The correct answer is A (9 units, because the intensity falls to one sixteenth of the original): the distance increases from 0.5 m to 2 m, a fourfold increase, and the ratio of old to new distance squared is one quarter squared, which is one sixteenth. Multiplying 144 units by one sixteenth gives 9 units. This is why moving back to the recommended operator position is the single most effective protective measure available without any equipment purchase. Treating the fall as a half or a quarter underestimates the benefit badly, and one thirty sixth would correspond to a sixfold rather than a fourfold increase in distance.
  12. The correct answer is D (0.20 seconds, because the product of milliamperage and time must stay 3.0): quantity of radiation is expressed as milliampere seconds, and the original setting delivers 10 multiplied by 0.30, which is 3.0 mAs. At a fixed 15 mA the time needed is 3.0 divided by 15, which is 0.20 seconds. Raising the time alongside the milliamperage would deliver 4.5 mAs and produce a badly overexposed image. Milliamperage and time are interchangeable controls of the same quantity, so leaving the time unchanged would also overexpose, and there is no rule that a higher milliamperage always halves the time.
  13. The correct answer is B (About 25 days, so the series is comparable to under a month): dividing the 150 microsievert effective dose of the series by the 6 microsieverts received each day from background gives 25 days. Converting a dose figure into days of ordinary living is the clearest way to help a client weigh a proposed examination. About 5 days would correspond to a dose near 30 microsieverts, which fits a lower dose full mouth series with modern technique rather than the figure quoted. The larger figures would require doses of roughly 540 and 1500 microsieverts, well beyond any intraoral examination.
  14. The correct answer is C (Photons splitting water into free radicals that then attack nearby molecules): this is the indirect effect, and because human tissue is largely water it accounts for roughly two thirds of the damage caused by a dental beam. The free radicals formed are highly reactive and travel a short distance before damaging DNA and other critical molecules. A photon striking DNA itself is the direct effect, which is real but far less common given how small a target DNA presents. Dental x-rays do not deposit meaningful heat in tissue, and the electrons stay within the tube.
  15. The correct answer is C (There is no known threshold, and dose changes probability, not severity): a stochastic effect has no dose below which it is known to be impossible, and increasing the dose raises the chance that the effect occurs rather than making it worse when it does. Cancer induction and heritable effects are the classic examples, and this is precisely why every dental exposure has to be justified individually. A threshold with severity rising above it describes a deterministic effect such as erythema or cataract. Radiation damage is cumulative across a lifetime because cellular repair is imperfect, so it cannot be described as always reversible.
  16. The correct answer is A (Cataract formation in the lens of the eye after a high dose): cataract is a deterministic effect, occurring only above a threshold dose and becoming more severe as the dose rises above it. Skin erythema and hair loss belong to the same category, and dental doses fall far below any of these thresholds. Leukemia after low occupational exposure, a heritable mutation and a solid tumour after a small dose are all stochastic outcomes, where dose alters the probability of the effect and not its severity. Sorting an effect into the right category is what determines whether a threshold argument can be made at all.
  17. The correct answer is D (Cells that divide frequently and remain undifferentiated, such as bone marrow): radiosensitivity rises with mitotic rate and falls with the degree of differentiation, so bone marrow, reproductive cells, the lens of the eye and intestinal lining are among the most sensitive tissues. In the head and neck this is why the thyroid gland and the parotid and submandibular glands are the tissues of concern in dental imaging. Mature neurons are highly differentiated and divide rarely, which makes them comparatively resistant. Cell size, mitochondrial content and low water content do not define radiosensitivity, and dense cortical bone is among the more resistant tissues.
  18. The correct answer is B (Provide the collar, following the provincial requirement and respecting his request): Canadian practice on shielding is in transition, and where a provincial regulation still directs the use of a thyroid collar, that requirement governs regardless of the newer guidance. The client has also asked for it, and honouring a reasonable request costs nothing and supports his trust in the appointment. Declining on the basis of guidance that the province has not adopted would place the hygienist outside her regulatory obligations. A collar is removed only where it would obscure the diagnostic field, which is not the case for posterior bitewings.
  19. The correct answer is A (It roughly halves the tissue area exposed and reduces scatter reaching the receptor): a rectangular collimator shapes the beam to the size of the receptor rather than to a circle that overlaps surrounding tissue, cutting the irradiated area by roughly half. Less irradiated tissue also means less scattered radiation reaching the receptor, so image contrast improves at the same time. Rectangular collimation demands more precise alignment and therefore raises rather than lowers the risk of cone cut, which an aiming ring manages. It does not remove any obligation for thyroid shielding or for occupational dosimetry.
  20. The correct answer is D (Each retake doubles the dose delivered to the client for that projection): the first exposure delivered its full dose to the client whether or not the resulting image was usable, so repeating it means that projection has been given twice the radiation. A retake rate of one in five is high and points to a correctable alignment problem rather than to chance. Digital sensors lower the dose of each individual exposure but do not change the fact that a second exposure doubles the total for that view. This is why a retake log is kept and why errors are traced to an operator or a machine.
  21. The correct answer is C (It removes low energy photons the skin would absorb without reaching the receptor): aluminium filtration hardens the beam by absorbing the weakest photons, which would otherwise be deposited in the client's skin and superficial tissues without contributing anything to the image. Removing the filter would raise the client's surface dose while adding no diagnostic information at all, so the unit must not be used until it is refitted. Restricting the size and shape of the beam is the job of the lead collimator, not the filter. Filtration reduces rather than increases the number of photons and never produces a single energy beam.
  22. The correct answer is B (Optimization, because she achieves the clinical answer at the lowest reasonable dose): optimization is the principle that once an examination is warranted, it is carried out with the lowest dose that still answers the clinical question. Limiting the number of images, choosing a fast receptor and collimating rectangularly are all dose reduction measures applied after the decision to image has been made. Justification is the separate earlier decision that the examination will do more good than harm for this individual. Dose limitation applies to occupational and public exposure and not to the client being imaged.
  23. The correct answer is D (Dose limits apply to occupational and public exposure, not the imaged client): the radiation protection framework sets numerical limits for workers and for members of the public, but no dose limit is placed on a client receiving a justified diagnostic examination. Client protection is delivered through justification and optimization instead, so the real question is whether each image is warranted and taken at the lowest reasonable dose. Inventing an annual client limit would lead a hygienist to refuse an examination that the client genuinely needs. Pregnancy does not create such a limit either, although it does sharpen the justification discussion.
  24. The correct answer is A (Behind the barrier, watching the client through the leaded glass window): a fixed lead lined barrier with a leaded window is the preferred operator position because it gives full shielding while still allowing direct observation of the client throughout the exposure. Where a barrier exists it is used in preference to distance alone. Standing in an open doorway leaves the operator unshielded and often in line with scattered radiation from adjacent rooms. One metre beside the tube head is far too close even at right angles to the beam, and standing behind the client's head places the operator near the beam path.
  25. The correct answer is B (Never hold the tube head; withdraw the unit from use until repaired): an operator never steadies the tube head during an exposure, because the hand sits in or beside the primary beam and the dose accumulates across every client for the rest of a career. Drift is an equipment fault, and a unit that will not hold its position is taken out of service and reported for repair. A lead glove attenuates only part of the beam and gives false reassurance about a practice that should not happen at all. Shortening the exposure time would only underexpose the image and force a retake.
  26. The correct answer is C (The stored image will be erased by the light before it is scanned): a photostimulable phosphor plate stores the exposure as trapped energy until a laser scanner releases it, and ordinary room or operatory light releases that energy prematurely and wipes the latent image. Exposed plates therefore stay inside a light tight envelope until the moment they are scanned. Light exposure produces a blank or badly faded image rather than a dark one. A herringbone pattern comes from a reversed film packet, and the plates themselves are not damaged by light, since they are erased and reused routinely.
  27. The correct answer is A (A reduction commonly cited at about 40 to 60 percent against D speed film): this is the range reported for direct digital sensors compared with D speed film, and quoting a realistic range is more defensible than a single dramatic number. The saving comes from the greater sensitivity of the receptor, which needs fewer photons to produce a diagnostic image. Claiming about 95 percent overstates the benefit and invites a client to believe the dose is effectively zero, which is not true of any ionizing examination. Digital receptors do reduce dose substantially, so both the no reduction and the ten percent figures are wrong.
  28. The correct answer is C (The original file is kept and the record notes that enhancement was applied): reasonable adjustment of brightness, contrast and magnification is an accepted part of digital imaging, and the safeguards are that the unaltered original is retained and the adjustment is documented. That way anyone reviewing the record later can see exactly what was exposed and what was changed. Overwriting the original destroys the evidence of what the exposure actually produced. A slightly dark but fully readable image does not warrant a retake, and routine on screen adjustment does not require separate written authorization from the dentist.
  29. The correct answer is D (Remove the sheath, discard barriers, remove gloves and perform hand hygiene): contaminated barriers and gloves are removed and hand hygiene is performed before anything clean is touched, which prevents the keyboard and the sensor from becoming a route of transmission to the next client. The sheath comes off while gloved so the contaminated outer surface is contained, then the barriers are discarded and the gloves follow. Handling a keyboard with contaminated gloves defeats the barrier system whether or not the keyboard is covered. An alcohol rub is not applied to gloves as a substitute for removing them.
  30. The correct answer is B (Retain it, because the exposure occurred and the record must reflect it): an exposed image is part of the health record from the moment the exposure is made, and it is never deleted for poor quality, because the client received the radiation and the record has to show that. The unusable image and the retake are both retained, and the reason for the retake is entered in the retake log. Deleting it, with or without a chart note, falsifies the dose history and hides a technique problem that quality assurance is meant to surface. Retention periods are set by the province, not by the retake log.
Case C061

A 34-year-old client attends a general practice for a full mouth series before periodontal therapy. The dental hygienist uses a paralleling instrument with a 30 cm position indicating device and a size 2 digital sensor. The client has a shallow palate and a moderate gag reflex. On reviewing the images the hygienist finds several projections that do not meet diagnostic standards and must decide, for each one, what went wrong and what to change before retaking.

  1. The correct answer is B (Elongation caused by insufficient vertical angulation; increase the vertical angulation): A tooth imaged longer than it truly is has been elongated, and elongation follows a vertical angle that was too flat for the relationship between receptor and tooth. Increasing the vertical angulation shortens the projected image back toward true length. Blaming an excessive angle reverses the rule, because a beam that is too steep produces foreshortening, an image shorter than the tooth. Horizontal angulation governs contact overlap, not projected length.
  2. The correct answer is D (Cone cut from beam misalignment; recentre the device on the receptor using the ring): A sharply bordered unexposed area means part of the receptor was never struck by the beam, which is a cone cut caused by misalignment of the position indicating device. Centring the beam on the receptor with the aiming ring of the holder corrects it. A backwards receptor would give a pale image with a herringbone pattern across the whole field, not one clean unexposed corner. Movement blurs the entire image and a steep vertical angle shortens it.
  3. The correct answer is A (Direct the central ray through the contact areas by correcting horizontal angulation): Superimposed proximal surfaces are overlapped contacts, and the cause is horizontal angulation that did not pass through the contact areas. Redirecting the central ray so it travels through the contacts separates the surfaces and makes proximal caries and crestal bone readable. Changing the vertical angle alters projected length rather than contact separation. Reducing exposure only lightens an image whose density was already acceptable.
  4. The correct answer is C (The plate was placed backwards, so the beam passed through the lead foil): A pale image carrying a repeating herringbone pattern is the signature of a receptor placed backwards, because the beam had to pass through the patterned lead foil backing before reaching the active surface. The correction is to place the active surface toward the beam and retake the projection. Light fogging darkens a phosphor plate rather than leaving a geometric pattern. Exposure settings that worked on every other projection did not suddenly become inadequate for one region.
  5. The correct answer is A (Bisecting angle technique; length is approximated, so crestal bone assessment is less reliable): The bisecting angle technique exists precisely for anatomy that defeats parallel placement, such as a shallow palate, tori or a strong gag reflex. Because the central ray is aimed at an imaginary bisecting line that the operator estimates, image length is only approximated and distortion is more likely, so bone levels read from it are less reliable and that limitation belongs in the record. Claiming the technique reproduces exact length removes the very reason paralleling is preferred. An occlusal projection and a panoramic image are both unsuitable for proximal caries or precise crestal bone.
Case C062

A 17-year-old client is referred for a panoramic image before assessment of the third molars 18, 28, 38 and 48. The client wears a fixed orthodontic appliance, has long hair tied back, and arrives wearing large hoop earrings and a necklace. The dental hygienist positions the client in the panoramic unit, and the first image shows several faults that must be corrected before the dentist can assess the third molars.

  1. The correct answer is D (Ghost image; all removable metal is taken off the head and neck first): A dense object lying between the source and the centre of rotation is recorded twice, and the second recording is the ghost image, which appears on the opposite side of the film, higher, larger and blurred than the real object. Removing every removable piece of metal from the head and neck before positioning prevents it. Head rotation gives unequal magnification of the two sides rather than a duplicated object. Movement produces a wavy step in the inferior border of the mandible.
  2. The correct answer is B (Chin too high; the chin should be lowered so the plane curves upward): Raising the chin above the correct position flattens or reverses the occlusal plane into a frown and drags the hard palate down over the maxillary apices, which is exactly what this image shows. Lowering the chin restores the gentle upward curve of a correctly positioned panoramic image. A chin held too low exaggerates the curve into a deep smile and blurs the anterior teeth instead. A slumped spine produces a pyramid shaped radiopacity in the midline, not a superimposed palate.
  3. The correct answer is B (The client should have held the tongue against the palate during the exposure): A tongue that is not pressed to the palate leaves an air space between the dorsum and the palatal vault, and that air records as a dark radiolucent band across the maxillary apices. Instructing the client to place the tongue firmly on the palate and hold it there for the whole rotation removes the shadow. A retained partial denture would give a radiopaque artefact rather than a dark band. Shoulder and neck position affect the spine shadow in the midline.
  4. The correct answer is C (No, resolution is low and superimposition heavy, so bitewings are still required): Panoramic imaging has low resolution, uneven magnification and heavy superimposition of structures, so it cannot show interproximal caries or precise crestal bone and does not replace bitewings. Bitewings remain the appropriate projection for both of those questions. Magnifying a low resolution image on any monitor adds no information that the exposure did not record. Repeating the panoramic image at a different kilovoltage changes contrast without changing the underlying geometry.
  5. The correct answer is A (Positioned too far forward of the focal trough; move the client slightly back): Anterior teeth that image narrow and blurred sat in front of the curved zone of sharpness, so the client was standing too far forward and must be moved back until the incisal edges rest correctly in the bite block groove. A client positioned too far back produces the opposite appearance, with anterior teeth wide and magnified. Head rotation makes one ramus wider than the other rather than blurring only the front. An apron placed too high gives a cone shaped radiopacity in the lower midline.
Case C063

A 52-year-old client with type 2 diabetes attends for periodontal reassessment. Vertical bitewings and selected periapicals are available. Probing depths of 6 to 8 mm are recorded at 16, 26 and 36, with bleeding on probing at most posterior sites. The hygienist reviews the images alongside the periodontal chart before writing the dental hygiene diagnosis and discussing the findings with the client and the dentist.

  1. The correct answer is C (Vertical or angular bone loss, since the crest is not parallel to that line): Bone loss is classified by the relationship of the crest to a line joining the cementoenamel junctions of adjacent teeth. When the crest is no longer parallel to that line and a defect angles down one root surface while the neighbouring tooth keeps higher bone, the pattern is vertical or angular. Horizontal loss keeps the crest parallel to the junction line while sitting further apical, which is not what this image shows. A defect on the mesial root surface is not by itself evidence that the furcation is involved.
  2. The correct answer is D (About 50 percent, reaching the middle third, suggesting moderate to severe loss): Bone loss is expressed as the distance from the cementoenamel junction to the crest as a proportion of the distance from that junction to the apex, so 7 mm of a 14 mm root length is 50 percent. Loss of half the root length has passed out of the coronal third and into the middle third, which is graded moderate to severe. Recording 25 or 33 percent misreads the arithmetic of the two measurements. Calling 50 percent coronal third loss misplaces the boundary between the thirds of the root.
  3. The correct answer is A (A radiograph always shows less destruction than exists, so the probing depths govern): A radiograph is a two dimensional record of a three dimensional defect and consistently underestimates the true extent of destruction, so a mismatch of this kind is expected rather than an error. The clinical attachment and probing findings therefore carry the diagnosis, with the images used as supporting evidence. Treating the probing depths as artefacts of inflammation would understate real disease in a client with diabetes. Vertical bitewings are a recognized projection for bone height and do not need replacing here.
  4. The correct answer is B (Describe and record the finding, bring it to the dentist promptly, record the referral): A hygienist may recognize, describe and record what is visible on an image and may use it in the dental hygiene diagnosis, but does not give a definitive diagnosis of pathology. A new periapical radiolucency is documented in the client's own record, brought to the dentist promptly, and the referral itself is recorded. Naming it an abscess and initiating antimicrobial care goes beyond the role and beyond the evidence. Dismissing a new radiolucency as normal anatomy, or leaving it out of the record until someone else confirms it, both fail the duty to document and refer.
  5. The correct answer is D (It reproduces true length and crest position, and a long device offsets magnification): Placing the receptor parallel to the long axis of the tooth with the central ray perpendicular to both reproduces length accurately and gives the truest crown to root relationship and alveolar crest, which is what periodontal assessment depends on. The receptor must sit away from the tooth to stay parallel, and that magnification is offset by a position indicating device of 30 cm or more. Magnification is reduced rather than abolished, so claiming it disappears is wrong. Paralleling depends on a holder and cannot be corrected by adjusting the image afterwards.
  6. The correct answer is C (Reasonable when anatomy defeats parallel placement, but limited for bone assessment): The bisecting angle technique is the recognized fallback when a shallow palate, tori, a gag reflex or a small mouth prevents parallel placement, because the receptor rests against the tooth and the ray is aimed perpendicular to an imaginary bisecting line. That imaginary line is estimated, so distortion is more likely and the technique is unsuitable for accurate assessment of alveolar bone. It is therefore not the technique of choice for periodontal measurement. Children are one indication among several, not the only one.
  7. The correct answer is C (Take vertical bitewings, turning the receptor so that more bone height is captured): Turning the receptor so its long axis is vertical captures more bone height on the same projection, which is why vertical bitewings are preferred once bone loss is moderate to advanced. A horizontal receptor simply does not extend far enough apically and will keep cutting off the crest no matter how the exposure factors are changed. Panoramic imaging lacks the resolution for precise crestal bone. Occlusal projections image the arch in a plane that does not display crestal bone levels.
  8. The correct answer is A (A maxillary occlusal radiograph, which covers the region and helps locate extra teeth): An occlusal radiograph uses a large receptor held on the occlusal plane and covers a broad area of one arch, which is why it is used to locate impacted or supernumerary teeth, trace a fracture line and image young children. It answers this clinical question with a single intraoral exposure. A bitewing records crowns and crestal bone and shows nothing of an unerupted tooth high in the alveolus. Cone beam imaging carries a higher dose and is justified only when conventional images cannot answer the question.
  9. The correct answer is B (In the lingual sulcus with the tongue displaced medially over the receptor edge): For mandibular posterior projections the receptor is placed down into the lingual sulcus with the tongue displaced medially over its upper edge, which lets the receptor sit low enough to record the apices while staying parallel to the long axis of the teeth. Resting the receptor against the lingual tooth surfaces keeps it too high and shallow, so the apices are again cut off. Buccal placement puts the receptor on the wrong side of the teeth. The palate is not involved in a mandibular projection.
  10. The correct answer is D (Toward the palatal midline with the anterior edge covering the distal of 15): A maxillary posterior receptor is carried well toward the palatal midline, which gives the height needed to record the apices, and for a molar projection its anterior edge is set to cover the distal surface of the second premolar so that the required field from that surface back through the tuberosity is included. Pressing the receptor against the palatal surfaces of the teeth tips it out of parallel and shortens the field. Buccal placement records the wrong side of the arch. Angling the receptor across the midline distorts the projection and does not add height on the side being imaged.
  11. The correct answer is A (Through the contact areas at about plus 10 degrees of vertical angulation): A standard bitewing is taken with the central ray passing through the contact areas at roughly plus 10 degrees of vertical angulation, which compensates for the slight upward tilt of the occlusal plane and records the crowns and crestal bone of both arches on one image.
