A negative angle would direct the beam upward and distort the relationship between the arches. Aiming at the cusp tips or the apices rather than the contacts leaves the proximal surfaces overlapped and unreadable.
  12. The correct answer is A (From the distal surface of the canine 43 through the first molar 46): a premolar bitewing is positioned to begin at the distal surface of the canine and to carry back through the first molar, so every proximal surface from the canine distal to the first molar is recorded on that one projection. The molar bitewing then takes over, centred on the second molar and picking up the distal of the second premolar and the molars behind it. Extending forward to the lateral incisor wastes field on surfaces that a bitewing is not designed to assess. Starting at the first premolar leaves the canine distal unrecorded by either projection, and stopping at the second premolar leaves the first molar contacts to be caught only by the molar projection.
  13. The correct answer is C (Movement of client, tube head or receptor; stabilize the head and use a holder): Loss of sharpness across every structure in the same direction is the signature of movement, whether of the client, the tube head or the receptor during the exposure. The correction is to stabilize the head, seat the receptor in a holder and confirm that the tube head has stopped drifting before exposing again. Too little exposure would give a pale image rather than a smeared one. A backwards receptor gives a pale image with a herringbone pattern, and a horizontal angle fault overlaps the contacts without blurring bone.
  14. The correct answer is B (The plate was exposed twice; keep exposed and unexposed plates clearly separated): Two full images superimposed at different angles means one receptor received two separate exposures, which happens when an already exposed plate is picked up again from a mixed pile. Keeping exposed receptors physically separated from unexposed ones, usually in a labelled disposable cup, prevents it, and both projections must be retaken. Rescanning cannot create a second set of anatomy that the exposure did not record. Light fogging lightens or veils a plate rather than adding a whole second image.
  15. The correct answer is D (A light image with a herringbone pattern; turn the active surface toward the beam): When the receptor is reversed the beam must pass through the lead foil backing before reaching the active surface, so it is attenuated and the embossed pattern of the foil is printed across the image as a herringbone or tyre track texture. The result is a pale image, and the correction is simply to turn the active surface toward the beam and retake. Blurring with doubled outlines indicates movement instead. Unexposed corners indicate a cone cut from beam misalignment.
  16. The correct answer is B (Reduce the exposure time or milliamperage, because those settings control density): Overall darkness is a density fault, and density is governed by the quantity of radiation delivered, which is the product of milliamperage and exposure time. Reducing either setting lightens every subsequent image and restores detail in the dense structures. Kilovoltage governs penetration and the contrast scale rather than overall blackness, so moving it either way is treating the wrong control. Shortening the indicating device increases intensity at the receptor and would make the images darker still.
  17. The correct answer is D (Kilovoltage is too high; lower it to separate enamel, dentin and bone): A flat grey image with many similar shades is a long scale of contrast, and a long scale is produced by kilovoltage set too high for the task, because more penetrating photons pass through structures of differing density with less difference between them. Lowering the kilovoltage shortens the contrast scale and separates enamel, dentin and bone. Raising it would flatten the image further. Milliamperage and time govern how dark the image is, and darkness was already correct here.
  18. The correct answer is C (A lower setting such as 65 kVp, giving high contrast that shows a lesion clearly): A lower kilovoltage produces high contrast on a short scale, mostly blacks and whites, and a small radiolucent notch in bright white enamel stands out sharply against that background. That is why caries detection favours the lower setting while periodontal bone assessment favours more shades of grey. A high setting gives a long grey scale, which flattens the difference between a small lesion and sound enamel. Pairing a high setting with a short scale reverses the relationship between kilovoltage and contrast.
  19. The correct answer is A (Foreshortening from excessive vertical angulation; decrease the vertical angulation): An image shorter than the tooth is foreshortened, and foreshortening follows a vertical angle that was too steep, so the correction is to decrease the vertical angulation before retaking. Pairing a short image with an insufficient angle reverses the rule, because a beam that is too flat produces elongation, an image longer than the tooth. The two elongation options describe the opposite finding from the one seen here. Open contacts confirm that horizontal angulation was not the problem.
  20. The correct answer is B (Describe the shadow, record it as apparent caries, and refer the diagnosis to the dentist): A hygienist may recognize, describe and record what is visible on an image, including apparent caries, and may use that finding in the dental hygiene diagnosis and care plan. What the hygienist does not do is give the definitive diagnosis or tell the client how serious a lesion is, so the finding is documented and referred to the dentist. Declaring the tooth in need of a restoration and booking the treatment claims a diagnosis that belongs to the dentist. Refusing to describe anything at all understates a role the hygienist genuinely holds.
  21. The correct answer is C (Cone beam imaging, because conventional projections cannot answer this clinical question): Cone beam computed tomography builds a three dimensional volume that shows bone width and the course of the mandibular canal, which is precisely what implant planning needs and what two dimensional projections cannot supply. The justification rests on conventional imaging having already failed to answer the question, and the smallest adequate field of view is then chosen. Repeating a panoramic image adds dose without adding the missing dimension. Neither an occlusal projection nor extra periapical angles gives a true buccolingual measurement.
  22. The correct answer is D (Unacceptable, because the dose is higher and cone beam is not a screening tool): Cone beam dose exceeds that of any two dimensional dental examination, running from roughly 20 to 100 microsieverts for a small field of view and several hundred or more for a large one, so the justification threshold is correspondingly higher. It is prescribed for an individual when conventional imaging cannot answer a specific question, never as a routine screen and not for caries detection. Consent and retention rules do not turn an unjustified exposure into a justified one. The barrier here is justification rather than who may refer.
  23. The correct answer is B (Only structures inside the curved zone of sharpness are clear, and contacts still overlap): The focal trough is the curved zone in which structures are recorded sharply as the tube head and receptor rotate in opposite directions, and anything outside it is blurred. Even for teeth sitting correctly inside the trough, resolution is low and proximal surfaces are superimposed, so interproximal caries cannot be assessed and bitewings are still required. Sharpness belongs inside the trough rather than outside it. Panoramic magnification is uneven rather than uniform, and both arches are recorded.
  24. The correct answer is A (The mental foramen, a normal landmark that is described and recorded as such): The mental foramen is commonly projected near the apex of 35 or 45 and is a classic mimic of a periapical lesion, which is why a vital, sound, asymptomatic tooth points to normal anatomy rather than disease. The finding is described and recorded as a landmark, and any residual doubt is resolved by bringing it to the dentist rather than by reassuring the client. The lingual foramen lies in the midline below the apices of 31 and 41, not beside a premolar. Treating a normal foramen as a granuloma or a cyst would trigger unnecessary intervention.
  25. The correct answer is D (The maxillary sinus and the zygomatic process, which are normal maxillary landmarks): A broad radiolucency above the maxillary posterior apices bounded by a thin cortical line is the maxillary sinus, and the U shaped radiopacity commonly projected over 16 or 26 is the zygomatic process. Recognizing both prevents a normal air space or a dense process being reported as pathology. The nasal fossa and the median palatal suture belong to anterior maxillary projections. The mandibular canal cannot appear on a maxillary periapical, and the incisive foramen sits between the central incisors.
  26. The correct answer is C (Horizontal bone loss, because the crest stays parallel but lies further apical): Horizontal bone loss is defined by the crest remaining parallel to a line joining the cementoenamel junctions while sitting further apical than normal, so adjacent teeth are affected fairly evenly. A crest 4 mm apical to that line is well beyond the normal 1 to 2 mm and cannot be called physiological. Vertical or angular loss would show the crest no longer parallel, with a defect angling down one root surface. Nothing in this description addresses the furcation.
  27. The correct answer is A (The incisive foramen, a normal landmark of the maxillary anterior midline region): The incisive foramen appears as an oval radiolucency between the roots of 11 and 21 on maxillary anterior projections and is a normal landmark rather than a lesion. Vital, sound, asymptomatic teeth with an intact periodontal ligament space around each root support that reading. The lingual foramen belongs to the mandibular midline, ringed by the genial tubercles below the apices of 31 and 41. The median palatal suture images as a thin vertical radiolucent line rather than an oval opening.
  28. The correct answer is C (A radiolucent notch just apical to the contact, its base at the outer enamel surface): Interproximal caries appears as a radiolucent notch on the proximal surface just apical to the contact point, classically triangular with its base at the outer enamel surface and its apex pointing inward toward the dentinoenamel junction. Demineralized enamel lets more of the beam through, so the lesion is dark rather than light. Placing the lesion coronal to the contact or reversing the triangle misdescribes where and how caries begins. A radiolucent band along the whole root surface suggests a different process altogether.
  29. The correct answer is B (Recurrent caries, described and recorded, then brought to the dentist for diagnosis): A radiolucency at or beneath the margin of an existing restoration, most often at the gingival margin of a proximal restoration, is the classic appearance of recurrent caries. The hygienist describes and records the finding and brings it to the dentist, who makes the diagnosis and decides on replacement. An overhang would appear as a radiopaque projection beyond the crown contour, not as a dark area under the margin. Calculus is radiopaque, and no radiographic finding is left out of the record.
  30. The correct answer is D (The spurs are calculus and the projection is an overhang, but facial deposits stay hidden): Calculus images as pointed radiopaque spurs or collars on proximal root surfaces, while a radiopaque projection extending past the crown contour at the margin of a restoration is an overhang. A radiograph shows only a fraction of the deposit present, because facial and lingual calculus is superimposed on the tooth itself and disappears, so the client is told the images underreport what is there. Swapping the two appearances misidentifies both findings. Neither appearance is created by adjusting brightness or contrast on a digital image.
Case C064

Marguerite is 68 years old and weighs 68 kg. She attends for periodontal debridement. Her health history records atrial fibrillation, hypertension and stable angina, with a myocardial infarction 14 months ago. Her medications are warfarin, amlodipine, metoprolol and sublingual nitroglycerin as needed. An international normalized ratio drawn yesterday was 2.4. Intraorally the interdental papillae of the anterior sextants are firm and enlarged, worst where heavy biofilm has accumulated, and she reports a persistently dry mouth that has worsened over the past year.

  1. The correct answer is C (Amlodipine, because calcium channel blockers cause enlargement beginning at the papillae): calcium channel blockers including nifedipine, amlodipine, verapamil and diltiazem produce gingival enlargement that starts at the interdental papillae and worsens wherever biofilm accumulates, which matches Marguerite's presentation exactly. Phenytoin and cyclosporine produce the same picture, but neither appears on her list. Anticoagulation raises bleeding risk and has no role in tissue overgrowth, so blaming warfarin would misdirect care and delay the real intervention. The primary response is rigorous biofilm control, because the tissue response is driven by plaque accumulation rather than by the drug alone.
  2. The correct answer is B (Two cartridges, because the epinephrine limit is reached before the lidocaine limit): lidocaine 2 percent is 20 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds 36 mg. Her drug limit is 7.0 mg per kg times 68 kg, which is 476 mg, below the 500 mg absolute ceiling, and 476 divided by 36 is 13.2, so 13 cartridges. Epinephrine 1:100,000 is 0.01 mg per mL, so a cartridge holds 0.018 mg, and her cardiovascular disease caps epinephrine at 0.04 mg per appointment, giving 0.04 divided by 0.018, which is 2.2, so 2 cartridges. The lower of the two limits always governs, and two cartridges deliver 0.036 mg of epinephrine and 72 mg of lidocaine.
  3. The correct answer is A (Proceed with local hemostatic measures and tell her never to self adjust warfarin): an international normalized ratio of 2.4 sits inside the usual therapeutic range of 2.0 to 3.0, and scaling and root planing can generally go ahead using pressure, a hemostatic dressing, atraumatic technique and quadrant by quadrant sequencing. Only the prescriber alters anticoagulant therapy, because the thromboembolic risk of stopping outweighs the oral bleeding risk, and this client has atrial fibrillation and a recent myocardial infarction. Advising her to omit doses could cause a stroke. Vitamin K reversal is a hospital intervention with no place before routine periodontal debridement.
  4. The correct answer is D (Acetaminophen 500 mg every six hours, because NSAIDs raise her bleeding risk): non steroidal anti inflammatory drugs inhibit platelet aggregation and irritate gastric mucosa, so combined with warfarin they raise bleeding risk substantially, which makes acetaminophen the preferred analgesic for an anticoagulated client. Ibuprofen and naproxen share that mechanism, and acetylsalicylic acid is itself an antiplatelet that would compound the effect further. Keep the acetaminophen dose modest and time limited, because high regular dosing can itself raise the international normalized ratio. Her total daily acetaminophen must stay below the adult 4000 mg ceiling, counting any combination cold or pain product she buys.
  5. The correct answer is A (Daily 5000 ppm sodium fluoride paste with fluoride varnish at short recall): losing saliva costs lubrication, buffering, mineral supply and antimicrobial proteins, so cervical and root caries appear rapidly, and high concentration fluoride with short recall intervals is the single most protective measure. Hyposalivation is measurable reduced flow, with unstimulated whole saliva below about 0.1 mL per minute as the usual threshold. Chlorhexidine targets biofilm rather than the mineral deficit and is not a long term caries strategy. Xylitol gum stimulates residual flow and is useful as an adjunct but cannot protect the dentition alone, and the hygienist asks the prescriber, never the client, about a less drying alternative.
Case C065

Harold is 74 years old and weighs 55 kg. He has taken oral alendronate for osteoporosis for six years and uses an inhaled corticosteroid twice daily with a salbutamol reliever for asthma. He also takes omeprazole for reflux and has a documented history of peptic ulcer disease. He attends with generalized moderate periodontitis. Intraorally there are removable white patches on the palate and buccal mucosa that leave a reddened base, and he has angular cheilitis at both commissures.

  1. The correct answer is B (Candidiasis from the inhaled corticosteroid; rinse with water and spit afterwards): pseudomembranous candidiasis wipes away to leave an erythematous base, which is exactly what the hygienist found, and inhaled corticosteroids predispose to oropharyngeal candidiasis by depositing drug on the mucosa. The angular cheilitis at both commissures supports the same diagnosis. Advise rinsing with water and spitting after every dose and using a spacer, and refer for antifungal treatment if the lesions persist. Leukoplakia is by definition a patch that cannot be wiped off, and lichen planus presents as fine white striae that also do not wipe away, so neither fits the finding.
  2. The correct answer is D (Proceed with non surgical periodontal therapy, which antiresorptives do not contraindicate): scaling and root planing is not contraindicated in a client taking an oral bisphosphonate, and untreated periodontitis is itself a risk factor for medication related osteonecrosis of the jaw, so withholding care would increase his risk rather than lower it. Risk is far higher with intravenous oncology dosing than with oral osteoporosis dosing, and highest after extraction. A drug holiday is a decision for the prescribing physician and is not requested by the dental team on routine grounds. Bone turnover marker testing is not a validated way to stratify this risk before dental hygiene care.
  3. The correct answer is C (Exposed or probeable bone persisting beyond eight weeks with no jaw radiation history): the definition requires exposed bone, or bone that can be probed through an intraoral or extraoral fistula, in the maxillofacial region, persisting for more than eight weeks, in a client taking or having taken an antiresorptive or antiangiogenic drug, with no history of radiation to the jaws and no obvious metastatic disease at that site. All parts of the definition must be present together. A single freshly exposed area seen at one visit does not yet meet the eight week criterion. Radiographic bone loss and non responding pockets describe periodontitis, which is a separate condition.
  4. The correct answer is A (Five cartridges, because the articaine dose limit governs before the epinephrine limit): articaine 4 percent is 40 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds 72 mg. His drug limit is 7.0 mg per kg times 55 kg, which is 385 mg, well below the 500 mg absolute ceiling, and 385 divided by 72 is 5.35, so 5 cartridges delivering 360 mg. Six cartridges would deliver 432 mg and exceed his limit. Epinephrine 1:200,000 is 0.005 mg per mL, so a cartridge holds 0.009 mg, and the healthy adult ceiling of 0.2 mg divided by 0.009 gives 22 cartridges. The lower limit governs, so the answer is five.
  5. The correct answer is C (Acetaminophen at a reduced daily ceiling, because NSAIDs suit ulcer disease poorly): non steroidal anti inflammatory drugs are contraindicated or used with great caution in peptic ulcer disease, and a proton pump inhibitor does not make them safe, so ibuprofen, naproxen and acetylsalicylic acid are all unsuitable here. Acetaminophen 325 to 1000 mg every four to six hours is the alternative when NSAIDs cannot be used. The healthy adult ceiling is 4000 mg in 24 hours, commonly reduced to 2000 to 3000 mg in older adults, in liver disease and with regular alcohol use, and Harold is 74. Warn him to count any combination cold or pain product toward that total.
Case C066

Devon is 34 years old and weighs 62 kg. He attended for full mouth debridement over two hours and received six 1.8 mL cartridges of prilocaine 4 percent plain. He has no cardiac or respiratory history, takes no medication and reports no allergies. Ninety minutes after leaving he telephones the clinic. He reports headache, fatigue, breathlessness and a bluish grey colour to his lips and fingertips. He sounds anxious but is still speaking in full sentences.

  1. The correct answer is D (Methemoglobinemia, for which prilocaine is the amide most often implicated): the iron in hemoglobin is oxidized to the ferric state and can no longer carry oxygen, and signs appear one to three hours after administration as cyanosis, headache, fatigue and breathlessness, which fits Devon's timing exactly. Prilocaine is the amide most implicated and benzocaine the topical most implicated. Systemic local anesthetic toxicity appears within minutes of injection with excitatory central nervous system signs, not ninety minutes later with cyanosis. A hypersensitivity reaction would present with urticaria, swelling and wheeze soon after exposure, and a vasovagal episode resolves within a minute of lying flat.
  2. The correct answer is B (Cyanosis unrelieved by oxygen, with oximeter saturation plateauing near 85 percent): oxygen cannot help when the hemoglobin itself is unable to bind it, so blue discolouration that does not lift with supplemental oxygen is the hallmark finding, and the pulse oximeter characteristically plateaus around 85 percent regardless of the true oxygen content. Drawn blood looks chocolate brown. Wheeze that responds to salbutamol points to bronchospasm, chest pain relieved by nitroglycerin points to angina, and perioral and digital tingling that settles with controlled breathing points to hyperventilation. Naming the oxygen unresponsive cyanosis to the paramedics gives them the fastest route to the correct treatment.
  3. The correct answer is A (Give high flow oxygen, monitor vital signs, and transfer for intravenous methylene blue): the dental team gives 100 percent oxygen and monitors airway, breathing, circulation and level of consciousness while waiting, and the definitive treatment is intravenous methylene blue given in hospital. Oxygen will not fully correct the colour because the affected hemoglobin cannot carry it, but it is still given because the unaffected fraction benefits. Intramuscular epinephrine is the first line drug for anaphylaxis and has no role here. An oral antihistamine treats a skin limited allergic reaction and would waste time. Oxygen is withheld only in hyperventilation, where the problem is loss of carbon dioxide.
  4. The correct answer is B (Six cartridges, so the dose was within his limit yet the reaction occurred): prilocaine 4 percent is 40 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds 72 mg. His weight based limit is 8.0 mg per kg times 62 kg, which is 496 mg, below the 600 mg absolute ceiling, and 496 divided by 72 is 6.9, so 6 whole cartridges. Six cartridges deliver 432 mg, which is inside his limit, while seven would deliver 504 mg and exceed it. The teaching point is that methemoglobinemia can still occur at doses within the recommended maximum, so prilocaine is avoided altogether in infants, in pregnancy, in congenital methemoglobinemia, in glucose 6 phosphate dehydrogenase deficiency and in significant anemia.
  5. The correct answer is D (As a side effect, because it is predictable and dose related, not immune mediated): a side effect is an expected, dose related action of the drug that is not the therapeutic goal, and nausea and mild drowsiness are the classic opioid examples. An allergy is immune mediated, requires prior sensitization, is not dose related and presents with hives, wheeze, swelling or anaphylaxis, none of which this client has ever had. Recording nausea as an allergy removes a useful drug for life and pushes prescribers toward less suitable alternatives. An adverse effect is harmful and unintended, which mild nausea is not, and an idiosyncratic reaction is an unexpected response unrelated to the known pharmacology.
  6. The correct answer is C (Dental pain is inflammatory, so ibuprofen 400 mg every four to six hours works): acute dental pain is driven by inflammation, and non steroidal anti inflammatory drugs inhibit cyclooxygenase and reduce prostaglandin synthesis at the injured site, which is why in postoperative dental pain trials they consistently outperform opioid combinations. Ibuprofen 400 mg every four to six hours is the usual adult dose, with an over the counter ceiling of 1200 mg in 24 hours and a prescription ceiling of 2400 mg in 24 hours. An 800 mg dose every four hours would breach both ceilings, and 200 mg four times daily underdoses him. Ibuprofen 400 mg combined with acetaminophen 1000 mg beats either drug alone.
  7. The correct answer is A (Sublingual absorption crosses a thin vascular membrane and avoids first pass hepatic metabolism): the floor of the mouth is thin and richly perfused, so drug passes straight into the systemic circulation instead of travelling through the portal vein to the liver, where a swallowed dose would be largely inactivated before reaching the heart. That is why sublingual nitroglycerin acts within minutes. Inhaled routes work the same way, and the oral route is the slowest and most variable of all. Gastric acid is not the limiting factor here, and the sublingual route changes absorption rather than excretion, which remains largely renal for most drugs.
  8. The correct answer is D (It is an antagonist that binds opioid receptors and blocks the agonist response): an agonist binds a receptor and produces a response, while an antagonist binds the same receptor and blocks it without producing a response of its own, and naloxone is the standard example at opioid receptors. It displaces the opioid from the receptor, which is why respiratory depression reverses within minutes. It does not stimulate the respiratory centre by an independent route, and it does not alter how quickly the liver metabolizes the opioid. Because naloxone has a shorter duration than many opioids, respiratory depression can return and the client always needs continued monitoring.
  9. The correct answer is B (Cyclosporine and nifedipine act additively, and biofilm control is the primary response): the immunosuppressant cyclosporine and the calcium channel blocker nifedipine each cause gingival enlargement, and a transplant client taking both carries additive risk, which is why his enlargement is so marked. Phenytoin produces the same picture through the same fibroblast response. The enlargement is driven by plaque accumulation, so meticulous biofilm control, short recall intervals and oral hygiene instruction come first, with surgical reduction reserved for tissue that does not resolve. Systemic corticosteroids and proton pump inhibitors are not recognized causes, and no medication is stopped or changed except by the prescribing physician.
  10. The correct answer is C (It is anticholinergic and potentiates vasoconstrictors, so limit epinephrine and avoid levonordefrin): tricyclic antidepressants such as amitriptyline are strongly anticholinergic, which explains the dry mouth and the resulting cervical caries, and they also potentiate injected vasoconstrictors, which can produce an exaggerated cardiovascular response. The practical result is to limit the epinephrine dose, aspirate carefully, inject slowly and avoid levonordefrin altogether. The dryness is very much drug related, so the hygienist should also start fluoride and saliva management. No antidepressant increases salivary flow, and levonordefrin is the vasoconstrictor most strongly potentiated by this drug class, so preferring it would be the opposite of correct.
  11. The correct answer is A (Anesthesia is not withheld, and lidocaine with epinephrine is the usual choice): untreated pain and infection carry greater risk to a pregnancy than a properly dosed local anesthetic, so anesthesia is given when it is needed, using the smallest effective dose, careful aspiration and slow injection. Lidocaine with epinephrine is the usual choice and the second trimester is a comfortable time for periodontal therapy. Prilocaine is avoided in pregnancy because of methemoglobinemia risk, and benzocaine carries the same concern, so a plain prilocaine cartridge is not a safer option. Deferring all treatment until after delivery would leave active periodontal disease untreated for months.
  12. The correct answer is D (Defer elective care, consult oncology, and give palliative mucositis and hygiene advice): elective dental hygiene care is deferred and the oncology team consulted when the neutrophil count falls below about 1.0 x 10^9/L or the platelet count below about 50 x 10^9/L, and this client is below both thresholds at 0.6 and 38. Instrumentation would risk both bacteremia in a neutropenic client and prolonged bleeding in a thrombocytopenic one. Ultrasonic scaling is not gentler in this respect, because it still breaches the sulcular epithelium and creates aerosol. Prophylactic antibiotics do nothing for thrombocytopenia. Offer bland rinses, gentle brushing with a soft brush, and coordination of care with the oncology team.
  13. The correct answer is C (Hyposalivation is permanent, so daily tray fluoride and biofilm control are needed): head and neck radiotherapy destroys salivary acinar tissue and the resulting hyposalivation is permanent rather than temporary, which produces rampant cervical radiation caries of the kind already appearing at his margins. Daily custom tray fluoride, a high concentration fluoride paste, rigorous biofilm control and short recall intervals are the standard response. Loss of taste, mucositis and trismus also occur but the caries risk is what threatens his dentition. Osteoradionecrosis risk is lifelong and rises sharply above roughly 60 Gy, so extractions become more hazardous after radiotherapy, never safer, and preservation of teeth is the priority.
  14. The correct answer is B (Amide metabolism is hepatic and slows in liver disease, so reduce the dose): amides including lidocaine, mepivacaine, prilocaine and bupivacaine are metabolized by hepatic enzymes, so impaired liver function slows clearance, raises plasma concentration and lowers the dose at which systemic toxicity appears. The practical response is to reduce the total dose, use the fewest cartridges that achieve profound anesthesia, and consider shorter appointments. Esters are hydrolyzed in plasma by pseudocholinesterase, but they now survive mainly as topicals and are not a practical injectable substitute in this setting. Nothing about liver disease speeds clearance, and amides are not excreted unchanged by the kidney.
  15. The correct answer is D (Contact the prescriber about a less drying option and start protective measures now): medications are the leading cause of hyposalivation by a wide margin, and this client takes three drying drugs, but only the prescriber may change a regimen, so the hygienist raises the question with the physician and never instructs the client to stop anything. Protective measures begin at once: frequent water, avoidance of caffeine, alcohol containing rinses and tobacco, sugar free xylitol gum to stimulate residual flow, saliva replacement gels, and a 5000 ppm sodium fluoride paste with varnish at short recall. Sialogogues such as pilocarpine are prescription drugs, not pharmacy purchases, and dismissing the dryness as age related would leave a real caries risk unmanaged.
  16. The correct answer is A (They bind voltage gated sodium channels internally, so the membrane cannot reach threshold): local anesthetics bind the intracellular portion of voltage gated sodium channels, sodium cannot enter, the membrane cannot depolarize to threshold, and the action potential cannot propagate along the axon. The drug sits in equilibrium between an uncharged free base, which is the only form that crosses the lipid nerve sheath, and an ionized cation, which is the only form that binds the receptor inside. Potassium efflux, acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction and neurotransmitter synthesis are not the targets, and describing any of them would leave the student unable to explain why an inflamed acidic field resists anesthesia.
  17. The correct answer is C (The acidic field leaves less free base to enter the nerve; use a block): inflamed tissue is acidic, so more of the injected drug exists as the ionized cation and less as the uncharged free base, and only the free base can cross the lipid nerve sheath to reach the receptor inside. Less drug gets in, so onset is slower and the block is weaker. Inflammation also raises local blood flow, which carries drug away, and sensitizes the nociceptors themselves. The practical adjustment is to place a nerve block in healthy tissue away from the inflamed site rather than infiltrating into it, and simply waiting longer or raising the concentration does not overcome the pH problem.
  18. The correct answer is B (Esters yield para aminobenzoic acid, so true allergy is far more common): esters including benzocaine, procaine and tetracaine are hydrolyzed in plasma by pseudocholinesterase to para aminobenzoic acid, a recognized allergen, which is why documented true allergy is far more frequent in this class, and why esters now survive mainly as topical agents such as the gel this client reacted to. Amides including lidocaine, mepivacaine, prilocaine and bupivacaine are metabolized hepatically and true allergy to them is rare. Cartridges containing a vasoconstrictor also hold sodium metabisulphite, which is a separate sensitivity worth asking about, but it is not what distinguishes ester from amide allergy risk.
  19. The correct answer is D (It is an amide carrying an ester side chain, with a short half life): articaine is classified as an amide, but its molecule carries an additional ester side chain, so plasma esterases do most of the metabolic work rather than the liver alone. That gives it a half life of roughly 27 to 45 minutes, against about 90 minutes for lidocaine, which lowers the risk of accumulation when several cartridges are given across a long appointment. The short half life does not change the maximum recommended dose, which remains 7.0 mg per kg for an adult with a 500 mg ceiling, reduced to 5 mg per kg in children.
  20. The correct answer is A (Cartridges with a vasoconstrictor contain sodium metabisulphite, so consider a plain solution): sodium metabisulphite is added as an antioxidant to stop the vasoconstrictor degrading, so it is present in cartridges containing epinephrine and absent from plain solutions, and a sulphite sensitive client can be given a plain agent such as mepivacaine 3 percent. The trade off must be explained, because a vasoconstrictor slows systemic absorption and so lowers peak plasma concentration and toxicity risk, prolongs and deepens the block and reduces field bleeding, and a plain solution gives shorter and shallower anesthesia. Dismissing the history without asking would miss a real and documented reaction.
  21. The correct answer is C (Five cartridges, because 6.6 mg per kg permits 297 mg here): mepivacaine 3 percent is 30 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds 54 mg. The weight based limit is 6.6 mg per kg times 45 kg, which is 297 mg, comfortably below the 400 mg absolute ceiling, so the weight based figure governs. Dividing 297 by 54 gives 5.5, so 5 whole cartridges may be given, delivering 270 mg. A sixth cartridge would bring the total to 324 mg and exceed the calculated maximum. Because mepivacaine 3 percent is a plain solution with no vasoconstrictor, there is no second epinephrine limit to calculate and compare.
  22. The correct answer is D (Six cartridges, because the absolute 500 mg articaine ceiling governs this case): articaine 4 percent is 40 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds 72 mg. The weight based figure is 7.0 mg per kg times 90 kg, which is 630 mg, but that exceeds the absolute ceiling of 500 mg, so 500 mg is the working limit. Dividing 500 by 72 gives 6.9, so 6 whole cartridges delivering 432 mg, since a seventh would deliver 504 mg. Epinephrine 1:100,000 gives 0.018 mg per cartridge, and the healthy adult ceiling of 0.2 mg divided by 0.018 allows 11 cartridges. The lower of the two limits governs, so six.
  23. The correct answer is B (Ten cartridges, because the absolute 90 mg bupivacaine ceiling governs here): bupivacaine 0.5 percent is 5 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds only 9 mg. The weight based figure is 1.3 mg per kg times 75 kg, which is 97.5 mg, but that exceeds the absolute ceiling of 90 mg, so 90 mg is the working limit and 90 divided by 9 gives exactly 10 cartridges. Epinephrine 1:200,000 gives 0.009 mg per cartridge, and the healthy adult ceiling of 0.2 mg divided by 0.009 allows 22 cartridges, so the drug limit governs. Bupivacaine is the most cardiotoxic agent in this group and is avoided in children.
  24. The correct answer is A (Seven cartridges, because the absolute 400 mg ceiling governs at this weight): mepivacaine 3 percent is 30 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds 54 mg. The weight based figure is 6.6 mg per kg times 70 kg, which is 462 mg, but that exceeds the absolute ceiling of 400 mg, so 400 mg is the working limit. Dividing 400 by 54 gives 7.4, so 7 whole cartridges delivering 378 mg, while an eighth would deliver 432 mg and breach the ceiling. Whenever the weight based figure and the absolute maximum disagree, the lower number is always the one used. A plain solution has no second epinephrine limit.
  25. The correct answer is C (Four cartridges, because each cartridge delivers 0.009 mg of epinephrine): epinephrine 1:200,000 is 0.005 mg per mL, so a 1.8 mL cartridge holds 0.009 mg, and the cardiovascular disease ceiling of 0.04 mg divided by 0.009 gives 4.4, so 4 cartridges delivering 0.036 mg. A fifth would deliver 0.045 mg and breach the limit. The articaine side allows far more: 7.0 mg per kg times 80 kg is 560 mg, which is cut to the 500 mg absolute ceiling, and 500 divided by 72 mg per cartridge gives 6 whole cartridges. The lower limit governs, so four cartridges, and the 1:50,000 concentration is avoided entirely in cardiovascular disease.
  26. The correct answer is B (Inhibitory pathways block first, so excitation precedes seizure and then depression): local anesthetic systemic toxicity tracks plasma concentration and progresses in a fixed order, and because inhibitory central nervous system pathways are blocked first the early picture is excitatory, giving circumoral numbness, metallic taste, tinnitus, lightheadedness, blurred vision, slurred speech and muscle twitching. That progresses to a tonic clonic seizure, then unconsciousness, respiratory depression and arrest, with cardiovascular effects following as an initial rise in heart rate and blood pressure and then myocardial depression, bradycardia, hypotension and arrest. Stop injecting, position supine, maintain the airway, give 100 percent oxygen, activate emergency medical services and protect rather than restrain during any seizure.
  27. The correct answer is D (Record, reassure, review regularly, and refer if it persists past two months): paresthesia is altered sensation persisting after the anesthetic should have worn off, most often affecting the lingual nerve after an inferior alveolar block, and the 4 percent formulations articaine and prilocaine are reported disproportionately. Most cases resolve spontaneously within eight weeks, so the correct approach is careful documentation of the extent and quality of the altered sensation, reassurance, and scheduled review, with referral to an oral and maxillofacial surgeon beyond about two months. Immediate surgical exploration is not indicated at three days, and a dental hygienist does not prescribe corticosteroids. Telling her nothing needs recording would leave no baseline to compare against.
  28. The correct answer is C (Apply direct pressure now, ice for one day, expect discolouration up to 14 days): a rapidly enlarging swelling in the cheek immediately after a posterior superior alveolar block is a hematoma, classically from puncture of the pterygoid venous plexus. Apply firm direct pressure at once over the area to limit further bleeding, then intermittent ice for the first 24 hours to promote vasoconstriction, and warn the client that the discolouration takes about 7 to 14 days to clear as the blood breaks down. Avoid heat and avoid aspirin for the first 24 hours, since both would worsen bleeding. Antibiotics are not routinely indicated, and heat is used only after the first day.
  29. The correct answer is A (Apply heat, take an analgesic, do gentle opening exercises and eat soft food): restricted opening a day or two after an inferior alveolar block, without fever, swelling or pus, is trismus from muscle trauma or bleeding into the medial pterygoid muscle. Management is moist heat, an analgesic, gentle jaw opening and lateral excursion exercises and a soft diet, with most cases settling within several days to a few weeks. Complete rest and prolonged ice would encourage further stiffness. There is no sign of infection so an antibiotic is not indicated, and a fractured needle is rare and would usually be evident at the time of injection.
  30. The correct answer is B (Intravascular injection; aspirate in two planes and inject 1 mL per 30 seconds): sudden palpitations and tachycardia within seconds of injection, settling within a couple of minutes, is the classic picture of epinephrine entering a vessel directly, because the epinephrine reaches the heart immediately and is then rapidly metabolized. Prevention is aspiration in two planes before depositing solution and a slow injection rate of no faster than 1 mL per 30 seconds. A metabisulphite reaction would produce allergic features rather than a brief tachycardia, systemic toxicity from lidocaine gives excitatory central nervous system signs rather than isolated palpitations, and a vasovagal episode produces pallor, sweating and a slow weak pulse.
Case C067

A 34-year-old client attends for quadrant scaling and root planing and reports marked dental anxiety. The dental hygienist holds current certification in nitrous oxide and oxygen sedation, and provincial legislation permits her to administer it. Health history lists seasonal allergic rhinitis and well controlled asthma, with no other conditions. Blood pressure is 118/74 mmHg and pulse is 82 beats per minute. Today the client says both sides of her nose feel completely blocked and she is breathing through her mouth.

  1. The correct answer is C (Complete bilateral nasal blockage, because the gas is delivered nasally): nitrous oxide and oxygen sedation is delivered through a nasal hood, so a client who cannot breathe through the nose cannot receive a titratable dose and will simply mouth breathe room air. Nasal obstruction from any cause, including the allergic rhinitis this client has today, is an absolute barrier to delivery and the appointment should be rescheduled once the nose is clear. Well controlled asthma is not a contraindication, and nitrous oxide is often helpful because it reduces anxiety triggered bronchospasm. Anxiety is the reason for offering the sedation, not a reason to withhold it, and her vital signs are entirely normal.
  2. The correct answer is A (Fit the nasal hood and deliver 100 percent oxygen at 6 litres per minute): administration always begins with the hood seated and a flow of 100 percent oxygen matched to the client's tidal volume, usually 5 to 7 litres per minute, so the reservoir bag can be watched and the fit checked before any nitrous oxide is added. Only then is nitrous oxide introduced in small increments with time between them to judge the response. Starting at a preset percentage ignores the fact that the correct dose is the individual clinical response and not a fixed number. Asking her to mouth breathe defeats a nasal delivery system entirely.
  3. The correct answer is D (Reduce the nitrous oxide percentage while leaving the nasal hood in place): sweating, nausea, dysphoria and a fixed stare are the classic signs of oversedation, and the response is to titrate the concentration downward in small steps until the client is comfortable again. The hood stays on because the client still needs the oxygen flowing through it, and because removing it abruptly loses all control of the gas mixture. Holding the same concentration allows the unpleasant effects to continue and increases the chance of vomiting. Raising the flow rate without dropping the percentage does not reduce the nitrous oxide the client is receiving.
  4. The correct answer is B (Deliver 100 percent oxygen through the hood for at least five minutes): at termination the client is given 100 percent oxygen for at least 3 to 5 minutes to prevent diffusion hypoxia, in which nitrous oxide floods rapidly out of the blood into the alveoli and dilutes the alveolar oxygen, producing headache, nausea and lethargy. Discharge follows only when the client is back at her preoperative baseline. Removing the hood immediately is precisely what allows diffusion hypoxia to occur. Holding a low percentage of nitrous oxide continues to deliver the drug rather than washing it out, and a single minute of nasal cannula oxygen is far too short.
  5. The correct answer is D (A well fitted scavenging nasal hood connected to a working vacuum system): scavenging at source is the single most effective control for occupational exposure to waste nitrous oxide, and it only works if the hood actually fits and the vacuum is running and checked. It is supported by good room air exchange, routine leak testing of hoses and connections, and periodic ambient monitoring. A respirator does not filter nitrous oxide and gives no protection against it. Arbitrarily capping appointment length and opening a door between clients do nothing to capture the gas escaping around the hood while the client is actually being sedated.
Case C068

A 27-year-old client with no known drug allergies rinses with chlorhexidine before periodontal debridement. Within four minutes she reports itching and a tightening throat. The hygienist sees widespread hives across the neck and chest, swelling of the lips, and hears audible wheeze and hoarseness. The client is pale and says she feels faint. Blood pressure is 82/48 mmHg and pulse is 128 beats per minute. The emergency kit, oxygen and an automated external defibrillator are in the operatory.

  1. The correct answer is B (Skin, respiratory and circulatory systems are all involved together): anaphylaxis is defined by rapidly progressive involvement of two or more body systems, or hypotension following exposure to a known allergen, and this client has hives and lip swelling, wheeze and hoarseness, and a blood pressure of 82/48 mmHg with a pulse of 128 beats per minute. That multisystem picture is what separates anaphylaxis from a skin limited reaction that could be observed and treated with an antihistamine. Rapid onset raises suspicion but by itself does not define the diagnosis. Absence of a previously documented allergy is irrelevant, because a first exposure history is common, and widespread hives alone would still be a skin limited reaction.
  2. The correct answer is A (Give epinephrine 0.5 mg of 1:1000 intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh): epinephrine is the first line drug in anaphylaxis and nothing is allowed to delay it, because it is the only agent that reverses airway swelling, bronchospasm and vascular collapse at the same time. It is given before the telephone call, before oxygen and before any other drug, then emergency medical services are activated, the client is positioned and high flow oxygen follows. Calling for paramedics first costs minutes that this airway does not have. An antihistamine does nothing for airway obstruction or shock and is a secondary measure only, and oxygen alone cannot reverse the reaction driving the collapse.
  3. The correct answer is C (0.5 mg of a 1:1000 solution given intramuscularly into the outer thigh): the office ampoule is the 1:1000 concentration, which is 1 mg per mL, and the adult dose is 0.3 to 0.5 mg, so 0.5 mg is half a millilitre injected intramuscularly into the anterolateral thigh. A child under 30 kg receives 0.15 mg. The 1:10,000 concentration is the intravenous cardiac arrest preparation and is far too dilute for an intramuscular anaphylaxis dose. The deltoid and the subcutaneous route both give slower and less reliable absorption than the vastus lateralis, and 1 mg sublingually is both the wrong dose and a route that has no place in this emergency.
  4. The correct answer is A (Supine with the legs elevated to support venous return to the heart): after epinephrine has been given, a hypotensive client who is still breathing is laid supine with the legs raised so that venous return and cerebral perfusion are maintained. A client who is struggling to breathe may be allowed to sit up, but this client's dominant problem is a blood pressure of 84/50 mmHg and lightheadedness whenever the chair is raised. Sitting her fully upright or putting her head between her knees risks sudden circulatory collapse. The recovery position is for an unresponsive client who is breathing normally, and this client is awake and being actively monitored.
  5. The correct answer is D (Repeat intramuscular epinephrine, which may be given every 5 to 15 minutes): persistent wheeze, persistent swelling and persistent hypotension seven minutes after the first dose mean the reaction has not been controlled, and epinephrine is repeated at intervals of 5 to 15 minutes until improvement or until paramedics take over. Waiting for the ambulance while the airway continues to swell is the most dangerous option on the list. A corticosteroid has a delayed onset measured in hours and does nothing for the airway now. A further antihistamine dose treats the itch and the hives only, and neither of those drugs substitutes for epinephrine.
Case C069

A 68-year-old client with stable angina, type 2 diabetes and hypertension is 20 minutes into quadrant scaling when he reports crushing substernal pressure spreading to his left jaw and arm. He is sweating, nauseated and short of breath. He carries his own nitroglycerin spray in his jacket. He mentions that he took tadalafil for erectile dysfunction last night. Blood pressure is 138/86 mmHg and pulse is 96 beats per minute.

  1. The correct answer is B (Withhold it, because tadalafil within 48 hours risks catastrophic hypotension): nitroglycerin is a vasodilator and combining it with a phosphodiesterase 5 inhibitor can cause a profound and unrecoverable fall in blood pressure. Sildenafil taken within 24 hours and tadalafil taken within 48 hours are both absolute reasons to withhold nitrates, and this client took tadalafil last night. The correct course is to continue oxygen, keep him at rest, and activate emergency medical services because the pain is severe and accompanied by sweating and breathlessness. A reduced dose still carries the same interaction, and a high systolic pressure does not make the combination safe.
  2. The correct answer is C (Acetylsalicylic acid 160 to 325 mg, chewed and then swallowed): pain that is severe, lasts beyond 15 minutes and comes with sweating, nausea and breathlessness is treated as a myocardial infarction, and after emergency medical services are activated and oxygen is running the client chews and swallows 160 to 325 mg of acetylsalicylic acid unless he is allergic or actively bleeding. Chewing speeds absorption and early antiplatelet effect improves survival. Nitroglycerin remains contraindicated here because of the tadalafil taken last night. Salbutamol treats bronchospasm, which he does not have, and oral glucose is irrelevant when there is no evidence of hypoglycemia.
  3. The correct answer is A (Begin chest compressions, because agonal gasping counts as absent breathing): a client who is unresponsive with no carotid pulse after a check of no more than 10 seconds is in cardiac arrest, and occasional irregular gasping is agonal breathing which is treated as no breathing at all. Compressions start immediately on the lower half of the sternum, at least 5 cm and no more than 6 cm deep, at 100 to 120 per minute with full recoil, in cycles of 30 compressions to 2 breaths. Delaying compressions to give breaths first or to wait for the defibrillator directly reduces survival. The recovery position is only for an unresponsive client who has a pulse and is breathing normally.
  4. The correct answer is B (Resume chest compressions immediately without pausing to reassess the pulse): after any shock is delivered, compressions restart at once, because a perfusing rhythm rarely returns instantly and every pause in compressions lowers coronary perfusion pressure. The defibrillator will prompt the team to stop again for its next rhythm analysis after about two minutes, which is also when the compressor changes over. Rechecking the pulse for ten seconds immediately after the shock wastes exactly the time compressions are needed most. Giving ventilations and then standing by leaves the client without circulation, and the pads stay on so that further shocks can be delivered without delay.
  5. The correct answer is D (Place the client supine in the chair with the legs slightly elevated): syncope is transient cerebral hypoperfusion from a vasovagal response, and the immediate priority after stopping treatment is to restore blood flow to the brain by laying the client flat with the legs slightly raised. Loosening tight clothing, opening the airway, checking breathing, giving oxygen and applying a cold compress all follow once she is positioned. Giving oxygen or a cold compress while she is still upright does not address the reason she lost consciousness. Emergency medical services are called only if consciousness fails to return within about a minute, which has not yet happened.
  6. The correct answer is C (Activate emergency medical services, since recovery should occur within a minute): a simple vasovagal faint reverses within seconds to about a minute once the client is supine with the legs raised, so a client who is still unconscious after that point has something more serious and needs paramedics. The hygienist keeps her supine, maintains the airway, continues oxygen and monitors breathing and pulse while waiting. Sitting her upright removes the very thing that is restoring cerebral perfusion and can deepen the episode. Repeated aromatic ammonia is not a substitute for calling for help, and there is no evidence of low blood sugar in this presentation.
  7. The correct answer is A (Orthostatic hypotension, from a blood pressure drop on changing position): the symptoms appeared at the moment he was moved from supine to upright, and his skin is warm and dry, which separates this from a vasovagal faint. Diuretics and angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors are two of the classic causes, along with nitrates and tricyclic antidepressants. A vasovagal episode is preceded by pallor, sweating and nausea, none of which he has. His breathing is not rapid or deep, so respiratory alkalosis does not fit, and low blood sugar would not appear at the exact instant the chair was raised.
  8. The correct answer is D (Return him to supine with legs raised, then raise the chair in stages): management of orthostatic hypotension is to reverse the position change that caused it, allow the blood pressure to recover, then bring the client back up gradually with a seated pause before standing. Escorting him only once he is steady prevents a fall on the way to reception. Keeping him upright leaves him hypotensive and at risk of collapsing in the chair. Standing him immediately makes the pressure drop worse, and oral glucose treats a problem he does not have while doing nothing about his position.
  9. The correct answer is B (Stop treatment, sit her upright and coach slow controlled breathing): rapid deep breathing driven by anxiety causes respiratory alkalosis, which produces the circumoral and fingertip tingling, lightheadedness and carpopedal spasm described here. Treatment is to stop the procedure, position the client upright, calm her and coach a slow rate of about one breath every 5 seconds, having her rebreathe into cupped hands to raise carbon dioxide. Laying her flat and applying oxygen would worsen the alkalosis, and hyperventilation is the one dental emergency in which supplemental oxygen is withheld. There is no bronchospasm to treat and no reason to suspect hypoglycemia.
  10. The correct answer is C (The problem is too little carbon dioxide, not too little oxygen): in hyperventilation the client is blowing off carbon dioxide, producing respiratory alkalosis, while arterial oxygen is already normal or high, so added oxygen corrects nothing and simply delays the return of carbon dioxide to normal. Coaching a slow rate and rebreathing exhaled air is what actually reverses the chemistry. This is the single recognized exception to the rule that oxygen is given in every dental office emergency. Oxygen does not mask anxiety, is not restricted to unconscious clients, and does not irritate the airway or provoke spasm.
  11. The correct answer is A (Sit her upright leaning forward and give two puffs of her salbutamol): an asthma attack is managed by stopping treatment, sitting the client upright and leaning forward to use the accessory muscles, and giving her own short acting bronchodilator, salbutamol two puffs of 100 micrograms each, with a spacer if one is available. Oxygen follows and the bronchodilator is repeated every few minutes as needed. Laying an asthmatic client supine makes the work of breathing harder rather than easier. Paramedics are called if there is no improvement or if the attack worsens, but the inhaler comes first, and an antihistamine has no role in acute bronchospasm.
  12. The correct answer is B (Airflow is severely reduced, so emergency medical services are needed now): a wheeze that fades while the client is still struggling is a silent chest, which means so little air is moving that there is not enough flow left to generate the sound. Speaking in single words and recruiting accessory muscles point the same way, and this combination after three failed bronchodilator doses is an immediate call for paramedics while oxygen and salbutamol continue. Reading the vanishing wheeze as improvement is the trap and it is potentially fatal. She is not hyperventilating, and salbutamol is not reversed in this situation.
  13. The correct answer is D (Stop the causative agent, give diphenhydramine 25 to 50 mg and observe): this reaction is limited to the skin, with normal speech, a clear chest, a normal blood pressure and a normal pulse, so it is a mild allergic reaction rather than anaphylaxis. Management is to remove the causative agent, give an oral antihistamine such as diphenhydramine 25 to 50 mg, observe for at least an hour in case the picture progresses, and document the reaction for the health history. Epinephrine and supine positioning with oxygen are reserved for multisystem involvement or hypotension, neither of which is present. Sending her home immediately removes the observation period during which deterioration would be detected.
  14. The correct answer is A (Encourage her to keep coughing forcefully and take no other action): an obstruction is partial while the client can speak, cough forcefully or breathe, and a forceful cough generates higher airway pressures than any manoeuvre a rescuer can apply. The hygienist stays with her, keeps her calm, keeps suction ready and watches closely for any change to a complete obstruction. Back blows and abdominal thrusts are for a complete obstruction and can dislodge a partly held object into a worse position. A blind finger sweep is never performed, because it risks pushing the object deeper into the airway.
  15. The correct answer is C (Give five back blows, then five abdominal thrusts, alternating them): inability to speak, cough or move air despite obvious effort defines a complete obstruction in a conscious client, and the sequence is 5 back blows between the scapulae with the client leaning forward, then 5 abdominal thrusts, alternating until the object clears or she becomes unresponsive. Chest compressions and rescue breathing belong to the unresponsive client, not to one who is standing and conscious. Laying her down while she is still conscious wastes time and removes the leverage the manoeuvres need. A blind finger sweep is never performed, and an object is removed only when it can actually be seen.
  16. The correct answer is B (Start chest compressions and check the mouth before each set of breaths): once a choking client becomes unresponsive she is lowered to the floor, emergency medical services are activated and chest compressions begin, because compressions generate the airway pressure most likely to expel the object. The mouth is inspected before each set of ventilations and an object is removed only if it is visible. Abdominal thrusts are not continued on an unresponsive client on the floor. A blind finger sweep of the throat can drive the object deeper, and the recovery position is inappropriate for a client whose airway is obstructed.
  17. The correct answer is D (0.3 to 0.4 mg sublingually, repeated every 5 minutes to three doses): nitroglycerin for angina is given sublingually as a 0.3 mg or 0.4 mg tablet or spray and may be repeated every 5 minutes to a maximum of three doses, alongside stopping treatment, semi upright positioning and oxygen. If pain persists after three doses or beyond about 15 minutes, the episode is treated as a myocardial infarction and paramedics are called. Swallowing the tablet sends it through first pass hepatic metabolism and destroys the rapid effect that the sublingual route provides. Doubling the individual dose and stopping after a single dose both depart from the recognized regimen.
  18. The correct answer is C (Place her in the recovery position, give oxygen and monitor her): an unresponsive client who has normal breathing and a strong pulse is not in cardiac arrest, so the airway is protected with the recovery position, oxygen is applied and breathing, pulse and responsiveness are monitored continuously until paramedics arrive. Starting compressions on a client with an output can cause injury and is not indicated. Bag valve mask ventilation at a rate of 30 compressions to 2 breaths belongs to an arrest, and this client is ventilating adequately on her own. A defibrillator will not advise a shock in a client with a perfusing rhythm.
  19. The correct answer is A (Activate emergency medical services and record when she was last well): facial droop, arm weakness and speech difficulty are the recognition triad for stroke, and time is the treatment, so paramedics are called at once with a clear statement that stroke is suspected and with the time the client was last known to be well, because that time determines eligibility for reperfusion. She is then kept at rest with the head slightly elevated, given oxygen, given nothing by mouth because swallowing may be impaired, and monitored. Acetylsalicylic acid is withheld until imaging excludes a bleed. Oral glucose is contraindicated when swallowing is unreliable, and lying her flat with legs raised is not the recommended stroke position.
  20. The correct answer is D (The stroke may be hemorrhagic, so brain imaging must come first): a stroke can be ischemic or hemorrhagic and the two cannot be told apart at the chairside, so an antiplatelet drug given before imaging could enlarge a bleed and cause catastrophic harm. This is the key contrast with a suspected myocardial infarction, where chewed acetylsalicylic acid is given early. The drug does not become useless after three hours, and the concern is not the swallowing route in a conscious client, although anything by mouth is avoided anyway when swallowing may be impaired. Acetylsalicylic acid has no interaction with supplemental oxygen.
  21. The correct answer is B (15 g of fast acting carbohydrate, then recheck after fifteen minutes): a conscious client who can swallow safely with a glucose of 3.1 mmol/L is hypoglycemic, and the rule is 15 g of fast acting carbohydrate such as 150 mL of juice, 3 teaspoons of sugar in water or 4 glucose tablets, wait 15 minutes, recheck and repeat once if she is still below 4.0 mmol/L, then give a longer acting snack. A protein rich snack is absorbed far too slowly to correct an acute drop. A very large carbohydrate load followed by immediate discharge skips the recheck that confirms recovery, and glucagon is reserved for a client who cannot swallow.
  22. The correct answer is A (Give nothing by mouth, use the recovery position and give glucagon 1 mg): an unresponsive client must receive nothing by mouth because of the aspiration risk, so the airway is protected in the recovery position, oxygen is applied, emergency medical services are activated and glucagon 1 mg is given intramuscularly by a trained team member from the office kit. Placing gel in the cheek and giving juice through a straw both put liquid into the mouth of a client who cannot protect his own airway. Withholding available glucagon while waiting for paramedics leaves treatable hypoglycemia untreated for exactly the minutes that matter most.
  23. The correct answer is C (Hyperglycemia, because the onset was slow with thirst and acetone breath): hyperglycemia develops over hours to days with thirst, frequent urination, dry mucosa, vomiting, abdominal pain, deep rapid Kussmaul breathing, an acetone breath odour and drowsiness, which is exactly this picture. Management is to stop treatment, activate emergency medical services because he is seriously unwell, give oxygen and monitor him. Hypoglycemia comes on within minutes and produces shakiness, sweating and pallor rather than a two day history of thirst. Anxious overbreathing does not cause vomiting or an acetone odour, and there is no allergen exposure, no urticaria and no airway swelling here.
  24. The correct answer is D (Treat as hypoglycemia, because untreated low glucose kills far faster): when a client with diabetes is unwell and the blood glucose cannot be measured, the safe default is to treat for hypoglycemia, because a low glucose causes irreversible neurological damage within minutes while a high glucose develops and worsens over hours. This client is alert and can swallow, so he receives 15 g of fast acting carbohydrate while help is arranged, and the small amount of added sugar does no meaningful harm if he turns out to be hyperglycemic. Withholding treatment until paramedics measure the glucose risks exactly the outcome the rule exists to prevent. Water treats neither state, and glucagon is reserved for a client who cannot swallow.
  25. The correct answer is B (Remove everything from her mouth and clear hard objects away from her): the first priority in a generalized tonic clonic seizure is to make the environment safe, which means clearing instruments, the saliva ejector and any materials from the mouth, moving the bracket table and sharp objects away, lowering the chair to supine, protecting the head and noting the start time. The client is never restrained, because forced resistance against a convulsion causes joint and muscle injury. Nothing is ever placed between the teeth, since it can fracture teeth and obstruct the airway. Sitting her upright during convulsions risks a fall from the chair.
  26. The correct answer is A (The convulsive activity has continued for longer than five minutes): emergency medical services are activated when a seizure lasts more than 5 minutes, when a second seizure follows without recovery in between, when it is a first ever seizure, or when consciousness does not return afterwards. Prolonged convulsive activity threatens the airway and oxygenation and needs drug treatment the office cannot give. A short postictal drowsy phase is expected and is managed with the recovery position, oxygen and observation. Incontinence and a bitten tongue are common features of an ordinary tonic clonic seizure and are not by themselves reasons to call.
  27. The correct answer is C (Early central nervous system signs of local anesthetic systemic toxicity): rising plasma concentration blocks inhibitory pathways first, so the earliest features are excitatory and sensory, namely circumoral numbness away from the injection sites, a metallic taste, tinnitus, lightheadedness, blurred vision and slurred speech, progressing to twitching and then to a tonic clonic seizure. Recognizing this stage and acting on it is what prevents the respiratory and cardiovascular collapse that follows. A vasovagal faint produces pallor, sweating and a slow weak pulse, not tinnitus and a metallic taste. Methemoglobinemia appears 1 to 3 hours later with cyanosis that does not respond to oxygen, and a true allergy to an amide is rare and presents with urticaria, wheeze or swelling.
  28. The correct answer is D (Position her supine, open the airway and give 100 percent oxygen): once injection has been stopped, management of local anesthetic systemic toxicity is supine positioning, airway maintenance, 100 percent oxygen and activation of emergency medical services, with protection from injury if a seizure develops and readiness for basic life support. Oxygen limits the hypoxia and acidosis that make the toxicity worse. Restraining a twitching or convulsing client causes injury and is never done. An oral antihistamine has no effect on anesthetic toxicity and cannot be given safely to someone who may seize, and sitting a client upright who may lose consciousness compromises both the airway and cerebral perfusion.
  29. The correct answer is B (Epinephrine, nitroglycerin, salbutamol, acetylsalicylic acid, an antihistamine and glucose): the dental office emergency kit carries epinephrine 1:1000 for anaphylaxis, nitroglycerin spray or tablets for angina, a salbutamol inhaler for asthma, chewable acetylsalicylic acid 325 mg for suspected myocardial infarction, an antihistamine such as diphenhydramine for mild allergic reactions, oral glucose for hypoglycemia and aromatic ammonia inhalants, alongside oxygen and its delivery devices. One named person checks contents and expiry dates on a documented schedule. Morphine, atropine, amiodarone, intravenous lipid emulsion and methylene blue are hospital and advanced life support agents that dental offices do not stock or administer. A list without epinephrine leaves the one drug that anaphylaxis absolutely requires.
  30. The correct answer is C (Non rebreather at 10 to 15 litres, then bag valve mask at 15 litres): a conscious client who is breathing spontaneously and adequately receives a non rebreather mask at 10 to 15 litres per minute, or a nasal cannula at 2 to 6 litres per minute for a milder need. A client who is not breathing adequately needs positive pressure ventilation by bag valve mask connected to oxygen at 15 litres per minute, one breath every 6 seconds while a pulse is present, or 2 breaths after every 30 compressions when there is no pulse. A nasal cannula cannot deliver 15 litres and a non rebreather at 4 litres will collapse its reservoir bag. A bag valve mask cannot ventilate anyone at 6 litres, and a nasal cannula gives no ventilation at all to an apnoeic client.
Case C070

A caregiver brings a 26-month-old child for a first dental hygiene visit. The child fell asleep with a bottle of milk most nights until three months ago and still drinks juice from a sippy cup through the day. Lift-the-lip screening shows chalky white demineralization along the cervical margins of teeth 51, 52, 61 and 62. The mandibular incisors look sound. The community water is fluoridated at 0.7 mg/L. The child cries and clings to the caregiver.

  1. The correct answer is B (The tongue and submandibular duct flow shield the mandibular incisors during feeding): During bottle or sippy cup feeding the tongue covers the mandibular incisors while pooled liquid bathes the maxillary incisors, and submandibular and sublingual saliva flows directly over the lower anterior teeth. That is why the classic early childhood caries pattern starts on teeth 51, 52, 61 and 62 and spares the lower incisors. The mandibular central incisors 71 and 81 actually erupt first at 6 to 10 months, so they have had the longest exposure, not the shortest. Primary enamel thickness does not differ meaningfully between arches, and cariogenic bacteria colonize the whole mouth.
  2. The correct answer is D (Teeth 55, 65, 75 and 85 erupt last, at 23 to 33 months, completing the dentition): The primary sequence runs central incisor, lateral incisor, first molar, canine, second molar, and the second primary molars are the last to arrive at 23 to 33 months, completing the dentition by about 30 to 36 months. At 26 months this child would be expected to have incisors, first molars and canines present with the second molars still erupting, which fits the reported night-time unsettledness. The first primary molars 54, 64, 74 and 84 come in at 13 to 19 months, before the canines at 16 to 23 months. The mandibular central incisors 71 and 81 are the first teeth of all, at 6 to 10 months.
  3. The correct answer is A (An adult brushes twice daily using a rice grain sized smear of fluoride paste): Under 3 years an adult does the brushing, and fluoridated toothpaste is used based on risk assessment in an amount the size of a grain of rice. This child has visible white spot lesions and a bottle and sippy cup history, so the risk assessment supports fluoride paste rather than withholding it. The green pea amount belongs to the 3 to 6 year band, and a toddler cannot brush effectively alone. A full brush head plus a rinse would deliver far too much fluoride to a child who swallows what is placed in the mouth.
  4. The correct answer is C (Use tell show do with the caregiver present and agreed with them beforehand): Tell show do is the foundation technique: describe the step in age-appropriate words, demonstrate it on a fingernail or a model, then perform it exactly as demonstrated. Caregiver presence is itself a behaviour guidance technique and the decision is made together with the caregiver, so removing a distressed toddler from the person they are clinging to works against the plan. Protective stabilization is a last resort needing specific informed consent and is restricted in some jurisdictions, and it is not justified for a screening. Postponing care for two years abandons active demineralization.
  5. The correct answer is A (5 percent sodium fluoride varnish, because very little fluoride is ingested): Fluoride varnish at 5 percent sodium fluoride, about 22 600 ppm, sets on contact with saliva and adheres to the tooth, so the amount swallowed is very small. That is exactly why it is the safest professional agent for a child who cannot expectorate. Tray delivered gel is not appropriate for a toddler who swallows, and mouthrinse is not recommended under 6 years for the same reason. Prescription strength 1.1 percent paste is a home product for older clients at high risk, not a professional application here.
Case C071

An 8-year-old child attends with a caregiver. Teeth 36 and 46 are fully erupted with deep, narrow, stained but noncavitated occlusal fissures. Teeth 16 and 26 are partially erupted with an operculum covering the distal half of each occlusal surface. The child has two restored primary molars, drinks sugary sports drinks after soccer, and brushes once a day. The practice has cotton rolls, dry angles and high volume evacuation, but no rubber dam.

  1. The correct answer is D (Seal 36 and 46 now and review 16 and 26 once they are fully erupted): Teeth 36 and 46 meet every indication: fully erupted, deep narrow retentive noncavitated fissures, in a child at elevated risk, and they can be isolated. Teeth 16 and 26 are covered by an operculum over the distal half of the occlusal surface, so the fissure cannot be cleaned, etched or kept dry, and a sealant placed there would fail. Waiting a further twelve months to seal the two teeth that are ready leaves active risk untreated. Partial eruption does not improve etching, it prevents reliable isolation.
  2. The correct answer is B (A glycoprotein film blocks resin from flowing into the etched enamel porosities): Saliva deposits a glycoprotein film on etched enamel within seconds, and that film occludes the microporosities created by the acid. Resin can then no longer flow in, so the resin tags that provide micromechanical retention never form and the sealant lifts off. Moisture contamination of etched enamel is the single commonest reason a sealant fails. The etchant has already been rinsed away so it is not still working, curing is not temperature limited in this way, and varnish is applied after placement, not before etching.
  3. The correct answer is C (Rinse, re-isolate with dry cotton rolls, re-etch and confirm a frosty appearance): Once etched enamel is contaminated the surface must be rinsed, re-isolated with fresh dry cotton rolls, re-etched and dried again until the frosty chalky white appearance is confirmed. Drying alone does not remove the glycoprotein film, so the porosities stay blocked and the sealant will still fail. Extra curing time cures resin that was never mechanically locked into the enamel, which changes nothing. Adding a bonding agent over a contaminated surface bonds to the film rather than to etched enamel.
  4. The correct answer is D (Adjust the high spot, then apply fluoride varnish to the treated teeth): The closing sequence is to check retention, check the occlusion and adjust any high spot, then apply fluoride varnish, because etching removed the fluoride-rich outer layer of the enamel surrounding the sealant. A high occlusal contact will not settle by itself and can fracture the sealant or cause discomfort, so it is adjusted at the appointment. Adding more resin to a surface that is already too high makes the problem worse. Normal brushing continues the same day, and withholding fluoride after etching removes exactly the protection the surrounding enamel now needs.
  5. The correct answer is A (Place a glass ionomer sealant now and replace it with resin once isolation is possible): Where isolation is impossible, a glass ionomer sealant is the interim choice, because it is far less moisture sensitive than resin, releases fluoride and can be replaced with a resin sealant once the tooth can be isolated. A resin sealant placed on a surface that cannot be kept dry will not retain, and knowingly placing one that fails gives false reassurance. Waiting does nothing for a child whose caries activity has clearly increased. Restoring sound occlusal surfaces removes healthy tooth structure for no diagnosed lesion.
Case C072

A 28-year-old client attends for scaling at 26 weeks of her first pregnancy, which is uncomplicated.
