She reports generalized bleeding when brushing, morning sickness that continues into the afternoon, and a painless red nodule on the labial gingiva between teeth 12 and 13 that bleeds when she eats. Her last radiographs were four years ago and a deep distal lesion is suspected on tooth 46. She asks whether anything should wait until after delivery.

  1. The correct answer is C (Dental hygiene care is safe now, and delaying it leaves infection untreated): Dental hygiene care is safe throughout pregnancy and should not be deferred, because untreated infection is the greater risk to both client and pregnancy. Assessment, scaling, root planing, justified radiographs, local anesthesia and preventive agents are all appropriate in every trimester. Elective care may be scheduled for the client's comfort, and the second trimester is often the most comfortable, but urgent care is never postponed. Restricting care to emergencies or to a single trimester is the myth that causes harm, and singling out radiographs and anesthesia repeats the same error.
  2. The correct answer is B (Roll her fully onto her left side to relieve inferior vena cava compression): After about 20 weeks the gravid uterus can compress the inferior vena cava in the supine position, cutting venous return and producing supine hypotensive syndrome with dizziness, nausea, sweating and falling blood pressure. Rolling the client fully onto her left side lifts the uterus off the vessel and restores venous return within minutes. Elevating the legs while she stays supine does not relieve the compression that is causing it. Fast carbohydrate treats hypoglycemia, which does not fit this picture, and a wedge belongs under the right hip to tilt her to the left as prevention, not as rescue.
  3. The correct answer is A (Take the justified bitewing using rectangular collimation and a fast digital receptor): Radiographs in pregnancy are taken when they are justified, not withheld, and fetal dose from a dental bitewing is negligible. The protections that matter most are justification, rectangular collimation and fast digital receptors, since these reduce the dose at source. A leaded apron and thyroid collar remain standard Canadian practice but they are not the main protection, so relying on the apron alone misses the point. Deferring a radiograph that would change the diagnosis leaves a possible dentinal lesion untreated, and there is no trimester in which the image becomes safer.
  4. The correct answer is D (Debride the local plaque, reinforce home care and review it after delivery): A pyogenic granuloma, also called a pregnancy granuloma, is a painless red mass that bleeds readily and sits interproximally on the labial gingiva of the anterior maxilla. It arises as an exaggerated inflammatory response to local plaque and it regresses after delivery, so local debridement, home care and review are the first line. Excision during pregnancy is reserved for a lesion that interferes with function or bleeds excessively, and this one does neither. The clinical picture is not candidal, and a classic pregnancy granuloma does not carry a malignant presentation.
  5. The correct answer is B (Teeth 36 and 46 with the mandibular central incisors, at six to seven years): The mandibular first molars 36 and 46 and the mandibular central incisors 31 and 41 erupt first in the permanent dentition at 6 to 7 years, along with the maxillary first molars 16 and 26. The first permanent molars erupt distal to the primary second molars rather than replacing anything, which is exactly why caregivers assume a primary tooth was lost. First premolars follow at 10 to 12 years in the mandible, permanent canines at 9 to 12 years depending on arch, and second molars at 11 to 13 years.
  6. The correct answer is C (Maxillary canines erupt after both premolars, so leftover space is often limited): In the maxilla the order reverses compared with the mandible: first premolars 14 and 24 erupt at 10 to 11 years and second premolars 15 and 25 at 10 to 12 years, and only then do canines 13 and 23 arrive at 11 to 12 years. Because the canines are last into that segment, they take whatever space is left, which is why the maxillary canine is so often displaced or impacted. The mandibular canines 33 and 43 erupt earlier, at 9 to 10 years, before their premolars, so the two arches differ. Maxillary lateral incisors erupt at 8 to 9 years, well before the canines.
  7. The correct answer is D (Thin enamel and dentin with large pulp horns let caries reach the pulp quickly): Primary enamel and dentin are about half as thick as in a permanent tooth, and the pulp chamber is proportionally larger with pulp horns lying close to the dentinoenamel junction. A lesion therefore has a very short distance to travel before it produces pulpal symptoms, which is why a shallow looking lesion becomes symptomatic within months. Slender divergent roots and a prominent cervical ridge are real features of primary molars, but neither shortens the path from the occlusal surface to the pulp. The occlusal table of a primary molar is narrow buccolingually, which does not drive this progression.
  8. The correct answer is A (Take posterior bitewings, since broad flat contacts conceal proximal lesions): Proximal contacts between primary molars are broad and flat rather than small and rounded, so a lesion can be well established before anything is visible or catchable with an explorer. Posterior bitewings are the appropriate assessment for a child with closed contacts, food packing and demonstrated caries activity. Transillumination is a useful adjunct but it does not replace bitewings for posterior primary surfaces. A panoramic image has overlapping contacts and much lower resolution in that region, and waiting until exfoliation would let lesions reach the pulp of teeth needed for years yet.
  9. The correct answer is C (Remineralize using fluoride and casein phosphopeptide amorphous calcium phosphate): A noncavitated white spot lesion is demineralized enamel, so remineralization with fluoride and casein phosphopeptide amorphous calcium phosphate comes first, alongside plaque control around the brackets. Starting with a surface treatment such as microabrasion or restoration removes tooth structure that might still remineralize, and neither addresses the cause while the appliances remain. Bleaching lightens the surrounding enamel and can make the opacities look more obvious rather than less. A heavily hypermineralized surface layer can lock the white appearance in, so early intervention matters.
  10. The correct answer is B (White spots can form within four weeks, so add interdental aids and a fluoride rinse): White spot lesions can appear within about four weeks of bonding, most often on the gingival third of the maxillary lateral incisors and canines, so preventive measures start at the bonding appointment rather than at a later review. Home care around brackets needs interdental brushes, an orthodontic brush, floss threaders and a daily 0.05 percent sodium fluoride rinse, with fluoride varnish at recall. Waiting a year or waiting until debonding both allow demineralization that is difficult to reverse. These lesions are common but they are not inevitable, since plaque control and fluoride prevent them.
  11. The correct answer is A (Repeated self-induced vomiting, since this combination of signs is typical): Perimylolysis is erosion of the palatal surfaces of teeth 13 to 23 caused by repeated exposure to gastric acid, and restorations standing proud of the eroded surface confirm that tooth structure has been lost around them. Bilateral painless parotid enlargement, xerostomia and angular cheilitis accompany it, and this cluster of signs together points to repeated vomiting rather than any single cause acting alone. Brushing abrasion affects buccal cervical areas, not palatal surfaces of the upper anterior teeth. Bruxism wears incisal edges and occluding surfaces, and whitening gel does not dissolve enamel in this pattern.
  12. The correct answer is C (Rinse with water or sodium bicarbonate and delay brushing until later): Immediately after an acid exposure the enamel surface is softened, so brushing at that moment abrades away the softened layer and increases the total tissue loss. Rinsing with water or a sodium bicarbonate solution neutralizes and clears the acid, and brushing is delayed until the surface has rehardened. A whitening paste is more abrasive, which makes immediate brushing worse rather than better. An acidulated rinse adds further acid challenge to eroded surfaces, and fluoride is protective here, so avoiding it removes the main remineralizing agent available.
  13. The correct answer is D (A custom fabricated guard, because it retains without clenching and fits accurately): A custom fabricated mouthguard is made on a cast of the client's own arch, so it stays in place without clenching, is thinner where bulk is not needed and interferes far less with speech and breathing. Those properties are exactly what fix a client who removes the guard because of gagging and poor retention. Stock guards are the least retentive of all and a larger one would gag him more, while boil-and-bite guards sit between the two and still rely on clenching. A cage reduces but does not remove dental injury risk, and it gives no protection when it lifts or shifts.
  14. The correct answer is B (Nicotine vasoconstriction suppresses bleeding, so disease may be underestimated): Nicotine causes vasoconstriction in the gingival microcirculation, so bleeding on probing is suppressed and the tissues can look healthier than they are while attachment loss continues. A 6 percent bleeding score alongside 5 mm pockets, calculus and marginal redness is internally inconsistent, and the vaping history explains why. Excessive probing force would produce more bleeding, not less. Vaping aerosol reduces salivary flow rather than increasing it, which adds caries and mucosal risk on top of the masked bleeding.
  15. The correct answer is C (Hormones exaggerate the response to existing plaque, and this resolves after delivery): Pregnancy gingivitis is an exaggerated inflammatory response to plaque that is already there, driven by estrogen and progesterone increasing vascular permeability and favouring Prevotella intermedia. It begins around the second month, peaks near the eighth, and resolves after delivery provided plaque control is maintained, so the client can be reassured while the emphasis stays on home care. Plaque is still required, so calling the inflammation hormone driven alone removes the reason to keep cleaning. With no bone loss and no attachment loss the condition is gingivitis, and it does not need surgery.
  16. The correct answer is A (Lidocaine 2 percent with epinephrine 1:100 000, aspirating and using the smallest dose): Lidocaine 2 percent with epinephrine 1:100 000 is the local anesthetic of choice in pregnancy, used with aspiration and the smallest effective dose. The vasoconstrictor prolongs the block and slows systemic uptake, so it reduces rather than increases exposure, and a plain solution given in larger volume works against that. Withholding anesthesia leaves the client in pain and makes thorough root planing impossible, which is the opposite of safe care. Nitrous oxide does not provide the local anesthesia root planing needs, and its routine use in pregnancy is generally avoided.
  17. The correct answer is B (Therapy is safe and beneficial, but it does not reliably reduce preterm birth): Periodontal therapy during pregnancy is safe and it improves periodontal outcomes, but the trial evidence does not show that it reduces preterm birth. The honest answer supports treatment on its own merits, which is control of a chronic infection and inflammation, rather than on a promise the evidence cannot support. Overstating the obstetric benefit sets the client up for misplaced blame if the pregnancy ends early. Deferring care is the myth that causes harm, and periodontal therapy works during pregnancy in the same way it works at any other time.
  18. The correct answer is D (A semi-supine position, a wedge under the right hip and frequent short breaks): Prevention is a semi-supine position, a wedge under the right hip so the client is tilted to the left, and frequent short breaks during a long appointment. Tilting to the left moves the uterus off the inferior vena cava and keeps venous return adequate, which is what a wedge under the right hip achieves. A wedge under the left hip tilts the client the wrong way and makes the compression worse. Full supine with no breaks at 30 weeks invites the very episode being prevented, and treating a client bolt upright for an hour is neither necessary nor practical.
  19. The correct answer is A (Book a first visit by 12 months, brush the teeth and lift the lip): The recommendation is a first dental visit by 12 months of age or within six months of the first tooth erupting, and daily caregiver brushing with lift-the-lip screening between visits. This child already has a recognized risk factor in night feeding after eruption, so early anticipatory guidance is exactly what changes the outcome. Waiting until three years or until the dentition is complete means the first assessment happens after early childhood caries has had time to establish. Waiting for permanent molars delays the first visit by more than five years.
  20. The correct answer is C (Avoid mouthrinse under six years, use a pea sized paste and apply fluoride varnish): Fluoride mouthrinse is not recommended below 6 years of age, because a young child swallows a large share of the rinse. For a 4-year-old at active risk the correct combination is a green pea sized amount of fluoridated toothpaste with adult supervision, spitting without rinsing, plus professionally applied fluoride varnish. Changing the rinse frequency does not solve the swallowing problem, since the issue is the child's ability to expectorate rather than the schedule. Removing fluoride altogether abandons the most effective preventive agent for a child with two new lesions.
  21. The correct answer is D (The arrested lesions will stain permanently black, and this cannot be reversed): The defining drawback of silver diamine fluoride is permanent black staining of the arrested lesion, and that must be explained and documented as part of informed consent before it is used. Caregivers who are not told in advance often reject the treatment afterwards, and the discoloured lesion cannot be reversed. Arrest is not achieved by one application alone, since it is applied about twice yearly and reviewed. The technique is to protect the soft tissue, dry the lesion, apply for about one minute and then not rinse, so rinsing it away defeats the application.
  22. The correct answer is B (Tooth 46, because a deep retentive noncavitated fissure is the classic indication): Sealants are indicated for deep, narrow, retentive pits and fissures that are noncavitated, which describes tooth 46 exactly. A shallow self-cleansing groove is a recognized contraindication, because it is at low risk and cleans itself, and an existing restoration means the surface is already restored rather than sealed. An unerupted tooth cannot be cleaned, etched, dried or isolated, so it cannot be sealed at all. Choosing the one tooth that meets the indication is the decision being tested.
  23. The correct answer is C (Re-isolate with fresh dry cotton rolls and confirm a frosty chalky appearance): After rinsing the etchant away, the surface must be re-isolated with fresh dry cotton rolls, because the existing rolls are now wet and would recontaminate the etched enamel. The dried surface is then checked for the frosty chalky white appearance that shows the etch pattern is present, and the tooth is re-etched if that appearance is absent. Placing fluoride gel or a fluoride containing pumice slurry on etched enamel before the resin blocks the porosities the etch just created. Air abrasion at that point is not part of the technique and removes sound enamel.
  24. The correct answer is A (Oil and fluoride residues interfere with etching and reduce resin retention): The surface is cleaned with a toothbrush, a plain pumice slurry or air polishing, using no paste that contains oil or fluoride. Oily residue leaves a film the etchant cannot remove, and fluoride makes the outer enamel more acid resistant, so both reduce the etch pattern and therefore the resin tags that retain the sealant. Fluoride hardens rather than softens the surface, which is the opposite of the stated reason. Staining is not the concern here, and cleaning the fissure is a genuine step, since acid alone does not remove plaque and debris from a deep fissure.
  25. The correct answer is B (Limit the drinks to mealtimes instead of sipping them across the whole shift): Caries risk is driven by the frequency and duration of acid exposure far more than by the total amount of sugar, so a drink sipped over four hours keeps the plaque pH low almost continuously. Confining the drinks to mealtimes collapses many small acid attacks into one, and saliva then has time to buffer and remineralize between them. A diet version removes the sugar but the drink stays acidic and the sipping pattern is unchanged, so erosion and demineralization continue. Brushing straight after an acid exposure abrades softened enamel, and an alcohol containing rinse dries the mouth further.
  26. The correct answer is D (Add interdental brushes, floss threaders and a daily sodium fluoride mouthrinse): Home care around fixed appliances needs interdental brushes for the spaces around brackets and under archwires, floss threaders so flossing becomes physically possible, an orthodontic brush, and a daily 0.05 percent sodium fluoride rinse, with fluoride varnish at recall. Changing the aid rather than repeating the same instruction is what fixes a client who has already abandoned conventional floss. Continuing unchanged for six months allows white spot lesions to form. Long term chlorhexidine causes staining and calculus and does not replace mechanical cleaning, and no irrigation device removes the need to brush.
  27. The correct answer is A (Rinse with water or sodium bicarbonate and brush later with a soft bristled brush): Gastric acid softens the enamel surface, so brushing straight afterwards removes the softened layer and accelerates the erosion. Rinsing with water or a sodium bicarbonate solution clears and neutralizes the acid, and brushing is delayed until the surface has rehardened, using a soft brush and a fluoridated paste. Keeping the firm brush and the immediate timing is precisely what is causing the palatal loss already seen. An acidic rinse adds acid rather than neutralizing it, and stopping oral hygiene for months would let plaque driven pregnancy gingivitis take hold.
  28. The correct answer is B (Use voice control, changing tone rather than volume, then return to tell show do): Voice control means a deliberate change in tone and cadence to regain attention, not a raised volume, and once attention returns the hygienist goes back to tell show do with descriptive praise and a stop signal. Shouting louder escalates the child further and models the behaviour being discouraged. Protective stabilization is a last resort requiring specific informed consent and is restricted in some jurisdictions, so it is not the next step for a routine scaling. Ending the visit without discussion leaves the caregiver without a plan and removes the chance to build cooperation.
  29. The correct answer is C (Primary canines and molars normally exfoliate between nine and twelve years): Primary incisors exfoliate at about 6 to 8 years, while primary canines and molars exfoliate later, between 9 and 12 years, so firm asymptomatic primary canines in a 10-year-old are entirely expected. The permanent mandibular canines 33 and 43 erupt at 9 to 10 years and the maxillary canines 13 and 23 at 11 to 12 years, which matches that exfoliation window. The 6 to 8 year figure belongs to the incisors, and the primary second molars are usually the last primary teeth lost, not the first. Extraction is not indicated for a normally timed retained primary canine.
  30. The correct answer is D (The order is normal, because mandibular canines erupt before their own premolars): In the mandible the canines 33 and 43 erupt at 9 to 10 years, ahead of the premolars 34, 44, 35 and 45, which follow between 10 and 12 years. This child at 11 years with erupted lower canines and unerupted lower premolars is following the normal pattern, so no referral is triggered by the sequence itself. The reverse order applies in the maxilla, where both premolars precede the canines, and that difference is why the caregiver has read something that conflicts with what she sees. Canines and premolars do not erupt at the same age in either arch.
Case C073

Mrs Dumont is 84 years old and lives in a long term care home. She has moderate dementia, takes an antidepressant, a diuretic and an antipsychotic, and reports a constantly dry mouth. She wears a maxillary complete denture day and night. The denture-bearing palatal mucosa is uniformly red. Teeth 33, 43 and 44 show soft, active cervical lesions on exposed root surfaces. Nursing staff report that she coughs when drinking thin fluids. Her daughter holds power of attorney for personal care.

  1. The correct answer is B (Denture stomatitis from Candida albicans, so the denture itself must be disinfected): erythema that is confined to the denture-bearing mucosa and stops at the denture border is the classic presentation of denture stomatitis, driven by continuous wear and poor appliance hygiene. Candida albicans colonizes the fitting surface of the acrylic, so treating only the mucosa leaves the reservoir intact and the redness returns. Management is daily denture brushing, overnight removal and storage in water or a cleanser. Traumatic ulceration from an overextended flange produces a localized sore at one border rather than uniform redness across the whole bearing area.
  2. The correct answer is D (Frequent water sips, an alcohol free saliva substitute and 1.1 percent sodium fluoride): her dryness is almost certainly medication induced, since antidepressants, diuretics and antipsychotics all reduce salivary flow, so comfort measures and caries protection must run together. Prescription strength 1.1 percent sodium fluoride targets the root lesions, which demineralize at a higher pH than enamel. Alcohol containing rinses dry the mucosa further and should be stopped. Lemon glycerin swabs and sweetened drinks add acid and fermentable sugar to already exposed root surfaces.
  3. The correct answer is A (Apply 38 percent silver diamine fluoride after explaining the permanent black staining): silver diamine fluoride arrests active caries when applied twice yearly, combining antimicrobial silver with fluoride remineralization, and it needs only about one minute of chair time at the bedside. The permanent black staining of the arrested lesion is its defining drawback and must be explained and documented as part of consent. Sealants are for retentive pits and fissures, not for cavitated cervical root lesions. Deferring everything until transport can be arranged lets active lesions progress in a client who is already at very high risk.
  4. The correct answer is C (Obtain consent from the daughter and still involve the client in the process): where dementia has removed the capacity to weigh the consequences of a treatment, consent comes from the substitute decision maker, and here that is the daughter holding power of attorney for personal care. Loss of legal capacity does not remove the client from the conversation, so she is still told what will happen and her expressed preferences are respected. Consent for admission to the home is not consent for a specific clinical procedure. The home director has no authority to consent on a resident's behalf, and moderate dementia will not resolve with waiting.
  5. The correct answer is C (Semi-upright position, high volume evacuation and frequent pauses to clear fluid): dysphagia raises aspiration risk, and the two modifications that matter most are keeping the client semi-upright so fluid drains away from the airway, and removing water and debris continuously with high volume evacuation. Regular pauses let the client clear and swallow rather than accumulating fluid in the pharynx. Laying a dysphagic client flat and flooding the mouth without adequate suction is exactly the combination that causes aspiration. Aspiration pneumonia is a leading cause of death in long term care, which is why daily oral care and safe positioning both matter here.
Case C074

Mr Okafor is 62 years old and attends for periodontal maintenance. He has type 2 diabetes with a glycated hemoglobin of 9.4 percent recorded last week, atrial fibrillation treated with apixaban, and hypertension treated with amlodipine. Blood pressure today is 146/88 mmHg. He last ate at 07:00 and took his medications as usual. Generalized probing depths of 5 to 6 mm bleed on probing, and the interdental papillae in the mandibular anterior region are firm and enlarged.

  1. The correct answer is A (Rebook to a morning appointment after his usual meal and confirm blood glucose): a client on an oral hypoglycemic agent who skips lunch is at real risk of hypoglycemia late in the day, and a morning appointment after eating and taking his usual medication is the standard modification. Confirming blood glucose before starting establishes that he is safe to treat, with a value below 4.0 mmol/L treated using 15 g of fast carbohydrate and rechecked after 15 minutes. Asking a diabetic client to fast before routine dental hygiene care actively creates the emergency being avoided. Poor glycemic control is a reason to prioritize non-surgical therapy, not to withhold it.
  2. The correct answer is B (Advise him to continue the drug and control bleeding with local measures): anticoagulation is generally not interrupted for routine dental hygiene care, because the thromboembolic risk of stopping the drug in a client with atrial fibrillation exceeds a bleeding risk that is local and manageable. Bleeding is controlled with atraumatic technique, quadrant rather than full mouth appointments, gauze pressure and a tranexamic acid mouthrinse. A hygienist never advises a client to stop or reduce an anticoagulant, since that decision belongs to the prescriber. The INR monitors warfarin and does not usefully measure the effect of a direct oral anticoagulant such as apixaban.
  3. The correct answer is D (Gauze pressure, a tranexamic acid rinse and acetaminophen rather than an NSAID): local hemostasis is achieved with firm gauze pressure and an antifibrinolytic rinse, which stabilize the clot without any change to his anticoagulant. Acetaminophen is preferred for analgesia because non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs add antiplatelet effect on top of anticoagulation and increase bleeding. Aspirin carries the same problem and is a poor choice here. Vigorous or hot rinsing dislodges the clot that has just formed, and escalating to electrosurgery and the emergency department is a large overreaction to expected post-scaling ooze.
  4. The correct answer is B (Amlodipine associated enlargement, so intensify plaque control and consult the physician): calcium channel blockers such as nifedipine and amlodipine are a well recognized cause of drug influenced gingival enlargement, alongside phenytoin and cyclosporine. The enlargement is plaque dependent, so meticulous plaque control plus professional debridement is the first intervention, with the physician consulted about whether an alternative antihypertensive is suitable. Surgical reduction is considered only if the enlargement persists after inflammation is controlled. A hereditary condition would have been present long before the drug was started, and pregnancy is not a possible cause in this client.
  5. The correct answer is A (Poor glycemic control worsens healing and periodontitis, so shorten his recall interval): glycated hemoglobin reflects average glycemia over roughly three months, and a value of 9.4 percent against a target of 7.0 percent or less signals impaired healing, greater infection risk and more severe periodontal breakdown. The appropriate response is to consult his physician, prioritize non-surgical therapy and shorten the maintenance interval so inflammation is controlled between visits. Periodontal therapy may modestly improve glycemic measures but it does not replace medical management. Refusing care outright would leave an infection that makes his glycemic control worse.
Case C075

Nadia is 27 years old, is autistic and has an intellectual disability. She uses a manual wheelchair and can bear weight only briefly with support. She attends with a support worker who assists with daily brushing. She becomes distressed by bright light and by high pitched sound, and she left her last appointment before any assessment was completed. Generalized marginal inflammation is visible, with heavy plaque along the gingival margins of teeth 16, 26, 36 and 46.

  1. The correct answer is D (Run short repeated visits with the same clinician, adding one new step each time): desensitization works by keeping the clinician, the room and the appointment time constant while one small new element is introduced at each visit, so the client learns that nothing unexpected happens. Predictability is the active ingredient, which is why familiarity matters more than speed. Protective stabilization is a last resort that requires specific informed consent and is restricted in some jurisdictions, so it is not the opening move. General anesthesia carries its own risks and should not be chosen before behavioural approaches have been tried.
  2. The correct answer is C (Dimmed light or sunglasses, noise reducing headphones and quiet hand instrumentation): each modification maps directly onto a reported sensitivity, reducing visual, auditory and vibratory input rather than adding to it. Hand instrumentation avoids the high pitched ultrasonic noise that she finds distressing, and headphones or sunglasses give her some control over the environment. Louder reassurance and background music increase the auditory load that is causing the problem. Removing all light and all explanation is also wrong, since autistic clients are supported by literal language, visual schedules and predictable steps, not by silence and speed.
  3. The correct answer is A (Ask her how she prefers to transfer and offer treatment in her own wheelchair): the client and her support worker know how she transfers safely every day, so asking first is both respectful and the safest starting point. Many clients can be treated directly in their own wheelchair with a headrest support, which avoids the risk that any transfer carries. Lifting a client under the arms alone risks injury to her shoulders and to the clinician, and it is never acceptable. Transferring with the brakes unlocked allows the chair to roll away mid-transfer, and refusing care for lack of independent transfer is discriminatory.
  4. The correct answer is D (Stand behind the seated client, support the head, and use hand over hand): standing behind a seated client with the head supported gives the caregiver stable access and clear vision of the gingival margins, and hand over hand technique keeps the client participating rather than having brushing done to her. Chaining and bridging preserve that participation in the same way. Reducing brushing to once weekly abandons plaque control altogether. Brushing quickly from the front with a firm brush gives poor access, risks traumatizing the tissue, and is more likely to provoke distress in a client with sensory sensitivities.
  5. The correct answer is B (Secondary dentin and attrition are age changes, while tooth loss is not): attrition, deposition of secondary dentin with a smaller pulp chamber and reduced sensitivity, darker tooth colour, sclerosed tubules and thinner mucosa are genuine age changes. Recession, xerostomia and tooth loss are consequences of disease, medication and trauma, and treating them as inevitable is the reasoning error the exam targets. Accepting tooth loss as normal removes the case for preventive care in exactly the group that benefits most. Sclerosed tubules are an expected age finding and by themselves need no restorative referral.
  6. The correct answer is C (Root surfaces demineralize at a higher pH than enamel, so daily fluoride matters more): exposed cementum and dentin begin to demineralize at a critical pH of roughly 6.2 to 6.7, whereas enamel holds until about 5.5. Root surfaces are therefore lost at acid levels that leave enamel untouched, which is exactly the pattern described. Management combines prescription strength 1.1 percent sodium fluoride, varnish, silver diamine fluoride where indicated and shorter recall intervals. Treating the lesions as simple abrasion would miss the active demineralization that is driving them.
  7. The correct answer is D (Build up the handle with foam tubing or provide a wide handled power brush): when the barrier is dexterity rather than knowledge, the aid has to change, not the instruction. Foam tubing, a tennis ball on the handle, an elastic strap for a client who cannot grip, or a wide handled power brush all restore control of the brush head. Repeating the same instruction more slowly has already failed three times and will fail again. Chlorhexidine is an adjunct that does not remove plaque mechanically, and a firmer brush increases the risk of abrasion and recession.
  8. The correct answer is A (Short morning visits, a quiet room, one instruction at a time, familiar caregiver): clients with dementia typically cope better earlier in the day, and a quiet environment with a single instruction at a time reduces the cognitive load that triggers agitation. A familiar caregiver provides reassurance and can continue the same approach at home using bridging and chaining. Long appointments late in the day with background noise reliably worsen agitation. Posted written handouts assume reading and recall she no longer has, and routine sedation is a disproportionate response when environmental modification works.
  9. The correct answer is C (Brush the denture daily, remove it overnight and store it in water or cleanser): mechanical brushing removes the biofilm that causes denture stomatitis, and overnight removal gives the mucosa a rest period that continuous wear denies it. Storage in water or a proprietary cleanser prevents the acrylic drying and distorting. Hot water warps acrylic and household bleach corrodes a cobalt chromium framework, so neither belongs in a home routine. Continuous wear is one of the strongest risk factors for the very inflammation she already has.
  10. The correct answer is B (Daily oral care is associated with less aspiration pneumonia among residents): reducing the oral bacterial load lowers the burden that is aspirated by residents with impaired swallowing, and this link is why staff education in daily oral care is treated as a clinical intervention rather than a comfort measure. That outcome is measurable and meaningful to a care home manager. Bone already lost to periodontitis is not regained by brushing. Professional care is still needed for calculus removal and assessment, and denture stomatitis recurs unless the appliance itself is cleaned and left out overnight.
  11. The correct answer is D (Raise the chair in stages and let her sit upright before she stands): this is orthostatic hypotension, a common effect of diuretics, antihypertensives and other medications in older adults, and it is prevented by giving the circulation time to adjust. Raising the chair in stages and pausing upright before standing is the standard modification, with support offered as she gets up. Standing quickly from supine produces exactly the symptoms described. A hygienist never advises a client to withhold a prescribed medication, and an isolated postural symptom with a normal seated pressure does not warrant emergency cardiac referral.
  12. The correct answer is B (Osteoradionecrosis risk is lifelong, so the radiation oncologist should be consulted): irradiated bone remains hypovascular, hypocellular and hypoxic indefinitely, so the risk does not expire once the acute effects settle. Any extraction within the irradiated field needs consultation with the radiation oncologist before it proceeds. Risk is greater in the mandible than the maxilla, and it climbs sharply above roughly 60 Gy, which this client has exceeded. Extractions needed before radiation are completed at least two to three weeks beforehand precisely because post-radiation surgery is so much riskier.
  13. The correct answer is A (Daily 1.1 percent sodium fluoride, three month recalls and xerostomia management): post-radiation clients face lifelong hyposalivation and rapid cervical and root caries, so they need prescription strength daily fluoride rather than an over the counter paste alone. A three month recall interval catches new lesions early, and saliva substitutes, frequent water and avoidance of drying rinses address the underlying dryness. Twice yearly review is far too infrequent for the rate of breakdown described. Alcohol based rinses worsen the dryness, and peroxide whitening on exposed root surfaces would add sensitivity without reducing caries risk.
  14. The correct answer is C (Proceed, since non-surgical dental hygiene care is safe and protective for her): medication-related osteonecrosis of the jaw is defined by exposed bone persisting more than eight weeks in a client on antiresorptive or antiangiogenic therapy without prior jaw radiation, and the risk at oral osteoporosis doses is very low compared with intravenous oncology dosing. Non-surgical periodontal care reduces the infection and extraction risk that actually precipitate osteonecrosis, so it protects her. Drug holidays are not routinely indicated and are a physician decision in any case. Prophylactic intravenous antibiotics have no role before routine scaling.
  15. The correct answer is A (Consult the transplant team, reinforce plaque control and defer elective debridement): transplant recipients defer elective care for roughly three to six months after surgery while immunosuppression is at its heaviest, and at seven weeks he is well inside that window. Contacting the transplant team establishes what care is safe now, and meticulous plaque control is both safe and directly useful, because cyclosporine induced enlargement is plaque dependent. A hygienist never advises a client to stop an immunosuppressant, since graft rejection is the consequence. Surgical reduction is considered only after inflammation is controlled and the medical team agrees.
  16. The correct answer is D (Defer elective care near the neutrophil nadir and check counts with his oncologist): the neutrophil nadir falls at roughly 7 to 14 days after a chemotherapy cycle, so day 10 is the period of greatest infection and bleeding risk. Current neutrophil and platelet counts obtained from the oncologist determine whether instrumentation is safe, and elective care waits for count recovery. Palliative measures for mucositis and gentle plaque control can continue in the meantime. Treating on the day without counts or antibiotic reasoning is unsafe, and telling him care is never possible during chemotherapy is simply wrong, since sources of infection are ideally eliminated two to three weeks before treatment begins.
  17. The correct answer is C (Book the first appointment of the day and remove every latex item beforehand): rapid hives and lip swelling suggest an immediate IgE-mediated latex reaction, and cross-reactivity with banana, avocado, kiwi and chestnut supports that. The first appointment of the day minimizes airborne latex protein that has accumulated from earlier glove use, and gloves, dam, prophy cups and bite blocks must all be checked and replaced with latex free alternatives. Powdered latex gloves are the worst possible choice, because the powder carries latex protein into the air. Premedication does not make continued latex exposure acceptable.
  18. The correct answer is B (Clear the mouth, lower to a supported supine position, turn the head, time the event): instruments, suction tips and debris are removed first so nothing can be aspirated, then the client is lowered to a supported supine position where a fall is impossible and the head is turned to the side to let secretions drain. Timing matters because convulsive activity beyond five minutes is status epilepticus and needs emergency services. Nothing is placed in the mouth and the limbs are not restrained, since both cause injury. Anything given by mouth to an unresponsive client risks aspiration.
  19. The correct answer is D (Reschedule beyond 24 hours and avoid any vasoconstrictor containing anesthetic today): cocaine and methamphetamine are potent sympathomimetics, and adding epinephrine within 24 hours of use can precipitate a hypertensive crisis, arrhythmia or myocardial infarction. His tachycardia shows the drug is still acting. Rescheduling is a clinical safety decision, delivered with non-stigmatizing language and without judgement, and the disclosure itself should be acknowledged as helpful. Increasing the vasoconstrictor dose makes the danger worse, and rushing through two quadrants without anesthesia neither removes the cardiovascular risk nor serves the client.
  20. The correct answer is A (Avoid the inferior alveolar block, use infiltration, and consult his hematologist first): an inferior alveolar nerve block in a client with hemophilia or von Willebrand disease can cause bleeding into the pterygomandibular space, and the resulting hematoma can compromise the airway. Infiltration or intraligamentary techniques deliver anesthesia with far less risk. The hematologist advises on factor replacement or antifibrinolytic cover before instrumentation, so that consultation happens before the appointment rather than after a complication. Root planing in an inflamed quadrant certainly does bleed, which is why planning matters here.
  21. The correct answer is B (Defer elective care and arrange prompt medical assessment for this client today): elective dental hygiene care is deferred and urgent medical assessment sought at readings of about 180/110 mmHg or higher, whether or not the client has symptoms. Feeling well is not reassurance, because severe hypertension is typically silent until an event occurs. The reading has already been confirmed on a repeat measurement, so the decision does not depend on further readings in the operatory. Proceeding without anesthesia does not lower the cardiovascular risk, and a one year delay in assessment would be indefensible.
  22. The correct answer is C (Postpone elective care for about four to six weeks and consult his physician): the myocardium is electrically and mechanically unstable in the weeks immediately after infarction, so elective care waits roughly four to six weeks and the physician confirms readiness. Urgent problems are managed with medical liaison rather than simply postponed. Symptom free does not mean recovered, since most of the risk in this period is silent. Permanently refusing care is wrong, and omitting blood pressure measurement removes the single most useful piece of monitoring for a client with this history.
  23. The correct answer is A (Confirm his nitroglycerin is present and in date, keep appointments short and calm): the client's own medication should be at chairside, checked for expiry, because nitroglycerin loses potency and an out of date spray may fail when it is needed. Short appointments, effective stress reduction, good pain control and blood pressure monitoring reduce the chance of an anginal episode occurring at all. Medication kept in another room is not available in the seconds that matter. Prophylactic double dosing is a prescribing decision that no hygienist makes, and it risks profound hypotension.
  24. The correct answer is B (Rule out caries, a cracked tooth, a defective restoration and irreversible pulpitis): dentin hypersensitivity is a diagnosis of exclusion, so other causes of thermal pain are eliminated before any desensitizing agent is chosen. Missing a cracked tooth or an irreversible pulpitis while applying varnish delays the treatment the client actually needs. Once other causes are excluded, management follows the hydrodynamic theory by occluding open tubules or blocking nerve transmission. Whitening would add sensitivity rather than clarify the diagnosis.
  25. The correct answer is D (It depolarizes the nerve and needs two to four weeks of consistent daily use): potassium nitrate works by raising extracellular potassium around the pulpal nerve, which depolarizes it and blocks transmission, and that effect accumulates only with regular use over several weeks. Four days is far too early to judge it, and stopping now is the most common reason home desensitizing therapy appears to fail. Agents that occlude tubules directly, such as varnish, oxalates, calcium phosphates or arginine with calcium carbonate, can be added if relief is still incomplete afterwards. An alcohol containing rinse would only irritate the exposed surfaces.
  26. The correct answer is C (Whitening will not lighten composite, and bonding should wait about two weeks): peroxide oxidizes chromogens within tooth structure and has no effect on composite or ceramic, so existing anterior restorations will look darker once the surrounding enamel lightens and will need replacing to the new shade. Bonding is delayed about two weeks after whitening, because residual oxygen in the tooth inhibits resin polymerization and weakens the bond. Replacing the restorations the same day would therefore fail on both shade and bond strength. Exposed root surfaces are a caution for whitening, not an indication, since sensitivity is the most common adverse effect.
  27. The correct answer is B (Protect soft tissue with petroleum jelly, dry, apply for one minute, do not rinse): petroleum jelly on the lips and adjacent gingiva prevents the temporary greyish staining that silver diamine fluoride causes on soft tissue. The lesion is isolated and dried so the agent contacts the demineralized dentin directly, applied for about one minute, and left unrinsed so it continues to act. Rinsing or polishing immediately afterwards removes the agent before it can work. Etching has no role, and applying over plaque prevents the silver and fluoride reaching the lesion at all.
  28. The correct answer is A (Titanium or plastic instruments, glycine powder polishing and neutral sodium fluoride): bleeding on probing without bone loss is peri-implant mucositis, which is reversible with biofilm removal, so the aim is thorough debridement that does not damage the implant surface. Titanium, plastic, resin or graphite instruments avoid the scratching that stainless steel causes, glycine powder is gentler on the surface than sodium bicarbonate, and neutral fluoride is used because acidulated preparations etch titanium. Annual probing alone would let reversible inflammation progress to bone loss unnoticed.
  29. The correct answer is C (This is peri-implantitis, since bone loss has been added to the inflammation): the distinction between peri-implant mucositis and peri-implantitis is whether progressive bone loss accompanies the inflammation, and suppuration with 3 mm of loss against a baseline film settles it. Mucositis is reversible with biofilm control, whereas peri-implantitis needs a managed intervention and continued monitoring, which is why the baseline radiograph and baseline probing depths are recorded at placement. Physiological remodelling does not produce suppuration or continuing loss of this magnitude.
  30. The correct answer is D (Review her plaque control and adherence, then check for residual calculus): non-response is worked through in a fixed order, and plaque control comes first because most failures are home care failures, which an unchanged plaque score here confirms. The next check is the clinician's own therapy, meaning residual calculus, deep or furcated sites and root anatomy that could not be reached. Only after those, and after systemic contributors such as smoking and glycemic control, is the diagnosis itself questioned. Repeating an identical appointment changes nothing, and referring for surgery over uncontrolled plaque would produce a poor surgical result.
